\end{array}\\
\textsf{{A}}/\phi&&(\textsf{{A}}/\theta)/(\phi/\theta)\end{array}

Figure 2.2:
Illustration of Proposition
2.3.6

The following proposition could be regarded as a reverse of Proposition
2.3.6
.

Proposition 2.3.7
.

Let
\theta
be a congruence on
A
. Then for any congruence
\phi
on
\textsf{{A}}/\penalty\theta
, there is a congruence
\eta
on
A
such that
\theta\subseteq\eta
and the map
(a/\penalty\theta)/\penalty\phi\mapsto a/\penalty\eta
is an isomorphism of
(\textsf{{A}}/\penalty\theta)/\penalty\phi
onto
\textsf{{A}}/\penalty\eta
.

Proof.

First of all, we note that the map
(a/\penalty\theta)/\penalty\phi\mapsto a/\penalty\eta
, where
a\in|\textsf{{A}}|
, is defined correctly. For, for any congruence
\eta
on
A
with
\theta\subseteq\eta
, if
a/\penalty\theta=b/\penalty\theta
, for some
a,b\in|\textsf{{A}}|
, then
a/\penalty\eta=b/\penalty\eta
.

Now we aim to define
\eta
. It can be done as follows.

(a,b)\in\eta\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ (a/\penalty\theta,b/\penalty\theta)\in\phi.

It is easily seen that
\eta
is a congruence on
A
and
\theta\subseteq\eta
. Also, it is not difficult to see that the map

(a/\penalty\theta)/\penalty\phi\mapsto a/\penalty\eta
is an isomorphism.
∎

Let
\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
. We denote by

\textsf{{A}}:=\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i}

the
direct product
of the algebras
\textsf{{A}}_{i}
, which is an algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
, whose carrier
|\textsf{{A}}|
is the Cartesian product of
\{|\textsf{{A}}_{i}|\}_{i\in I}
and each
n
-ary operation
f
is defined pointwise: for any
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in|\textsf{{A}}|
,

f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}):=(f((x_{1})_{i},\ldots,(x_{n})_{i}))_{i\in I},

and each
\mathcal{L}
-constant
c:=(c_{i})_{i\in I}
.

If all
\textsf{{A}}_{i}
are isomorphic to one another, we call
A
a
direct power
.

Proposition 2.3.8

(comp.
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, chapter II,theorem 7.9)
.

Given two sets
\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{\textsf{{A}}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
with
I\cap J=\varnothing
, the algebras
\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i}\times\prod_{j\in J}\textsf{{A}}_{j}
and
\prod_{k\in I\cup J}\textsf{{A}}_{k}
are isomorphic with respect to the map

f(a,b)=(c_{k})_{k\in I\cup J}
, where
c_{k}=a_{k}
, if
k\in I
, and
c_{k}=b_{k}
, if
k\in J
.

A subalgebra
B
of the direct product
\textsf{{A}}=\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i}
is called a
subdirect product
of the family
\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
if each projection
p_{i}:x\mapsto x_{i}
is an epimorphism of
B
onto
\textsf{{A}}_{i}
. If an isomorphic image of
B
is a subalgebra of
\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i}
, we speak of a
subdirect embedding
.

An algebra
A
is
subdirectly irreducible
if
A
is trivial or
\textbf{Con}\,\textsf{{A}}\setminus\{\Delta\}
has a least congruence. It is equivalent to say that
A
is subdirectly irreducible if, and only if, for any subdirect embedding
f:\textsf{{A}}\longrightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i}
, there is
i\in I
such that
p_{i}\circ f:\textsf{{A}}\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}_{i}
is an isomorphism. Next we define a map
h:x\mapsto(f(x),g(x))
, for any
x\in|\textsf{{B}}|
. It is clear that
h
is a homomorphism from
B
into
\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i}\times\textsf{{A}}
.

Proposition 2.3.9

(Birkhoff)
.

Every algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
is isomorphic to a subdirect product of subdirectly irreducible algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
.

We denote by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{L}}
the abstract class
1
1

1

The term
abstract class
(of algebras) is defined in the next subsection.
of all subdirectly irreducible algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
.

References

1.

[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]

2.

[
Cohn, 1981
]

3.

[
Grätzer, 2008
]

2.3.2
Class operators

Given a class
\mathcal{K}
of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
, we employ the following class operators:

•

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{I}\mathcal{K}
if, and only if,
A
is an isomorphic image of some algebra of
\mathcal{K}
;

•

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{S}\mathcal{K}
if, and only if,
A
is isomorphic to a subalgebra of some algebra of
\mathcal{K}
;

•

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{H}\mathcal{K}
if, and only if,
A
is a homomorphic image of some algebra of
\mathcal{K}
;

•

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}
if, and only if,
A
is isomorphic to the direct product of a nonempty set of algebras in
\mathcal{K}
;

•

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{P_{s}}\mathcal{K}
if, and only if,
A
is a subdirect product of a nonempty set of algebras in
\mathcal{K}
;

•

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{P_{r}}\mathcal{K}
if, and only if,
A
is isomorphic to a reduced product
2
2

2

The notion of reduced product is defined in Section
2.4
.
of a nonempty set of algebras in
\mathcal{K}
;

•

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{P_{u}}\mathcal{K}
if, and only if,
A
is isomorphic to an ultraproduct
3
3

3

The notion of ultraproduct is defined in Section
2.4
.
of a nonempty set of algebras in
\mathcal{K}
.

A class
\mathcal{K}
is called
abstract
if, along with each algebra in it,
\mathcal{K}
contains its isomorphic images; in other words,
\mathbf{I}\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}
. We note that, applying any of the above operators to
\mathcal{K}
, we obtain an abstract class.

In the sequel, we will find useful another class operator.
Proposition
2.3.9
induces the following definition.

Given a class
\mathcal{K}
of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
,

\begin{array}[]{rl}\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}&\text{there is $\textsf{{B}}\in\mathcal{K}$ and there is $\mathcal{K}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{L}}$ such that $\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{K}_{0}$}\\
&\text{and $\textsf{{B}}$ is a subdirect product of all algebras of $\mathcal{K}_{0}$}.\end{array}

In terms of operators
\mathbf{P_{s}}
and
\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}
, Proposition
2.3.9
can be reformulated as follows.

Proposition 2.3.10
.

Given a class
\mathcal{K}
of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{P_{s}}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

Let
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{K}
. In virtue of Proposition
2.3.9
, there is a set
\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that

A
is a subdirect product of
\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. By definition,
\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}
. ∎

For any class operators
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
, we denote

O_{1}\leq O_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\text{for any class $\mathcal{K}$, $O_{1}\mathcal{K}\subseteq O_{2}\mathcal{K}$}.

Proposition 2.3.11

(
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, chapter II, lemma 9.2 )
.

The following inequalities hold:
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{H}\leq\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}
,
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S}\leq\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}
,

\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}\leq\mathbf{H}\mathbf{P}
, and
\mathbf{P_{s}}\leq\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}
. Also,
\mathbf{H}\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{H}
,
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{S}
, and
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}
.

There is a simple characterization of the class operator
\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}
. In fact, it collects all subdirectly irreducible homomorphic images of any algebra of a given class
\mathcal{K}
.

Proposition 2.3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a class of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
. Then
\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}=(\mathbf{H}\mathcal{K})\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}
. Then, by the definition of the
\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}
operator and by the definition of subdirect products, the algebra
A
is subdirectly irreducible and is a homomorphic image of an algebra from
\mathcal{K}
.

Now assume that
\textsf{{A}}\in(\mathbf{H}\mathcal{K})\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{L}}
. This implies that there is an algebra
B
such that
A
is a homomorphic image with respect to an epimorphism
g:\textsf{{B}}\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}
.
Further, in virtue of Proposition
2.3.13
, there is a set
\{\textsf{{A}}\}_{i\in I}
of subdirectly irreducible algebras and a subdirect embedding
f:\textsf{{B}}\longrightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i}
.
In virtue of Proposition
2.3.8
, the map

h:\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i}\times\textsf{{A}}\rightarrow\prod\{\textsf{{A}}^{\prime}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{A}}^{\prime}\in\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cup\{\textsf{{A}}\}\}

where
h(a,b)=(c)_{k\in I\cup\{j\}}
with
j\notin I
,
c_{k}=c_{k}
, if
k\in I
, and

c_{k}=b
, if
k=j
, is an isomorphism.

Next we define a map:

f^{\ast}:x\mapsto(f(x),g(x)),

for any
x\in|\textsf{{B}}|
. It is clearly seen that the map
h\circ f^{\ast}
is a subdirect embedding of
B
into
\prod\{\textsf{{A}}^{\prime}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{A}}^{\prime}\in\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cup\{\textsf{{A}}\}\}
.
∎

References

1.

[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]

2.

[
Grätzer, 2008
]

2.3.3
Term algebra, varieties and quasi-varieties

Below we give algebraic characterizations of
equational classes
and of
implicational classes
of algebras of the same type. These characterizations require formulas of first-order. We defer the definition of a first-order formula to Section
2.4
. Here we use the first-order formulas of two special kinds.

If we want to represent the signature of the algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
symbolically, we introduce for each operation from
\mathcal{F}
and for each constant from
\mathcal{C}
the symbols, usually denoted by the same letters, corresponding to those operations and constants. Thus we generate the set of function symbols
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
and the set of constant symbols
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}}
. In addition, we introduce a nonempty set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
of (individual) variables.
Using these categories of symbols, we generate the set of
terms
, by including in the latter all variables and constants, as well as the strings that are obtained according to the following rule: if
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
are terms and
F
is a functional symbol of arity
n
, then
Ft_{1}\ldots t_{n}
is a term. The last procedure can be regarded as an operation on the set of terms with the constant symbols as 0-ary operations. Thus we obtain an algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
, which is called a
term algebra
and denoted by
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
. The specificity of the term algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
is that, given any algebra
A
of type
\mathcal{L}
and an arbitrary map
f:\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow|\textsf{{A}}|
,
f
can be extended to a homomorphism

\hat{f}:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}
. It is customarily to denote the initial map
f
and its extension
\hat{f}
by the letter
f
. The property just described is called a
universal mapping property
of algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}

relative to
the class of all algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
. Below we will see that some algebras have the universal property relative to special subclasses of the class of all algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
.

In this section, we describe first-order languages with equality and without additional predicate symbols. More information about first-order languages and their semantics can be found in the references at the end of Section
2.4
. We use a unifying notation ‘
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
’ to denote first-order languages which are discussed in this subsection and in Section
2.4
.
In Chapter
9
, we will deal with first-order languages of a different grammar, called there
\mathcal{Q}
-languages.

Any
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
consists of a set
\mathcal{V}
of individual variables, logical constants ‘
\boldsymbol{\land}
’, ‘
\boldsymbol{\lor}
, ‘
\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}
’, ‘
\boldsymbol{\neg}
’, and quantifiers ‘
\boldsymbol{\forall}
’ and ‘
\boldsymbol{\exists}
’, as well as parentheses ‘
(
’ and
‘
)
’.

The formal language
\mathcal{L}
of terms is an integral part of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
. In this subsection, we use a simplified version of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
, which is not reflected in the notion of a term but is reflected in the notion of a formula.

If
t
and
s
are
\mathcal{L}
-terms, then
t\approx s
, where
\approx
is regarded as the only (binary) predicate symbol (of this version of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
), is a first-order formula; we also call any such formula an
equality
. If
A
and
B
are first-order formulas, then:

(1)

(A\boldsymbol{\land}B)
is a first-order formula;

(2)

(A\boldsymbol{\lor}B)
is a first-order formula;

(3)

(A\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}B)
is a first-order formula;

(4)

\boldsymbol{\neg}A
is a first-order formula;

(5)

\boldsymbol{\forall}xA
, where
x\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, is a first-order formula;

(6)

\boldsymbol{\exists}xA
, where
x\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, is a first-order formula.

Only equalities and the formulas that can be built according to (1)–(6) are
first-order formulas
(or
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-
formulas
or simply
formulas
) of (this simplified version of)
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

A formula of the form (5) or (6) can be a constituent of a larger formula. Whether a formula of the form (5) or (6) is considered individually of as a constituent of a larger formula,
A
is called the scope of the quantifier
\boldsymbol{\forall}
or of the quantifier
\boldsymbol{\exists}
, respectively; and the variable
x
following the quantifier, as well any occurrence of
x
in
A
is called
bound
.
The occurrences of variables that are not in the scope of any quantifier with the equal variable are called
free
. If an
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-formula is closed, it is called an
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-
sentence
. A formula which does not contain quantifiers is called
quantifier-free
.

Let
A
be a formula, all free variables of which constitute a set
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
. The formula
\forall x_{1}\ldots\forall\ x_{n}A
is called a
universal closure
of
A
. It should be clear that if the formula
A
has more than one free variable, it has more than one universal closures. However, from a semantic viewpoint, all these closures have equal truth values in all interpretations.
4
4

4

This is explained in Section
2.4
.
Thus, at least semantically, they are indistinguishable. Therefore, we use the term
the universal closure
of a formula
A
, which may have several instances. We denote the universal closure of
A
by
\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}A
. In particular, the universal closure
\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\,\alpha\approx\beta
of an equality
\alpha\approx\beta
is called an
identity
. A formula of the form

(\alpha_{1}\approx\beta_{1}\boldsymbol{\land}\ldots\boldsymbol{\land}\alpha_{n}\approx\beta_{n})\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\beta,

where
n\geq 0
, is called a
quasi-equality
, and its universal closure

\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}((\alpha_{1}\approx\beta_{1}\boldsymbol{\land}\ldots\boldsymbol{\land}\alpha_{n}\approx\beta_{n})\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\beta)

a
quasi-identity
. In particular, each equality is a quasi-equality, and each identity is a quasi-identity, with
n=0
in both cases.

Given an
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-equality
\alpha\approx\beta
and an algebra
A
of type
\mathcal{L}
, we say that
\alpha\approx\beta
is
valid
in
A
, symbolically

\textsf{{A}}\models\alpha\approx\beta
, if for any homomorphism
v:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}
,
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
.

Similarly, a quasi-equality, say
(\ast)
, is
valid
in
A
, symbolically

\textsf{{A}}\models((\alpha_{1}\approx\beta_{1}\boldsymbol{\land}\ldots\boldsymbol{\land}\alpha_{n}\approx\beta_{n})\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\beta),

if for any
v
,
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
, whenever all the equalities
v[\alpha_{i}]=v[\beta_{i}]
, where
1\leq i\leq n
, hold.

Remark 2.3.1
.

In Section
2.4
, we will introduce a relation
\langle\textsf{{A}},\Delta\rangle\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}A
, where

\langle\textsf{{A}},\Delta\rangle
is an expansion of an algebra
A
and
\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}A
is an identity of quasi-identity. From our explanation there, it will be clear that

\textsf{{A}}\models A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \langle\textsf{{A}},\Delta\rangle\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}A.

This is supposed to show a relationship between equalities and identities, on the one hand, and quasi-equalities and quasi-identities, on the other.

Let
\Sigma
be a nonempty set of equalities or quasi-equalities. We write

\textsf{{A}}\models\Sigma

if
\textsf{{A}}\models A
, for all
A\in\Sigma
.

A nonempty class
\mathcal{K}
of
\mathcal{L}
-algebras is an
equational class
if there is a set
\Sigma
of identities such that

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{K}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \textsf{{A}}\models\Sigma.

There is a very well-known and useful characterization of equational classes due G. Birkhoff.

Proposition 2.3.13

(Birkhoff, cf.
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, § 13, theorem 1)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a nonempty class of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
. Then
\mathcal{K}
is an equational class if, and only if, the following conditions are satisfied:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\mathbf{S}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K};\\
(\text{b})&\mathbf{H}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K};\\
(\text{c})&\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K}.\end{array}

A class
\mathcal{K}
is called a
variety
if the closures (a)–(c) of Proposition
2.3.13
hold. Thus for a class to be equational and to be a variety are two equivalent conditions.

Proposition 2.3.14

(Tarski, cf.
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, § 13, theorems 2)
.

The smallest variety containing a class
\mathcal{K}
equals
\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}
.

Given a class
\mathcal{K}
of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
, the variety
\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}
is said to be
generated
by
\mathcal{K}
, or one can also say that
\mathcal{K}

generates
this variety.

Here is another useful characterization.

Proposition 2.3.15

(
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, chapter II, theorem 11.12)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a class of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
. Then

\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}=\mathbf{H}\mathbf{P_{s}}\mathcal{K}

We conclude our discuss about equational classes with the following important property formulated in terms of variety.

Proposition 2.3.16
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a class of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
. Then the variety generated by
\mathcal{K}
coincides with the variety generated by
\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}
, that is,
\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}=\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

Our starting point is the inclusion
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{P_{s}}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}
which is true in virtue of Proposition
2.3.10
. Then, we continue:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{P_{s}}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}&\Longrightarrow\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}\quad[\text{by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:class-operators-inequalities}}]\\
&\Longrightarrow\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{P}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}\quad[\text{by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:class-operators-inequalities}}]\\
&\Longrightarrow\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{S}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}\quad[\text{by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:class-operators-inequalities}}]\\
&\Longrightarrow\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}.\end{array}

To prove the other inclusion, we show that
\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}
. In proving the last inclusion, we have to remember that the last class is a variety and therefore, it is closed under the operator
\mathbf{H}
. (Proposition
2.3.13
)

Assume
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}
. Then there is an algebra
\textsf{{B}}\in\mathcal{K}
and a set
\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that
B
is a subdirect product of

\{\textsf{{A}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\textsf{{A}}=\textsf{{A}}_{i}
, for some
i\in I
.
Since
A
is a homomorphic image of
B
,

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}
.

Finally, applying the second part of Proposition
2.3.13
, we conclude that

\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}
.
∎

Now we turn to implicative classes, or quasi-varieties.

A nonempty class
\mathcal{K}
of
\mathcal{L}
-algebras is an
implicative class
, or
quasi-variety
if there is a set
\Sigma
of quasi-equalities such that the equivalence
(\ast\ast)
holds.

The following characterization is due to A. Mal’cev.

Proposition 2.3.17

(
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, § 11, theorem 2)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a nonempty abstract class of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
. Then
\mathcal{K}
is a quasi-variety if, and only if, the following conditions are satisfied:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\text{a trivial (degenerate) $\mathcal{L}$-algebra belongs to $\mathcal{K}$};\\
(\text{b})&\mathbf{S}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K};\\
(\text{c})&\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K};\\
(\text{d})&\mathbf{P_{u}}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K}.\end{array}

For any nonempty class
\mathcal{K}
of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
and any set
X\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we define:

\theta_{\mathcal{K}}(X):=\bigcap\{\theta\in\textbf{Con}\,\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ X^{2}\subseteq\theta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta\in\mathbf{I}\mathbf{S}\mathcal{K}\}.

This gives rise for the following definition of algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{K}}(X)
with the generators
\overline{X}:=\{x/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{K}}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in X\}
. This algebra will also be denoted by

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(\overline{X})
.

Proposition 2.3.18

(
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, theorem 10.10 and corollary 10.11)
.

Given a nonempty class
\mathcal{K}
of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
, for any set
X\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(\overline{X})
has the universal mapping property relative to the class
\mathcal{K}
. Moreover, given an algebra
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{K}
,
for sufficiently large
X\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbf{H}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(\overline{X})
.

The following proposition is a generalization of theorem 10.12 of
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
.

Proposition 2.3.19
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a nonempty class of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
. Then the
algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{K}}(X)
belongs to the class
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}
. Hence, if
\mathbf{S}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
and

\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
, in particular if
\mathcal{K}
is a quasi-variety, then
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{K}}(X)
belongs to
\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

First we note that

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{K}}(X)\in\mathbf{P_{s}}\{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\subseteq\theta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta\in\mathbf{S}\mathcal{K}\};

see
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, §8, exercise 11. This implies that

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{K}}(X)\in\mathbf{P_{s}}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}.

Using Proposition
2.3.11
, we obtain that

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{K}}(X)\in\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{K}
.
∎

Given a variety (or quasi-variety)
\mathcal{K}
of algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
and
X\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(\overline{X})
is called a
free algebra relative to

\mathcal{K}
. It is well-known that, given
X\cup Y\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, if
\textit{card}(\overline{X})=\textit{card}(\overline{Y})
, then
the algebras
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(\overline{X})
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(\overline{Y})
are isomorphic. Then, if
\kappa=\textit{card}(\overline{X})
, then the algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(\overline{X})
is also denoted by
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{K}}(\kappa)
and call the
free algebra of rank

\kappa
relative to
\mathcal{K}
.

References

1.

[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]

2.

[
Mal’cev, 1973
]

2.3.4
Hilbert algebras

An algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
, where
\rightarrow
is a binary operation and
\mathbf{1}
(the
unit
) a 0-ary operation (or a constant), is called a
Hilbert algebra
5
5

5

The notion of a Hilbert algebra was introduced in
[
Diego, 1962
]
; see also
[
Diego, 1966
]
. In
[
Rasiowa, 1974
]
Hilbert algebras are called
positive implication algebras
. Diego also proved (see
[
Diego, 1966
]
, theorem 3) that the Hilbert algebras form an equational class.
if for arbitrary elements
x,y,z\in\textsf{A}
, the following conditions are satisfied:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{d}_{1})&x\rightarrow\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1},\\
(\text{d}_{2})&x\rightarrow(y\rightarrow x)=\mathbf{1},\\
(\text{d}_{3})&(x\rightarrow(y\rightarrow z))\rightarrow((x\rightarrow y)\rightarrow(x\rightarrow z))=\mathbf{1},\\
(\text{d}_{4})&(x\rightarrow y=\mathbf{1}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ y\rightarrow x=\mathbf{1})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x=y.\end{array}

In any Hilbert algebra the following hold:

x\rightarrow x=\mathbf{1},

and

(x=\mathbf{1}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ x\rightarrow y=\mathbf{1})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ y=\mathbf{1}.

(2.6)

The relation
\leq
defined as

x\leq y\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ x\rightarrow y=\mathbf{1}

(2.7)

is a partial ordering on
A
and, hence,
\mathbf{1}
is the greatest element with respect to
\leq
.

References

1.

[
Diego, 1962
]

2.

[
Rasiowa, 1974
]

2.3.5
Distributive lattices

An algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee\rangle
of type
\langle 2,2\rangle
, where
\wedge
(
meet
) and
\vee
(
join
) are binary operations, is called a
distributive lattice
if the following equalities hold in
A
for arbitrary elements
x
,
y
and
z
of
A
:

\begin{array}[]{cll}(\text{l}_{1})&i)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ x\wedge y=y\wedge x,&ii)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ x\vee y=y\vee x,\\
(\text{l}_{2})&i)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ x\wedge(y\wedge z)=(x\wedge y)\wedge z,&ii)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ x\vee(y\vee z)=(x\vee y)\vee z,\\
(\text{l}_{3})&i)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (x\wedge y)\vee y=y,&ii)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ x\wedge(x\vee y)=x,\\
(\text{l}_{4})&i)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ x\wedge(y\vee z)=(x\wedge y)\vee(x\wedge z),&ii)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ x\vee(y\wedge z)=(x\vee y)\wedge(x\vee z).\\
\end{array}

In any distributive lattice the following identities hold:

x\wedge x=x\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ x\vee x=x

Indeed, for any
x,y\in\textsf{L}
,

\begin{array}[]{rl}x&=x\wedge(x\vee y)\\
&=(x\wedge x)\vee(x\wedge y)\quad[\mbox{according to ($\text{l}_{4}$--$i$)}]\\
&=((x\wedge y)\vee x)\wedge((x\wedge y)\vee x)\quad[\mbox{in virtue of ($\text{l}_{1}$--$ii$) and ($\text{l}_{4}$--$ii$)}]\\
&=x\wedge x.\quad[\mbox{according to ($\text{l}_{3}$--$i$)}]\end{array}

The second idempotent law can be proven in a similar fashion.

If in the definition of a distributive lattice, we replace the laws
(\text{l}_{4})
with the idempotent laws, we receive the definition of a

lattice
. In this book, we will mostly deal with distributive lattices. However, some laws below are true for any lattice.

We observe that if
A
is a lattice, then for any
x,y\in\textsf{A}
,

x\wedge y=x\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x\vee y=y.

Indeed, assume that
x\wedge y=x
. Then we have:
x\vee y=(x\wedge y)\vee y=y
.

On the other hand, if
x\vee y=y
, then
x\wedge y=x\wedge(x\vee y)=x
.

The last equivalence induces the following definition. Given a lattice
A
, we define a binary relation on
A
as follows:

x\leq y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\wedge y=x.

(2.8)

According to the last equivalence, we have:

x\leq y\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x\vee y=y.

Further, we notice that
\leq
is a partial ordering on
A
and, according to this ordering,
x\wedge y
is the greatest lower bound and
x\vee y
is the least upper bound of
\{x,y\}
, respectively. This, in particular, implies that the operations
\wedge
and
\vee
are monotone with respect to
\leq
. Namely,

x\leq y\Longrightarrow(\text{$x\wedge z\leq y\wedge z$ and $x\vee z\leq y\vee z$}).

A lattice
A
is
complete
if for any subset
X\subseteq\textsf{A}
, there are a greatest lower bound of
X
, symbolically

\bigwedge X
, and a least upper bound of
X
, symbolically
\bigvee X
,
with respect to
\leq
.

References

1.

[
Rasiowa, 1974
]

2.

[
Rasiowa and Sikorski, 1970
]

2.3.6
Brouwerian semilattices

An algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\land,\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a
Brouwerian semilattice
(aka
relatively pseudo-complemented semilattice
or
implicative semilattice
) if
\langle\textsf{A};\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a Hilbert algebra and
\langle\textsf{A};\land\rangle
is a meet semilattice, that is, the properties
(
\text{l}_{1}
–
i
) and (
\text{l}_{2}
–
i
) are satisfied; in addition, the following equalities hold:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{s}_{1})&(x\land y)\rightarrow x=\mathbf{1},\\
(\text{s}_{2})&(x\rightarrow y)\rightarrow((x\rightarrow z)\rightarrow(x\rightarrow(y\land z)))=\mathbf{1}.\\
\end{array}

In any Brouwerian semilattice the equivalence

x\land y=x\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x\rightarrow y=\mathbf{1}.

is true. This implies that the relations defined by (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) coincide. Thus we employ one symbol,
\leq
, for this relation and use (
2.6
) and (
2.8
) either as a defining equivalence or as a property.

The following properties hold in any Brouwerian semilattice:

x\leq y\rightarrow z\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x\land y\leq z

(2.9)

and

x\land y=\mathbf{1}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (x=\mathbf{1}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ y=\mathbf{1}).

(2.10)

References

1.

[
Köhler, 1981
]

2.

[
Nemitz, 1965
]

2.3.7
Relatively pseudo-complemented lattices

An algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a
relatively pseudo-complemented lattice
if
\langle\textsf{A};\land,\lor\rangle
is a distributive lattice and

\langle\textsf{A};\land,\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a Bouwerian semilattice; in addition, the following equalities hold:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{p}_{1})&x\rightarrow(x\lor y)=\mathbf{1},\\
(\text{p}_{2})&(x\rightarrow z)\rightarrow((y\rightarrow z)\rightarrow((x\lor y)\rightarrow z))=\mathbf{1}.\\
\end{array}

In fact, there is no need to require
\langle\textsf{A};\land,\lor\rangle
to be distributive. The distributivity follows from (
2.9
).

Also, in virtue of (
2.9
), in any relatively pseudo-complemented lattice,

x\leq y\rightarrow y;

that is, for any element
y
,
y\rightarrow y
is the greatest element with respect to
\leq
, that is,
y\rightarrow y=\mathbf{1}
.

References

1.

[
Rasiowa, 1974
]

2.

[
Rasiowa and Sikorski, 1970
]

2.3.8
Boolean algebras

Boolean algebras belong to the class of distributive lattices.

A
Boolean algebra
is an algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
, where
\wedge
and
\vee
are the operations of a distributive lattice, that is, they satisfy the equalities
(\text{l}_{1})
–
(\text{l}_{4})
above,
\neg
(
complementation
) is a unary operation and
\mathbf{1}
is a 0-ary operation, if
(\text{b}_{1})
–
(\text{b}_{2})
below are satisfied in
A
for arbitrary elements
x
,
y
and
z
of
A
:

\begin{array}[]{cll}(\text{b}_{1})&i)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ x\wedge\mathbf{1}=x,&ii)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ x\vee\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1},\\
(\text{b}_{2})&i)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (x\wedge\neg x)\vee y=y,&ii)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (x\vee\neg x)\wedge y=y.\\
\end{array}

If we apply the definition (
2.8
) for a Boolean algebra
A
, we realize that
\mathbf{1}
is the greatest element and
\neg\mathbf{1}
the least element in
A
, respectively. Indeed, the former comes from
(
\text{b}_{1}
–
i
) and the latter, with the help of (
\text{b}_{2}
–
i
)
and (
\text{b}_{1}
–
i
), can be obtained as follows:

x=(\mathbf{1}\wedge\neg\mathbf{1})\vee x=\neg\mathbf{1}\vee x.

Denoting

\mathbf{0}:=\neg\mathbf{1},

(2.11)

we observe that for any
x\in\textsf{B}
,
\neg x
is a unique element
y
such that
x\wedge y=\mathbf{0}
and
x\vee y=\mathbf{1}
. The element
\neg x
is called a
complement
of
x
. Thus any Boolean algebra is a distributive lattice with a greatest element
\mathbf{1}
, a least element
\mathbf{0}
and a
complementation

\neg x
. This implies that, in view of commutativity of
\wedge
and
\vee
(the equalities
(\text{l}_{1})
), we can say the
x
is the complement of
\neg x
, which immediately implies the equality

\neg\neg x=x.

(2.12)

We also observe that

\mathbf{1}=x\vee\neg x\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{0}=x\wedge\neg x,

(2.13)

for an arbitrary element
x\in\textsf{A}
.

We denote:

x\rightarrow y:=\neg x\lor y.

and

x\leftrightarrow y:=(x\rightarrow y)\land(y\rightarrow x).

The just defined implication
x\rightarrow y
satisfies all the laws of the relatively complemented lattice. Thus a Boolean algebra can be defined as an expansion of a relatively complemented lattice with a new unary operation
\neg
which satisfies (
\text{b}_{1}
)–(
\text{b}_{2}
) and the identity
x\rightarrow y=\neg x\lor y
. This allows one to consider a Boolean algebra
in the signature
\langle\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
.

It is well known that given a Boolean algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
, the algebra

\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a relatively pseudo-complemented lattice; cf.
[
Rasiowa and Sikorski, 1970
]
, theorem II.1.1, or
[
Rasiowa, 1974
]
, theorem VI.1.2. Then, with the help of (
2.10
), we immediately obtain:

x\leftrightarrow y=\mathbf{1}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x=y.

(2.14)

It is obvious that a Boolean algebra is nontrivial if, and only if,
\mathbf{0}\neq\mathbf{1}
. This implies that the simplest nontrivial Boolean algebras consists of two elements —
\mathbf{1}
and
\mathbf{0}
.
Usually, this algebra is depicted by the following diagram.

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\mathbf{1}}}

{{\displaystyle\mathbf{0}}}

Figure 2.3:
A 2-element Boolean algebra

A simple observation gives us the following.

Proposition 2.3.20
.

In any Boolean algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
, the algebra

\langle\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\};\wedge,\vee,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a subalgebra of
A
.

Proposition 2.3.21
.

Let a Boolean algebra
A
be generated by a nonempty set
\textsf{A}_{0}
. Then any element
x\in\textsf{A}
can be represented in each of the forms:

x=\bigvee_{i=1}^{m}\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n_{i}}b_{ij},

or in the form:

x=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\bigvee_{j=1}^{n_{i}}b_{ij},

where for any
i
and
j
either
b_{ij}\in\textsf{A}_{0}

or
\neg b_{ij}\in\textsf{A}_{0}
.

Proof.

To prove this proposition, it suffices to notice that both decompositions belong to
A
. Then, one shows that all elements that have a disjunctive normal decomposition constitute a subalgebra of
A
; and the same is true for the elements that have a conjunctive normal decomposition; see
[
Rasiowa and Sikorski, 1970
]
, chapter II, theorem 2.1, for detail.
∎

Proposition 2.3.22

(
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, corollary 1.9)
.

Up to isomorphism, there is only one subdirectly irreducible Boolean algebra, a 2-element Boolean algebra.

As we will see below (Proposition
2.3.23
), the class of Boolean algebras can be regarded as a subclass of a larger class of Heyting algebras which are discussed in the next subsection. Therefore, many properties related to Heyting algebras are applicable to Boolean algebras.
For example, the properties that are discussed below in Proposition
2.3.24
for Heyting algebras (Section
2.3.9
) can be applied to Boolean algebras as well.

References

1.

[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]

2.

[
Rasiowa, 1974
]

3.

[
Rasiowa and Sikorski, 1970
]

2.3.9
Heyting algebras

A
Heyting algebra
(aka
pseudo-Boolean algebra
) is an algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
of type
\langle 2,2,2,1,0\rangle
, where
\wedge
and
\vee
,
\rightarrow
(
relative pseudo-complementation
) are binary operations,
\neg
(
pseudo-complementation
) is a unary operation and
\mathbf{1}

(the unit) is a 0-ary operation, if, besides the equalities (
\text{l}_{1}
)–(
\text{l}_{2}
) and (
\text{b}_{1}
) (Section
2.3.8
), the following equalities are satisfied for arbitrary elements
x
,
y
,
z
of
H
:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{h}_{1})&x\wedge(x\rightarrow y)=x\wedge y,\\
(\text{h}_{2})&(x\rightarrow y)\wedge y=y,\\
(\text{h}_{3})&(x\rightarrow y)\wedge(x\rightarrow z)=x\rightarrow(y\wedge z),\\
(\text{h}_{4})&x\wedge(y\rightarrow y)=x,\\
(\text{h}_{5})&\neg\mathbf{1}\vee y=y,\\
(\text{h}_{6})&\neg x=x\rightarrow\neg\mathbf{1}.\\
\end{array}

It is customary to denote:

x\leftrightarrow y:=(x\rightarrow y)\wedge(y\rightarrow x).

As in the case of Boolean algebras, in Heyting algebras, in virtue of

(\text{b}_{1})
, the element
\mathbf{1}
is a greatest element in Heyting algebra.
The property
(\text{h}_{4})
implies that

x\rightarrow x=\mathbf{1}.

(2.15)

Using the notation (
2.11
) and the property
(\text{h}_{5})
, we conclude that
\mathbf{0}
is a least element in Heyting algebra. According to
(\text{h}_{6})
, we have:

\neg x=x\rightarrow\mathbf{0}.

Thus, the last identity and
(\text{h}_{1})
imply the following:

x\wedge\neg x=\mathbf{0}.

(2.16)

The following property characterizes pseudo-complementation:

x\leq y\rightarrow z\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x\wedge y\leq z.

(2.17)

Indeed, assume first that
x\leq y\rightarrow z
. Then, in view of (
\text{l}_{1}
–
i
), the monotonicity of
\wedge
with respect to
\leq
and
(\text{h}_{1})
, we have:

x\wedge y\leq y\wedge(y\rightarrow z)=y\wedge z\leq z.

Conversely, suppose that
x\wedge y\leq z
, that is
x\wedge y=x\wedge y\wedge z
. Then, in virtue of
(\text{h}_{2})
,
(\text{h}_{4})
,
(\text{h}_{3})
,
we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rl}x\leq y\rightarrow x&=(y\rightarrow x)\wedge(y\rightarrow y)=y\rightarrow(x\wedge y)=y\rightarrow(x\wedge y\wedge z)\\
&=(y\rightarrow(x\wedge y))\wedge(y\rightarrow z)\leq y\rightarrow z.\end{array}

Using (
2.17
), we receive immediately:

x\leq y\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x\rightarrow y=\mathbf{1};

(2.18)

which in turn implies:

x=y\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x\leftrightarrow y=\mathbf{1}.

Proposition 2.3.23
.

Let
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
be a Heyting algebra. Then its restriction
\textsf{{B}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a Boolean algebra if the identity
x\rightarrow y=\neg x\vee y
holds in
A
.
Conversely, if
\textsf{{B}}=\langle\textsf{B};\wedge,\vee,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a Boolean algebra. Then its expansion
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{B};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
,
where
x\rightarrow y:=\neg x\vee y
, is a Heyting algebra.

Proof.

Let
A
be a Heyting algebra in which
x\rightarrow y=\neg x\vee y
.
We observe that, because of (
2.16
), the identity (
\text{b}_{2}
–
i
) is true in
A
. The identity (
\text{b}_{2}
–
ii
) is also true, for we have:

(x\vee\neg x)\wedge y=(x\rightarrow x)\wedge y=y.

Now assume that
A
is an expansion of a Boolean algebra
B
, where, by definition,
x\rightarrow y=\neg x\vee y
. We aim to show that all the properties
(\text{h}_{1})
–
(\text{h}_{6})
are valid in
A
. Indeed, we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{l}x\wedge(x\rightarrow y)=x\wedge(\neg x\vee y)=(x\wedge\neg x)\vee(x\wedge y)=x\wedge y;\\
(x\rightarrow y)\wedge y=(\neg x\vee y)\wedge y=y;\\
(x\rightarrow y)\wedge(x\rightarrow z)=(\neg x\vee y)\wedge(\neg x\vee z)=\neg x\vee(y\wedge\neg x)\vee(\neg x\wedge z)\vee(y\wedge z)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\penalty\ =\neg x\vee(y\wedge z)=x\rightarrow(y\wedge z);\\
x\wedge(y\rightarrow y)=x\wedge(\neg y\vee y)=x;\\
\neg x=\neg x\vee\mathbf{0}=\neg x\vee\neg\mathbf{1}=x\rightarrow\neg\mathbf{1}.\end{array}

∎

Corollary 2.3.23.1
.

Let
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
be a Boolean algebra. Then for an arbitrary
x\in\textsf{A}
,
\neg\neg x\rightarrow x=\mathbf{1}
, where the operation
x\rightarrow y
is understood as
\neg x\vee y
.

Proof.

Augmenting
A
with
\rightarrow
defined as above, we receive a Heyting algebra. Then we use (
2.15
) and (
2.12
).
∎

It should be clear that the simplest nontrivial Boolean algebra (Fig.
2.3
) is also the simplest non-generate Heyting algebra.

We will be using Heyting algebras as a basic semantics for the intuitionistic propositional logic (Section
6.3.3
). For this purpose, in the sequel, we will need the following propertied of Heyting algebras.

Proposition 2.3.24
.

Let
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
be a Heyting algebra. For arbitrary elements
x
,
y
and
z
of
H
the following properties hold:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&x\leq y\rightarrow x,\\
(\text{b})&x\rightarrow y\leq(x\rightarrow(y\rightarrow z))\rightarrow(x\rightarrow z),\\
(\text{c})&x\leq y\rightarrow(x\wedge y),\\
(\text{d})&x\wedge y\leq x,\\
(\text{e})&x\leq x\vee y,\\
(\text{f})&x\rightarrow z\leq(y\rightarrow z)\rightarrow((x\vee y)\rightarrow z),\\
(\text{g})&x\rightarrow y\leq(x\rightarrow\neg y)\rightarrow\neg x,\\
(\text{h})&x\leq\neg x\rightarrow y.\end{array}

Proof.

We obtain successively the following.

(a) follows straightforwardly from
(\text{h}_{2})
.

To prove (b), using (
2.17
), we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}(x\rightarrow y)\wedge(x\rightarrow(y\rightarrow z))\wedge x&=(x\rightarrow y)\wedge x\wedge(y\rightarrow z)\\
&=x\wedge y\wedge(y\rightarrow z)=x\wedge y\wedge z\leq z.\end{array}

Then, we apply (
2.17
) again twice to get (b).

We derive (c) immediately from
x\wedge y\leq x\wedge y
.

(d) and (e) are obvious.

To prove (f), we first have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}(x\rightarrow z)\wedge(y\rightarrow z)\wedge(x\vee y)&=((x\rightarrow z)\wedge(y\rightarrow z)\wedge x)\vee((x\rightarrow z)\wedge(y\rightarrow z)\wedge y)\\
&=(x\wedge z\wedge(z\rightarrow y))\vee(y\wedge z\wedge(x\rightarrow z))\\
&=(x\wedge z)\vee(y\wedge z)=(x\vee y)\wedge z\leq z.\end{array}

Then, we apply (
2.17
) twice.

To prove (g), applying (
2.17
) twice, we have:

(x\rightarrow y)\wedge(x\rightarrow\neg y)\wedge x=x\wedge y\wedge\neg y=x\wedge\mathbf{0}\leq\mathbf{0},

which implies that

(x\rightarrow y)\wedge(x\rightarrow\neg y)\leq x\rightarrow\mathbf{0}=\neg x.

It remains to apply (
2.17
).

Finally, we obtain (h) as follows:

x\wedge\neg x=\mathbf{0}\leq y.

Then, we apply (
2.17
).
∎

Two obvious ways to obtain new Heyting algebras from old ones are the formation of subalgebras and homomorphic images. For instance, given a nontrivial Heyting algebra
\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
,
the algebra
\langle\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
, where
\mathbf{0}:=\neg\mathbf{1}
, the smallest subalgebra of the first algebra.

The following method is very useful for obtaining homomorphic images of a given Heyting algebra.

Let
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
be a nontrivial Heyting algebra and
b\in\textsf{A}
. We consider the algebra

\textsf{{A}}_{b}=\langle\{x\in\textsf{A}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\leq b\};\wedge_{b},\vee_{b},\rightarrow_{b},\neg_{b},\mathbf{1}_{b}\rangle
, where, by definition:

\begin{array}[]{l}x\wedge_{b}y:=x\wedge y;\\
x\vee_{b}{b}y:=x\vee y;\\
x\rightarrow_{b}y:=(x\rightarrow)\wedge b;\\
\neg_{b}x:=(\neg x)\wedge b;\\
\mathbf{1}_{b}:=b.\end{array}

It turns out that
\textsf{{A}}_{b}
is a homomorphic image of
A
with respect to the homomorphic map
x\mapsto x\wedge b
. See
[
Rasiowa and Sikorski, 1970
]
, chapter IV, § 8, where the formation of
\textsf{{A}}_{b}
is called
relativization
.

References

1.

[
Rasiowa, 1974
]

2.

[
Rasiowa and Sikorski, 1970
]

2.4
Preliminaries from model theory

Let a first-order language
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
consist of a set
\mathcal{V}
of individual variables, logical constants ‘
\boldsymbol{\land}
’, ‘
\boldsymbol{\lor}
, ‘
\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}
’, ‘
\boldsymbol{\neg}
’ and quantifiers ‘
\boldsymbol{\forall}
’ and ‘
\boldsymbol{\exists}
’, as well as parentheses ‘
(
’ and
‘
)
’. Moreover, a signature of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
includes a set
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
of functional symbols of arity greater than or equal to
1
, a set
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}}
of individual constants which are treated as 0-ary functional symbols.

We continue to call the terms of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}

\mathcal{L}
-
terms
.

Any
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
must have a nonempty set
\mathcal{P}
of predicate symbols; some have equality
\approx
, some don’t. We will be assuming that the arity of each predicate symbol
P\in\mathcal{P}
is greater or equal to 1.

The notion of a formula is similar to the one given in Section
2.3.3
with a change in the clause for atomic formulas:
if
P\in\mathcal{P}
of arity
n
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
are
\mathcal{L}
-terms,
then
P(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
is an (
atomic
)
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-
formula
.
The other clauses are the same as (1)–(6) on p.
2.3.3
.
Thus defined formulas we call
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-
formulas
, or simply
formulas
; the latter is used in the contexts where confusion is unlikely.

If an
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-formula is closed, it is called an
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-
sentence
. As in Section
2.3.3
, a formula which does not contain quantifiers is called
quantifier-free
.

Models of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
(
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-
models
for short, or simply
models
) which we use in Chapters 4–8 of this book, where we apply some of the results of model theory, have one of the following two forms:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}(\text{a})\quad\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}},D\rangle&\text{or}&(\text{b})\quad\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}},\varDelta\rangle,\end{array}

(2.19)

where
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}}
are the sets of interpreted functional symbols and constants of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
D
is a unary predicate and
\varDelta
, the interpretation of
\approx
in
\mathfrak{A}
, is the diagonal of
\textsf{A}\times\textsf{A}
; usually,
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}}\rangle
is called an algebra of the corresponding model. If
M
is a model, its algebra is denoted by
\mathcal{A}[\textsf{{M}}]
. For a class
\mathcal{M}
of models,
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{M}]
denotes the class of their algebras. Given a model
M
, we denote by
|\textsf{{M}}|
its carrier.

The models of type (
2.19
–b) we call
e
-
models
.

Further, let
v
be a map of the set of individual variables into
|\textsf{{M}}|
. We will be using (a well-known) satisfactory relation of a first-order formula

A
in a model
M
, saying that
M

satisfies

A

at

v
, symbolically
(\textsf{{M}},v)\models A
or
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}A
.
6
6

6

Other utterances of this relation are:
‘
v
satisfies
\varphi
in
M
’ (
[
Bell and Slomson, 1969
]
, chapter 3, § 2); ‘
\varphi
is true of
v
in
M
’ (
[
Hodges, 1993
]
, section 1.3); ‘
M
satisfies
\varphi
with
v
’ (
[
Enderton, 2001
]
, section 2.2); ‘
(\textsf{{M}},\sigma)
satisfies
\varphi
’ or ‘
M
is a model for
\varphi
with respect to
v
’ (
[
Rautenberg, 2010
]
, section 2.3); ‘
\varphi
is satisfied by a sequence
v:\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textsf{A}
in
M
’ or
‘a sequence
v:\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textsf{A}
satisfies
\varphi
in
M
(
[
Chang and Keisler, 1990
]
, definition 1.3.12).
A model
M

satisfies

\varphi
, or
\varphi

is satisfiable in

M
, if there is such a
valuation

v
of the individual variables in
M
that
(\textsf{{M}},v)\models\varphi
.

We write
\textsf{{M}}\models A
if
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}A
, for any valuation
v
in
M
, and say that
M

validates

A
. Given a model
M
and a quantifier-free formula
A
, the following equivalence must be clear:

\textsf{{M}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\models A.

(2.20)

It must be clear that any identity, say
\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\,(\alpha\approx\beta)
, and the quasi-identity

\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\,(p\approx p\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\beta)
are validated by the same
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-models.

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a set of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-sentences and let
\mathcal{M}
be a nonempty class of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-models. We say that
\Sigma
is
satisfiable
in

\mathcal{M}
if there a model
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{M}
, which satisfies all sentences of
\Sigma
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a nonempty class of
e
-models. The set of equalities validated by all models from
\mathcal{M}
is called the
equational theory
of
\mathcal{M}
; this equational theory will be denoted by
\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{M}]
.
Now, we define:

\text{Mod}_{\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{M}):=\{\textsf{{M}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\models\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{M}]\}.

It is clear that
\mathcal{A}[\text{Mod}_{\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{M})]
(of algebras) is an equational class (or variety) generated by the class
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{M}]
.

Proposition 2.4.1

(
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
,
[
Grätzer, 2008
]
,
[
McKenzie et al., 2018
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a nonempty class of models of the form (
2.19
–b). Then
\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{M}]=\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}[\text{Mod}_{\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{M})]
. Furthermore,

\mathcal{A}[\text{Mod}_{\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{M})]=\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{M}]=\mathbf{H}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{M}]
.

Remark 2.4.1
.

The last conclusion of Proposition
2.4.1
follows from
Proposition
2.3.13
, Proposition
2.3.11
and Proposition
2.3.16
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}},\varDelta\rangle
be a model of type (
2.19
–b). We define:

\{\textsf{{M}}\}_{\textit{si}}:=\{\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}},\varDelta\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ \langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}}\rangle\in\mathcal{A}[\textsf{{M}}]_{\textit{si}}\}

Proposition 2.4.2
.

Given a model
M
of type (
2.19
–b),

\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}[\textsf{{M}}]=\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}[\{\textsf{{M}}\}_{\textit{si}}]
.

Proof.

We prove that for any identity
\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon
,

\textsf{{M}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \{\textsf{{M}}\}_{\textit{si}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon.

Indeed, suppose
\textsf{{M}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon
and
\textsf{{N}}\in\{\textsf{{M}}\}_{\textit{si}}
. Since
\mathcal{A}[\textsf{{N}}]
is a homomorphic image of
\mathcal{A}[\textsf{{M}}]
, in virtue of Proposition
2.4.6
–a,
\textsf{{N}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon
.

Conversely, assume that
\{\textsf{{M}}\}_{\textit{si}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon
. By definition, there is a set
\{\textsf{{M}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\{\textsf{{M}}\}_{\textit{si}}

such that the algebra
\mathcal{A}[\textsf{{M}}]
is subdirectly embedded into

\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}[\textsf{{M}}_{i}]
. According to Proposition
2.4.6
–b,
\langle\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}[\textsf{{M}}_{i}],\varDelta\rangle\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon
and hence, in virtue of Proposition
2.4.6
–c,
\textsf{{M}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon
.
∎

In the sequel, we will use the following fact.

Proposition 2.4.3
.

There are only two varieties of Boolean algebras — the trivial variety and the variety of all Boolean algebras.

Proof.

Let us consider a nontrivial variety. And let us take any nontrivial algebra in it. According to Proposition
2.3.20
, a 2-element algebra is a subalgebra of the latter. Hence, according to Proposition
2.3.13
, this 2-element algebra belongs to the variety. By the same proposition, any direct power of the 2-element algebra belongs to the variety. However, any Boolean algebra is isomorphic to some direct power of a 2-element Boolean algebra (
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, corollary IV.1.12). Therefore, any Boolean algebra belongs to any nontrivial variety.
∎

Let
\{\textsf{{M}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-models. Also, let
\nabla
be a filter over
I
. We fix the direct product

\textsf{{M}}:=\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{M}}_{i}

and define:

x\equiv_{\nabla}y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\{i\in I\penalty\ |\penalty\ x_{i}=y_{i}\}\in\nabla.

It is well known that
\equiv_{\nabla}
is congruence relation on the algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A},\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C}\rangle
. We denote by
\textsf{{A}}/\penalty\nabla
the quotient algebra with respect to this congruence. To obtain a model,
\mathfrak{A}/\penalty\nabla
, we have to expand
\textsf{{A}}/\penalty\nabla
with an interpretation of
\approx
in this algebra. Let us denote (temporarily) this interpretation by
\varDelta_{D}
. By definition, we have:

(x/\penalty\nabla,y/\penalty\nabla)\in\varDelta_{\nabla}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\{i\in I\penalty\ |\penalty\ (x_{i},y_{i})\in\varDelta_{i}\}\in\nabla.

Since each
\varDelta_{i}
is a diagonal, we observe that

(x/\penalty\nabla,y/\penalty\nabla)\in\varDelta_{\nabla}\Longleftrightarrow x/\penalty\nabla=y/\penalty\nabla.

This means that
\varDelta_{\nabla}
is the diagonal of
|\mathfrak{A}/\penalty F|\times|\mathfrak{A}/\penalty F|
; we denote it simply by
\varDelta
, if no confusion arises.

We denote

\prod_{\nabla}\textsf{{M}}_{i}:=\langle\textsf{{A}}/\penalty\nabla,\varDelta\rangle

and call the last model a
reduced product
of the family
\{\mathfrak{A}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
with respect to the filter
\nabla
. If
\nabla
be an ultrafilter over
I
, we call
\prod_{\nabla}\textsf{{M}}_{i}
an
ultraproduct
with respect to the ultrafilter
\nabla
.

In case if each
\textsf{{M}}_{i}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{i},D_{i}\rangle
, where
D_{i}
is a unary predicate on
|\mathfrak{A}_{i}|
, then, by definition, we have:

\prod_{\nabla}\textsf{{M}}_{i}:=\langle\textsf{{A}}/\penalty\nabla,D\rangle,

(2.21)

where

x/\penalty\nabla\in D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\{i\in I\penalty\ |\penalty\ x_{i}\in D_{i}\}\in\nabla.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a nonempty class of models of the form (
2.19
–b). The set of quasi-identities validated by all models from
\mathcal{M}
is called the
quasi-equational theory
of
\mathcal{M}
; this quasi-equational theory will be denoted by
\textbf{QE}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{M}]
.
Now, we define:

\text{Mod}_{\textbf{QE}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{M}):=\{\textsf{{M}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\models\textbf{QE}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{M}]\}.

The class
\mathcal{A}[\text{Mod}_{\textbf{QE}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{M})]
(of algebras) is obviously an implicative class (or a quasi-variety) generated by the class
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{M}]
.

Proposition 2.4.4

(
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, theorem V.2.25,
[
Gorbunov, 1998
]
, corollary 2.3.4)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a nonempty class of
e
-models. Then
\textbf{QE}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{M}]=\textbf{QE}_{\mathcal{L}}[\text{Mod}_{\textbf{QE}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{M})]
. Furthermore,

\mathcal{A}[\text{Mod}_{\textbf{QE}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{M})]=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P_{u}}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{M}]
.

Remark 2.4.2
.

The last conclusion of Proposition
2.4.4
follows from Proposition
2.3.17
and Proposition
2.3.11
.

In the sequel, we will need the following refined variant of compactness theorem.

Proposition 2.4.5

(
[
Bell and Slomson, 1969
]
,
[
Chang and Keisler, 1990
]
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a set of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-sentences and let
\mathcal{M}
be a nonempty class of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-models closed under ultraproducts. Then
\Sigma
is satisfiable in
\mathcal{M}
if, and only if, every finite subset of
\Sigma
is satisfiable in
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\Sigma
is satisfiable in
\mathcal{M}
; that is, there is a model
\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{M}
, which satisfies all sentences of
\Sigma
. Then, obviously,
\mathfrak{A}
satisfies any subset, in particular any finite subset, of
\Sigma
.

Conversely, let
I
denote the set of all finite subsets of
\Sigma
. By premise, for each
i\in I
, there is a model
\textsf{{M}}_{i}
which satisfies
i
.
According to Corollary 4.1.11 of
[
Chang and Keisler, 1990
]
, there is an ultrafilter
\nabla
over
I
such that the ultraproduct
\prod_{\nabla}\textsf{{M}}_{i}
satisfies
\Sigma
. By premise, this model belongs to
\mathcal{M}
. Hence,
\Sigma
is satisfiable in
\mathcal{M}
.
∎

A first-order formula
A
is said to be
preserved
:

•

under homomorphisms
if for any homomorphism
f
of a model
M
into a model
N
and any valuation
v
in
M
,

(\textsf{{M}},v)\models\phi\Longrightarrow(\textsf{{N}},v_{f})\models\phi,

where
v_{f}
is a valuation in
N
such that
v_{f}[p]=f(v[p])
, for any individual variable
p
;

•

under direct products
if for any class
\{\textsf{{M}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-models and any valuation
v
in

\textsf{{M}}=\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{M}}_{i}
, whenever
(\textsf{{M}}_{i},v_{i})\models\phi
, for every
i\in I
, where
v_{i}[p]
is the projection of
v[p]
on
\textsf{{M}}_{i}
, then
(\textsf{{M}},v)\models\phi
,

Proposition 2.4.6
.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\textit{Any equality and any identity is preserved under homomorphisms}.\\
(\text{b})&\textit{Any equality and any identity, as well as any quasi-equality and any quasi-identity},\\
&\text{are preserved under direct products}.\\
(\text{c})&\text{Both identities and quasi-idenities are preserved on subalgebras}.\end{array}

Proof.

(a) is part of the proof of theorem 3.2.4 of
[
Chang and Keisler, 1990
]
. (b) is covered by theorem 1 of
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, chapter III, § 7. For the proof of (c), see, e.g.,
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, § 7, theorem 1.
∎
