3.1.4
.

3.2
Semantics of sentential formal language

One of our concerns in this book is to show how truth can be transmitted through formulas of a formal language by formally presented deduction. Calling a formula true, we will mean a kind of truth which does not depend on any factual content. Moreover, sharing the view of the multiplicity of the nature of truth, we will be dealing rather with degrees of truth, calling them
truth values
. Then, the idea of truth will find its realization in the idea of
validity
.

Concerning the independence of any factual, contingent content in relation to truth values, let us consider the following two sentences — ‘Antarctica is large’ and ‘Antarctica is large or Antarctica is not large’. If our formal language contains a symbol for the grammatical disjunction “
\ldots_{1}\penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \ldots_{2}
”, say “
\ldots_{1}\vee\ldots_{2}
”
(as in the languages
\mathcal{L}_{A}
–
\mathcal{L}_{C}
of Section
3.1
), and a symbol for creating the denial of sentence, say “
\neg\ldots
” (also as in
\mathcal{L}_{A}
–
\mathcal{L}_{C}
), we can write in symbolic form the first sentence as
p
and the second as
p\vee\neg p
. From the viewpoint of Section
3.1
, both formal expressions are formulas of the language
\mathcal{L}_{A}
. Now, depending on familiarity with geography, as well as of personal experience, one can count
p
true or false. The second formula
p\vee\neg p
, however, in some semantic systems takes steadily the truth value
true
, regardless of any personal relation towards
p
. The
universality
of this kind will be stressed in the idea of validity.

From the last example, it should be clear the distinction between the statements, the truth value of which (understood as the degree of trust or that of informativeness) is dependent on the contingency of facts, the level of knowledge or personal beliefs, and the formal judgments, which obtain their logical status or meaning determined by
semantic rules
. These rules will allow us to interpret the formal judgments of a language in abstract structures, reflecting the structural character of these judgments, as well as the universal character of their validity, thereby understanding their invalidity as the failure to satisfy this universality.

In this book, semantics will play pragmatic rather than explanatory role;
4
4

4

However, since we state validity conditions for formal judgments, these conditions generate the meanings of the judgments, according to the point of view that has been emphasized by Wittgenstein in
Tractatus
; cf.
[
Wittgenstein, 2001
]
, 4.024. Also, compare with Curry’s statement that “the meaning of a concept is determined by the conditions under which it is introduced into discourse;” cf.
[
Curry, 1966
]
, section II.2.
namely we will be stressing the use of semantic structures as
separating tools
. This will be explained in Section
5.3
. Moreover, we want to emphasize that in our approach to semantics we adhere to the relativistic position, according to which a single formal judgment can be valid according to one semantic system and invalid with respect to another.

Now we turn to the definition of semantics of the schematic language
\mathcal{L}
exemplified by the languages
\mathcal{L}_{A}
–
\mathcal{L}_{C}
in Section
3.1
. Depending on one’s purposes, or depending on the lines, along which the semantics in question can be employed, the semantics we propose can use the items of the following two categories — the
algebras of type

\mathcal{L}
and expansions of the latter called
logical matrices
.
Later on, we will generalize the notion of logical matrix, when we discuss consequence relation in Chapter
4
.

The following semantic concepts will be specified later on as needed.

Definition 3.2.1

(algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
)
.

An
algebra of type

\mathcal{L}

(
or an
\mathcal{L}
-
algebra
or simply an
algebra
when a type is specified ahead and confusion is unlikely
)
is a structure
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C}\rangle
, where
A
is a nonempty set called the
carrier

(
or
universe
)

of

A
;

\mathcal{F}
, which in the notion of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas represents logical connectives, now represents the set of finitary operations on
A
of the corresponding arities
;
and
\mathcal{C}
, which is a set of logical constants, now represents a set of nullary operations on
A
. As usual, we use the notation
|\textsf{{A}}|=\textsf{A}
. The part
\langle\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C}\rangle
of the structure
A
is called its
signature
. Thus, referring to an algebra
A
, we can say that it is an algebra of signature
\langle\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C}\rangle
or of signature
\mathcal{F}
, if
\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
.

Thus any
\mathcal{L}
-formula in relation to any algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
becomes a
term
. Naturally, the sentential variables of a formula understood as term become the
individual variables
of this term. Since each algebraic term of, say,
n
variables can be read as an
n
-ary derivative operation in an algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
, so can be understood any
\mathcal{L}
-formula containing
n
sentential variables.

Definition 3.2.2

(inessential expansion of an algebra, equalized algebras)
.

Given an algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C}\rangle
, we obtain an
inessential expansion of

A
if we add to
\mathcal{F}
one or more terms regarding them as new signature operations, or if we add to
\mathcal{C}
one or more symbols with their intended interpretation as elements of
A
. Two algebras
(
or their signatures
)
are called
equalized
if they have inessential expansions of one and the same type.

The central notion of Lindenbaum method is that of formula algebra associated with a particular language. The
formula algebra
of type
\mathcal{L}
has
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
as its carrier and the signature operations as defined in Definition
3.1.1
. This algebra is denoted by
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Thus we have:

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}};\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C}\rangle.

Analogously, the set of
\mathcal{L}
-metaformulas along with the set
\mathcal{C}
regarded as nullary operations and the set

\mathcal{F}
regarded as non-nullary operations constitute a
metaformula algebra

\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{L}}
of type
\mathcal{L}
.

A relation between the
\mathcal{L}
-formulas and the algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
is established through the following notion.

Definition 3.2.3

(valuation, value of a formula in an algebra)
.

Let
A
be an algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
. Then any map
v:\mathcal{V}\longrightarrow|\textsf{{A}}|
is called a
valuation

(
or an
assignment
)
in the algebra
A
. Then, given a valuation
v
, the value of an
\mathcal{L}
-formula

\alpha
with respect to
v
, in symbols
v[\alpha]
, is defined inductively as follows
\,:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&v[p]=v(p),\penalty\ \textit{for any $p\in\mathcal{V}$};\\
(\text{b})&v[c]\penalty\ \textit{is the value of the nullary operation of}\penalty\ \textsf{{A}}\penalty\ \textit{corresponding to}\\
&\text{the constant $c$ of $\mathcal{L}$};\\
(\text{c})&v[\alpha]=Fv[\alpha_{1}]\ldots v[\alpha_{n}]\penalty\ \textit{if $\alpha=F\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}$}.\end{array}

If
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
, then a map
v:\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\longrightarrow|\textsf{{A}}|
is called a
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
-
valuation
, or a
valuation restricted to

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

It makes sense to denote the mapping

\alpha\mapsto v[\alpha]

by the same letter
v
. The following observation is a justification for this usage.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

Let
v
be a valuation in an algebra
A
. Then the mapping
v:\alpha\mapsto v[\alpha]
defines a homomorphism
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}
. Conversely, each homomorphism
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}
is a valuation.

Proof
of the first part is based on the clauses (a)–(c) of Definition
3.2.3
. The second part is obvious.

Thus, given a valuation
v
in
A
and a formula
\alpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
where
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
are all variables occurring in
\alpha
, we observe that

v[\alpha]=\alpha(v[p_{1}],\ldots,v[p_{n}]).

Grounding on the last observation base in turn on Proposition
3.2.1
, very often a valuation is defined as a
homomorphism
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}
.

Comparing (b)–(c) of Definition
3.2.3
with (a)–(b) of Definition
3.1.3
, we obtain the following.

Proposition 3.2.2
.

Any substitution is a valuation in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
and vice versa. Hence any substitution is an endomorphism
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

The converse of the last conclusion is also true: any endomorphism
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
can be regarded as a substitution. (Exercise
3.2
.
1
.)

The next property, which a combination of Proposition
3.2.1
and Proposition
3.2.8
, will be useful in the sequel.

Proposition 3.2.3
.

Given an
\mathcal{L}
-algebra
A
and a valuation
v
in
A
, for any substitution
\sigma
, there is a valuation
v_{\sigma}
in
A
such that for any formula
\alpha
,
v[\sigma(\alpha)]=v_{\sigma}[\alpha]
.

Proof.

Indeed, we simply define

v_{\sigma}:=v\circ\sigma.

By Proposition
3.2.2
,
v_{\sigma}
is a homomorphism, and, in virtue of Proposition
3.1.2
,
v_{\sigma}
is a valuation in
A
.
∎

Proposition 3.2.4
.

Let
v_{0}
be a valuation in an algebra
A
of type
\mathcal{L}
. Assume that

A
is generated by the set
\{v_{0}[p]\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\}
.
Then for any valuation
v
in
A
, there is a substitution
\sigma
such that
v=v_{0}\circ\sigma
.

Proof.

Let
v:p\mapsto a_{p}
be a valuation in
A
. In virtue of Proposition
2.3.1
, for each
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, there is an
\mathcal{L}
-formula,
\alpha_{p}
, such that

a_{p}=\alpha_{p}[p_{i}\backslash v_{0}[p_{j}],\ldots,q_{i}\backslash v_{0}[q_{J}]]
, where
p_{i},\ldots,q_{i}
are all variables occurring in
\alpha_{p}
. Without loss of generality , one can count that
p_{i}=p_{j},\ldots,q_{i}=q_{j}
. Hence
a_{p}=v_{0}[\alpha_{p}]
, that is
v[p]=v_{0}[\alpha_{p}]
, for any
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Now we define a substitution as follows:
\sigma(p)=\alpha_{p}
, where
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Thus, we have:
v[p]=v_{0}(\sigma(p))
, that is
v=v_{0}\circ\sigma
.
∎

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Let
\theta
be a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Then for valuation
v
in
{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta}
, there is an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
such that for any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\alpha
,
v[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta
.
Conversely, for any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
, there is a unique valuation
v_{\sigma}
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta
such that for any formula
\alpha
,
v_{\sigma}[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta
.

Proof.

Suppose according to
v
,
v:p\mapsto\gamma/\penalty\theta
, where
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Then we define:
\sigma:p\mapsto\gamma
, where
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
5
5

5

Of course, the definition of such a substitution is not unique, and the axiom of choice is required here.
Thus, in virtue of Proposition
3.2.2
, we have:
v[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta
, for any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\alpha
.

The converse-part is obvious.
∎

Now we turn to a central notion of any semantics — the notion of
validity
.
6
6

6

W. and M. Kneale note, “[…] logic is not simply valid argument but the reflection upon principles of validity;”
[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, section I.1.
This notion in turn is defined in the framework of the notion of logical matrix.

Definition 3.2.4

(logical matrix of type
\mathcal{L}
)
.

A
logical matrix

(
or simply a
matrix
)
of type
\mathcal{L}
is a structure
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D(x)\rangle
, where
A
is an algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
, which, if considered as part of
M
, is called an
M
-
algebra
, and
D(x)
is a predicate on
|\textsf{{A}}|
. The matrix
M
is
nontrivial
if the algebra
A
is nontrivial, and
trivial
otherwise. We will also denote a matrix as a structure
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C},D(x)\rangle
or as a structure

\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F},D(x)\rangle
, if
\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
. We will also denote a matrix as
\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
or as
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F},D\rangle

or as
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F},D\rangle
, if
\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
, where
D\subseteq|\textsf{{A}}|
. In the last three notations
D
is called the set of
designated elements
or a
logical filter

(
or simply a
filter
)
in
(
or of
)

M
.

We should remark that using “logical” in the last definition was a bit hasty, for the first connection with logic emerges, at least in the case of logical matrices, in the next definition
when we separate semantically valid
\mathcal{L}
-formulas from the other formulas.

Definition 3.2.5
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a logical matrix of type
\mathcal{L}
. Then a formula
\alpha
is
satisfied
by a valuation
v
in
A
if
v[\alpha]\in D
, in which case we say that
v

satisfies

\alpha
in
M
; further,
\alpha
is
semantically valid

(
or simply
valid
)
in
M
, symbolically
\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha
or
\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha
, if for any valuation
v
in
A
,

v[\alpha]\in D
. Given a set
X
of formulas, we write
\textsf{{M}}\models X
if
\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha
, for all
\alpha\in X
. If a formula is not valid in a matrix, it is called
invalid
in this matrix or is
rejected
by the matrix or one can say that this matrix
rejects
the formula. If
v[\alpha]\notin D
in
M
,
v
is a
valuation rejecting
or
refuting

\alpha
. The set of all formulas valid in a matrix
M
is denoted by
L\textsf{{M}}
and is called the
logic
of this matrix.

Proposition 3.2.6
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a matrix
(
of type
\mathcal{L}
)
. Then for any substitution
\sigma
, if
\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha
, so is
\textsf{{M}}\models\sigma(\alpha)
.

Proof.

Let
v
be any valuation in
A
. According to Propositions
3.2.2
and
3.2.1
,
\sigma:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
and

v:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}
are homomorphisms. Then
v\circ\sigma
is also a homomorphism
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}
and hence, by Proposition
3.2.1
, is a valuation. Therefore,

\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha
implies that
v[\sigma(\alpha)]\in D
.
∎

The last proposition induces a way of how, given a matrix, to obtain a matrix on the formula algebra so that both matrices validate the same set of formulas.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a matrix. We denote

D_{\textsf{M}}=\{\alpha\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\models\alpha\}

and then define a matrix

\textsf{{M}}_{\textit{Fm}}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},D_{\textsf{M}}\rangle.

We observe that for any formula
\alpha
,

\textsf{{M}}_{\textit{Fm}}\models\alpha\Longleftrightarrow\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha.

Indeed, let
\textsf{{M}}_{\textit{Fm}}\models\alpha
. Then for any substitution
\sigma
(which, according to Proposition
3.2.2
, is a valuation in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
),
\sigma(\alpha)\in D_{\textsf{M}}
; in particular,
\iota(\alpha)=\alpha\in D_{\textsf{M}}
, that is
\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha
.

Now suppose that
\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha
, that is
\alpha\in D_{\textsf{M}}
. In virtue of Proposition
3.2.6
,
D_{\textsf{M}}
is closed under substitution. This completes the proof of the equivalence above.

Next let us consider
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},D\rangle
. We observe that if
\alpha\in D_{\textsf{M}}
, that is
\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha
, then, since, by virtue of Proposition
3.2.2
, each substitution is a valuation in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
and vice versa,
\alpha\in D
.

Now let
D^{\prime}\subseteq D
and
D^{\prime}
be closed under substitution. Assume that
\alpha\in D^{\prime}
. Then, obviously,
\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha
and hence
\alpha\in D_{\textsf{M}}
.

Thus we have obtained the following.

Proposition 3.2.7
.

For any matrix
M
, there is a matrix on the formula algebra, namely
\textsf{{M}}_{\textit{Fm}}
, such that both matrices validate the same set of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas. Moreover, given a matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},D\rangle
, the filter of the matrix

\textsf{{M}}_{\textit{Fm}}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},D_{\textsf{M}}\rangle
is the largest subset of
D
closed under substitution. Thus, if
D
itself is closed under substitution, then
D=D_{\textsf{M}}
.

The importance of the last proposition for what follows is that it shows that the matrices of the form

\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},D\rangle
, where a filter
D
is closed under substitution, suffice for obtaining all sets of valid
\mathcal{L}
-formulas, which can be obtained individually by the matrices of type
\mathcal{L}
. This observation will be clarified below as
Lindenbaum’s Theorem
; see Proposition
4.3.12
.

Now we turn to some special matrices which have played their important role in the development of sentential logic. In all these matrices, the filter of designated elements consists of one element. In this connection, we note the following equivalence, which will be used throughout:
If
D=\{c\}
, then

x\in D\Longleftrightarrow x=c.

(3.4)

In the sequel, we find useful the following property.

Proposition 3.2.8
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1},D_{1}\rangle
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{2},D_{2}\rangle
be matrices of the same type.
Also, let
h
be an epimorphism of
\textsf{{A}}_{1}
onto
\textsf{{A}}_{2}
such that

h[D_{1}]\subseteq D_{2}
. Then
L\textsf{{M}}_{1}\subseteq L\textsf{{M}}_{2}

Proof.

Let
v
be a valuation in
\textsf{{A}}_{2}
. Since
h
is an epimorphisms, for each variable
p_{\gamma}
, there is an element
x_{\gamma}\in|\textsf{{A}}_{1}|
such that
h(x_{\gamma})=v[p_{\gamma}]
. We define a valuation
v^{\prime}
in
\textsf{{A}}_{1}
as follows:
v^{\prime}[p_{\gamma}]=x_{\gamma}
, for any variable
p_{\gamma}
. It is obvious that
h(v^{\prime}[p_{\gamma}])=v[p_{\gamma}]
. Assume that
\alpha\in L\textsf{{M}}_{1}
; in particular,
v^{\prime}[\alpha]\in D_{1}
. This implies that
v[\alpha]\in D_{2}
.
∎

3.2.1
The logic of a two-valued matrix

We begin with a well-known matrix of the so-called “two-valued logic.” The significance of this matrix in the history of logic is difficult to overestimate. The two-valued matrix was assumed, first, to distinguish true statements from false ones; and, second, to validate only the former. As we will see below, the main logic laws such as, for example, the law of identity, the law of contradiction, the law of the excluded middle (see below) and others can be demonstrated as valid formulas in this matrix. For this reason, it is worth considering this matrix first. Later on, we will explain another significance of this matrix, purely algebraic in nature, which leads to a very important view on this matrix as a refuting and separating tools.

The two-valued matrix assumes two main variants of formulation — as a matrix of type
\mathcal{L}_{A}
and as that of type
\mathcal{L}_{B}
. As will be seen below, the difference between these variants concerns only semantic rules and does not affect essentially the set of valid formulas in the corresponding matrices.
For this reason, we denote both variants by
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
. The elements of
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
are denoted by
\mathbf{0}
(
false
) and
\mathbf{1}
(
true
), which constitute a Boolean algebra with
\mathbf{0}
as the least element and
\mathbf{1}
as the greatest with respect to the partial ordering
\mathbf{0}\leq\mathbf{1}
. According to the intended interpretation,
\mathbf{1}
represents a truth value
true
,
\mathbf{0}
represents
false
. Being viewed as a 2-element Boolean algebra of type
\mathcal{L}_{A}
, the connectives
\wedge
,
\vee
and
\neg
are interpreted as meet, join and complementation, respectively, while

x\rightarrow y:=\neg x\vee y.

(3.5)

This leads to the familiar truth tables:

x\wedge y

x\backslash y

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

x\vee y

x\backslash y

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

x

\neg x

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

x\rightarrow y

x\backslash y

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

We observe that for any
x,y\in|\textsf{{B}}_{2}|
,

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{c}x\rightarrow y=\mathbf{1}\Longleftrightarrow x\leq y;\\
x\rightarrow\mathbf{0}=\neg x;\\
\mathbf{1}\rightarrow x=x.\end{array}\end{cases}

(3.6)

If one regards
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
(more exactly, its counterpart) as an algebra of type
\mathcal{L}_{B}
, two nullary operations,
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{1}
, should be added to the signature, along with the intended interpretation of
\bot
and
\top
as
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{1}
, respectively. Making of
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
a logical matrix, we add a logical filter
\{\mathbf{1}\}
. Thus a formula
\alpha
of type
\mathcal{L}_{A}
(or of type
\mathcal{L}_{B}
) is valid in
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
if for any valuation
v
,

v[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}.

We denote both, the algebra and the matrix, grounded on this algebra, by
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
. It must be clear that any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula is valid in the matrix
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
of type
\mathcal{L}_{B}
if and only if it is valid in
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
of type
\mathcal{L}_{A}
.

A formula
\alpha
valid in
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
is called a
classical tautology
. It is not difficult to see that the following formulas are classical tautologies:

\begin{array}[]{cl}p\rightarrow p,&(\textit{law of identity})\\
\neg(p\wedge\neg p),&(\textit{law of contradiction})\\
p\vee\neg p;&(\textit{law of the excluded middle})\end{array}

as well as the following:

\begin{array}[]{cl}\begin{cases}p\rightarrow(q\rightarrow p);\\
\neg p\rightarrow(p\rightarrow q).\end{cases}&(\text{``paradoxes of material implication''})\end{array}

The above tautologies had been known from ancient times. In modern times, such tautologies as the following have been in focus:

\begin{array}[]{cl}((p\rightarrow q)\rightarrow p)\rightarrow p;&(\textit{Peirce's law})\\
\neg\neg p\leftrightarrow p;&(\textit{double negation law})\\
\begin{cases}\neg(p\wedge q)\leftrightarrow\neg p\vee\neg q;\\
\neg(p\vee q)\leftrightarrow\neg p\wedge\neg q.\end{cases}&(\textit{De Morgan's laws})\end{array}

By the
two-valued logic
, one usually means the set of all classical tautologies.

3.2.2
The Łukasiewicz logic of a three-valued matrix

Perhaps, the “Łukasiewicz 3-valued logic” is the best-known of all multiple-valued systems alternative to the set of classical tautologies.
The matrix which defines this “logic” has three values,
\mathbf{0}
(
false
),
\bm{\tau}
(
indeterminate
) and

\mathbf{1}
(
true
), which are arranged by a linear ordering as follows:
\mathbf{0}\leq\bm{\tau}\leq\mathbf{1}
. On this carrier, an algebra of type
\mathcal{L}_{A}
is defined in such a way that
\wedge
and
\vee
are interpreted as meet and join in the 3-element distributive lattice; then,
\neg
and
\rightarrow
are defined according to the following tables:

x

\neg x

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\bm{\tau}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

x\rightarrow y

x\backslash y

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\bm{\tau}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

We denote this algebra by
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
.
Actually, when we have defined
\neg
in
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
as above, the operation
\rightarrow
can be defined according to (
3.6
). Moreover, the following identities can be easily checked:

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{cases}x\vee y=(x\rightarrow y)\rightarrow y;\\
x\wedge y=\neg(\neg x\vee\neg y).\end{cases}\end{array}

(3.7)

(Exercise
3.2
.
6
.)
Also, we note that, as algebras of type
\mathcal{L}_{A}
,
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
is a subalgebra
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
.
(See Exercise
3.2
.
7
.)

Now taking
\{\mathbf{1}\}
as a logical filter, we obtain the Łukaciewicz 3-valued matrix which is also denoted by
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
.

We note that the law of the excluded middle and Peirce’s law, which are tautologies of the two-valued logic, fail to be tautologous in
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
. (Exercise
3.2
.
8
.)
The law of contradiction is also invalid in
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
, for the set
\{\bm{\tau}\}\subseteq|\textsf{{\L}}_{3}|
is closed under
\wedge
and
\neg
.

By the Łukasiewicz 3-valued logic one means the set of formulas which are validated by
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
. These formulas are called
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
-
tautologies
. It should be clear that the set

\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
-tautologies is a proper subset of the set of classical tautologies.
(Exercise
3.2
.
9
.) One time, it had been assumed that the matrix
\langle\textsf{{\L}}_{3},\{\bm{\tau},\mathbf{1}\}\rangle
validates the classical tautologies and only them. If the first part of the last statement is obviously true, the second is false. The following counterexample is due to A. R. Turquette: the formula
\neg(p\rightarrow\neg p)\vee\neg(\neg p\rightarrow p)
is a classical tautology but is refuted in the last matrix by the assignment
v[p]=\bm{\tau}
.

3.2.3
The Łukasiewicz modal logic of a three-valued matrix

Both the algebra and matrix
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
can be expanded to type
\mathcal{L}_{C}
as follows:

x

\Box x

\Diamond x

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

The last definitions of the modalities
\Box
and
\Diamond
are not independent of the other assertoric connectives, for in the expanded algebra
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
the following identities hold:

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{cases}\Diamond x=\neg x\rightarrow x;\\
\Box x=\neg\Diamond\neg x.\end{cases}\end{array}

(3.8)

We leave for the reader to check this. (See Exercise
3.2
.
12
.)

We observe that the formulas

\Box p\rightarrow p,\penalty\ p\rightarrow\Diamond p\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \Box p\rightarrow\Diamond p

are valid in the expanded matrix, while their converses,

p\rightarrow\Box p,\penalty\ \Diamond p\rightarrow p\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \Diamond p\rightarrow\Box p,

are rejected by it. (See Exercise
3.2
.
13
.)

3.2.4
The Gödel
n
-valued logics

As in the case
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
, let us consider the three distinct “truth values,”
\mathbf{0}
,
\bm{\tau}
and

\mathbf{1}
, arranged by a linear ordering as follows:
\mathbf{0}\leq\bm{\tau}\leq\mathbf{1}
. Regarding this chain as a Heyting algebra with
\wedge
,
\vee
,
\rightarrow
and
\neg
as meet, join, relative pseudo-complementation and pseudo-complementation, respectively, we get an algebra
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
of type
\mathcal{L}_{A}
. Thus we arrive at the following truth tables:

x\wedge y

x\backslash y

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

x\vee y

x\backslash y

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

\bm{\tau}

\bm{\tau}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

x

\neg x

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

x\rightarrow y

x\backslash y

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{1}

\mathbf{0}

\bm{\tau}

\mathbf{1}

We notice that all properties (
3.6
) are satisfied in
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
.

Taking into account the relation
\leq
, the above truth tables can be read through the following equalities.

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{l}x\wedge y=\min(x,y)\\
x\vee y=\max(x,y)\\
x\rightarrow y=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{1}&\text{if $x\leq y$}\\
y&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\end{cases}\\
\neg x=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{0}&\text{if $x\neq\mathbf{0}$}\\
\mathbf{1}&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\end{cases}\end{array}\end{cases}

(3.9)

Taking
\{\mathbf{1}\}
as a logical filter, we obtain a logical matrix
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
.
The last matrix can be generalized if instead of 3 elements we take 4 and more elements and use (
3.9
) to define the same signature operations as in
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
. Thus we obtain Gödel’s
n
-valued logics
\textbf{G}_{n}
, where
n\geq 3
. We note that (
3.9
) are also valid in
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
; therefore, it is sometimes convenient to rename the algebra
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
by
\textbf{G}_{2}
.

The matrices
\textbf{G}_{n}
,
n\geq 2
, have been in particular important in investigation of the lattice of intermediate logics, that is the logics the valid formulas of which contain all
intuitionistic tautologies
and are included into the set of classical tautologies. In particular, all the matrices
\textbf{G}_{n}
,
n\geq 3
, fail Peirce’s law, the double negation law, as well as both De Morgan’s laws. (Exercise
3.2
.
14
.)

3.2.5
The Dummett denumerable matrix

An denumerable generalization of
\textbf{G}_{n}
,
n\geq 2
, can be obtained if we take take a denumerable set and arrange it by a linear ordering
\leq
according to type
1+\omega^{\ast}
. We denote the least element by
\mathbf{0}
and the greatest by
\mathbf{1}
. (We note: the ordinal notation of the former is
\omega
and of the latter is
0
.) Then, introducing the operations
\wedge
,
\vee
,
\rightarrow
and
\neg
as meet, join, relative pseudo-complementation and pseudo-complementation, respectively, of a Heyting algebra and equipping the latter algebra with a logical filter
\{\mathbf{1}\}
, we receive the
Dummett denumerable matrix

LC
. As for
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
, the algebra of the Dummett matrix satisfies the properties (
3.6
) and also (
3.9
). We note that
LC
validates the formula

(p\rightarrow q)\vee(q\rightarrow p).

The set of all formulas valid in this matrix,
L\textsf{{LC}}
, is called the
Dummett logic
.

M. Dummett shows in
[
Dummett, 1959
]
, theorem 2, that

L\textsf{{LC}}=\bigcap_{n\geq 2}L\textsf{{G}}_{n}.

(3.10)

In the sequel, we will need another logical matrix whose logic coincides with the Dummett logic
LC
.

Let us consider a denumerable set arranged linearly according to type
\omega+1
.
We treat this structure as a Heyting algebra with a least element
\mathbf{0}
, greatest element
\mathbf{1}
and operations
\wedge
,
\vee
,
\rightarrow
and
\neg
that are defined according to (
3.9
). Announcing
\{\mathbf{1}\}
a logical filter, we define the logical matrix
\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
.

Proposition 3.2.9
.

L\textsf{{LC}}=L\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
.

Proof.

We show that

L\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}=\bigcap_{n\geq 2}L\textsf{{G}}_{n}.

It is clear that for any
n\geq 2
, the algebra of
\textsf{{G}}_{n}
is a homomorphic image of the algebra of
\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
; see Section
2.3.9
. In virtue of Proposition
3.2.8
,
L\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}\subseteq\bigcap_{n\geq 2}L\textsf{{G}}_{n}
.

Now, assume that a valuation
v
in
\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
refutes a formula
\alpha
, that is
\alpha\notin L\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
. Let
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}
be all subformulas of
\alpha
. Then, according to (
3.9
), the set
\{v[\beta_{1}],\ldots,v[\beta_{n}],\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
is the carrier of a subalgebra of the
\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
-algebra, which in turn is isomorphic to a
\textsf{{G}}_{n}
-algebra, for some
n\geq 2
. This implies that
\alpha\notin\bigcap_{n\geq 2}L\textsf{{G}}_{n}
.
∎

3.2.6
Two infinite generalizations of Łukasiewicz’s
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}

The Łukasiewicz algebra
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
can be easily generalized if we take for a carrier the interval
[0,1]
or the rational scale
\mathbb{Q}
or on the real scale
\mathbb{R}
arranged in both cases by the ordinary less-than-or-equal relation
\leq
. The set of designated elements in both cases is
\{1\}
.
The operations
\neg
and
\rightarrow
are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\neg x:=1-x,\\
x\rightarrow y:=\min(1,1-x+y);\end{array}

then, the other operations are defined according to (
3.7
). This leads to the identities

\begin{array}[]{c}x\wedge y=\min(x,y)\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ x\vee y=\max(x,y),\end{array}

which can be taken as definitions of
\wedge
and
\vee
as well.

The set of formulas valid in the first matrix, that is when
[0,1]
is considered in the rational scale, is denoted by
\textsf{{\L}}_{\aleph_{0}}
and the formulas valid in the second matrix by
\textsf{{\L}}_{\aleph_{1}}
.

So far, we have discussed the semantic rules for the language
\mathcal{L}
. As we introduce other schematic languages later on (in Chapters
7
), we will have to adjust these rules for them. However, although semantic rules allow us to distinguish valid formulas from refuted ones, they, taken alone, do not suffice to
analyze reasoning. This echoes the remark due to Aristotle that ‘‘it is absurd to discuss refutation (’´ελεγχος) without discussing reasoning (ςυλλογιςμ´ος).’’
8
8

8

Cf.
[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, chapter I, section 4.
We discuss the relation of formal deduction between
\mathcal{L}
-formulas in the form of
consequence relation
. This is a topic of the next chapter.

Exercises
3.2

1.

Show that any endomorphism
f:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
can be regarded as a substitution; that is
f
is a unique extension of
v:\mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that
v(p)=f(p)
, for any
p\in\mathcal{V}
.

2.

Prove the second part of Proposition
3.2.1
.

3.

Prove that the laws of identity, of contradiction, of the excluded middle, as well as Peirce’s and double negation laws are tautologies in
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
.

4.

Prove the properties (
3.6
).

5.

Show that for any two
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
,
\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta
is a classical tautology if and only if for any
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
-valuation
v
,
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
.

6.

Prove the identities (
3.7
).

7.

Prove that
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
is a subalgebra of
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
.

8.

Show that the law of the excluded middle and Peirce’s law are not tautologous in
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
.

9.

Prove that the set of
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
-tautologies is properly included in the set of classical tautologies.

10.

Prove that the “paradoxes of material implications” (p.
3.6
) are
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
-tautologous.

11.

Show that the following formulas are
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
-tautologies.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(a)&p\rightarrow(q\rightarrow p);\\
(b)&(p\rightarrow q)\rightarrow((q\rightarrow r)\rightarrow(p\rightarrow r));\\
(c)&(\neg p\rightarrow\neg q)\rightarrow(q\rightarrow p);\\
(d)&((p\rightarrow\neg p)\rightarrow p)\rightarrow p.\end{array}

Prove that the identities (
3.8
) hold the algebra
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
expanded by the modalities
\Box
and
\Diamond
.

Show that the logical matrix of type
\mathcal{L}_{C}
of Section
3.2.3
validates the formulas

and rejects their converses
p\rightarrow\Box p,\penalty\ \Diamond p\rightarrow p\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \Diamond p\rightarrow\Box p
.

Show that Peirce’s law, the double negation law and both De Morgan’s laws can be refuted in
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
.

Let us designate on the algebra
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
the logical filter
\{\bm{\tau},\mathbf{1}\}
. We denote the new matrix by
\textsf{{G}}_{3}^{\prime}
. Show that
\textsf{{G}}_{3}^{\prime}
validates a formula
\alpha
if and only if
\alpha
is a classical tautology.

3.3
Historical notes

The idea that logic, as the science of reasoning, deals with the universal rather than the particular can be traced back to Plato and Aristotle. According to the Platonic doctrine of Forms, a single Form is associated with each group of things to which a common name can be applied. Plato believed that each Form differs from the particulars it represents but, at the same time, is a particular of its own kind.
Applying this doctrine to reasoning, he believed that correct reasoning is based on the connections of the involved Forms. This is how W. Kneale and M. Kneale summarize Plato’s view on reasoning.

“For Plato necessary connexions hold between Forms, and inference is presumably valid when we follow in thought the connexions between Forms as they are. How is this view related to the theory which assigns truth and falsity to sentences made up of nouns and verbs? Plato seems to hold that a sentence is true if the arrangement of its parts reflects or corresponds to connexion between Forms.”

[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, section I.5.

However, what Plato wrote about correct thinking can be regarded as philosophy of logic rather than logic itself. The first construction of formal logic we encounter in Aristotle. Although Aristotle was influenced by Plato’s doctrine of Forms, he rejected it. Nevertheless, we dare to assert that Aristotle’s doctrine of the syllogism is
formal
in the Platonic sense. Namely Aristotle was the first to introduce variables into logic. In his influential book on Aristotle’s syllogistic J. Łukasiewicz makes the following comment about this fact.

“It seems that Aristotle regarded his invention as entirely plain and requiring no explanation, for there is nowhere in his logical works any mention of variables. It was Alexander who first said explicitly that Aristotle presents his doctrine in letters, ςτοιχει̂α, in order to show that we get the conclusion not in consequence of the matter of the premisses, but in consequence of their form and combination; the letters are marks of universality and show that such a conclusion will follow always and for any term we may choose.”
[
Łukasiewicz, 1951
]
, § 4.

Although in the course of the development of logic, when after Aristotle the generalized nature of formal judgments was reflected in the use of letters, and the latter were perceived, at least gradually, as sentential variables,
9
9

9

It is appropriate to quote J. Łukasiewicz, who wrote, “[…] the Stoics prepared the way for formalism, and they cannot be credited highly enough for that. They held strictly to
words
and not to their
meanings
, which is the principle requirement of formalization, and they even did so in conscious opposition to the Peripatetics.”
[
Łukasiewicz, 1934
]
and therefore it would be natural to consider the uniform substitution, as defined above, as a legitimate
operation
that would apply to all formal judgments, however, the uniform substitution was first proposed as an
inference rule
, that is, only in conjunction with (and for constructing) logical inferences. A brief history of how the rule of uniform substitution has paved its way to existence will be discussed in more detail in Section
5.4
.

Nonetheless, the realization, though implicit and at least for propositional languages such as
\mathcal{L}
-languages above, of that the set of formulas is closed under substitution and that the notion of validity should be defined in such a way to maintain this closedness for the formulas identified as valid one can find in George Boole. As to the first part of this observation, Boole puts it as follows.

“Let us imagine any known or existing language freed from idioms and divested of superfluity and let us express in that language any given proposition in a manner the most simple and literal […] The transition from such a language to the notation of analysis would consist of no more than the substitution of one set of signs for another, without essential change either in form or character.”
[
Boole, 2003
]
, chapter 11, section 15.

As to the second, he writes:

“I shall in some instances modify the premises by […] substitution of some new proposition, and shall determine how by such change the ultimate conclusions are affected.”
[
Boole, 2003
]
, chapter 13, section 1.

It appears that this idea has become fully realized by the 1920s in the Lvov-Warsaw School, for without this, Lindenbaum’s Theorem (Proposition
4.3.12
) would be impossible. Although we can only guess, but it seems plausible to suggest that the confusion between the concepts of predicate and propositional term was an obstacle for such a long delay.

With merely some reservation, one can say that George Boole was the first who interpreted
symbolic expressions representing “laws of thought” as algebraic terms. However,
his algebraic treatment of logic was merely the beginning of a long way. This is how T. Hailperin estimates Boole’s contribution.

“Boole wrote his
Laws of Thought
before the notion of an abstract formal system, expressed within a precise language, was fully developed. And, at that time, still far in the future was the contemporary view which makes a clear distinction between a formal system and its realization or models. Boole’s work contributed to bringing these ideas to fruition.”
[
Hailperin, 1986
]
, chapter 1, § 1.0.

We find interpretation of formal judgments in an abstract logical matrix, namely
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
, in
Tractatus
; cf.
[
Wittgenstein, 2001
]
, 4.441 and further. Also, Wittgenstein used the term
tautology
, applying it to any classical tautology. Later on, logical matrices turned to be a natural way to define numerous deductive systems. We owe to Alfred Tarski a systematic approach to deductive systems through logical matrices. (See more about this in Section
5.4
.)

At first matrices were employed in the search for an independent axiomatic system for the set of classical tautologies in
[
Bernays, 1926
]
and
[
Łukasiewicz, 1964
]
, section 6; in particular, Bernays coined the term a ‘‘designated element.’’
10
10

10

Cf.
[
Bernays, 1926
]
, p. 316.

Also, J. C. C. McKinsey used in
[
McKinsey, 1939
]
matrices to prove independence of logical connectives in intuitionistic propositional logic.

The idea of the use of a 3-valued matrix was expressed by J. Łukasiewicz in his inaugural address as Chancellor of the University of Warsaw in 1922. Later on, a revised version of this speech was published as
[
Łukasiewicz, 1961
]
, where one can read:

“I maintain that there are propositions which are neither true nor false but
indeterminate
. All sentences about future facts which are not yet decided belong to this category. Such sentences are neither true at the present moment, for they have no real correlate, nor are they false, for their denials too have no real correlate. If we make use of philosophical terminology which is not particularly clear, we could say that ontologically there corresponds to these sentences neither being nor non-being but possibility. Indeterminate sentences, which ontologically have possibility as their correlate, take the third truth-value.”
[
Łukasiewicz, 1970
]
, p. 126.

Technically, however, Łukasiewicz proposed
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
already in 1920; cf.
[
Łukasiewicz, 1920
]
. In
[
Łukasiewicz, 1930
]
he expands
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
to include modalities and thus obtain the matrix of Section
3.2.3
. In fact, he defines only
\Diamond
, but it is easy to check that the definition

\Box x:=\neg\Diamond\neg x

gives operation
\Box
as introduced in Section
3.2.3
directly.

It is worth noting that Lukasiewicz’s interest in ”multivalued logic” (in the sense of logical matrices having more than two values) was motivated by his interest in modal contexts, and not vice versa. Indeed, he wrote:

“I can assume without contradiction that my presence in Warsaw at a certain moment of next year, e.g. at noon on 21 December, is at the present time determined neither positively nor negatively, Hence it is
possible
, but not
necessary
, that I shall be present in Warsaw at the given time. On this assumption the proposition ‘I shall be in Warsaw at noon on 21 December of next year’, can at the present time be neither true nor false. For if it were true now, my future presence in Warsaw would have to be necessary, which is contradictory to the assumption. If it were false now, on the other hand, my future presence in Warsaw would have to be impossible, which is also contradictory to the assumption. Therefore the proposition considered is at the moment
neither true nor false
and must possess a third value, different from ‘0’ or falsity and ‘1’ or truth. This value we can designate by ‘
\frac{1}{2}
’. It represents ‘the possible’, and joins ‘the true’ and ‘the false’ as a third value.”
[
Łukasiewicz, 1930
]
, § 6; cf. English translation in
[
McCall (editor), 1967
]
, pp. 40–65.

The logical matrix
\textbf{\L}_{\aleph_{1}}
and its logic for the first time were discussed by Łukasiewicz in his address delivered at a meeting of the Polish Philosophical Society in Lvov on October 14, 1922. The report of this lecture was published in
Ruch Filozoficzny
, vol. 7 (1923), no. 6, pp. 92–93; see English translation in
[
Łukasiewicz, 1970
]
, pp. 129–130. According to this report, Łukasiwicz’s motivation for this matrix was as follows.

“The application of this interpretation is twofold: 1) It can be demonstrated that if those verbal rules, or directives, which are accepted by the authors of
Principia Mathematica
(the rule of deduction and the rule of substitution) are adopted, then no set of the logical laws that have the numerical value 1 can yield any law that would have a lesser numerical value. 2) If 0 is interpreted as falsehood, 1 as truth, and other numbers in the interval 0–1 as the degrees of probability corresponding to various possibilities, a many-valued logic is obtained, which is an expansion of three-valued logic and differs from the latter in certain details.”
[
Łukasiewicz, 1961
]
, p. 130.

It is clear that
\textbf{\L}_{\aleph_{0}}
is an obvious simplification of
\textbf{\L}_{\aleph_{1}}
.

Answering a question of Hahn, K. Gödel considered
[
Gödel, 1932
]
(see English translation in
[
Gödel, 1986
]
) an infinite series of finite logical matrices and their logics, where the matrix
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
was the second and
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
the third in the series. All these matrices were partially ordered chains with the maximal element designated. Gödel’s goal was twofold: to show that 1) the intuitionistic propositional calculus cannot be determined by a single matrix; and that 2) there are at least countably many logics between the
intuitionistic
and
classical logics
. (These logics will be descussed in more detail in Chapter
4
.)

The aforementioned series of Gödel’s matrices produces a descending chain of logics all containing the intuitionistic propositional logic. Michael Dummett showed
[
Dummett, 1959
]
that
LC
equals the intersection of all Gödel’s logics. He also gave an axiomatization of
LC
, which will be discussed in Chapter
4
.

Chapter 4
Logical Consequence

The conception of logical consequence which we discuss in this chapter can be traced to
Aristotle’s distinction between demonstrative and dialectical argument. The essence of their difference lies in the status of the premises of the argument in question. In a demonstrative argument, its premises are regarded as true. Aristotle called the argument with such premises “didactic” and compared them with what a teacher lays down as a starting point of the development of his subject. We would call such premises the axioms of a theory. In a dialectical argument, according to Aristotle, any assumptions can be employed for premises. More than that, in the
reductio ad absurdum
argument one starts with a premise which is aimed to be proven false.

Thus, the focus in the dialectical reasoning is transferred on the argument itself. This involves considering such an argumentation as a binary relation between a set of premises and what can be obtained from them in the course of this argumentation. And the first question raised by this point of view is this: What characteristics of the dialectical argument could qualify it as sound?

4.1
Consequence relations

From the outset, we are faced with a choice between two paths each addressing one of the questions: Should consequence relation state determination between a set of premises (which can be empty) and a set of conclusions (which can also be empty), or should it do it between a set of premises (possibly the empty one) and a single formula? The first type is known as a
multiple-conclusion
, or
poly-conclusion
, consequence, the second — as a
single-conclusion
consequence, or simply a
logical consequence
. In this book, we discuss only the second option. As to the first, we refer the reader to
[
Shoesmith and Smiley, 2008
]
.

Talking about consequence relation in general, we denote it by “
\vdash
” and equip this sign with subscript when referring to a specific consequence relation.

In the context of consequence relation, we use the following notation:

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{c}X,Y,\ldots,Z:=X\cup Y\cup\ldots\cup Z;\\
X,\alpha,\ldots,\beta:=X\cup\{\alpha,\ldots,\beta\},\end{array}\end{cases}

(4.1)

for any sets
X,Y,\ldots,Z
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas and any
\mathcal{L}
-formulas
\alpha,\ldots,\beta
.

Definition 4.1.1

(single-conclusion consequence relation)
.

A relation
\vdash\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
is an
\mathcal{L}
-
single-conclusion consequence relation

(
or simply an
\mathcal{L}
-
consequence relation
or just a
consequence relation
when consideration occurs in a fixed, though unspecified, formal language
)
if the following conditions are satisfied:

\begin{array}[]{cll}(\text{a})&\alpha\in X\penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ X\vdash\alpha;&(\textit{reflexivity})\\
(\text{b})&X\vdash\alpha\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ X\subseteq Y\penalty\ \textit{imply}\penalty\ Y\vdash\alpha;&(\textit{monotonicity})\\
(\text{c})&\textit{if $X\vdash\beta$, for all $\beta\in Y$, and
$Y,Z\vdash\alpha$, then $X,Z\vdash\alpha$},&(\textit{transitivity})\\
\end{array}

for any sets
X
,
Y
and
Z
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas.

In case
Y=\{\beta\}
, the property (c) reads:

\begin{array}[]{cll}(\text{c}^{\ast})&\textit{$X\vdash\beta$ and $Z,\beta\vdash\alpha$ imply $X,Z\vdash\alpha$}.&\quad\quad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (\textit{cut})\end{array}

A consequence relation
\vdash
is called
nontrivial
if

\vdash\penalty\ \subset\,\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}};

otherwise it is
trivial
.

A consequence relation
\vdash
is called
finitary
if

\textit{$X\vdash\alpha$ implies that there is $Y\Subset X$ such that $Y\vdash\alpha$}.

The last notion can be generalized as follows. Let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal. A consequence relation
\vdash
is called
\kappa
-
compact
if

\textit{$X\vdash\alpha$ implies that there is $Y\subseteq X$ such that $Y\vdash\alpha$
and $\textit{card}(Y)<\kappa$}.

Thus finitariness is simply
\aleph_{0}
-compactness.

A consequence relation
\vdash
is
structural
if

X\vdash\alpha\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \sigma(X)\vdash\sigma(\alpha),\\

for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
.
\vdash
is
structural in a weak sense
if the previous implication holds for all nonempty sets
X
.
1
1

1

The concept of structurality in a weak sense will be used when we want to show that abstract logics obtained in a certain way have a very weak connection with structurality.

We will be using the term
sequent
for a statement ‘
X\vdash\alpha
’.

Remark 4.1.1

(Note on monotonicity of
\vdash
)
.

.
The property (b) of Definition
4.1.1
characterizes the category of monotone consequence relations as opposed to that of non-monotone consequence relations when the property (b) does not hold. In this book we consider only the first category of consequence relations.

Other, specific, properties in addition to (a)–(c) have been considered in literature. For instance, for the language
\mathcal{L}_{B}
and its expansions the following property is typical for many consequence relations:

X,\alpha\vdash\bot\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash\neg\alpha.

We will be using indices to distinguish in the same formal language either two different consequence relations or two equal consequence relation that are defined differently. The following three propositions will be used in the sequel.

Proposition 4.1.1
.

Let
\{\vdash_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of consequence relations in a language
\mathcal{L}
. Then the relation
\vdash
defined by
\bigcap_{i\in I}\{\vdash_{i}\}
is also a consequence relation. Moreover, if each
\vdash_{i}
is structural, so is
\vdash
. In addition, if
I
is finite and each
\vdash_{i}
is finitary, then
\vdash
is also finitary.

Proof
is left to the reader. (See Exercise
4.1
.
2
.)

Given a consequence relation
\vdash
and a set
X_{0}
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas. We define a relation as follows:

X\vdash_{X_{0}}\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}X,X_{0}\vdash\alpha.

(4.2)

Proposition 4.1.2
.

For any consequence relation
\vdash
and arbitrary fixed set
X_{0}
,
\vdash_{X_{0}}
is a consequence relation such that

\varnothing\vdash_{X_{0}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X_{0}\vdash\alpha.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.1
.
3
)

For any consequence relation
\vdash
, we define:

X\vdash^{\circ}\alpha\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ X\vdash\alpha\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ X\neq\varnothing.

(4.3)

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Let
\vdash
be a consequence relation. Then
\vdash^{\circ}
is also a consequence relation. In addition, if
\vdash
is structural,
\vdash^{\circ}
is also structural; further, if
\vdash
is
\kappa
-compact, so is
\vdash^{\circ}
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.1
.
4
)

Now for any consequence relation
\vdash
, we define:

X\vdash^{\bullet}\alpha\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \text{there is a set $X_{0}\Subset X$ such that}\penalty\ X_{0}\vdash\alpha.

(4.4)

Proposition 4.1.4
.

Given a consequence relation
\vdash
, the relation
\vdash^{\bullet}
is the largest finitary consequence relations among those finitary consequence relations that are included in
\vdash
. Hence, if
\vdash
is finitary then
\vdash=\,\vdash^{\bullet}
. In addition, if
\vdash
is structural, then
\vdash^{\bullet}
is also structural.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.1
.
5
)

As is seen from the Proposition
4.1.1
, Definition
4.1.1
is essentially extensional, because it does not show how the transition from premises to conclusion can be carried out.
However, before addressing this issue, we will discuss how the concept of single-conclusion consequence relation can be represented purely in terms of sets of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas.

Exercises
4.1

1.

Consider the properties (a), (b) and (c) of Definition
4.1.1
. Show that (a) and (c) imply (b).

2.

Prove Proposition
4.1.1
.

3.

Prove Proposition
4.1.2
.

4.

Prove Proposition
4.1.3
.

5.

Prove Proposition
4.1.4
.

4.2
Consequence operators

Let
\vdash
be a consequence relation for
\mathcal{L}
-formulas. Then, we define:

\textbf{Cn}(X):=\{\alpha\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\vdash\alpha\},

(4.5)

for any set
X
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas. We observe the following connection:

X\vdash\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(X).

(4.6)

The last equivalence induces the following definition.

Definition 4.2.1
.

A map
\textbf{Cn}:\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})
is called a
consequence operator
if it satisfies the following three conditions:

\begin{array}[]{cll}(\text{a}^{{\dagger}})&X\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(X);&(\textit{reflexivity})\\
(\text{b}^{{\dagger}})&X\subseteq Y\penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(Y);&(\textit{monotonicity})\\
(\text{c}^{{\dagger}})&\textbf{Cn}(\textbf{Cn}(X))\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(X),&(\textit{closedness})\\
\end{array}

for any sets
X
and
Y
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas.

A consequence operator
Cn
is called
finitary
(or
compact
) if

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{d}^{{\dagger}})&\textbf{Cn}(X)\subseteq\bigcup\{\textbf{Cn}(Y)\penalty\ |\penalty\ Y\Subset X\}.\end{array}

As in case of consequence relation, we generalize the last notion for any infinite cardinal
\kappa
: an consequence operator
Cn
is
\kappa
-
compact
if

\textbf{Cn}(X)\subseteq\bigcup\{\textbf{Cn}(Y)\penalty\ |\penalty\ Y\subseteq X\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ \textit{card}(Y)<\kappa\}.

And a consequence operator
Cn
is called
structural
, if for any set
X
and any substitution
\sigma
,

\sigma(\textbf{Cn}(X))\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(\sigma(X)).

We say that
Cn
is
structural in a weak sense
if the previous inclusion is true for all nonempty
X
.

Remark 4.2.1

(Note on monotonicity of
Cn
)
.

A consequence operator as defined in Definition
4.2.1
is called monotone as opposed to non-monotone consequence operators.

A set
X
of formulas is called
closed
, or is a
theory
, (with respect to
Cn
) if
X=\textbf{Cn}(X)
. This, in view of
(\text{a}^{{\dagger}})
and
(\text{c}^{{\dagger}})
, any set
\textbf{Cn}(X)
is a theory; we call it the
theory generated by

X
(with respect to
Cn
).

A theory
X
(with respect to
Cn
) is called
finitely axiomatizable
if there is a set
X_{0}\Subset X
such that
X=\textbf{Cn}(X_{0})
.

For any consequence operator
Cn
, the theory
\textbf{Cn}(\varnothing)
is finitely axiomatizable. Indeed, it is obvious when
\textbf{Cn}(\varnothing)=\varnothing
. If
\varnothing\subset\textbf{Cn}(\varnothing)
, then for any
X
with
\varnothing\subset X\Subset\textbf{Cn}(\varnothing)
, we have:
\textbf{Cn}(\varnothing)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(\varnothing)
.

On the other hand, even if every theory of a consequence operator is finitely axiomatizable, it does not guarantee that the consequence operator is finitary.

Indeed, let
\textit{card}(\mathcal{L})=\aleph_{0}
; thus

\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots\}.

We define:

\textbf{Cn}(X):=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}&\text{if $\alpha_{0}\in X$};\\
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}&\text{if $\alpha_{0}\notin X$ and $\textit{card}(X)=\aleph_{0}$};\\
X&\text{if $\alpha_{0}\notin X$ and $\textit{card}(X)<\aleph_{0}$}.\end{array}\end{cases}

It must be clear that
\textbf{Cn}(X)=X
if, and only if, either
X=\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
or both
\alpha_{0}\notin X
and
\textit{card}(X)<\aleph_{0}
. This implies that every theory with respect to
Cn
is finitely axiomatizable. For if
X=\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, then

X=\textbf{Cn}(\alpha_{0})
. If
X
is a theory with
\textit{card}(X)<\aleph_{0}
, then
X
is obviously finitely axiomatizable.

On the other hand, the operator
Cn
is not finitary. Indeed, let
X
be any infinite set with
\alpha_{0}\notin X
. Then
\alpha_{0}\in\textbf{Cn}(X)
, but
\alpha_{0}\notin\bigcup\{\textbf{Cn}(Y)\penalty\ |\penalty\ Y\Subset X\}
.

There is a very simple and effective criterion for finitely axiomatizable theories.

Proposition 4.2.1

(Tarski’s criterion)
.

Let
X
be a theory with respect to a consequence operator
Cn
in a language
\mathcal{L}
with
\textit{card}(\mathcal{L})=\aleph_{0}
. Then
X
is finitely axiomatizable if, and only if, there is no countable-infinite family

\{X_{i}\}_{i<\omega}
of theories
(
all with respect to
Cn
)
such that

X_{0}\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subset X

and
\bigcup_{i<\omega}X_{i}=X
.

Proof.

The ‘if’ part is true, even if we drop the restriction on the cardinality of
\mathcal{L}
. We leave for the reader to prove this part of the criterion.
(Exercise
4.2
.
1
)

To prove the ‘only-if’ part, we assume that a theory
X
is not finitely axiomatizable. Since
\textit{card}(X)=\aleph_{0}
, we can arrange all formulas of
X
as an infinite sequence:

\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots

Then, we define:

X_{0}:=\textbf{Cn}(\{\alpha_{0}\}),\penalty\ X_{1}:=\textbf{Cn}(\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\})\penalty\ \text{and so on}.

It is clear that the so obtained theories
X_{i}
satisfy the conditions of the proposition.
∎

We observe the following.

Proposition 4.2.2
.

Given a consequence operator
Cn
, any set
\bigcap_{i\in I}\{\textbf{Cn}(X_{i})\}
is closed.

Proof
is left to the reader. (See Exercise
4.2
.
2
.) Compare this proposition with Proposition
4.1.1
.

The next proposition establishes connection between the notion of consequence relation and that of consequence operator.

Proposition 4.2.3
.

Let
\vdash
be a consequence relation. The operator
Cn
defined by (
4.5
) is a consequence operator. Conversely, for a given consequence operator
Cn
, the corresponding relation defined by (
4.6
) is a consequence relation. Moreover,
Cn
is finitary
(
\kappa
-compact
)
if, and only if, so is
\vdash
.
In addition,
Cn
is structural if, and only if, so is
\vdash
.

Proof
can be obtained by routine check and is left to the reader. (See Exercise
4.2
.
3
.)

Proposition
4.2.3
allows us to use the notion of consequence relation and that of consequence operator interchangeably.

The next observation confirms the last remark; it just mirrors Proposition
4.1.1

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Let
\{\textbf{Cn}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of consequence operators in a language
\mathcal{L}
. Then the operator
Cn
defined by
the equality

\textbf{Cn}(X):=\bigcap_{i\in I}\textbf{Cn}_{i}(X)

is also a consequence operator. Moreover, if each
\textbf{Cn}_{i}
is structural, so is
Cn
. In addition, if
I
is finite and each
\textbf{Cn}_{i}
is finitary, then
Cn
is also finitary.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.2
.
6
)

Besides the properties
(\text{a}^{{\dagger}})
–
(\text{c}^{{\dagger}})
, finitariness and
\kappa
-compactness, other properties of a map
\textbf{Cn}:\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})
related to logical consequence have been considered in literature. These are some of them.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{e}^{{\dagger}})&X\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(Y)\penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(Y);\quad(\textit{cumulative transitivity})\\
(\text{f}^{{\dagger}})&X\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(Y)\penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}(X\cup Y)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(Y);\quad(\textit{strong cumulative transitivity})\\
(\text{g}^{{\dagger}})&X\subseteq Y\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(X)\penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}(Y)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(X);\quad(\textit{weak cumulative transitivity})\\
(\text{h}^{{\dagger}})&\bigcup\{\textbf{Cn}(Y)\penalty\ |\penalty\ Y\Subset X\}\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(X);\quad(\textit{finitary inclusion})\\
(\text{i}^{{\dagger}})&\textit{if}\penalty\ X\neq\varnothing,\penalty\ \textit{then}\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}(X)\subseteq\bigcup\{\textbf{Cn}(Y)\penalty\ |\penalty\ Y\neq\varnothing\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ Y\Subset X\};\quad(\textit{strong finitariness})\\
(\text{j}^{{\dagger}})&\textit{if {$\alpha\notin\textbf{Cn}(X)$}, then there is
a maximal $X^{\ast}$ such that $X\subseteq X^{\ast}$}\\
&\textit{and}\penalty\ \alpha\notin\textbf{Cn}(X^{\ast}).\quad(\textit{maximalizability})\end{array}

Proposition 4.2.5
.

Let
Cn
be a map from
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})
into
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})
, Then the following implications hold
\,:

\begin{array}[]{rl}i)&(\text{a}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ (\text{g}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{imply}\penalty\ (\text{c}^{{\dagger}});\\
ii)&(\text{b}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ (\text{c}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{imply}\penalty\ (\text{e}^{{\dagger}});\\
iii)&(\text{e}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ (\text{g}^{{\dagger}});\\
iv)&(\text{f}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ (\text{g}^{{\dagger}});\\
v)&(\text{a}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ (\text{g}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{imply}\penalty\ (\text{f}^{{\dagger}});\\
vi)&(\text{a}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ (\text{f}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{imply}\penalty\ (\text{c}^{{\dagger}});\\
vii)&(\text{a}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ (\text{b}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ (\text{c}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{imply}\penalty\ (\text{f}^{{\dagger}});\\
viii)&(\text{i}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ (\text{d}^{{\dagger}});\\
ix)&(\text{b}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ (\text{d}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{imply}\penalty\ (\text{i}^{{\dagger}});\\
x)&(\text{b}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ (\text{h}^{{\dagger}});\\
xi)&(\text{b}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ (\text{d}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{imply}\penalty\ (\text{j}^{{\dagger}});\\
xii)&(\text{d}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ (\text{h}^{{\dagger}})\penalty\ \textit{imply}\penalty\ (\text{b}^{{\dagger}}).\\
\end{array}

Proof.

We prove
(vi)
,
(xi)
and
(xii)
, in this order, and leave the rest to the reader. (See Exercise
4.2
.
7
.)

Suppose
X\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(Y)
, Then, by virtue of
(\text{a}^{\dagger})
,
X\cup Y\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(Y)
. According to
(\text{b}^{\dagger})
and
(\text{c}^{\dagger})
, we have:

\textbf{Cn}(X\cup Y)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(\textbf{Cn}(Y))\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(Y)
.

To prove
(xi)
, we assume that
\alpha\notin\textbf{Cn}(X)
, for some set
X
.

Let us denote

C_{\alpha}:=\{Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\subseteq Y\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \alpha\notin\textbf{Cn}(Y)\}.

We aim to show that
C_{\alpha}
contains a maximal set.

First, we observe that
C_{\alpha}
is nonempty, for
X\in C_{\alpha}
. Regarding
C_{\alpha}
as a partially ordered set with respect to
\subseteq
, we consider a chain
\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
C_{\alpha}
. That is, either
Y_{i}\subseteq Y_{j}
or
Y_{i}\subseteq Y_{j}
, and
\alpha\notin Y_{i}
, for any
i\in I
. Next we denote

Y_{0}:=\bigcup_{i\in I}\{Y_{i}\}.

We aim to show that
\alpha\notin\textbf{Cn}(Y_{0})
. For contradiction, assume that
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(Y_{0})
. Then, by
(\text{d}^{{\dagger}})
, there is a set
Y^{\prime}\Subset Y_{0}
such that
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(Y^{\prime})
. We note that
Y^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
, for otherwise, by
(\text{b}^{{\dagger}})
, we would have that
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(X)
. Since
Y^{\prime}
is finite, there is
{i_{0}}\in I
such that
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y_{i_{0}}
. Then, by virtue of
(\text{b}^{{\dagger}})
,
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(Y_{i_{0}})
. We have obtained a contradiction. Thus
C_{\alpha}
satisfies the conditions of Zorn’s lemma, according to which
C_{\alpha}
contains a maximal set.

Finally, to prove (
xii
), we assume that
X\subseteq Y
and
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(X)
. In virtue of
(\text{d}^{\dagger})
, there is a finite
Y^{\prime}\subseteq X
such that
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(Y^{\prime})
. We note that, by premise,
Y^{\prime}\Subset Y
. Then, by
(\text{h}^{\dagger})
,
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(Y)
.
∎

In the sequel, we employ a letter ‘
\mathcal{S}
’ to denote both
\vdash
and
Cn
interrelated in the sense of (
4.6
).
More specifically, we call a consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
and the corresponding consequence operator
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}
an
abstract logic

\mathcal{S}
.
Thus, given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, for any sets
X
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas and any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\alpha
, we have:

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\Longleftrightarrow\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X).

(4.7)

Further, we denote

\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}:=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\varnothing)

and call the elements of the latter set
theorems
, or
theses
,
of

\mathcal{S}
, or simply
\mathcal{S}
-
theorems
, or
\mathcal{S}
-
theses
.
Any set
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
is call the
\mathcal{S}
-
theory generated by a set

X
. Thus
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the least
\mathcal{S}
-theory with respect to
\subseteq
.

We note that definition (
4.2
) in conjunction with Proposition
4.1.2
shows a simple way to define an abstract logic without theorems. Indeed, let
\mathcal{S}
be any abstract logic.
Then the abstract logic corresponding to the consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
has no theorems.

A set
X
, as well as the theory
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
, is called
inconsistent
(relative to
\mathcal{S}
) if

\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)=\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
; otherwise both are
consistent
.
An abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
is
trivial
if
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
is trivial, and otherwise is
nontrivial
. We observe that any structural
\mathcal{S}
is trivial if, and only if, for any arbitrary variable
p
,

p\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\varnothing)
. (Exercise
4.3
.
1
)
We note that for any nontrivial abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
,
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. (Exercise
4.3
.
2
)
) We also note that a theory is not required to be closed under substitution. A bit more we can say about substitution in the structural abstract logics: every theory of a structural abstract logic is closed under substitution if, and only if, this theory is generated by a set itself closed under substitution. (Exercise
4.3
.
3
)

We denote

\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}:=\{X\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)=X\}

and

\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}:=\{X\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)=X\subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\}

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an abstract logic and
X_{0}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. For future reference, we denote:

\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}[X_{0}]}(X):=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X_{0}\cup X),

where
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

It must be clear that
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}[X_{0}]}
is a consequence operator. The corresponding abstract logic we denote by
\mathcal{S}[X_{0}]
.
(See Exercise
4.2
.
8
.)

Exercises
4.2

1.

Prove that if
X
is a theory with respect to to
Cn
and there is a countable-infinite family
\{X_{i}\}_{i<\omega}
of theories (all with respect to
Cn
) such that

X_{0}\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subset X

and
\bigcup_{i<\omega}=X_{i}=X
, then
X
is not finitely axiomatizable.

2.

Prove Proposition
4.2.2
.

3.

Prove Proposition
4.2.3
.

4.

Prove that a consequence operator
Cn
is structural if, and only if,
\textbf{Cn}(\sigma(\textbf{Cn}(X)))=\textbf{Cn}(\sigma(X))
, for any set
X
and any substitution
\sigma
.

5.

Let
Cn
be a structural operator. Prove that a set
\sigma(\textbf{Cn}(X))
is closed (or is a theory) if, and only if,

\sigma(\textbf{Cn}(X))=\textbf{Cn}(\sigma(X))
, for any set
X
and any substitution
\sigma
. (Cf.
[
Zygmunt, 2012
]
, section 2.2.)

6.

Prove Proposition
4.2.4
.

7.

Complete the proof of Proposition
4.2.5
.

8.

Given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
and
X_{0}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, show that

\mathcal{S}[X_{0}]
is an abstract logic.

4.3
Realizations of abstract logic

Abstract logic, understood either as a consequence relation or as a consequence operator, as we will see below, can be implemented in different forms. The implementation of abstract logic by the closure system (see Section
4.3.2
) is quite technical and does not reveal the meaning of the term ‘consequence’. Secondly, we note that perhaps historically arose first in the discourse of logic, namely, a logical consequence that can be determined by a deductive system, that is, through the concepts of a rule of inference and formal deduction. It is no coincidence that A. Tarski
[
Tarski, 1936b
]
called this method of defining the logical consequence “the old concept of consequence” in the following passage:

“It is perhaps not superfluous to point out in advance that — in comparison with the new — the old concept of consequence as commonly used by mathematical logicians in no way loses its importance. This concept will probably always have a decisive significance for the practical construction of deductive theories, as an instrument which allows us to prove or disprove particular sentences of these
theories.” (quoted from
[
Tarski, 1956
]
, p. 413)

We present this way of defining abstract logic in Section
4.3.5
, and then use it throughout. What Tarski called above the “new” concept of consequence is connected with semantics. This will be our main way of representing abstract logic. Therefore, we dwell on this in more detail. A general exposition of semantic approach begins in Section
4.3.3
, a detailed implementation of it continues in Section
4.3.4
. This brings us to the conception of separating tools, which we will discuss in Section
5.3
.

The presence of two forms of representation of abstract logic as a consequence relation and as consequence operator, gives us a certain flexibility. Thus, we can choose one form or another that is more convenient in the given circumstances. We start with a fairly general way of defining a new abstract logic from a given one using the concept of substitution.

4.3.1
Defining new abstract logics from given ones by substitution

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an abstract logic in a language
\mathcal{L}
. We define a binary relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}
on
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
as follows:

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha.

(4.8)

First, we notice the following.

Proposition 4.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic.
Then for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\sigma(\alpha).

Proof.

Assume that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
, that is
\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.
Since
\mathcal{S}
is structural, we have that

\sigma(\textbf{Sb}X)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\sigma(\alpha)
. And, in virtue of Proposition
3.1.3
–e, we derive that
\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\sigma(\alpha)
, that is
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\sigma(\alpha)
.
∎

Proposition 4.3.2
.

If
\mathcal{S}
is a structural abstract logic, then
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}
is
a consequence relation.

Proof.

To check the properties (a)–(c) of Definition
4.1.1
for
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}
, we will use Proposition
3.1.3
.

To prove the condition (a), we notice that if
\alpha\in X
, then
\alpha\in\textbf{Sb}X
(Proposition
3.1.3
–a) and hence, be definition (
4.8
),
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
.

Next, suppose that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
and
X\subseteq Y
. In virtue of
Proposition
3.1.3
–d,
\textbf{Sb}X\subseteq\textbf{Sb}Y
. Using (
4.8
) twice, we conclude that
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
. Using the first premise and Proposition
4.3.1

Now, assume that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\beta
, for all
\beta\in Y
, and
Y\cup Z\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
. Applying the property Proposition
3.1.3
–b to the second premise, we get:

\textbf{Sb}Y\cup\textbf{Sb}Z\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
. Using the first premise and Proposition
4.3.1
, we obtain that

\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\gamma
, for all
\gamma\in\textbf{Sb}Y
. Therefore,

\textbf{Sb}X\cup\textbf{Sb}Z\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, that is
X\cup Z\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
.
∎

We denote by
\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}
the abstract logic corresponding to the relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}
, providing that
\mathcal{S}
is a structural abstract logic.

Proposition 4.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic. Then

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha.

Proof.

Assume that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Then, obviously,
X\cup\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. According to the property Proposition
3.1.3
–a,
\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, that is

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
.
∎

The converse of the implication in Proposition
4.3.3
in general does not hold. See Exercise
4.3
.
9
below.

In order to define
\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}
, we need a structural logic
\mathcal{S}
. However,
\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}
itself is almost never structural, although for any substitution
\sigma
,
\sigma(\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}(\varnothing))\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}(\varnothing)
. More precisely,
\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}
is structural in a weak sense if, and only if,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
, for any nonempty set
X
and any formula
\alpha
.

Indeed, in view of Proposition
4.3.1
, we have:
p\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}q
. Let
X
be a nonempty set with
\beta\in X
and
\alpha
be an arbitrary formula. Then, if
\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}
is structural in a weak sense, we have:
\beta\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
and hence
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
.

The converse is obvious.

Proposition 4.3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic. If
\mathcal{S}
is finitary, then
\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}
is also finitary.

Proof.

Suppose
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
, that is
\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is finitary, there is a set
Y_{0}\Subset\textbf{Sb}X
such that
Y_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. The latter implies that
\textbf{Sb}Y_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Further, in virtue of Proposition
3.1.3
–e,
\textbf{Sb}Y_{0}\subseteq\textbf{Sb}X
.

Now, we define:
X_{0}:=Y_{0}\cap X
. Using the property (c) of Proposition
3.1.3
and the above inclusion, we conclude that
\textbf{Sb}X_{0}=\textbf{Sb}Y_{0}
. This implies that
X_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
and, hence,
X_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
.
∎

4.3.2
Consequence operator via a closure system

A set
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})
which contains
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and is closed under any nonempty intersections in
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})
is called a
closure system over

\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. That is, for any closure system
\mathcal{A}
,
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}
and for any
I\neq\varnothing
,

\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}\Longrightarrow\bigcap_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}.

All operators in this subsection are considered over a fixed language
\mathcal{L}
.

Proposition 4.3.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}
be a closure system. Then the operator

\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}(X):=\bigcap\{Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\subseteq Y\penalty\ \textit{and}\,\penalty\ Y\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}\}

is a consequence operator and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}=\{X\penalty\ |\penalty\ X=\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}(X)\}
. Conversely, if
Cn
is a consequence operator and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\textbf{Cn}}
is the family of its closed sets, then

\textbf{Cn}=\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\textbf{Cn}}}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}
is a closure system. It is easy to check that the operator
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{A}}
satisfies the properties
(\text{a}^{{\dagger}})
and
(\text{b}^{{\dagger}})
of Definition
4.2.1
. We prove that it also satisfies
(\text{c}^{{\dagger}})
. For this, given a set
X
, we denote

X_{0}:=\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}(X)

and suppose that
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}(X_{0})
. Aiming to show that
\alpha\in X_{0}
, we note that

\alpha\in X_{0}\Longleftrightarrow\forall Y\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}.\penalty\ X\subseteq Y\Rightarrow\alpha\in Y.

Thus, we assume that
X\subseteq Y
, for an arbitrary
Y\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}
. In virtue of

(\text{b}^{\dagger})
,
X_{0}\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}(Y)
and hence
\textbf{Cn}(X_{0})\subseteq Y
, for
Y\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}
. This implies that
\alpha\in Y
and, using the equivalence above, we obtain that
\alpha\in X_{0}
.

Finally, one can easily observe that

X\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}\Longleftrightarrow\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)=X.

Conversely, suppose
Cn
is a consequence operator and

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\textbf{Cn}}:=\{X\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}(X)=X\}.

Assume that
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\textbf{Cn}}
and denote

X_{0}:=\bigcap_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}.

(4.9)

Since
X_{0}\subseteq X_{i}
, for each
i\in I
,
\textbf{Cn}(X_{0})\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(X_{i})=X_{i}
, also for each
i\in I
. Hence
\textbf{Cn}(X_{0})\subseteq X_{0}
, that is
X_{0}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\textbf{Cn}}
.

Finally, we show that for any formula
\alpha
and any set
X
of formulas,
