\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(X)\Longleftrightarrow\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\textbf{Cn}}}(X).

First, we notice that

\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\textbf{Cn}}}(X)\Longleftrightarrow\forall Y.\penalty\ (X\subseteq Y\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}(Y)=Y)\Rightarrow\alpha\in Y.

Then, in virtue of
(\ast)
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\textbf{Cn}}}(X)&\Longrightarrow[(X\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(X)\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \textbf{Cn}(\textbf{Cn}(X))=\textbf{Cn}(X))\Rightarrow\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(X)]\\
&\Longrightarrow\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(X).\end{array}

Now we suppose that
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(X)
,
X\subseteq Y
and
\textbf{Cn}(Y)=Y
. From the second premise, we derive that
\textbf{Cn}(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}(Y)
and hence
\textbf{Cn}(X)\subseteq Y
. Thus
\alpha\in Y
. Since
Y
is an arbitrary set satisfying the aforementioned conditions, using
(\ast)
, we conclude that
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\textbf{Cn}}}(X)
.
∎

Given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, we denote:

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{S}}:=\{X\penalty\ |\penalty\ X=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\}.

We conclude with the following observation.

Proposition 4.3.6

(Brown-Suszko theorem)
.

An abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
in a language
\mathcal{L}
is structural if, and only if, for any set
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and any substitution
\sigma
, the following holds:

X\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{S}}\Longrightarrow\sigma^{-1}(X)\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{S}}.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{S}
is structural. Let
\sigma
be a substitution and
X\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Assume that
\sigma^{-1}(X)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.
Since
\mathcal{S}
is structural, we have:
\sigma(\sigma^{-1}(X))\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\sigma(\alpha)
. In virtue of (
3.2
–
i
),
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\sigma(\alpha)
, that is
\sigma(\alpha)\in X
. This means that
\alpha\in\sigma^{-1}(X)
.

To prove the converse, we assume that the conditional above holds. Then we, with help of (
3.2
–
ii
), obtain that
X\subseteq\sigma^{-1}(\sigma(X))\subseteq\sigma^{-1}(\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\sigma(X)))
. This implies that
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\sigma^{-1}(\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\sigma(X))))
. Applying the assumption, we get that
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\subseteq\sigma^{-1}(\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\sigma(X)))
. Thus, if
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, that is
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
, then
\alpha\in\sigma^{-1}(\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\sigma(X)))
, that is
\sigma(X)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\sigma(\alpha)
.
∎

4.3.3
Defining abstract logic semantically: a general approach

Consider an abstract object
\mathfrak{M}
that does not belong to the language
\mathcal{L}
, which can
verify

\mathcal{L}
-formulas. The term ‘verify’ will remain undefined during consideration until the end of this subsection, when we propose two explications for this term and for the object
\mathfrak{M}
. We call
\mathfrak{M}
a
model
. If the model
\mathfrak{M}
, verifying an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\alpha
,
accepts
it, we write
\mathfrak{M}\models\alpha
, if it does not, we write
\mathfrak{M}\not\models\alpha
and say that
\mathfrak{M}

rejects

\alpha
. Thus a metalogic here is two-valued, which is useful to remember. The relation of verification of an
\mathcal{L}
-formula in a model
\mathfrak{M}
is extended to any set
X
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas in a usual way:

\mathfrak{M}\models X\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ (\mathfrak{M}\models\alpha,\penalty\ \text{for any $\alpha\in X$}).

The relation of
semantic consequence
is defined as follows: Given a model
\mathfrak{M}
, for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ (\mathfrak{M}\models X\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\models\alpha).

(4.10)

As an auxiliary notation, we also define:

X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}Y\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ (\mathfrak{M}\models X\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\models Y),

where
X\cup Y\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proposition 4.3.7
.

The semantic consequence (
4.10
) is a consequence relation.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a model that can verify or reject every
\mathcal{L}
-formula. We have to show that the properties (a)–(c) of Definition
4.1.1
are valid for the relation
\models_{\mathfrak{M}}
. In terms of this relation, these properties can be written as follows.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a}^{\ast})&X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha,\penalty\ \text{whenever $\alpha\in X$};\\
(\text{b}^{\ast})&(X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X\subseteq Y)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ Y\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha;\\
(\text{c}^{\ast})&(X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}Y\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ Y\cup Z\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow X\cup Z\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha.\end{array}

We prove
(\text{c}^{\ast})
and leave the properties
(\text{a}^{\ast})
and
(\text{b}^{\ast})
to the reader. (Exercise
4.3
.
4
)

Assume that
X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}Y,\penalty\ Y\cup Z\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha
and
\mathfrak{M}\models X\cup Z
. Then the first and third premises imply that
\mathfrak{M}\models Y
and hence
\mathfrak{M}\models Y\cup Z
. This, in conjunction with the second premise, implies that
\mathfrak{M}\models\alpha
.
∎

Let
\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}
be the set of all
\mathcal{L}
-formulas that are verified by a model
\mathfrak{M}
, that is,

\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}:=\{\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\models\alpha\}.

It is obvious that, given a model
\mathfrak{M}
, the consequence relation
\models_{\mathfrak{M}}
can be formulated in terms of
\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}
as follows:

X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\not\subseteq\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}\penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \alpha\in\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}.

(4.11)

The last observation gives rise to the following definitions:

\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast}:=\{(X,\alpha)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \alpha\in\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}\}

and

\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast\ast}:=\{(X,\alpha)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X\not\subseteq\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}\}.

Using (
4.11
), one can easily conclude that

\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast}\cup\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast\ast}\subseteq\,\models_{\mathfrak{M}}.

On the other hand, if
\vdash
is a consequence relation with
\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast}\cup\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast\ast}\subseteq\,\vdash
, then

\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\subseteq\,\vdash
. (See Exercise
4.3
.
5
.)

Thus, we have proved the following.

Proposition 4.3.8
.

Given a model
\mathfrak{M}
, the consequence relation
\models_{\mathfrak{M}}
is the least consequence relation
\vdash
with
\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast}\cup\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast\ast}\subseteq\,\vdash
.

The discussion above can be easily extended to the case when instead of one model we have a class of models, each of which can verify or reject each
\mathcal{L}
-formula.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be such a class of models. Then we define:

X\models_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ (X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha,\penalty\ \text{for any $\mathfrak{M}\in\mathcal{M}$}).

(4.12)

Proposition 4.3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a class of models that can verify or reject
\mathcal{L}
-formulas. Then the relation
\models_{\mathcal{M}}
defined in (
4.12
) is a consequence relation.

Proof.

In virtue of Proposition
4.3.7
and Proposition
4.1.1
, the relation
\models_{\mathcal{M}}
is a consequence relation.
∎

We note that, in virtue of Proposition
4.3.8
, we have:

\models_{\mathcal{M}}=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{M}\in\mathcal{M}}\{\vdash\penalty\ |\penalty\ \vdash\penalty\ \text{is a consequence relation with}\penalty\ \bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast}\cup\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\ast\ast}\subseteq\,\vdash\}.

(4.13)

(Exercise
4.3
.
6
)

Now, let us fix a set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and a class
\mathcal{M}
of models. We denote:

\textbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,\alpha):=\{\mathfrak{M}\in\mathcal{M}\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha\},

\textbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{M}}(X):=\{\mathfrak{M}\in\mathcal{M}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\models X\},

and as a particular case of the last definition

\textbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{M}}(\alpha):=\{\mathfrak{M}\in\mathcal{M}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\models\alpha\}.

Proposition 4.3.10
.

Given a set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and a class

\mathcal{M}
of models,

\textbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,\alpha)=\textbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{M}}(\alpha)\cup(\mathcal{M}\setminus\textbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{M}}(X)).

Proof.

Indeed, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathfrak{M}\in\textbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,\alpha)&\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\models\alpha\penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ X\not\subseteq\bm{T}_{\mathfrak{M}},\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\in\mathcal{M}\quad[\text{in virtue of\penalty\ \eqref{E:semantic-consequence-equivalent}}]\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\in\textbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{M}}(\alpha)\cup(\mathcal{M}\setminus\textbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{M}}(X)).\end{array}

∎

Now, if we do not limit ourselves to the class of
\mathcal{M}
models, but take into account all models that have the ability to accept or reject
\mathcal{L}
-formulas, we can offer similar definitions.

\textbf{Mod}(X,\alpha):=\{\mathfrak{M}\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}\alpha\},

\textbf{Mod}(X):=\{\mathfrak{M}\in\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\models X\},

and

\textbf{Mod}(\alpha):=\{\mathfrak{M}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\models\alpha\}.

Also, as in Proposition
4.3.10
, we have the following equality:

\textbf{Mod}(X,\alpha)=\textbf{Mod}(\alpha)\cup\{\mathfrak{M}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}\notin\textbf{Mod}(X)\}.

(4.14)

Let
R\subset\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. We define:

\textbf{Mod}(R):=\bigcap_{(X,\alpha)\in R}\textbf{Mod}(X,\alpha).

In particular, we have:

\textbf{Mod}(\models_{\mathcal{M}})=\bigcap\{\textbf{Mod}(X,\alpha)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\models_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha\}.

For brevity, we denote:

\mathcal{M}^{\ast}:=\textbf{Mod}(\models_{\mathcal{M}}).

The, we observe that

\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\ast}.

(4.15)

and

\models_{\mathcal{M}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \,\models_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\ast}}

(4.16)

(We leave for the reader to prove (
4.15
) and (
4.16
); see Exercise
4.3
.
7
and Exercise
4.3
.
8
, respectively.)

This takes us to the conception of separating tools which will be discussed in Section
5.3
. Indeed, if we aim to show that
X\not\models_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha
, it may be easier to find a model that
separates

X
from
\alpha
in
\mathcal{M}^{\ast}
, not
\mathcal{M}
.

In this book, the term
model
will be refined in two different ways. These two refinements will lead to two different types of consequence relation given semantically.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a logical matrix of type
\mathcal{L}
and let
\Phi_{\textbf{M}}
be the set of all valuations in the algebra
A
.
We call a
model of type
(A) a pair
\langle\textsf{{M}},v\rangle
, where
v\in\Phi_{\textbf{M}}
. The model
\langle\textsf{{M}},v\rangle

accepts
a formula
\alpha
if
v[\alpha]\in D
; otherwise it
rejects

\alpha
. The models of type (A) are used in the definition of matrix consequence for the formulas of language
\mathcal{L}
(see Section
4.3.4
), in the definition of
\mathcal{E}
-consequence for
\mathcal{L}
-equalities (see Section
7.2
), and in the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas (see Section
9.3
).

A
model of type
(B) is a pair
\langle\textsf{{M}},\Phi_{\textbf{M}}\rangle
.
The model
\langle\textsf{{M}},\Phi_{\textbf{M}}\rangle

accepts
a formula
\alpha
if, and only if,
v[\alpha]\in D
, for every
v\in\Phi_{\textbf{M}}
. This type is used in the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
-consequence for
\mathcal{L}
-equalities (see Section
8.1
).

4.3.4
Consequence relation via logical matrices

The importance of this section is that the consequence operator of any abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
is determined by
\mathcal{S}
-theories.

Given a (logical) matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
, we define a relation

\models_{\textbf{M}}
on
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
as follows:

X\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}(v[X]\subseteq D\Rightarrow v[\alpha]\in D,\penalty\ \text{for any valuation $v$ in {{A}}}),

where

v[X]:=\{v[\beta]\penalty\ |\penalty\ \beta\in X\}.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a nonempty class of matrices. We define the relation of
matrix consequence
(with respect to a class
\mathcal{M}
) as follows:

X\models_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}(X\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha,\penalty\ \text{for all $\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{M}$}).

(4.17)

Accordingly, the relation
\models_{\textbf{M}}
is called a
single-matrix consequence
, or
M
-
consequence
for short.
If a class
\mathcal{M}
is known, we call a matrix consequence associated with a class
\mathcal{M}
an
\mathcal{M}
-
consequence
. Given a nonempty set of matrices
\mathcal{M}
, we will be denoting the
\mathcal{M}
-consequence by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
and will be saying that an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
is
determined
by
\mathcal{M}
if
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
; and we use the notation
\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}
if
\mathcal{M}=\{\textsf{{M}}\}
.

The justification for the terminology ‘
\mathcal{M}
-consequence’ and ‘
M
-consequence’ is the following.

Proposition 4.3.11
.

Any matrix consequence is a structural consequence relation.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\{\textsf{{M}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a nonempty set of matrices. We have to check that the conditions (a)–(c) of Definition
4.1.1
are satisfied for the relation
\models_{\mathcal{M}}
. In addition, we have to show that this consequence relation is structural. We leave this routine check to the reader. (Exercise
4.3
.
11
)
∎

Single-matrix consequence gives us examples of both finitary and non-finitary consequence relations. The first type is exemplified by
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
, a single-matrix consequence with respect to the matrix
\textbf{B}_{2}
(Section
3.2.1
). This will be explained in Section
4.3.5
. Now, we show that the single-matrix consequence
\models_{\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}}
(with respect to the matrix
\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
(Section
3.2.5
)) is not finitary.

Indeed, let us denote:

X:=\{(p_{i}\leftrightarrow p_{j})\rightarrow p_{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 0<i<j<\omega\}.

Let
v
be a valuation in the
\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
-algebra. Assume that

v[X]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
. If for some
i
and
j
with
i<j
,
v[p_{i}]=v[p_{j}]
, then
v[p_{0}]=\mathbf{1}
. If for any
i
and
j
with
i<j
,

v[p_{i}]\neq v[p_{j}]
, then each
v[p_{i}\leftrightarrow p_{j}]=v[p_{i}]\land v[p_{j}]
. Hence, there is an ascending chain, all element of which are less than or equal to
v[p_{0}]
. This implies that
v[p_{0}]=\mathbf{1}
. From this we conclude that
X\models_{\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}}p_{0}
.

On the other hand, let
X_{0}
be any finite subset of
X
. If
X_{0}=\varnothing
, then any valuation
v^{\prime}
with
v^{\prime}[p_{0}]\neq\mathbf{1}
“separates”
X
from
p_{0}
. Suppose that
X_{0}\neq\varnothing
. Let us take a valuation
v^{\ast}
with the property:
v^{\ast}[p_{i}]=a_{i}
, where

\mathbf{0}=a_{1}<a_{2}<\ldots<\mathbf{1}

are the elements of the matrix
\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
. It must be clear that for any
i
,
v[p_{i}\leftrightarrow p_{j}]=a_{i}
. Thus
v^{\ast}[X_{0}]=\{a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{k}}\}
. If we define
v^{\ast}[p_{0}]
to satisfy the conditions
\max\{a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{k}}\}<v^{\ast}[p_{0}]<\mathbf{1}
,
then
v^{\ast}[X_{0}]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
, but
v^{\ast}[p_{0}]\neq\mathbf{1}
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an abstract logic and
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a matrix. Accordingly, we have the consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
and a single-matrix consequence
\models_{\textbf{M}}
. If
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\models_{\textbf{M}}
, we call the filter
D
an
\mathcal{S}
-
filter
, in which case the matrix
M
is called an
\mathcal{S}
-
matrix
(or an
\mathcal{S}
-
model
).

Definition 4.3.1

(Lindenbaum matrix)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic and
D_{\mathcal{S}}
be an
\mathcal{S}
-theory. The matrix
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},D_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
is called a Lindenbaum matrix
(
relative to
\mathcal{S}
)
. For an arbitrary
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we denote:

\mathbf{Lin}_{\,\mathcal{S}}[X]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\rangle

and

\mathbf{Lin}_{\,\mathcal{S}}:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\varnothing)\rangle.

We remind the reader that

any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution is a valuation in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
and vice versa.

(Cf. Proposition
3.2.2
.)

It is obvious that, given a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, for any formula
\alpha
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]\models_{\iota}\alpha,

where
\iota
is the identity substitution.

This shows the prospect that we need to explore if we want to find a method for determining whether
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
or
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.

We begin with the following.

Proposition 4.3.12

(Lindenbaum’s theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic. Then
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}
is an
\mathcal{S}
-model. Also, if a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
is closed under arbitrary
\mathcal{L}
-substitution, then for any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\alpha
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]\models\alpha;

in particular,

\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}\models\alpha.

Proof.

Let us take any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Then, for an arbitrary
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
,
\sigma(X)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\sigma(\alpha)
. Therefore, if
\sigma(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\varnothing)
, then
\sigma(\alpha)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\varnothing)
. Thus

\mathbf{Lin}_{\,\mathcal{S}}
is an
\mathcal{S}
-model.

Now, if, in addition,
\sigma(X)\subseteq X
, then
\sigma(\alpha)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
. Since
\sigma
is an arbitrary
\mathcal{L}
-substitution, we have that

{\textsf{{Lin}}_{\,\mathcal{S}}[X]}\models\alpha
.

Conversely, if
{\textsf{{Lin}}_{\,\mathcal{S}}[X]}\models\alpha
, then, in particular,
\iota(\alpha)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
, that is
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.

The second equivalence is a specification of the first.
∎

We say that a matrix is
weakly adequate
for an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
if this matrix validates all
\mathcal{S}
-theorems and only them. Thus, by virtue of the last equivalence of Proposition
4.3.12
,
we obtain the following.

Corollary 4.3.12.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be structural abstract logic. Then any Lindenbaum matrix relative to
\mathcal{S}

is an
\mathcal{S}
-model. Moreover,
\mathbf{Lin}_{\,\mathcal{S}}
is weakly adequate for
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.3
.
12
)

Proposition
4.3.12
can be generalized as follows.

Proposition 4.3.13
.

Given a structural abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
be the set of all Lindenbaum matrices relative to
\mathcal{S}
. Then
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\,=\,\models_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}
; that is for any set
X
of formulas and any formula
\alpha
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\Longleftrightarrow X\models_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}\alpha.

Proof.

First we suppose that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Let us take any
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},T\rangle
, where
T\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}
. Assume that for some substitution
\sigma
,
\sigma(X)\subseteq T
.
Since
\mathcal{S}
is structural,
\sigma(X)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\sigma(\alpha)
.
This implies that
\sigma(\alpha)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\sigma(X))
and hence

\sigma(\alpha)\in T
.

Conversely, assume that
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. This means (see (
4.7
)) that
\alpha\notin\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
.
Let us denote
\textbf{M}:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\rangle
and take the identity substitution
\iota
. It is obvious that
\iota(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
but
\iota(\alpha)\notin\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
; that is
X\not\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha
.
∎

The last proposition inspires us for the following (complex) definition.

Definition 4.3.2
.

A
(
nonempty
)
family
\mathcal{B}=\{\langle\textsf{{A}}_{i},D_{i}\rangle\}_{i\in I}
of matrices of type
\mathcal{L}
is called a
bundle
if for any
i,j\in I
, the algebras
\textsf{{A}}_{i}
and
\textsf{{A}}_{j}
are isomorphic. A pair
\langle\textsf{{A}},\{D_{i}\}_{i\in I}\rangle
, where each
D_{i}
is a logical filter in
(
or of
)

A
, is called an
atlas
. By definition, the
matrix consequence relative to an atlas

\langle\textsf{{A}},\{D_{i}\}_{i\in I}\rangle
is the same as the matrix consequence related the bundle
\{\langle\textsf{{A}},D_{i}\rangle\}_{i\in I}
, which in turn is defined according to (
4.17
).
Given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, the bundle
\{\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},T\rangle\}_{T\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}}
, where
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}
is the set of all
\mathcal{S}
-theories, is called a
Lindenbaum bundle
relative to
\mathcal{S}
and the atlas
\textsf{{Lin}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
, is called a
Lindenbaum atlas
relative to
\mathcal{S}
.

We say that a given matrix (bundle or atlas) is
adequate
for an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
if
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
and the corresponding matrix relation coincide.

We define:

\begin{array}[]{rl}X\models_{\textsf{{Lin}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}]}\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}&\!\!\!\text{for any $\mathcal{L}$-substitution $\sigma$ and any $T\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}$},\\
&\!\!\!\text{$\sigma(X)\subseteq T$ implies $\sigma(\alpha)\in T$},\end{array}

for any
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Thus we conclude with the following.

Corollary 4.3.13.1
.

The Lindenbaum atlas relative to a structural abstract logic is adequate for this logic.
That is, for any
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\models_{\textsf{{Lin}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}]}\alpha.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.3
.
13
)

Corollary 4.3.13.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic and
\mathcal{M}
be the set of all
\mathcal{S}
-models. Then
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.3
.
14
)

Any
\mathcal{L}
-atlas (or
\mathcal{L}
-bundle) defines a structural consequence relation. The converse is also true as the next proposition shows.

Proposition 4.3.14
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\{\langle\textsf{{A}}_{i},D_{i}\rangle\}_{i\in I}
be a nonempty set of
\mathcal{L}
-matrices. There is an atlas
\mathcal{M}^{\ast}
such that for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\mathcal{M}^{\ast}}\alpha.

Proof.

We define an atlas
\mathcal{M}^{\ast}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\{D_{i}^{\ast}\}_{i\in I}\rangle
as follows.

\textsf{{A}}:=\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i}

and for every
i\in I
,

D_{i}^{\ast}:=\prod_{j\in I}H_{j},

where, given
i\in I
,

H_{j}:=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}D_{i}&\text{if $j=i$}\\
|\textsf{{A}}_{j}|&\text{if $j\neq i$}.\end{array}\end{cases}

We aim to prove that

X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{M}^{\ast}}\alpha.

Assume first that
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha
, that is, for some valuation
v
in
\textsf{{A}}_{i_{0}}
, although
v[X]\subseteq D_{i_{0}}
,
v[\alpha]\notin D_{i_{0}}
. Since each set
|\textsf{{A}}_{i}|
is not empty, we select elements
a_{i}\in|\textsf{{A}}_{i}|
, foe each
i\in I
. Next, we define a valuation
v^{\ast}
in
A
as follows:

v^{\ast}[p]=(a_{i}(p))_{i\in I},\penalty\ \text{for every $p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$},

where

a_{i}(p):=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}a_{i}&\text{if $i\neq i_{0}$}\\
v[p]&\text{if $i=i_{0}$}.\end{array}\end{cases}

It must be clear that

v^{\ast}[X]\subseteq D^{\ast}_{i}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ v^{\ast}[\alpha]\notin D^{\ast}_{i}.

We leave for the reader to prove (Pr
4.3.14
–
\ast
). (See Exercise
4.3
.
15
.)

Now, we suppose that at some valuation
v^{\circ}
in
A
,
v^{\circ}[X]\subseteq D^{\ast}_{j_{0}}
and
v^{\circ}[\alpha]\notin D^{\ast}_{j_{0}}
, for some
j_{0}\in I
. This means that for any
\beta\in X
, the projection of
v^{\circ}[\beta]
on the
j_{0}
th component of
A
belongs to
D_{j_{0}}
but the projection of
v^{\circ}[\alpha]
does not; in symbols

(v^{\circ}[\beta])_{j_{0}}\in D_{j_{0}},\penalty\ \text{for every $\beta\in X$,
but $(v^{\circ}[\alpha])_{j_{0}}\notin D_{j_{0}}$}.

We leave for the reader to prove (Pr
4.3.14
–
\ast\ast
). (See Exercise
4.3
.
15
.)

Thus we obtain that
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha
.
∎

4.3.5
Consequence relation via inference rules

Since we perceive the sentential formulas as forms of judgments (Section 2.1), our understanding of a rule of inference, at least in one important case, will be a relation in the class of forms of formulas. As in the first event, where substitution plays a key role in seeing any sentential formula as an abstract representation of infinitely many judgments, it will play a similar role in the concept of inference rule, at least in this important class of inference rules. It is a way, by which we create an abstraction of infinitely many instances of one element that falls under the name of
structural inference rule
2
2

2

This notion (see definition below) should not be confused with the notion of a structural rule in structural proof theory; for the latter see, e.g., in
[
Negri and von Plato, 2001
]
, section 1.3.
.

Rules and hyperrules

An
inference rule
is a set
R\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. An inference rule receives its significance, when it is a subset of some consequence relation, say
\vdash
. We say that a rule
R
is
sound with respect to

\vdash
if for any
\langle\Gamma,\alpha\rangle\in R
,
\Gamma\vdash\alpha
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of inference rules. Taking into account that
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
is the trivial consequence relation (relative to a language
\mathcal{L}
), we define

\vdash_{[\mathcal{R}]}:=\bigcap\{\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall R\in\mathcal{R}.\penalty\ \text{$R$ is sound with respect to $\mathcal{S}$}\}.

(4.18)

(We note that the right-hand set is nonempty.)

It is not difficult to see that
\vdash_{[\mathcal{R}]}
is a consequence relation. (Exercise
4.3
.
16
) We say that an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
is
determined by
a set
\mathcal{R}
of inference rules if
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\,=\,\vdash_{[\mathcal{R}]}
. Given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, one can treat the consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
as an inference rule that determines
\mathcal{S}
.

Now we turn to structural rules.

Let us consider a pair
\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle\in\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Mf}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Mf}_{\mathcal{L}}
. We call the set

R_{\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle}:=\{\langle\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma),\bm{\sigma}(\phi)\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ \bm{\sigma}\penalty\ \text{is an instantiation of}\penalty\ \textit{{Mv}}_{\mathcal{L}}\}

a
structural rule
(in
\mathcal{L}
) with the premises
\Gamma
and conclusion
\phi
. Each pair
\langle\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma),\bm{\sigma}(\phi)\rangle
is an
instance of the rule

R_{\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle}
(with the premises
\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma)
and conclusion
\bm{\sigma}(\phi)
). It is convenient (and we follow this practice) to identify the rule
R_{\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle}
with its label
\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle
, using them interchangeably. It is customary to write a structural rule
\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle
in the forms
\Gamma/\penalty\phi
or

\frac{\Gamma}{\phi}
. (It is customary to omit the set-builder notation “
\{\ldots\}
”, when a set of premises is finite, and leave the space blank if
\Gamma=\varnothing
.)

Given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, specifically, a structural rule
\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle
is
sound with respect to

\mathcal{S}
if for any instantiation
\bm{\sigma}
,
\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\bm{\sigma}(\phi)
.

A structural rule is called
finitary
(or a
modus
or
modus rule
) if
\Gamma\Subset\textbf{Mf}_{\mathcal{L}}
. If an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
is determined by a finite set of modus rules, one says that set of modus rules is a
calculus
for
\mathcal{S}
. An abstract logic may be defined by more than one calculus, if any. An example of a modus rule is
modus ponens
which is the rule

\bm{\alpha},\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta}/\penalty\bm{\beta}
.

Not all useful inference rules are structural.

Let
\sigma
be a fixed
\mathcal{L}
-substitution. Then we define

R_{\sigma}:=\{\langle\{\alpha\},\sigma(\alpha)\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\}.

Then the
rule of substitution
is the set

R_{\text{sub}}:=\bigcup\{R_{\sigma}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sigma\penalty\ \text{is an $\mathcal{L}$-substitution}\}.

Each
\langle\alpha,\sigma(\alpha)\rangle
is called an
application of the substitution

\sigma

to

\alpha
. A customary utterance is:
\sigma(\alpha)

is obtained from
\alpha
by substitution

\sigma
.

Although substitution is an important component of any structural rule (see Proposition
3.1.4
), the rule of substitution itself is not structural, providing that
\mathcal{L}
contains sentential connectives.

Indeed, for contradiction, we assume that

R_{\text{sub}}=R_{\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle},

for some
\Gamma\cup\{\phi\}\subseteq\textbf{Mf}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Let us fix an arbitrary
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
. By definition, it is clear that for any
\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
\langle\{p\},\alpha\rangle\in R_{\text{sub}}
.
This implies that for any
\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, there is an instantiation
\bm{\xi}_{\alpha}
such that
\bm{\xi}_{\alpha}(\Gamma)=\{p\}
and
\bm{\xi}_{\alpha}(\phi)=\alpha
. Thus, we conclude that
\Gamma\cup\{\phi\}\subseteq\textit{{Mv}}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\Gamma\cap\{\phi\}=\varnothing
; moreover, it must be clear that
\Gamma
cannot have more than one metavariables, because if it were otherwise, the claim

\langle\{p\},\alpha\rangle\in R_{\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle}
would not be true. Thus,
\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle=\langle\{\gamma\},\beta\rangle
, for two distinct metavariables
\gamma
and
\beta
. Then, providing that
\mathcal{L}
contains sentential connectives, there is an instantiation
\bm{\xi}
such that the degree of
\bm{\xi}(\gamma)
is greater than the degree of
\bm{\xi}(\beta)
. A contradiction.

In the sequel (Chapter
8
), we will discuss other non-structural rules of inference.

Proposition 4.3.15
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic in
\mathcal{L}
. Then there are structural rules that determine
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Since
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})\leq\textit{card}(\textit{{Mv}}_{\mathcal{L}})
, there is a one-one map
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textit{{Mv}}_{\mathcal{L}}
which can, obviously, be extended to
f:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{L}}
. (We employ the same notation for both maps.)

Next, we define:

\mathcal{R}:=\{\langle f(X),f(\alpha)\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\}.

It must be clear that any rule
R\in\mathcal{R}
is structural. And if
R=\langle f(X),f(\alpha)\rangle
, then there is an instantiation
\bm{\xi_{0}}
such that
\bm{\xi_{0}}(f(X))=X

and
\bm{\xi_{0}}(f(\alpha))=\alpha
. This implies that any rule
R\in\mathcal{R}
is sound with respect to
\mathcal{S}
. Hence
\vdash_{[\mathcal{R}]}\subseteq\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Now we assume that any rule
R\in\mathcal{R}
is sound with respect to some
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. Suppose
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Then

\langle f(X),f(\alpha)\rangle\in\mathcal{R}
. Therefore, for any instantiation
\bm{\eta}
,
\bm{\eta}(f(X))\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\bm{\eta}(f(\alpha))
.
In particular,
\bm{\xi_{0}}(f(X))\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\bm{\xi_{0}}(f(\alpha))
.
Hence
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\alpha
.
∎

For defining consequence relations, especially structural consequence relations, sometimes even more powerful machinery is convenient. We mean the use of hyperrules.

An (n+1)-tuple

\Theta:=\langle\langle\Gamma_{1},\phi_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle\Gamma_{n},\phi_{n}\rangle,\langle\Delta,\psi\rangle\rangle,

where
\Gamma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\Gamma_{n}\cup\Delta\cup\{\phi_{1},\ldots\phi_{n},\psi\}\subseteq\textbf{Mf}_{\mathcal{L}}
, is called a (
structural
)
hyperrule
.

A hyperrule

\Theta
is
sound with respect to
an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
if for any
\mathcal{L}
-instantiation
\bm{\xi}
and any set
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

[X\cup\,\bm{\xi}(\Gamma_{1})\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\bm{\xi}(\phi_{1}),\ldots,X\cup\,\bm{\xi}(\Gamma_{n})\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\bm{\xi}(\phi_{n})]\Longrightarrow X\cup\,\bm{\xi}(\Delta)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\bm{\xi}(\psi).

If a hyperrule
\Theta
is employed in relation to an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, it is customary to write this hyperrule in the following form:

\dfrac{X,\Gamma_{1}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\phi_{1};\ldots;X,\Gamma_{n}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\phi_{n}}{X,\Delta\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\psi}.

These are examples of rules and hyperrules. (Later in this section we will introduce other inference rules.) We will use them to define consequence relations.

Inference rules:

\begin{array}[]{cllll}(\text{a})&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ i)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\alpha}\wedge\bm{\beta}}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ ii)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\alpha}\vee\bm{\beta}}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ iii)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\alpha}\vee\bm{\beta}}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ iv)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha}}{\neg\neg\bm{\alpha}}\\
\\
(\text{b})&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ i)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha}\wedge\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\alpha}}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ ii)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha}\wedge\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\beta}}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ iii)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha},\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\beta}}&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ iv)\penalty\ \dfrac{\neg\neg\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\alpha}}.\end{array}

Hyperrules:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}(\text{c})&i)\penalty\ \dfrac{X,\bm{\alpha}\vdash\bm{\beta}}{X\vdash\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta}}&ii)\penalty\ \dfrac{X,\bm{\alpha}\vdash\bm{\beta};\penalty\ X,\bm{\alpha}\vdash\neg\bm{\beta}}{X\vdash\neg\bm{\alpha}}&iii)\penalty\ \dfrac{X,\bm{\alpha}\vdash\bm{\gamma};\penalty\ X,\bm{\beta}\vdash\bm{\gamma}}{X,\bm{\alpha}\vee\bm{\beta}\vdash\bm{\gamma}},\end{array}

where
\vdash
in the hyperrules above denotes a fixed binary relation on

\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Consequence relations
\vdash_{1}
,
\vdash_{2}
,
\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}
,
\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}^{\ast}}
, and
\vdash_{3}

First we define a relation
\vdash_{1}
on
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and then prove that it is a consequence relation. Then, we do the same for relations
\vdash_{2}
and
\vdash_{3}
. In all three definitions, we use the concept of (
formal
)
derivation
(or
formal proof
). The relations being defined are written as
X\vdash_{i}\alpha
, where
i=1,2,3
. (For convenience we call
X\vdash_{i}\alpha
a
sequent
, even when we may not know yet that
\vdash_{i}
is a consequent relation.) If
X\vdash_{i}\alpha
is justified according to the clauses below, we say that
X
derives
\alpha
, or
\alpha
is derivable from
X
(all with respect to
\vdash_{i}
). It is for such a justification we use the notion of derivation.

Relation
\vdash_{1}

Given a set
X
of
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas and an
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\alpha
, derivation confirming (or establishing or justifying) a sequent
X\vdash_{1}\alpha
is a finite (nonempty) list of
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}

(4.19)

such that

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{d}_{1})&\alpha_{n}=\alpha;\penalty\ \text{and}\\
(\text{d}_{2})&\text{each $\alpha_{i}$ is either in $X$ or obtained from one or more formulas}\\
&\text{preceeding it on the list \eqref{E:list} by one of the rules (a)--(b)}.\end{array}

It is easy to see that
\vdash_{1}
satisfies the properties (a)–(c) of Definition
4.1.1
and thus is a consequence relation. Moreover, this relation is obviously finitary and structural. One peculiarity of it is that the set of the theses of this relation is empty. Another property of
\vdash_{1}
is that

X\vdash_{1}\alpha\Longrightarrow X\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}\alpha,

(4.20)

where
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
is a single-matrix consequence with respect to matrix
\textbf{B}_{2}
(Section
3.2.1
). To prove (
4.20
), one needs to show that each rule of (a)–(b) preserves validity in
\textbf{B}_{2}
.
We leave this task to the reader. (Exercise
4.3
.
18
)

Clearly, the converse of (
4.20
) is not true, for if
\alpha
is a classical tautology, then

\varnothing\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}\alpha
but
\varnothing\not\vdash_{1}\alpha
.

Relation
\vdash_{2}

Our next example, relation
\vdash_{2}
, is a modification of
\vdash_{1}
. Namely we define:

X\vdash_{2}\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}X,L\textbf{B}_{2}\vdash_{1}\alpha,

where
L\textbf{B}_{2}
is the logic of matrix
\textbf{B}_{2}
(Definition
3.2.5
), that is the set of classical tautologies. According to Proposition
4.1.2
,
\vdash_{2}
is a consequence relation whose set of theses is
L\textbf{B}_{2}
.

In addition, we claim that

X\vdash_{2}\alpha\Longrightarrow X\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}\alpha.

(4.21)

The proof of (
4.21
) follows from the proof of (
4.20
), where we have to add one more (obvious) case — when
\alpha_{i}
in (
4.19
) is a classical tautology, that is
\alpha_{i}\in L\textbf{B}_{2}
.

The converse of (
4.21
) is also true, that is

X\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}\alpha\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{2}\alpha.

(4.22)

To prove (
4.22
), we first prove that the consequence relation
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
is finitary. Indeed, the premise
X\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}\alpha
is equivalent to that the set
X\cup\{\neg\alpha\}
is not
satisfiable
in
\textbf{B}_{2}
, that is for any valuation
v
in
\textbf{B}_{2}
,
v[X\cup\{\neg\alpha\}]\not\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
.
Then, by compactness theorem,
3
3

3

Cf.
[
Barwise, 1977
]
, theorem 4.2. See also
[
Chang and Keisler, 1990
]
, corollary 1.2.12.

X\cup\{\neg\alpha\}
is not
finitely satisfiable
in
\textbf{B}_{2}
, that is, there is
X_{0}\Subset X
such that
X_{0}\cup\{\neg\alpha\}
is not satisfiable in
\textbf{B}_{2}
.
The latter is equivalent to
X_{0}\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
.
4
4

4

We will consider this argument in a more general setting in Section
5.2
.

In the next step, having
X_{0}\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}\alpha
with
X_{0}\Subset X
, we show that
X_{0}\vdash_{2}\alpha
.

If
X_{0}=\varnothing
, then
\alpha
is a classical tautology and the conclusion is obvious. Assume that

X_{0}:=\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}\}.

We denote

\wedge X_{0}:=(\ldots(\beta_{1}\wedge\beta_{2})\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta_{n})

and observe that
\wedge X_{0}\rightarrow\alpha
is a classical tautology, that is

\wedge X_{0}\rightarrow\alpha\in L\textbf{B}_{2}
. Further, it is easy to see that

X_{0}\vdash_{2}\wedge X_{0}
. The two last observations imply that
X_{0}\vdash_{2}\alpha
and hence
X\vdash_{2}\alpha
.

According to Proposition
4.3.11
and the consideration above,
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
, as well as
\vdash_{2}
, is a finitary structural consequence relation.

Relation
\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}

We begin with the remark that
\vdash_{2}
is not a calculus, for the set
L\textbf{B}_{2}
of classical tautologies infinite. However,
\vdash_{2}
can be reformulated in such a way that in a new formulation, we denote it by
\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}
, the latter is a calculus. First of all, we remove from the definition of
\vdash_{2}
all rules except
\alpha,\alpha\rightarrow\beta/\penalty\beta
(known as
modus ponens
or
detachment
). The removed rules are actually replaced with rules without premises, that is rules of type
\varnothing/\penalty\varphi
, where
\varphi
is one of the following forms of formulas (which are called
axiom schemata
):

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{ax1}&\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow(\bm{\beta}\rightarrow\bm{\alpha}),\\
\text{ax2}&(\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta})\rightarrow((\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow(\bm{\beta}\rightarrow\bm{\gamma}))\rightarrow(\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\gamma})),\\
\text{ax3}&\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow(\bm{\beta}\rightarrow(\bm{\alpha}\wedge\bm{\beta})),\\
\text{ax4}&(\bm{\alpha}\wedge\bm{\beta})\rightarrow\bm{\alpha},\\
\text{ax5}&(\bm{\alpha}\wedge\bm{\beta})\rightarrow\bm{\beta},\\
\text{ax6}&\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow(\bm{\alpha}\vee\bm{\beta}),\\
\text{ax7}&\bm{\beta}\rightarrow(\bm{\alpha}\vee\bm{\beta}),\\
\text{ax8}&(\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\gamma})\rightarrow((\bm{\beta}\rightarrow\bm{\gamma})\rightarrow((\bm{\alpha}\vee\bm{\beta})\rightarrow\bm{\gamma})),\\
\text{ax9}&(\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta})\rightarrow((\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\neg\bm{\beta})\rightarrow\neg\bm{\alpha}),\\
\text{ax10}&\neg\neg\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\alpha}.\end{array}

The abstract logic
Cl
, corresponding to
\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}
is called
classical propositional logic
. It is obvious that
Cl
is a calculus.

Proposition 4.3.16
.

The abstract logic
Cl
is finitary.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.3
.
19
)

To prove that
\vdash_{2}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}
, it suffices to show that

L\textsf{{B}}_{2}=\{\alpha\penalty\ |\penalty\ \varnothing\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\alpha\}.

(4.23)

For the last equality, we refer to
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 28, theorem 9, and § 29, theorem
10^{\circ}
. We leave the remaining part of the proof to the reader. (Exercise
4.3
.
20
)

We note the aforementioned removal of rules is not necessary; it would suffice to add to the rules (a)–(b) nine new rules of type
\varnothing/\penalty\varphi
, where
\varphi
is one of the schemata ax1–ax10; in this case, the notion of derivation should be modified from
\vdash_{1}
(namely the clause (
\text{d}_{2}
)) by including the new rules. We treat the forms ax1–ax10 as
premiseless modus rules
.

Relation
\vdash_{3}

For the next example, we employ the hyperrules (c). We state that
X\vdash_{3}\alpha
when the latter can be confirmed by one of the following types of
confirmation
or their combination. A formal definition is the following.

1^{\circ}

The first type is a derivation of
\alpha
from
X
by using steps determined by (
\text{d}_{1}
)–(
\text{d}_{2}
), that is a derivation confirming
X\vdash_{1}\alpha
.

2^{\circ}

The second type is when confirmed premises of one of the hyperrules (c) are satisfied for
\vdash_{3}
, we can confirm the conclusion of this hyperrule for
\vdash_{3}
. (This means an application of the hyperrule in question.)

3^{\circ}

The third type consists in the following.
When we have confirmed
X\vdash_{3}\alpha_{1},\ldots,X\vdash_{3}\alpha_{n}
, we confirm

X\vdash_{3}\alpha
if
X,\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\vdash_{1}\alpha
.

Remark 4.3.1
.

In practice, it is assumed that for each confirmed premise of a hyperrule there is a deduction (confirming it), which is called in
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 21,
a
subsidiary deduction
, and the deduction confirming the conclusion of the rule a
resulting deduction
.

Thus, for any set
X
of
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas and any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\alpha
,
X\vdash_{3}\alpha
is true if, and only if, there is a consecutive finite number of confirmations of the types of
1^{\circ}
–
3^{\circ}
. In particular,

X\vdash_{1}\alpha\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{3}\alpha.

(4.24)

We illustrate the last definition on the following example. Using
X=\{\alpha,\beta\}
(for concrete formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
), we confirm
X\vdash_{3}\alpha\wedge\beta
by the derivation

\alpha,\beta,\alpha\wedge\beta.

Then, we rewrite the last sequent as
\{\alpha\},\beta\vdash_{3}\alpha\wedge\beta
and apply (c-
i
) to obtain
\{\alpha\}\vdash_{3}\beta\rightarrow(\alpha\wedge\beta)
(confirmation of the second type). Now, we rewrite the last sequent as
\varnothing,\alpha\vdash_{3}\beta\rightarrow(\alpha\wedge\beta)
and apply this hyperrule one more time. The resulting sequent is
\varnothing\vdash_{3}\alpha\rightarrow(\beta\rightarrow(\alpha\wedge\beta))
, or
simply
\vdash_{3}\alpha\rightarrow(\beta\rightarrow(\alpha\wedge\beta))
.

It should be clear that the relation
\vdash_{3}
is monotone; that is

(X\vdash_{3}\alpha\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ X\subseteq Y)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ Y\vdash_{3}\alpha.

(4.25)

At the point, we do not know yet if
\vdash_{3}
is a consequence relation. Our aim is to prove this. More than that, we shall show that
\vdash_{3}
and
\vdash_{2}
are equal as relations, though defined differently. We discuss advantage and disadvantage of
\vdash_{3}
in relation to
\vdash_{2}
at the end of this subsection.

Aiming to prove
\vdash_{3}\,=\,\vdash_{2}
, we notice that the relation
\vdash_{3}
enjoys the following variant of cut (the property
(\text{c}^{\ast})
from Definition
4.1.1
).

(X,\beta\vdash_{3}\alpha\textit{ and }\vdash_{3}\beta)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{3}\alpha.

(4.26)

Indeed, applying the hyperrule of (c-
i
), we confirm that
X\vdash_{3}\beta\rightarrow\alpha
. Using (
4.25
), we obtain that
X\vdash_{3}\beta
. Finally, we use a confirmation of type
3^{\circ}
to conclude that
X\vdash_{3}\alpha
.

On the next step, we prove that

X\vdash_{2}\alpha\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{3}\alpha.

(4.27)

Our proof of (
4.27
) is based on the following observation.

\alpha\in L\textbf{B}_{2}\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \vdash_{3}\alpha.

(4.28)

To prove (
4.28
), first, we show that for any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\alpha
,

\vdash_{3}\alpha\vee\neg\alpha.

(4.29)

Indeed,
5
5

5

The following steps 1–6 are the adaptation of the proof of
51^{\ast}
from
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 27.

\begin{array}[]{cl}1.&\{\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha)\},\alpha\vdash_{3}\alpha\vee\neg\alpha\quad[\text{for $\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha),\alpha\vdash_{1}\alpha\vee\neg\alpha$ and\penalty\ \eqref{E:classic-1-implies-classic-3}}]\\
2.&\{\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha)\},\alpha\vdash_{3}\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha)\quad[\text{for $\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha),\alpha\vdash_{1}\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha)$ and\penalty\ \eqref{E:classic-1-implies-classic-3}}]\\
3.&\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha)\vdash_{3}\neg\alpha\quad[\text{by (c-$ii$) with 1 and 2 as premises}]\\
4.&\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha)\vdash_{3}\neg\neg\alpha\quad[\text{analogously to 1--3}]\\
5.&\vdash_{3}\neg\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha)\quad[\text{by (c-$ii$) with 3 and 4 as premises}]\\
6.&\vdash_{3}\alpha\vee\neg\alpha\quad[\text{by applying \eqref{E:calculus-3-has-cut} to 5 and $\neg\neg(\alpha\vee\neg\alpha)\vdash_{1}\alpha\vee\neg\alpha$}]\end{array}

Also, we will use lemma 13 of
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 29, which we formulate in a paraphrased form as follows.

Let
\alpha
be an
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula in the distinct propositional variables
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
; let
v
be a valuation in
\textbf{B}_{2}
; and let a sequence of
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas is produced according to the rule: if
v(p_{j})=\mathbf{1}
, then

\beta_{j}=p_{j}
, and if
v(p_{j})=\mathbf{0}
, then
\beta_{j}=\neg p_{j}
. Then

\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}\vdash_{3}\alpha
or
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}\vdash_{3}\neg\alpha
, according as for the given valuation
v

\alpha
takes the value
\mathbf{1}
or the value
\mathbf{0}
.

Now, to prove (
4.28
), we assume that
\alpha\in L\textbf{B}_{2}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
are all variables that occur in
\alpha
. Then, according to the last property,
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\vdash_{3}\alpha
and

p_{1},\ldots,\neg p_{n}\vdash_{3}\alpha
. This, in virtue of (c-
iii
), implies that

p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1},p_{n}\vee\neg p_{n}\vdash_{3}\alpha
. Since, by (
4.26
),
\vdash_{3}p_{n}\vee\neg p_{n}
, we obtain that

p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1}\vdash_{3}\alpha
. Continuing the elimination of the variables
p_{j}
, we come to the conclusion that
\vdash_{3}\alpha
.

Now we obtain the proof of (
4.27
) as follows.

Assume that
X\vdash_{2}\alpha
; that is for some
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in L\textbf{B}_{2}
,
X,\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\vdash_{1}\alpha
. According to (
4.24
),
X,\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\vdash_{3}\alpha
,
where for each
\alpha_{j}
, in virtue of (
4.28
),

\vdash_{3}\alpha_{j}
. Using (
4.26
)
n
times, we obtain that

X\vdash_{3}\alpha
.

Finally, we show that

X\vdash_{3}\alpha\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{2}\alpha.

(4.30)

The last implication will be proven if we show that

X\vdash_{3}\alpha\Longrightarrow X\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}\alpha

(4.31)

and then apply (
4.22
).

We note that (
4.31
) will be proven if we establish that every rule of (a)–(b) and every hyperrule of (c) preserves the truth value
\mathbf{1}
. Let us check this property for the hyperrules.

We begin with (c-
i
). Assume that for any valuation
v^{\prime}
in
\textbf{B}_{2}
,

v^{\prime}[X,\alpha]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}\Longrightarrow v^{\prime}[\beta]=\mathbf{1}.

Suppose for some valuation
v
,
v[X]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
. We observe that if
v[\alpha]=\mathbf{0}
, then
v[\alpha\rightarrow\beta]=\mathbf{1}
, regardless of the value of
v[\beta]
. However, if
v[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}
, then
v[\alpha\rightarrow\beta]=\mathbf{1}
, by premise.

Now we turn to (c-
ii
). Suppose that for any valuation
v^{\prime}
in
\textbf{B}_{2}
,

v^{\prime}[X,\alpha]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}\Longrightarrow\text{both $v^{\prime}[\beta]=\mathbf{1}$ and $v^{\prime}[\neg\beta]=\mathbf{1}$}.

It is clear that this premise is equivalent to that for any valuation
v^{\prime}
in
\textbf{B}_{2}
,

v^{\prime}[X,\alpha]\not\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}.

Thus, if for some valuation
v
,
v[X]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
, then obviously

v[\alpha]=\mathbf{0}
, that is
v[\neg\alpha]=\mathbf{1}
.

To prove this preservation property for (c-
iii
), we suppose that for any valuation
v^{\prime}
in
\textbf{B}_{2}
, both

\begin{array}[]{l}v^{\prime}[X,\alpha]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}\Longrightarrow v^{\prime}[\gamma]=\mathbf{1}\penalty\ \text{and}\\
v^{\prime}[X,\beta]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}\Longrightarrow v^{\prime}[\gamma]=\mathbf{1}\end{array}

hold. Now assume that for some valuation
v
,
v[X,\alpha\vee\beta]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
. It is clear that either
v[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}
or
v[\beta]=\mathbf{1}
(or both). In each case, we obtain that
v[\gamma]=\mathbf{1}
.

Thus we have obtained the equality
\vdash_{2}\,=\,\vdash_{3}
. As a byproduct of this equality, we conclude that
\vdash_{3}
is a structural consequence relation.

Comparison of
\vdash_{2}
and
\vdash_{3}

Although
\vdash_{3}
is not a calculus, it is very convenient in confirming sequents that are true with respect to
\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}
. For instance, we show that the schema ax8 above is a thesis of
\vdash_{3}
. Indeed, we have:

\begin{array}[]{cl}1.&\{\alpha\rightarrow\gamma,\beta\rightarrow\gamma\},\alpha\vdash_{3}\gamma;\penalty\ \{\alpha\rightarrow\gamma,\beta\rightarrow\gamma\},\beta\vdash_{3}\gamma\quad[\text{two $\vdash_{1}$-derivations}]\\
2.&\{\alpha\rightarrow\gamma,\beta\rightarrow\gamma\},\alpha\vee\beta\vdash_{3}\gamma\quad[\text{from 1 by (c-$iii$)}]\\
3.&\{\alpha\rightarrow\gamma\},\beta\rightarrow\gamma\vdash_{3}(\alpha\vee\beta)\rightarrow\gamma\quad[\text{from 2 by (c-$i$)}]\\
4.&\alpha\rightarrow\gamma\vdash_{3}(\beta\rightarrow\gamma)\rightarrow((\alpha\vee\beta)\rightarrow\gamma)\quad[\text{from 3 by (c-$i$)}]\\
5.&\vdash_{3}(\alpha\rightarrow\gamma)\rightarrow((\beta\rightarrow\gamma)\rightarrow((\alpha\vee\beta)\rightarrow\gamma)).\quad[\text{from 4 by (c-$i$)}]\end{array}

However, there is a price for such a convenience. While in proving
X\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\alpha
(or in proving
X\vdash_{2}\alpha
) a set
X
stays the same in each application of a rule; in proving
X\vdash_{3}\alpha
, when we need to apply a hyperrule, the set
X
in the premise(s) of a hyperrule may differ from the original
X
in the sequent we are proving. In addition, in each application of a hyperrule, the
X
in its premise(s) may vary from hyperrule to hyperrule or from one application to another of the same hyperrule.

The idea of semantic completeness

The equalities like
\vdash_{2}\,=\,\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
or
\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\,=\,\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
or
\vdash_{3}\,=\,\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
, but not
\vdash_{2}\,=\,\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}
or
\vdash_{2}\,=\,\vdash_{3}
, (see Section
4.3.5
) are typical examples of semantic completeness. That is, a completeness result is claimed when one and the same consequence relation admits two different definitions — one purely syntactic and the other with the help of semantic concepts, such as, e.g., logical matrices. The former is regarded as a convenient way to define an abstract logic, say
\mathcal{S}
, the latter as an effective way to show that for a particular set
X
and formula
\alpha
, the sequent
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
does not hold. This issue will be further discussed in the next chapter (Section
5.3
).

To demonstrate, e.g., the validity of
\vdash_{2}\,=\,\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
, we proved the properties (
4.21
) and (
4.22
). The former is known in literature as
soundness
, the latter as (properly)
semantic completeness
. Thus we can state that the abstract logic
\vdash_{2}
is
complete with respect to
the logical matrix
\mathbf{B}_{2}
, or that

\vdash_{2}
is
determined by

\mathbf{B}_{2}
.

Although the main concept of this book is consequence relation, the equalities
like

\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{T}_{\textsf{LC}}=L\textsf{{LC}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{T}_{\textsf{LC}}=L\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}\end{array}

(compare with the
LC
completeness above) will attract our attention too.
We call them also completeness results but in a weaker sense of the term “completeness”.

4.4
Abstract logics defined by modus rules

When we use or investigate calculi, we inevitably deal with modus rules.
As we will see below (Corollary
4.4.1.2
), for a structural logic to be finitary and defined as calculus, are equivalent properties. On the other hand, the modus rules are naturally modeled in first order logic.

4.4.1
General characterization

If
\mathcal{R}
is a set of modus rules, there is an effective way to determine the consequence relation
\vdash_{[\mathcal{R}]}
defined in (
4.18
) (Section
4.3.5
). For this, we define a relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
on
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, by generalizing (for
\mathcal{L}
-formulas) the notion of derivation which earlier was defined for
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas via (
\text{d}_{1}
)–(
\text{d}_{2}
).

The relation
X\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\alpha
holds, by definition, if there is a list

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},\alpha

(4.32)

(the list
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
can be empty), where each formula on (
4.32
) is

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{d}^{\ast}_{1})&\text{either in $X$ or}\\
(\text{d}^{\ast}_{2})&\text{obtained from the formulas preceding it on \eqref{E:list-2} by one of the rules $\mathcal{R}$.}\end{array}

We note that so defined
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
is finitary. About (
4.32
), we say that it is an
\mathcal{R}
-
derivation
of
\alpha
from
X
.

Proposition 4.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of modus rules. Then
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\,=\,\vdash_{[\mathcal{R}]}
. Hence
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
is a consequence relation.

Proof.

We shall prove successively the following three claims.

\begin{array}[]{cl}1^{\circ}&\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ \text{is a structural consequence relation};\\
2^{\circ}&\text{for any $R\in\mathcal{R}$, $R$ is sound with respect to $\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}$};\\
3^{\circ}&\text{if an abstract logic $\mathcal{S}$ is such that if for any $R\in\mathcal{R}$,}\\
&\text{$R$ is sound with respect to $\mathcal{S}$, then $\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}$.}\end{array}

We notice that the properties (a)–(b) of Definition
4.1.1
are obviously true for
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
; and we leave for the reader to check the property (c) and structurality property. (Exercise
4.4
.
2
)

Next, suppose that
\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle\in\mathcal{R}
. Let
\bm{\sigma}
be an arbitrary instantiation in
\mathcal{L}
. Since
\Gamma
is finite, we denote

\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma):=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}.

According to the clauses
(\text{d}^{\ast}_{1})
–
(\text{d}^{\ast}_{2})
, the list

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},\bm{\sigma}(\phi)

is an
\mathcal{R}
-derivation of
\bm{\sigma}(\phi)
from
\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma)
. Hence
\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\bm{\sigma}(\phi)
.

Finally, let
\mathcal{S}
be an abstract logic and for any
R\in\mathcal{R}
,
R
is sound with respect to
\mathcal{S}
. We show that then

X\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\alpha\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha.

Suppose (
4.32
) is a derivation with respect to
\mathcal{R}
. We prove that for each formula
\alpha_{i}
of (
4.32
),
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{i}
.
We employ induction on the number of the formulas preceding
\alpha
in (
4.32
).
Indeed, we see that, if
\alpha=\alpha_{1}
, then
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{1}
, for either
\alpha\in X
or
\alpha
is a conclusion of a premiseless rule.

Now, suppose
\alpha
is obtained by application of a rule
R_{\langle\Gamma,\bm{\beta}\rangle}
. That is, for some formulas
\alpha_{i_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{i_{k}}
preceding
\alpha
and some instantiation
\bm{\sigma}
,

\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma)=\{\alpha_{i_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{i_{k}}\}
and
\bm{\sigma}(\bm{\beta})=\alpha
. Since, by induction,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{i_{j}}
,
1\leq j\leq k
, and, by soundness of
R_{\langle\Gamma,\bm{\beta}\rangle}
with respect to
\mathcal{S}
,
\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, we conclude that

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.
∎

Given a set
\mathcal{R}
of modus rules, we denote by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
the abstract logic corresponding to
\vdash_{[\mathcal{R}]}
(see the definition (
4.18
)) or, equivalently (due to Proposition
4.4.1
), to
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Corollary 4.4.1.1
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is a set of modus rules, then the abstract logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
is structural and finitary.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.4
.
1
)

Corollary 4.4.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic. Then
\mathcal{S}
is finitary if, and only if, there is a set
\mathcal{R}
of modus rules such that
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proof.

The if-part follows from Corollary
4.4.1.1
.

To prove the ‘only-if’ part, we assume that
\mathcal{S}
is finitary. Next we define:

\mathcal{R}:=\{\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ \Gamma\cup\{\phi\}\Subset\textbf{Mf}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ \text{and $\bm{\xi}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\bm{\xi}(\phi)$, for any instantiation $\bm{\xi}$}\}.

We aim to prove that
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

First, assume that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
, for a particular set
X\cup\{\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Since
\mathcal{S}
is finitary, there is a set

Y\Subset X
such that
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
. Now, replacing the variables
\mathcal{V}(Y\cup\{\beta\})
with metavariables according to arbitrary one-one correspondence, we obtain a finite set
\Gamma\cup\{\phi\}
of metaformulas such that for any instantiation
\bm{\xi}
,

\bm{\xi}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\bm{\xi}(\phi)
and for a particular instantiation
\bm{\xi}_{0}
,
\bm{\xi}_{0}(\Gamma)=Y
and

\bm{\xi}_{0}(\phi)=\beta
. This implies that

\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle\in\mathcal{R}
. The latter in turn allows us to conclude that
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\beta
and hence
X\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\beta
.

Now, we suppose that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\beta
. That is, there is an
\mathcal{R}
–derivation, determined by
(\text{d}^{\ast}_{1})
–
(\text{d}^{\ast}_{2})
:

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},\beta.

It is obvious that the list
(\ast)
is not empty. We prove (by induction) that for each formula
\gamma
of
(\ast)
,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\gamma
.

It is true for the first formula
\gamma
in
(\ast)
, in which case
\gamma=\alpha_{1}=\beta
, for, then,
\gamma\in X
and hence
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\gamma
.

Suppose we proved that for each
\alpha_{i}
,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{i}
. If
\beta\in X
, we are done. If
\beta\notin X
, then
\beta
is obtained from
\alpha_{i_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{i_{k}}
by a rule

\langle\Gamma,\phi\rangle\in\mathcal{R}
. By induction, this implies that
\{\alpha_{i_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{i_{k}}\}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
. By the transitivity property, we conclude that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
.
∎

4.4.2
Abstract logics
\mathcal{S}^{\star}

Let an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
be equal
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
, for some set
\mathcal{R}
of modus rules. We define a relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{\star}}
on the set
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
by adding to
(\text{d}_{1}^{\ast})
–
(\text{d}_{2}^{\ast})
one more clause:
any formula of‘(
4.32
) can be obtained from one of the preceding formulas by (uniform) substitution.

Proposition 4.4.2
.

\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{\star}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}
. Hence
\mathcal{S}^{\star}
is a finitary abstract logic.

Proof.

Suppose for some set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{\star}}\alpha
. This means that there is a derivation

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},

where
\alpha_{n}=\alpha
and each
\alpha_{i}
is either obtained by one of a given set of modus rules (which includes instantiations of axioms, if any) belong to
X
or obtained by substitution from one of the preceding formulas in the derivation.

It is clear that only a finite set
X_{0}
of the formulas of
X
may occur in such a derivation. According to a technique developed by B. Sobociński
[
Sobociński, 1974
]
(see also
[
Lambros, 1979
]
), the above derivation can be reconstructed in such a way that a new derivation will have the last formula identical with
\alpha
and, in addition, all substitution applications can only occur with the formulas of
X_{0}
. This implies
that
\textbf{Sb}X_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, that is
X_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
; hence
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
.

Conversely, assume that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\alpha
, that is
\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is finitary (Corollary
4.4.1.1
), there is a set
Y_{0}\Subset\textbf{Sb}X
such that
Y_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. This implies that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{\star}}\alpha
.

The equality
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{\star}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}
in turn implies that
\mathcal{S}^{\star}
is an abstract logic (because
\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}
is); and from the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\star}
we derive that the latter is finitary.
∎

Although a logic
\mathcal{S}
defined by modus rules is always structural (Corollary
4.4.1.1
),
\mathcal{S}^{\star}
is not necessarily so. We conclude this subsection with the following example.

Since the abstract logic
Cl
is defined by modus rules, we define an abstract logic
\textsf{Cl}^{\star}
. The latter logic is not structural. (See Exercise
4.4
.
3
.) It is noteworthy that
\textsf{Cl}^{\star}
has only two theories. Namely the following proposition holds.

Proposition 4.4.3
.

For any set
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}
,

\textbf{Cn}_{\textsf{Cl}^{\star}}(X)=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}&\text{if $X\subseteq\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}$}\\
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\end{cases}

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.4
.
4
)

4.4.3
The class of
\mathcal{S}
-models

The class of
\mathcal{S}
-models, when
\mathcal{S}
is given semantically, was discussed in Section
4.3
.
In this subsection we focus on the class of
\mathcal{S}
-models, when an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
can be determined by a set of modus rules. Given a structural abstract logic

\mathcal{S}
in a language
\mathcal{L}
, we employ for our characterization of
\mathcal{S}
-models a special language of first order. This language depends on
\mathcal{L}
and we denote it by
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

The denumerable set of the individual variables of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
consists of the
symbols
\bm{x_{\alpha}}
, for each metavariable
\bm{\alpha}
. The functional
connectives coincide with the connectives of
\mathcal{L}
. Also, there is one unary predicate
symbol,
D
, two logical connectives,
\boldsymbol{\land}
(conjunction) and
\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}

(implication), universal quantifier
\forall
, as well the parentheses, ‘(’ and ‘)’. The rules of formula formation are usual, as well as is usual the notion of an
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-structure (including the notions of
satisfactory
and
validity
). If
\Theta
is an
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-formula containing only free variables, its universal closure is denoted by
\forall\ldots\forall\Theta
.
Main reference here is
[
Chang and Keisler, 1990
]
.

It must be clear that if
\phi
is a metaformula relevant to
\mathcal{L}
, then, replacing each metavariable
\bm{\alpha}
in
\phi
with the individual variable
\bm{x_{\alpha}}
, we obtain a term
of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
. We denote this term (obtained from
\phi
)
by
\phi^{\ast}
.

Further, any modus rule
R:=\dfrac{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n}}{\phi}
is translated to the formula

R^{\ast}:=\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}((D(\psi_{1}^{\ast})\boldsymbol{\land}\ldots\boldsymbol{\land}D(\psi_{n}^{\ast}))\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}D(\phi^{\ast}))

(4.33)

and any premiseless rule
T:=\varnothing/\penalty\phi
to the formula

T^{\ast}:=\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}D(\phi^{\ast}).

(4.34)

The formulas of types (
4.33
) and (
4.34
) belong to the class of
basic Horn sentences
; cf.
[
Chang and Keisler, 1990
]
, section 6.2.

It is clear that any matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
of type
\mathcal{L}
can be regarded as a model for
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
-formulas. We denote the
validity
of a closed formula
\Theta
in
M
by
\textsf{{M}}\Vdash\Theta
.

Let
\psi
be a metaformula of
\textbf{Mf}_{\mathcal{L}}
containing only metavariables
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
. We note that there is a strong correlation between the standard valuation of the term
\psi^{\ast}
(containing only individual variables
x_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,x_{\alpha_{n}}
) in a first order structure
M
and a valuation of a formula
\bm{\xi}(\psi)
, obtained from
\psi
by a simple instantiation
\bm{\xi}
, in a logical matrix
M
. For instance, let us denote the value of
\psi^{\ast}
on a sequence
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
in
M
(as a first order structure) by
\psi^{\ast}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]
. Now let
\bm{\xi}
be a simple instantiation with

\bm{\xi}(\alpha_{i})=p_{i}
. Then, if
v
is a valuation in
A
with
v[p_{i}]=a_{i}
,
then
\psi^{\ast}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]=v[\bm{\xi}(\psi)]
. This can be proven by induction on the degree of
\psi
.

Grounding on the last conclusion, we obtain The first order formula
D(\psi^{\ast})
is satisfied on the sequence
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
in the first order structure
M
if, and only if,
v[\bm{\xi}(\psi)]\in D
in the logical matrix
M
.

This argument leads to the following lemma.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a matrix of type
\mathcal{L}
. For any modus rule
R
,
R
is sound with respect to
\models_{\textbf{M}}
if, and only if,
\textsf{{M}}\Vdash R^{\ast}
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.4
.
7
)

Proposition 4.4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of modus rules in
\mathcal{L}
. Then for any
\mathcal{L}
-matrix
M
,
M
is an
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
-model if, and only if,

\textsf{{M}}\Vdash R^{\ast}
, for any
R\in\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

We fix an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
. In view of Proposition
4.4.1
, we can associate with the abstract logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
the consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
.

We begin with the ‘only-if’ part. Thus we assume that the inclusion
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\,\subseteq\,\models_{\textbf{M}}
holds. This implies that any
R\in\mathcal{R}
is sound with respect to
\models_{\textbf{M}}
. By Lemma
4.4.1
, this in turn implies that
\textsf{{M}}\Vdash R^{\ast}
, for any
R\in\mathcal{R}
.

To prove the ‘if’ part, we assume that the last conclusion holds. By Lemma
4.4.1
, we derive that any
R\in\mathcal{R}
is sound with respect to

\models_{\textbf{M}}
.

Now, suppose that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\alpha
. That is, there is an
\mathcal{R}
-derivation (
4.32
). By induction on the length of (
4.32
),
one can prove that for any valuation
v
in
A
, if
v[X]\subseteq D
, then
v[\alpha]\in D
. We leave this task to the reader. (Exercise
4.4
.
8
)
∎

Corollary 4.4.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of modus rules in
\mathcal{L}
. Then the class of all

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
-models is closed under reduced products, in particular under ultraproducts.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the class of all
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
-models. According to Proposition
4.4.4
, an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix
\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{M}
if, and only if, for any
R\in\mathcal{R}
,
R\in\mathcal{R}
. This means that
\mathcal{M}
is determined by a set of Horn formulas. It is well known that every Horn sentence is preserved under reduced products, in particular under ultraproducts; cf.
[
Chang and Keisler, 1990
]
, proposition 6.2.2.
∎

4.4.4
Modus rules vs. non-finitary rules

Proposition
4.4.1
and Corollary
4.4.1.1
show limitation of modus rules in defining consequent relations. It should not be surprising that there are consequence relations that cannot be defined by any set of modus rules. Below we discuss an example of the consequence relation of that kind; moreover, the theorems of that abstract logic, as we will show, coincide with the theorems of a calculus,
LC
. (See
LC
completeness below.)

In the language
\mathcal{L}_{A}
, we formulate two more premiseless structural rules:

\begin{array}[]{cl}\text{ax11}&\bm{\beta}\rightarrow(\neg\bm{\beta}\rightarrow\bm{\alpha}),\\
\text{ax12}&(\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta})\vee(\bm{\beta}\rightarrow\bm{\alpha}).\end{array}

A calculus
LC
is defined through derivations according to
(\text{d}_{1})
–
(\text{d}_{2})
where in
(\text{d}_{2})
, however, we use only the rules
ax1–ax9, ax11-ax12 and (
b
-
iii
) (modus ponens).

We note two properties of
LC
. The first is that for any set
X_{0}\cup\{\alpha\}\Subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}
,

X_{0}\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \wedge X_{0}\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}\alpha.

(4.35)

(See Exercise
4.4
.
5
.)

The second property is known as the “deduction theorem” (for
LC
), which in its
\Rightarrow
-part, in essence, asserts that the hyperrule (c-
i
) is sound with respect to
\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}
. Namely, for any set
X\cup\{\alpha,\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}
,

X,\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}\beta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta.

(4.36)

(See Exercise
4.4
.
6
.)

In our consideration, we employ the single-matrix relation
\models_{\textbf{LC}}
which is grounded on the logical matrix
LC
; see Section
3.2.5
.

We will need the following property.

For any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
,

\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \models_{\textbf{LC}}\alpha.

The last equivalence is due to M. Dummett
[
Dummett, 1959
]
, theorem 1.

Next, we define a consequence relation,
\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}
and, accordingly, an abstract logic
LC
), analogously to the relation
\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}
(Section
), but by using the rules ax1–ax9, ax11–ax12 and modus ponens (the rule (b-
iii
)) for the notion of
LC
-derivation defined by the clauses (
\text{d}_{1}^{\ast}
)–(
\text{d}_{2}^{\ast}
) above.

In addition, we consider the non-modus structural rule
R^{\ast}:=R_{\langle\Gamma^{\ast},\bm{\alpha}_{0}\rangle}
with

\Gamma^{\ast}:=\{(\bm{\alpha}_{i}\leftrightarrow\bm{\alpha}_{j})\rightarrow\bm{\alpha}_{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 0<i<j<\omega\},

where
\bm{\alpha}_{i}
,
0\leq i<\omega
, are pairwise distinct metavariables. Also, we define an infinite set of modus rules

\mathcal{R}:=\{\Gamma/\penalty\bm{\alpha}_{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \varnothing\subset\Gamma\Subset\Gamma^{\ast}\}.

Then, two consequence relations,
\vdash^{\ast}
and
\vdash^{\star}
, are defined in
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}

similarly to the notion of
LC
-derivation but also for
\vdash^{\ast}
with
R^{\ast}
as an additional inference rule and for
\vdash^{\star}
with the rules of
\mathcal{R}
as additional inference rules.

We aim to show that, although for any set
X_{0}\cup\{\alpha\}\Subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}
,

X_{0}\vdash^{\star}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X_{0}\vdash^{\ast}\alpha,

(4.37)

the consequence relation
\vdash^{\ast}
cannot be defined by any set of structural modus rules, for, as we will show, it is not finitary; see Corollary
4.4.1.1
.

As concerns (
4.37
), it is obvious, for in any
\vdash^{\ast}
-derivation with a finite set
X_{0}
as a set of premises,
the rule
R^{\ast}
can be applied only under such an instantiation
\bm{\xi}
, for which
there is an instantiation
\bm{\zeta}
such that
\bm{\xi}(\Gamma^{\ast})/\penalty\bm{\xi}(\bm{\alpha_{0}})=\bm{\zeta}(\Gamma)/\penalty\bm{\zeta}(\bm{\alpha})
, for some
\Gamma\Subset\Gamma^{\ast}
; and vice versa.

Finally, we demonstrate that the consequence relation
\vdash^{\ast}
differs from
\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}
, for the latter is finitary (Corollary
4.4.1.1
), while the former cannot be defined by any set of modus rules.

For contradiction, assume that
\vdash^{\ast}
is finitary. Let
\bm{\sigma}
be an instantiation such that
\bm{\sigma}(\bm{\alpha}_{i})=p_{i}
,
0\leq i<\omega
. We note that
\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma^{\ast})\vdash^{\ast}p_{0}
. By assumption, for some finite
X_{0}\Subset\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma^{\ast})
,
X_{0}\vdash^{\ast}p_{0}
. In virtue of (
4.37
),
X_{0}\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}p_{0}
. This in turn,
according to (
4.35
) and (
4.36
), implies
that
\vdash_{\textsf{LC}}\wedge X_{0}\rightarrow p_{0}
. By
LC
completeness,
we obtain that
\models_{\textbf{LC}}\wedge X_{0}\rightarrow p_{0}
and hence

X_{0}\models_{\textbf{LC}}p_{0}
. Thus, by monotonicity, we have that

\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma^{\ast})\models_{\textbf{LC}}p_{0}
. However, let
v
be such a valuation that
v[p_{0}]
is a pre-last element
a
of
LC
with respect to
\leq
and
v[p_{i}]\neq v[p_{j}]
, whenever
i<j
. Then, since

v[p_{i}\leftrightarrow p_{j}]\leq a
, we have:
v[\bm{\sigma}(\Gamma^{\ast})]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
, but
v[p_{0}]=a\neq\mathbf{1}
.

Exercises
4.4

1.

Prove Corollary
4.4.1.1
.

2.

Show that if
\mathcal{R}
is a set of structural modus rules, then the relation
\vdash_{[\mathcal{R}]}
satisfies the property (c) and structurality property of Definition
4.1.1
.

3.

The logic
\textsf{Cl}^{\star}
is obtained from
Cl
as explained in Section
4.4.2
.
Show that
\textsf{Cl}^{\star}
is not structural.

4.

Prove Proposition
4.4.3
.

5.

Prove the equivalence (
4.35
).

6.

Prove the equivalence (
4.36
) (the deduction theorem for
LC
).

7.

Prove Lemma
4.4.1
.

8.

Finish the proof of Proposition
4.4.4
.

4.5
Extensions of abstract logics

In this section we consider interaction of abstract logics defined in different formal languages. We begin with the following definition.

Definition 4.5.1

(extension, conservative extension)
.

Let a language
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be an extension of a language
\mathcal{L}
. Also, let
\mathcal{S}^{+}
and
\mathcal{S}
be abstract logics in the languages
\mathcal{L}^{+}
and

\mathcal{L}
, respectively. The abstract logic
\mathcal{S}^{+}
is an
extension of

(
or
over
)

\mathcal{S}
if for any set
X
of
\mathcal{L}
​-formulas,

\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}^{+}}(X).

We call
\mathcal{S}^{+}
a
conservative extension of

(
or
over
)

\mathcal{S}
if for any set
X
of
\mathcal{L}
​-formulas,

\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}^{+}}(X)\cap\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X).

(4.38)

The following two observation are almost obvious.

Proposition 4.5.1
.

Let a language
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be an extension of a language
\mathcal{L}
. Assume that
\mathcal{S}
is a structural logic in
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{S}^{+}
is its structural extension in
\mathcal{L}^{+}
. Then if
\mathcal{S}
is trivial, then so is
\mathcal{S}^{+}
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{S}
is trivial, then
p\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\varnothing)
, for an arbitrary
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Since
\mathcal{S}^{+}
is an extension of
\mathcal{S}
,
p\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}^{+}}(\varnothing)
. And since
\mathcal{S}^{+}
is structural, the latter implies that
\mathcal{S}^{+}
is trivial.
∎

Proposition 4.5.2
.

Let a language
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be an extension of a language
\mathcal{L}
and let
\mathcal{S}
be an abstract logic in
\mathcal{L}
.
The intersection of any nonempty set of conservative extensions of
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{L}^{+}
is a conservative extensions of
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{L}^{+}
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
4.5
.
1
)

In the sequel, we will need to keep in mind that, given a language
\mathcal{L}
and its extension
\mathcal{L}^{+}
, for any structural logic
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{L}
, there is
a conservative structural extension
\mathcal{S}^{+}
.

Indeed, assume that
\mathcal{S}
is a structural logic in
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{+}
is an extension of
\mathcal{L}
. According to Corollary
4.3.13.1
, the atlas
\mathbf{Lin}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}]=\left\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\right\rangle
is adequate for
\mathcal{S}
.
Let us select an arbitrary formula
\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and expand the signature of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
to
\mathcal{L}^{+}
, denoting a new algebra by
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\alpha]^{+}
, as follows. We interpret in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\alpha]^{+}
any constant
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}}
as the element
\alpha\in|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}|
. Similarly, we interpret any term
F(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})
, where
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}\setminus\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
, as
\alpha
. Then, we define an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}^{+}
as the one determined by the atlas
\left\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\alpha]^{+},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\right\rangle
.

According to Proposition
4.3.11
,
\mathcal{S}^{+}
is structural. Further,
\mathcal{S}^{+}
is a conservative extension of
\mathcal{S}
, because for any set
X\cup\{\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{+}}\beta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\models_{\textsf{{Lin}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}]}\beta.

(4.39)

We leave the reader to prove (
4.39
); see Exercise
4.5
.
2
.

In virtue of Proposition
4.5.1
,
\mathcal{S}^{+}
is trivial if
\mathcal{S}
is trivial.

We summarize as follows.

Proposition 4.5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an structural abstract logic in
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be an extension of
\mathcal{L}
. Then there is a conservative structural extension
\mathcal{S}^{+}
of
\mathcal{S}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}
