is trivial,
\mathcal{S}^{+}
is also trivial.

Corollary 4.5.3.1
.

Let a language
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be an extension of a language
\mathcal{L}
. Any structural abstract logic in
\mathcal{L}
has a least structural conservative extension in
\mathcal{L}^{+}
.

Proof.

We use Proposition
4.5.3
and Proposition
4.5.2
. ∎

Our next definition will play a key part in the sequel.

Definition 4.5.2

(relation
\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}
)
.

Let a language
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be an extension of a language
\mathcal{L}
. Also, let
\mathcal{S}
be an abstract logic in
\mathcal{L}
. We define a relation
\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}
in
\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rl}X\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}&\textit{there is a set {$Y\cup\{\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}$} and an $\mathcal{L}^{+}$\!-substitution}\\
&\textit{$\sigma$ such that $\sigma(Y)\subseteq X$, $\alpha=\sigma(\beta)$ and $Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta$}.\end{array}

We find a justification for the last definition in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.5.4
.

Let a language
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be an extension of a language
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic in
\mathcal{L}
. If the relation
\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}
is a consequence relation, then
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is the least structural conservative extension of
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{L}^{+}
. In addition,
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is nontrivial if, and only if,
\mathcal{S}
is nontrivial.

Proof.

Assume that
\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}
is a consequence relation.
Then it is obvious that
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is a conservative extension of
\mathcal{S}
.
(The structurality of
\mathcal{S}
should be used.)

Next, we prove the structurality of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
.
For this, assume that
X\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
. By definition, there are a set
Y\cup\{\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}
and an
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-substitution
\sigma
such that
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
,
\sigma(Y)\subseteq X
and
\sigma(\beta)=\alpha
.
Let
\xi
be any
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-substitution. We observe:
\xi\circ\sigma(Y)\subseteq\xi(X)
and
\xi\circ\sigma(\beta)=\xi(\alpha)
. Then, by definition,
\xi(X)\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\xi(\alpha)
.

Now, let
\mathcal{S}^{+}
be an arbitrary structural conservative extension of
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{L}^{+}
. Suppose that
X\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
. Then there is a set

Y\cup\{\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and an
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-substitution
\sigma
such that
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
,
\sigma(Y)\subseteq X
and
\sigma(\beta)=\alpha
. By premise,
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{+}}\beta
. Since

\mathcal{S}^{+}
is structural, we also have that
\sigma(Y)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{+}}\sigma(\beta)
. This implies that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}^{+}}\alpha
.

Finally, the last claim of the proposition follows from the conservativeness of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
over
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

In the next proposition, we give a sufficient condition for
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
to be a consequence relation.

Proposition 4.5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a language with
\kappa=\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})\geq\aleph_{0}
and
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be a primitive extension of
\mathcal{L}
. Assume that
\mathcal{S}
is a structural abstract logic in
\mathcal{L}
. Then the relation
\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}
is a consequence relation in
\mathcal{L}^{+}
. Moreover, the abstract logic
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is
\kappa
-compact. Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}
is finitary, then
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is also finitary.

Proof.

First, we have to show that
\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}
satisfies the properties of (a)–(c) of Definition
4.1.1
.
Leaving the check of the properties (a)–(b) to the reader, we turn to the property (c). (Exercise
4.5
.
3
)

Assume that for a set
X\cup Y\cup Z\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}
,

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{P1})&X\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\gamma,\penalty\ \text{for all $\gamma\in Y$};\\
(\text{P2})&Y,Z\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha.\end{array}

In virtue of (P2) and Definition
4.5.2
(further in this proof, simply definition), there are sets
Y_{0}
and
Z_{0}
with
Y_{0}\cup Z_{0}\cup\{\beta_{0}\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and an
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-substitution
\sigma_{0}
such that
\sigma_{0}(Y_{0})\subseteq Y
,
\sigma_{0}(Z_{0})\subseteq Z
,
\sigma_{0}(\beta_{0})=\alpha
and
Y_{0},Z_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta_{0}
.

We denote:

\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in I}:=\sigma_{0}(Y_{0}).

In virtue of (P1) and definition, for each
i\in I
, there is a set
X_{i}\cup\{\beta_{i}\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and an
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-substitution
\sigma_{i}
such that
\sigma_{i}(X_{i})\subseteq X
,
\sigma_{i}(\beta_{i})=\gamma_{i}
and

X_{i}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta_{i}
.

Now the assumptions (P1)–(P2) can be refined as follows.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{P1}^{\ast})&\bigcup_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}(X_{i})\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\gamma_{i},\penalty\ \text{for all $i\in I$};\\
(\text{P2}^{\ast})&\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in I},\sigma_{0}(Z_{0})\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha.\end{array}

We denote:

X^{\ast}:=\bigcup_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}(X_{i})\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ Z^{\ast}:=\sigma_{0}(Z_{0}).

We aim to show that
X^{\ast},Z^{\ast}\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
. Since
X^{\ast}\subseteq X
and
Z^{\ast}\subseteq Z
, we will thus obtain that
X,Z\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
, for we have already proved that
\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}
is monotone.

Let us make notes about the cardinalities of the
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-variables of some formula sets. (This is where we use that the extension
\mathcal{L}^{+}
is primitive.)

•

\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}(\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in I}))\leq\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})
;

•

\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}(Z^{\ast}))=\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}(\sigma_{0}(Z_{0})))\leq\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})
;

•

\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}(X^{\ast}))=\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}(X_{i})))\leq\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})
.

Thus we have:
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}(\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cup X^{\ast}\cup Z^{\ast}\cup\{\alpha\}))\leq\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})
. This allows us to claim that there is a one-one map

f:\mathcal{V}(\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cup X^{\ast}\cup Z^{\ast}\cup\{\alpha\})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{1--1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}.

For simplicity, we denote:

\mathcal{V}^{\ast}:=\mathcal{V}(\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cup X^{\ast}\cup Z^{\ast}\cup\{\alpha\}).

Next we define two
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-substitutions. Let
q
be an arbitrary fixed
\mathcal{L}
-variable.
Then, we define:

\mu(p):=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}f(p)&\text{if $p\in\mathcal{V}^{\ast}$}\\
q&\text{otherwise};\end{array}\end{cases}

\nu(p):=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}f^{-1}(p)&\text{if $p\in f(\mathcal{V}^{\ast})$}\\
p&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\end{cases}

We observe the following:

•

\mu\circ\sigma_{i}
, for all
i\in I
, and
\mu\circ\sigma_{0}
are
\mathcal{L}
-substitutions;

•

\nu\circ\mu\circ\sigma_{i}(X_{i})=\sigma_{i}(X_{i})
and
\nu\circ\mu(\gamma_{i})=\gamma_{i}
, for all
i\in I
,
\nu\circ\mu\circ\sigma_{0}(Z_{0})=\sigma_{0}(Z_{0})
and

\nu\circ\mu\circ\sigma_{0}(\beta_{0})=\sigma_{0}(\beta_{0})
;

•

thus
\nu(\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu\circ\sigma_{i}(X_{i}))=\nu(\mu(X^{\ast}))=X^{\ast}
,
\nu(\mu\circ\sigma_{0}(Z_{0}))\nu(\mu(Z^{\ast}))=Z^{\ast}
and
\nu(\mu\circ\sigma_{0}(\beta_{0}))=\nu(\mu(\alpha))=\alpha
. And, by definition, we conclude that
X^{\ast},Z^{\ast}\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
.

From this, since
\mathcal{S}
is structural, we obtain:

•

\mu\circ\sigma_{i}(X_{i})\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\mu\circ\sigma_{i}(\beta_{i})
, for each
i\in I
, which implies
\mu(\bigcup_{i\in I}\sigma_{i}(X_{i}))\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\mu(\gamma_{i})
, for each
i\in I
;

•

\{\mu(\gamma_{i})\}_{i\in I},\mu\circ\sigma_{0}(Z_{0})\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\mu\circ\sigma_{0}(\beta_{0})
.

Then, from the two last assertions and the transitivity of
\mathcal{S}
, we derive:

\mu(X^{\ast}),\mu(Z^{\ast})\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\mu(\alpha)
.
∎

Exercises
4.5

1.

Prove Proposition
4.5.2

2.

Prove the equivalence (
4.39
), that is for any set
X\cup\{\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}
,

X\models_{\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\alpha]^{+},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle}\beta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\models_{\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle}\beta.

(
Hint
: Show that for any valuation
v
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
, there is a valuation
v^{\ast}
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\alpha]^{+}
such that
v^{\ast}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}=v
.)

3.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be a primitive extensions of a language
\mathcal{L}
and let
\mathcal{S}
be an abstract logic in
\mathcal{L}
. Show that the relation
\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}
of Definition
4.5.2
satisfies the properties (a)–(b) of Definition
4.1.1
.

4.

Prove that
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
in Proposition
4.5.5
is
\kappa
-compact.

4.6
Historical notes

As was mentioned in Section
1.1
and in the beginning of this chapter, the distinction in the argumentation of reasoning with predetermined premises from arguments with arbitrary premises goes back to Aristotle, who called the former a
demonstrative
argument, and the latter a
dialectical
one. It is very likely that the notion of demonstration has gradually developed in connection with geometry from “empirical study” to “a demonstrative
a priory
science;”
[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, chapter I, section 2. Dialectical arguments, on the other hand, were characteristic of metaphysics. In the early twentieth century, this distinction was emphasized by C. I. Lewis:

“The consequences of this difference between the “implies” of the algebra and the “implies” of valid inference are most serious.”
[
Lewis, 1913a
]
, p. 242.
6
6

6

It should be noted that Lewis did not distinguish between the “algebra of implication” and “calculus of propositions.” See, for example, the first line of
[
Lewis, 1913a
]
or of
[
Lewis, 1914
]
.

The formalization of logical consequence (a dialectical argument in the sense of Aristotle) in the framework of modern logic was undertaken by A. Tarski in the 1930s, by introducing the notion of consequence operator, not that of consequence relation; see
[
Tarski, 1930b
,
Tarski, 1930a
]
7
7

7

The English translations of these important papers were later on published in
[
Tarski, 1956
,
Tarski, 1983
]
. It seems that an earlier attempt by P. Hertz
[
Hertz, 1922
]
was made from an essentially different perspective, since
he interpreted ‘
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\rightarrow b
’ (where
a_{1},,\ldots,a_{n},b
are sentences) as

“If
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
altogether holds, so does
b
,”

(quoted from
[
Beziau (editor), 2012
]
, p. 11)

while Tarski would interpret ‘
\beta\in\textbf{Cn}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
’ that
\beta
is obtained from
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
with respect to
Cn
, regardless whether any
\alpha_{i}
(or they altogether) holds or not. The following passage shows that Hertz regarded ‘
\rightarrow
’ as a relation of demonstration, and not as a dialectical argument when he wrote:

“Whenever a system of sentences is recognized to be valid, it is often not necessary to convey each or every sentence to memory; it is sufficient to choose some of them from which the rest can follow. Such sentences, as it is generally known, are called axioms.” Quoted from
[
Beziau (editor), 2012
]
, p. 11.

Also, it seems that D. Scott was the first to start using consequence as a relation; cf.
[
Scott, 1974a
]
. He also stated explicitly therein that the two notions can be used interchangeably; cf.
[
Shoesmith and Smiley, 2008
]
, Historical note, pp. 19--20.
8
8

8

D. J. Shoesmith and T. J. Smiley also used consequence relation but did not relate it to Tarski’s consequence operator; cf.
[
Shoesmith and Smiley, 1971
]
.

It is worthwhile to dwell on the works of Tarski in the 1930s in a little more detail. As can be assumed from the title of
[
Tarski, 1930a
]
, Tarski had in mind a far-reaching program. So he writes in
[
Tarski, 1930a
]
, § 1,

“
The two concepts — of sentence and of consequence — are the only primitive concepts which appear in this discussion.
”

(italics highlighted by Tarski)

The first concept in Tarski’s passage is a nonempty set of cardinality less than or equal to
\aleph_{0}
, and the second is a map, defined on the power set of all sentences and satisfying
(\text{a}^{\dagger})
–
(\text{c}^{\dagger})
of Definition
4.2.1
. Interestingly, Tarski admits, at least implicitly, that the set of sentences may be finite, a condition that was changed to ‘greater than or equal to
\aleph_{0}
’ in later expositions; cf., e.g.,
[
Czelakowski, 2001
,
Font et al., 2009
,
Font, 2016
]
.

It may seem surprising that there is no structure in Tarski’s “meaningful sentences” (from the first concept). Perhaps he wanted to propose a concept of logical consequence independent of the grammatical structure of the objective language. Indeed, criticizing Carnap’s definitions of logical consequence, he wrote,

“These definitions, in so far as they set up on the basis of ‘general syntax’, seem to me to be materially inadequate, just because the defined concepts depend essentially, in their extension, on the richness of the language investigated.”
[
Tarski, 1936b
]

However, the deprivation of sentences from a grammatical structure immediately blocks the Lindenbaum method, not allowing to define the formula algebra.

In
[
Tarski, 1930b
]
and
[
Tarski, 1930a
]
, the property
(\text{d}^{\dagger})
(finitariness) is a mandatory axiom, while the structurality property is not mentioned at all, even as an option.
Structurality is defined through the notion of substitution. However, substitution plays a dual role in logic.

Although Aristotle was familiar with the idea of a variable, but he “never argued by means of substitutions, as we have been doing, except in proofs of invalidity,” that is, only using instantiation; “for example, no thought of substituting variables for variables”
[
Bocheński, 1970
]
, § 13, sections B and C, respectively. Alexander of Aphrodisias, who lived at the turn of the fourth century AD, already knew the operation of identification of sentential variables and Boethius (c. 477–524 AD) knew substitution of sentential formulas for variables. He would probably be the first philosopher to agree, if he had a chance, that any map
\sigma:\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
satisfying the conditions of Definition
3.1.3
conveys the idea that logic deals with forms rather than with their instances.
9
9

9

I. M. Bocheński calls this “the clear distinction between propositional functions and propositions themselves;” cf.
[
Bocheński, 1970
]
, § 24, section I.
Boethius also showed his “aspiration to the rule of substitution” but never used it as a rule of inference; cf.
[
Bocheński, 1970
]
, § 24, section E.

G. Frege was perhaps the first to start using substitution as a rule of inference, though implicitly. This is what van Heijenoort wrote in his introductory note to Frege’s
Begriffsschrift
(1879),

“His axioms for the propositional calculus (they are not independent) are formulas […]. His rules of inference are the rule of detachment and an unstated rule of substitution.” Quoted from
[
van Heijenoort, 2002
]
, p. 2.

Although A. Church credits L. Couturat for being the first to explicitly formulate the substitution rule “specifically for the propositional calculus,” this is hardly justified. Indeed in
[
Couturat, 1980
]
, the first edition of which was published in 1905, Couturat wrote,

“That is not all: to that principle one must add the substitution principle which is described thusly: “In a general formula, to a general or indeterminate term one may substitute a particular or individual term.” That is evident since a general formula has no value nor even meaning except in the way it can be applied to particular terms”

[
Couturat, 1980
]
, chapter 1.
10
10

10

We are indebted to Dr. L. Wolffe for translating this passage from the French.

Fair enough, right after his remark, Church adds,‘‘but his [Couturat’s] statement is perhaps insufficient as failing to make clear that the expression substituted for a (propositional) variable may itself contain variables.’’
11
11

11

Cf.
[
Church, 1996
]
, § 29.

B. Russell did use the substitution rule in
[
Russell, 1906
]
in formal derivations of sentential formulas, but there is still confusion for the author in using the term “substitution” in the sense of Definition
3.1.3
and as universal instantiation in higher-order logic. After Russell, Lewis illustrates in
[
Lewis, 1913b
,
Lewis, 1914
]
the use of the substitution rule in the sense of Section
4.3.5
, but without such generality; and after
[
Lewis, 1960
]
(the first publication appeared in 1918) substitution as an operation and as a rule of inference became standard.

Yet, the structurality principle understood as implication

X\vdash\alpha\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \sigma(X)\vdash\sigma(\alpha),

for any substitution
\sigma
, does not come down to the rule of substitution since it is a characteristic of an entire deductive system with this property.
The structurality as a important property of a consequence operator was introduced by J. Łoś and R. Suszko in
[
Łoś and Suszko, 1958
]
, although was anticipated by Lewis in
[
Lewis, 1913b
]
, when he wrote,

“Proof takes place through the collusion of two factors; first, postulates or propositions of the particular mathematical system in hand; secondly, postulates of the logical or mathematical type and the desired conclusion. A mathematical operation is ideally no more than this: the substitution of the variables or functions of variables of the particular system — say, of cardinal number — for the logical variables in some proposition about implication. This proposition is more than a rule for inference; when the substitution is made, it
states
the implication.”

We conclude the theme of substitution with the following passage by B. Russell in
[
Russell, 1993
]
(the second edition appeared in 1920), where he wrote,

“The primitive propositions, whatever they may be, are to be regarded as asserted for all possible values of the variable propositions
p,q,r
which occur in them. We may therefore substitute for (say)
p
any expression whose value is always a proposition,
e.g.
not-
p
, “
s
implies
t
,” and so on. By means of such substitutions we really obtain sets of special cases of our original proposition, but from a practical point of view we obtain what are virtually new propositions. The legitimacy of substitutions of this kind has to be insured by means of a non-formal principle of inference. [Footnote: No such principle is enunciated in
Principia Mathematica
[…] But this would seem to be an omission.]” (ibid, chapter XIV)

From this passage, we learn that although the authors of
Principia Mathematica
were unable to appreciate the importance of the concept of substitution, by 1920 Russell already realized (or was close to this) that propositions themselves can be used as (truth) values for propositions, which leads directly to Lindenbaum method.

The properties
(\text{e}^{\dagger})
and
(\text{j}^{\dagger})
of
Cn
in Section
4.2
were considered earlier by D. Makinson in
[
Makinson, 2005
]
, section 1.2.

Given a consequence operator
Cn
, the notion of a theory (under the name “deductive system”) with respect to
Cn
is due to
[
Tarski, 1930b
]
, as well as Proposition
4.2.1
, ibid, theorem 20.
12
12

12

A revised text of this article appeared in English translation in
[
Tarski, 1983
]
, pp. 30–37, under the title ‘On some fundamental concepts of metamathematics’.

A. Tarski introduced a matrix method for studying ‘‘deductive systems,’’ that is, theories of abstract logics.
13
13

13

We read in
[
Łukasiewicz and Tarski, 1930
]
, footnote 2, “The view of matrix formation as a general method of constructing systems is due to Tarski.”
In print form, the concept of a logical matrix was defined in
[
Łukasiewicz and Tarski, 1930
]
, definition 3.

The theorem, which was originally known as the
Lindenbaum theorem
first appeared in print without proof in
[
Łukasiewicz and Tarski, 1930
]
, theorem 3. This theorem reads that, given a consequence operator
Cn
, for any “deductive system”
X_{0}
, there exists “a normal matrix,” say
M
, such that

\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(X_{0})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X_{0}\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha.

(4.40)

Since
X_{0}
in (
4.40
) was tacitly assumed to be fixed, defining
an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
by the equivalence

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \alpha\in\textbf{Cn}(X_{0}\cup X),

for any set
X
and formula
\alpha
, we can reduce (
4.40
) to the second part of Proposition
4.3.12
.

The condition that
X_{0}
is an
\mathcal{S}
-theory, that is
X_{0}=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\varnothing)
, is not important, but it is important to add that
X_{0}
is closed under substitution, which must have been tacitly assumed. This observation implies two conclusions. First, participants in the seminar for mathematical logic at the University of Warsaw, where, according to
[
Łukasiewicz and Tarski, 1930
]
, footnote
\ddagger
, the result was reported, perhaps focused on the set of theorems of a deductive system, and not on the consequence relation associated with this system; and secondly, the set of theorems was assumed to be closed under substitution.

Further, in
[
Łukasiewicz and Tarski, 1930
]
, there was no indication of what kind of matrix
M
is, in particular, whether
\textsf{{M}}=\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
Moreover, although the nature of
M
was very likely known (to Lindenbaum at least), the statement of theorem 3 did not contribute to the take-off of the Lindenbaum method. The birth of the concept of a matrix consequence was also delayed until the publication of
[
Łoś and Suszko, 1958
]
; see definition (8.1) therein. The proof of Lindenbaum’s theorem was published in
[
Łoś, 1949
]
, proposition 10, and, independently, in
[
Hermes, 1951
]
. (To prove (
4.40
), Łoś used
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}
as
M
.) Further, since the authors of
[
Łukasiewicz and Tarski, 1930
]
did not require explicitly the closedness under substitution for an
\mathcal{S}
-theory
X
(as Łoś did
14
14

14

Cf.
[
Łoś, 1949
]
, definition 3.
), they missed to notice that any Lindenbaum matrix
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]
is an
\mathcal{S}
-model, which was noted in
[
Łoś, 1949
]
, proposition 7.
15
15

15

Actually,
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]
is an
\mathcal{S}
-model, even if
X
is not closed under substitution, but
\mathcal{S}
is structural; see Corollary
4.3.12.1
.

The last omission and the omission of structurality in the 1930s however delayed the result, due to
[
Wójcicki, 1969
]
, theorem 4, that any structural abstract logic is determined by its Lindenbaum matrices (Corollary
4.3.13.1
). After this, the role of substitution was clearly understood, since any valuation in the formula algebra is a substitution.

The Wójcicki’s observation opened up a way to define consequence relations using bundles or atlases of logical matrices. Instead of the term ‘atlas’ Wójcicki used the term ‘generalized matrix’. But even before him, T. Smiley
[
Smiley, 1962
]
used a similar concept, known as a ‘Smiley matrix’. The term ‘atlas’ was coined by M. J. Dunn and G. M. Hardegree in
[
Dunn and Hardegree, 2001
]
. The concept and term of the Lindenbaum bundle were used by G. Malinowski in
[
Malinowski, 2007
]
.

Almost certainly, introducing the concept of a logical consequence, A. Tarski had in mind the usual practice of obtaining new hypothetical truths in mathematics and other deductive sciences. So in his very first article
[
Tarski, 1930b
]
on logical consequence, he wrote,

“From the sentences of any set
X
cernain other sentences can be obtained by means of certain operations called
rules of inference
. These sentences are called the
consequences of the set

X
. The set of all consequences is denoted by the symbol ‘
Cn
(
X
).” Quoted from
[
Tarski, 1983
]
, p. 30.

In antiquity, according to Plato, in the framework of his Theory of Forms, ‘‘inference was [is] presumably valid when we follow in thought the connexions between Forms as they are.’’
16
16

16

Cf.
[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, chapter I, section 5.
And in later antiquity, Galen and Boethius presented Aristotle’s view on inference ‘‘as a theory of inference schemata, i.e. as a theory in which rules of inference are thought by means of skeleton arguments.’’
17
17

17

Cf.
[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, chapter II, section 6.
And later, in the development of logic, ‘‘the Stoics generally concentrated attention on valid inference schemata (ςυναϰτιϰὰ ςχήματα).’’
18
18

18

Cf.
[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, chapter III, section 5.

The modern name for “inference schemata” is
structural
(
inference
)
rules
. The last concept and term was introduced in
[
Łoś and Suszko, 1958
]
, section 3.

However, any system of inference rules, structural (such as modus ponens) or non-structural (such as substitution), does not suffice to define a consequence relation. Perhaps G. Leibniz was the first logician who understood the importance of the notion of formal derivation, or formal proof, or formal deduction.
19
19

19

See
[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, chapter V, section 2, where we read: “The most fruitful idea that Leibniz derived from his study of Aristotelian logic was the notion of formal proof.”
Also, Bolzano is recognized as ‘‘a noteworthy precursor of modern proof-theory.’’
20
20

20

Cf.
[
Bocheński, 1970
]
, § 38, section B1.

G. Boole used derivations in abstract manner to obtain consequences without referring to interpretation. G. Frege was close to formalizing the concept of deduction when he wrote:

“Inference is conducted in my symbolic system (
Begriffsschrift
) according to a kind of calculation. I do not mean this in the narrow sense, as though an algorithm was in control, the same as or similar to that of ordinary addition and multiplication, but in the sense that the whole is algorithmic, with a complex of rules which so regulate the passage from one proposition or from two such to another, that nothing takes place but what is in accordance with these rules.” Quoted from
[
Bocheński, 1970
]
, § 38, section B2.

But only D. Hilbert and P. Bernays (1934) gave an exact definition of formal derivation, or formal proof, as a sequence of formulas satisfying certain conditions based on the rules of detachment (modus ponens) and substitution.
21
21

21

Cf.
[
Hilbert and Bernays, 1968
]
, chapter III, § 2.

Moreover, they formulated the first rule in terms of metavariables, that is, as a structural inference rule. The importance of such a definition is that it makes it possible to study formal derivations as words in a formal alphabet, using mathematical induction on the length of the derivation in question.

The inference rules (a)–(b) were introduced by G. Gentzen; cf.
[
Gentzen, 1969b
]
.
The hyperrules (c) were used by S. C. Kleene in
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 23, although as derived rules, not as postulated ones.
22
22

22

Consult
[
Scott, 1974b
]
about the difference between (postulated) rules and derived rules; the idea of a ‘derived rule of inference’ is also explained on the example of uniform substitution in
[
Church, 1996
]
, § 19. It seems that the idea of derived inference rules was familiar to the Stoics; cf.
[
Łukasiewicz, 1934
]
.
The inference rules (a)–(b) and the hyperrules (c) are similar to the proper rules and, respectively, to deduction rules of
[
Prawitz, 1965
]
, chapter 1, § 1.

Proposition
4.3.6
first appeared in
[
Brown and Suszko, 1973
]
as theorem 1 of section XII.

Chapter 5
Matrix Consequence

5.1
Single-matrix consequence

Two circumstances draw our attention to a single-matrix consequence. The first one is Lindenbaum’s theorem (Proposition
4.3.12
) which provides a universal way of definition of a weakly adequate logical matrix, namely the Lindenbaum matrix, which being defined for a given consequence relation, validates all of the theses of this consequence relation and only them. The other circumstance is related to the consequence relation
\vdash_{2}
(Section
4.3.5
) which turned out to be equal to the single-matrix relation
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
. These circumstances lead to the the following concept.

We recall (Section
4.3.4
) that
a logical matrix
M
is called adequate for an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
if
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\,=\,\models_{\textbf{M}}
; that is, for any set
X
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas and any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\alpha
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\Longleftrightarrow X\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha.

In connection with the notion of an adequate matrix, two questions arise. Is it true that any abstract logic has an adequate matrix? And if the answer to the first question is negative, what are the properties of an abstract logic that make it have an adequate matrix?

The first question can be answered in the negative as follows. Suppose
\mathcal{S}
is a nontrivial abstract logic in
\mathcal{L}
with
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})\geq\aleph_{0}
, which has a nonempty consistent set, say
X_{0}
, and such that
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\varnothing
. Now, let

\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
be a relation obtained from
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
according to (
4.3
). In virtue of Proposition
4.1.3
,
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
is a consequence relation. We claim that there is no adequate matrix for
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
. (Such a relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
can be exemplified, e.g., by
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}^{\circ}
with
X_{0}=L\textsf{{B}}_{2}
, where
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
is a single-matrix consequence of Section
4.3.4
.)

Indeed, for contradiction, assume that a matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
is adequate for
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
. Since
X_{0}
is consistent with respect to
\mathcal{S}
, it is also consistent with respect to
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
.
This and the assumption that
X_{0}
is nonempty imply that
D\neq\varnothing
.
Assume that
a\in D
.
By premise, there is a thesis
\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Let us take a variable
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\mathcal{V}(\alpha)
. By definition,
\varnothing\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}\alpha
and
p\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}\alpha
. The former implies that there is a valuation
v
in
A
such that
v[\alpha]\notin D
. Now we define a valuation:

v^{\prime}[q]:=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}v[q]&\text{if $q\neq p$}\\
a&\text{if $q=p$}.\end{array}\end{cases}

It is clear that
v^{\prime}[\alpha]=v[\alpha]
and hence
p\not\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha
.

Trying to find a characteristic for abstract logic to have an adequate matrix, we consider any logical matrix
\textsf{{M}}:=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
of type
\mathcal{L}
.

Now, let
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma
be
\mathcal{L}
-formulas with
(\mathcal{V}(\alpha)\cup\mathcal{V}(\beta))\cap\mathcal{V}(\gamma)=\varnothing
. Assume that for some valuation
v_{0}
,
v_{0}(\gamma)\in D
. (This assumption is equivalent to the condition that the set
\{\gamma\}
is consistent with respect to the consequence relation
\models_{\textbf{M}}
.) We prove that

\gamma,\alpha\models_{\textbf{M}}\beta\penalty\ \penalty\ \textit{implies}\penalty\ \penalty\ \alpha\models_{\textbf{M}}\beta.

Indeed, for contrapositive, suppose that
\alpha\not\models_{\textbf{M}}\beta
;
that is, for some valuation
v
,
v(\alpha)\in D
, but
v(\beta)\notin D
.

Let us define a valuation

v_{1}(p)=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}v_{0}[p]&\text{if $p\in\mathcal{V}(\gamma)$}\\
v[p]&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\end{cases}

We note that
v_{1}[\gamma]=v_{0}[\gamma]
,
v_{1}[\alpha]=v[\alpha]
and
v_{1}[\beta]=v[\beta]
. This implies that
\gamma,\alpha\not\models_{\textbf{M}}\beta
.

The above consideration leads to the following definition.

Definition 5.1.1

(uniform abstract logic)
.

An abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
is said to be
uniform
if for any set

X\cup Y\cup\{\alpha\}
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas with
\mathcal{V}(X\cup\{\alpha\})\cap\mathcal{V}(Y)=\varnothing
and
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\neq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, if
X,Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, then
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.

We note that the consequence relation
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}^{\circ}
mentioned above is finitary but not uniform. (Exercise
5.1
.
1
)
The importance of the last definition becomes clear from Proposition
5.1.1
below. We prove this proposition by means of two lemmas.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

If an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
has an adequate matrix, then
\mathcal{S}
is uniform.

Proof.

Let a matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be adequate for
\mathcal{S}
.
Suppose
X,Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, where
\mathcal{V}(X,\alpha)\cap\mathcal{V}(Y)=\varnothing

and
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\neq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. We will show that
X\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha
, which, in virtue of the adequacy of
M
for
\mathcal{S}
, will in turn imply that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.

For contradiction, assume that for some valuation
v
in
A
,
v[X]\subseteq D
but
v[\alpha]\notin D
. Since
Y
is consistent with respect to
\mathcal{S}
, there is a valuation
v^{\prime}
such that
v^{\prime}[Y]\subseteq D
. Then, we define a valuation
v^{\prime\prime}
as follows:

v^{\prime\prime}[p]:=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}v[p]&\text{if $p\in\mathcal{V}(X,\alpha)$}\\
v^{\prime}[p]&\text{if $p\in\mathcal{V}(Y)$}.\end{array}\end{cases}

It must be clear that
v^{\prime\prime}[\beta]=v[\beta]
, whenever
\beta\in X\cup\{\alpha\}
, and
v^{\prime\prime}[\beta]=v^{\prime}[\beta]
, if
\beta\in Y
. Therefore,
X,Y\not\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha
; that is
X,Y\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. A contradiction.
∎

As preparation to the next proposition we observe the following.

Lemma 5.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a uniform structural abstract logic in a language
\mathcal{L}
with
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})\geq\aleph_{0}
and
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be a primitive extension of
\mathcal{L}
. Then the abstract logic
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is also uniform.

Proof.

Suppose for a set
X\cup Y\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}
,

X,Y\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
, providing that
\mathcal{V}(X\cup\{\alpha\})\cap\mathcal{V}(Y)=\varnothing
and
Y
is consistent with respect to
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
.
Then, by definition, there is
X_{0}\cup Y_{0}\cup\{\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and an
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-substitution
\sigma
such that
\sigma(X_{0})\subseteq X
,
\sigma(Y_{0})\subseteq Y
,
\sigma(\beta)=\alpha
and
X_{0},Y_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
. It is clear that

\mathcal{V}(X_{0}\cup\{\beta\})\cap\mathcal{V}(Y_{0})=\varnothing
. Also,
Y_{0}
is consistent with respect to
\mathcal{S}
, for if it were otherwise, in virtue of conservativity of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
over
\mathcal{S}
, we would have
\sigma(Y_{0})\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\gamma
, for any arbitrary
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-formula
\gamma
. This would imply the inconsistency of
Y
in
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
.

Since
\mathcal{S}
is uniform,
X_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
and hence
X_{0}\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\beta
, for
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is a conservative extension of
\mathcal{S}
. And since
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is structural (Proposition
4.5.4
),
\sigma(X_{0})\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
and hence

X\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
.
∎

Proposition 5.1.1

(Łoś-Suszko-Wójcicki theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a finitary structural abstract logic in a language
\mathcal{L}
with
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})\geq\aleph_{0}
. Then
\mathcal{S}
has an adequate matrix if, and only if,
\mathcal{S}
is uniform.

Proof.

The ‘only-if’ part follows from Lemma
5.1.1
. So we continue with the proof of the ‘if’ part.

We distinguish two cases:
\mathcal{S}
is trivial and
\mathcal{S}
is nontrivial. If the former is the case, then the matrix
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\rangle
is adequate for
\mathcal{S}
.

Now, we assume that
\mathcal{S}
is nontrivial; that is, the set
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
of consistent sets with respect to
\mathcal{S}
is nonempty.

We introduce for each
X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
and each
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, a new sentential variable
p_{X}
so that for any
p,q\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
and any
X,Y\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
,

p_{X}=q_{Y}\Longleftrightarrow p=q\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X=Y.

Accordingly, for an arbitrary
X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
, we denote:

\mathcal{V}_{X}=\{p_{X}\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\}.

Thus for any
X,Y\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
,

X\neq Y\Longrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{X}\cap\mathcal{V}_{Y}=\varnothing.

(5.1)

A language
\mathcal{L}^{+}
is defined as a primitive extension of
\mathcal{L}
, by adding the new sentential variables to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Then, we introduce
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
according to Definition
4.5.2
.

Next, for each
X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
, we define two
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-substitutions:

\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma_{X}:p\mapsto p_{X},\penalty\ \text{for any $p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$},\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \sigma_{X}:p_{Y}\mapsto p_{Y},\penalty\ \text{for any $p_{Y}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$};\\
\xi_{X}:p_{X}\mapsto p,\penalty\ \text{for any $p_{X}\in\mathcal{V}_{X}$ and $\xi_{X}:q\mapsto q$, for any $q\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{X}$}.\end{array}

We observe: for any
X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
,

\xi_{X}\circ\sigma_{X}(\beta)=\beta,\penalty\ \text{for all $\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}$}.

(5.2)

Then, we make two observations.
First, since for any
X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
,
\mathcal{V}(\sigma_{X}(X))\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{X}
, (
8.3
) yields that for any
X,Y\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
,

X\neq Y\Longrightarrow\mathcal{V}(\sigma_{X}(X))\cap\mathcal{V}(\sigma_{Y}(Y))=\varnothing.

(5.3)

Second,

for each
X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
, the set
\sigma_{X}(X)
is consistent with respect to
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
.

(5.4)

Indeed, for contradiction, assume that
\sigma_{X}(X)
is inconsistent. Let
\beta
be any formula of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. By assumption,
\sigma_{X}(X)\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\sigma_{X}(\beta)
. Since
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is structural (Proposition
4.5.4
), we have that
\xi_{X}(\sigma_{X}(X))\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\xi_{X}(\sigma_{X}(\beta))
and hence, in virtue of (
5.2
),
X\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\beta
. Since
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is conservative over
\mathcal{S}
, we obtain that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
. A contradiction.

Now, we define:

\textsf{{M}}:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}},\textbf{Cn}_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}(\bigcup\{\sigma_{X}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}\})\rangle.

We aim to show that
M
is adequate for
\mathcal{S}
; that is, for any set
Y\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\Longleftrightarrow Y\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha.

Suppose
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Since
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is conservative over
\mathcal{S}
, this implies that
Y\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
. Now, let
\sigma
be any
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-substitution.
Assume that
\sigma(Y)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}(\bigcup\{\sigma_{X}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}\})
. Since
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is structural, we have:
\sigma(\alpha)\in\textbf{Cn}_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}(\sigma(Y))
and hence

\sigma(\alpha)\in\textbf{Cn}_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}(\bigcup\{\sigma_{X}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}\})
.

Conversely, suppose that
Y\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. We denote:

X_{0}:=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y).

Thus
\alpha\notin X_{0}
and hence
X_{0}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
. The latter implies that

\sigma_{X_{0}}(X_{0})\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}(\bigcup\{\sigma_{X}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}\})
.

For contradiction, we assume that

\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha)\in\textbf{Cn}_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}(\bigcup\{\sigma_{X}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}\}).

In virtue of finitariness of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
(Proposition
4.5.5
), there is a finite number of sets
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}
such that

\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha)\in\textbf{Cn}_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}(\sigma_{X_{0}}(X_{0})\cup\sigma_{X_{1}}(X_{1})\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{X_{n}}(X_{n})).

Since, according to (
5.4
),
\sigma_{X_{n}}(X_{n})
is consistent and

\mathcal{V}(\sigma_{X_{0}}(X_{0})\cup\sigma_{X_{1}}(X_{1})\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{X_{n-1}}(X_{n-1}))\cap\mathcal{V}(\sigma_{X_{n}}(X_{n}))=\varnothing,

in virtue of the uniformity of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
(Lemma
5.1.2
),

\sigma_{X_{0}}(X_{0}),\sigma_{X_{1}}(X_{1}),\ldots\sigma_{X_{n-1}}(X_{n-1})\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha)

Repeating this argument several times, we arrive at the conclusion that

\sigma_{X_{0}}(X_{0})\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha)
. Then, applying the substitution
\xi_{X_{0}}
, we get
X_{0}\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
. Finally, the conservativity of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
over
\mathcal{S}
yields
X_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. A contradiction.
∎

The case of nonfinitary abstract logics needs an additional investigation.

Definition 5.1.2

(couniform abstract logic)
.

An abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
in a language
\mathcal{L}
is called couniform if for any collection
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of formula sets with
\mathcal{V}(X_{i})\cap\mathcal{V}(X_{j})=\varnothing
, providing that
i\neq j
, and
\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\})\neq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, there is at least one
X_{i}
which is inconsistent, whenever the union
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}
is inconsistent.

The next lemma shows that couniformity is necessary for an abstract logic to have an adequate matrix.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

If an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
has an adequate matrix, then
\mathcal{S}
is couniform.

Proof.

Let a matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be adequate for
\mathcal{S}
. We assume that a family
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of formula sets satisfies all the premises of couniformity. Suppose each
X_{i}
is consistent with respect to
\mathcal{S}
. Then for each
i\in I
, there is a formula
\alpha_{i}
such that
X_{i}\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{i}
. This implies that there is a valuation
v_{i}
in
A
such that
v_{i}[X_{i}]\subseteq D
but

v_{i}[\alpha_{i}]\notin D
. Let us select either one of
v_{i}[\alpha_{i}]
and denote

a:=v_{i}[\alpha_{i}].

Then, we select any variable
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\})
and for each
v_{i}
, define:

v_{i}^{\ast}[q]:=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}v_{i}[q]&\text{if $q\in\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\})$}\\
a&\text{if $q\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\})$}.\end{array}\end{cases}

It must be clear that for each
i\in I
,
v_{i}^{\ast}[X_{i}]\subseteq D
. And since
\mathcal{V}(X_{i})\cap\mathcal{V}(X_{j})=\varnothing
, providing
i\neq j
, we also have that
v_{i}^{\ast}[\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}]\subseteq D
. Thus
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}p
, that is the set
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}
is consistent.
∎

The next lemma is rather technical and we will need it for the proof of Proposition
5.1.2
below.

Lemma 5.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a couniform structural abstract logic in a language
\mathcal{L}
with
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})\geq\aleph_{0}
and
\mathcal{L}^{+}
be a primitive extension of
\mathcal{L}
. Then the abstract logic
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is also couniform.

Proof.

Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a nonempty family of nonempty sets of
\mathcal{L}^{+}
-formulas, about which we assume that the union
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}
is inconsistent and
\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\})\neq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}
. Let us select any
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{+}}\setminus\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\})
. Since
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}
is inconsistent, we have that
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}p
. By definition of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
(Definition
4.5.2
), there are a set
Y
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas, an
\mathcal{L}
-variable
q
and an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
such that

\sigma(Y)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}
,
\sigma(q)=p
and
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}q
.

Next we define: for each
i\in I
,

Y_{i}:=\{\gamma\in Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sigma(\gamma)\in X_{i}\}.

Firstly, we notice that for each
i\in I
,
\sigma(Y_{i})\subseteq X_{i}
, and
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{Y_{i}\}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}q
.
(The latter is true because
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{Y_{i}\}=Y
.)
Secondly,
q\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{Y_{i}\})
. Indeed, if it were the case that
q\in\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{Y_{i}\})
, then we would have that
p\in\mathcal{V}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\})
.

Thus we obtain that
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{Y_{i}\}
is inconsistent with respect to
\mathcal{S}
. Since, by definition,
\mathcal{S}
is couniform, there is
i_{0}\in I
such that
Y_{i_{0}}
is inconsistent with respect to
\mathcal{S}
. This implies that Since
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is a conservative extension of
\mathcal{S}
, we receive that
Y_{i_{0}}\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}q
. And since

(\mathcal{S})^{+}
is structural (Proposition
4.5.4
),
\sigma(Y_{i_{0}})\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}p
and hence
X_{i_{0}}\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}p
. We recall that
p\notin\mathcal{V}(X_{i_{0}})
. Therefore, the structurality of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
implies that
X_{i_{0}}
is inconsistent.
∎

Proposition 5.1.2

(Wójcicki theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic in a language
\mathcal{L}
with
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})\geq\aleph_{0}
. Then
\mathcal{S}
has an adequate matrix if, and only if,
\mathcal{S}
is both uniform and couniform.

Proof.

The ‘only-if’ part follows from Lemmas
5.1.1
and
5.1.3
.

The proof of the ‘if’ part repeats the steps of the “if” part of the proof of the Loś-Suszko theorem (Proposition
5.1.1
) up to the assumption
(\ast)
.
Then, we continue as follows.

First, we notice that, in virtue of (
5.4
) and of the couniformity
of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
(Lemma
5.1.4
), the set
\bigcup\{\sigma_{X}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}\}
is consistent. Thus, we have:

\sigma_{X_{0}}(X_{0}),\bigcup\{\sigma_{X}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X\neq X_{0}\}\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha),

where the set
\bigcup\{\sigma_{X}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X\neq X_{0}\}
is consistent with respect to
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
.

Let us denote

Y_{0}:=\bigcup\{\sigma_{X}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{c}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X\neq X_{0}\}.

With the help of (
8.3
) and (
5.3
), we conclude that
\mathcal{V}(\sigma_{X_{0}}(X_{0})\cup\{\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha)\})\cal\mathcal{V}(Y_{0})=\varnothing
.
Then, using the uniformity of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
, we derive that
\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha)\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha)
. This, in virtue of structurality of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
(Proposition
4.5.4
), implies that

\xi_{X_{0}}(\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha))\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\xi_{X_{0}}(\sigma_{X_{0}}(\alpha))
and hence, according to (
5.2
),
X_{0}\vdash_{(\mathcal{S})^{+}}\alpha
. Because of the conservativeness of
(\mathcal{S})^{+}
over
\mathcal{S}
, the latter leads to
X_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. A contradiction.
∎

Definition 5.1.3
.

An abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
has the
cancellation property
if for any system
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of consistent sets with
\mathcal{V}(X_{i})\cap\mathcal{V}(X_{j})=\varnothing
, whenever
i\neq j
, and a set
X\cup\{\alpha\}
of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas such that for all
i\in I
,
\mathcal{V}(X_{i})\cap\mathcal{V}(X\cup\{\alpha\})=\varnothing
, if

X\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, then
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.

Lemma 5.1.5
.

Let an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
be structural. Then
\mathcal{S}
has the cancellation property if, and only if,
\mathcal{S}
is uniform and couniform.

Proof.

Assume first that
\mathcal{S}
has the cancellation property. Then, obviously,
\mathcal{S}
is also uniform.

Next, let a system
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of consistent sets satisfy all the premises of couniformity. If
\textit{card}(I)=1
, that is
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}=\{X\}
, then, by premise
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\})=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\neq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Otherwise, let us fix any
X_{i_{0}}
, where
i_{0}\in I
.

Now, let us take
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}
.
By premise,
X_{i_{0}}
is consistent. For contradiction, assume that
\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}
is inconsistent, that is,
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X_{i_{0}}\cup\bigcup_{j\in I\setminus\{i_{0}\}}\{X_{j}\})=\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. By the cancellation property,

X_{i_{0}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}p
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is structural, we derive that
X_{i_{0}}
is inconsistent. A contradiction.

Conversely, suppose
\mathcal{S}
is uniform and couniform. Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
X\cup\{\alpha\}
be a system of consistent sets and, respectively, a set of formulas satisfying all the premises of the cancellation property.

We denote

Y:=\bigcup_{i\in I}\{X_{i}\}.

Since
\mathcal{S}
is couniform,
Y
is consistent. Now assume that
X,Y\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is uniform,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.
∎

Proposition 5.1.3

(Shoesmith-Smiley theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic in a language
\mathcal{L}
with
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})\geq\aleph_{0}
. Then
\mathcal{S}
has an adequate matrix if, and only if,
\mathcal{S}
has the cancellation property.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
5.1.5
to Proposition
5.1.2
.
∎

The following two criteria for single-matrix consequence are formulated in terms of matrix consequence.

Suppose we have an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
. We define:

X\in\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{M}})\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \text{there is a valuation $v$ in {{A}} such that $X=\{\alpha\penalty\ |\penalty\ v[\alpha]\in D\}$}.

Then, we define:

X\in\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}})\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \text{there is $Y\in\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{M}})$ such that $X\subseteq Y$}.

(5.5)

According to (
5.5
), we have:

\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{M}})\subseteq\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}}).

Next, given
\mathcal{L}
-matrices
M
and
N
, we observe:

\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{M}})\subseteq\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{N}})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}})\subseteq\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{N}}).

Indeed, let
X\in\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}})
. In virtue of (
5.5
),
X\subseteq Y
, for some
Y\in\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{M}})
. By premise,
Y\in\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{N}})
and hence, by (
5.5
),

X\in\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{N}})
.

The last implication obviously implies that

\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{M}})=\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{N}})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}})=\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{N}}).

(5.6)

First, we use the
\mathfrak{R}
-operator to characterize single-matrix consequence.

Proposition 5.1.4
.

A structural consequence relation in a language
\mathcal{L}
is a single-matrix consequence if, and only if, it can be determined by a class
\mathcal{M}=\{\textsf{{M}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{L}
-matrices such that for any
i,j\in I
,
\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}}_{i})=\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}}_{j})
.

Proof.

The ‘only-if’ implication is obvious.

So, assume that the given consequence relation is determined by a class
\mathcal{M}=\{\textsf{{M}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
with
\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}}_{i})=\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}}_{j})
, for any
i,j\in I
.

Using notation
\textsf{{M}}_{i}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{i},D_{i}\rangle
, we define the matrix

\textsf{{M}}^{\ast}:=\langle\prod_{i\in I}\textsf{{A}}_{i},\prod_{i\in I}D_{i}\rangle,

where the first component is the direct product of the algebras
\textsf{{A}}_{i}
and the second is the Cartesian product of the sets
D_{i}
.

We intend to prove that the
\mathcal{M}
-consequence equals
\textsf{{M}}^{\ast}
-consequence.

Suppose
X\vdash_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha
and let
v
be a valuation with
v[X]\subseteq\prod_{i\in I}D_{i}
. We denote by
v_{i}
the projection of
v
on
\textsf{{A}}_{i}
. Since each
v_{i}
is a valuation, respectively, in
\textsf{{A}}_{i}
with

v_{i}[X]\subseteq D_{i}
, then, by premise,
v_{i}[\alpha]\in D_{i}
, for any
i\in I
. Hence
v[\alpha]\in\prod_{i\in I}D_{i}
.

Next, assume that
X\vdash_{\textsf{{M}}^{\ast}}\alpha
. Let
v_{i_{0}}
be a valuation in
\textsf{{M}}_{i_{0}}
with
v_{i_{0}}[X]\subseteq D_{i_{0}}
, for arbitrary
i_{0}\in I
. This means that
X\in\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}}_{i_{0}})
. By premise, for all other
j\in I
,
X\in\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}}_{j})
with respect to corresponding valuations
v_{j}
in
\textsf{{M}}_{j}
. This allows us to define the valuation
v
in
\textsf{{M}}^{\ast}
so that for each
i\in I
, the projection of
v
on
\textsf{{A}}_{i}
equals
v_{i}
.
Then, by premise, we have that
v[\alpha]\in\prod_{i\in I}D_{i}
which in turn implies that
v_{i}[\alpha]\in D_{i}
, for each
i\in D_{i}
, in particular,
v_{i_{0}}[\alpha]\in D_{i_{0}}
.
∎

An advantage of the
\mathfrak{S}
-operator over the
\mathfrak{R}
-operator is that in terms of the former, matrix consequence can be easily formulated.

Indeed, let
\mathcal{M}:=\{\textsf{{M}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of
\mathcal{L}
-matrices. It is obvious that

X\vdash_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \text{for every $i\in I$, for every $Y\in\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{M}}_{i})$, if $X\subseteq Y$, then $\alpha\in Y$}.

(5.7)

(Exercise
5.1
.
3
)

Proposition 5.1.5
.

A structural consequence relation in a language
\mathcal{L}
is a single-matrix consequence if, and only if, it can be determined by a class
\mathcal{M}=\{\textsf{{M}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{L}
-matrices such that for any
i,j\in I
,
\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{M}}_{i})=\mathfrak{S}(\textsf{{M}}_{j})
.

Proof.

Indeed, the ‘only-if’ part is obvious.

To prove the ‘if’ part, we notice that, in virtue of (
5.6
),
for any
i,j\in I
,
\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}}_{i})=\mathfrak{R}(\textsf{{M}}_{j})
. It remains to apply Proposition
5.1.4
.
∎

We note that the restriction on the language ‘
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})\geq\aleph_{0}
’ imposed in Proposition
5.1.1
, Proposition
5.1.2
and Proposition
5.1.3
in Proposition
5.1.4
and Proposition
5.1.5
can be dropped.

Exercises
5.1

1.

Prove that the relation
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}^{\circ}
obtained from
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
according to (
4.3
) with
X_{0}=L\textsf{{B}}_{2}
is finitary but not uniform.

2.

Prove that a consequence relation defined by premiseless rules and modus ponens is uniform.

3.

Prove the equivalence (
5.7
).

5.2
Finitary matrix consequence

Given a (nonempty) class
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathcal{L}
-matrices, an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
, according to Proposition
4.3.11
,
is structural and, according to Corollary
4.3.13.2
, is determined by the class of all
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
-models. The last class definitely contains
\mathcal{M}
. For the purpose of this section, it will not be an exaggeration to assume that
\mathcal{M}
coincides with the class of all
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
-models.
Under this assumption, the question arises: What characteristics should
\mathcal{M}
have in order for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
to be finitary? Proposition
5.2.2
below proposes an answer to this question.

However, first we discuss the property that the matrix consequence of any finite matrix is finitary. To prove this, we use topological methods discussed in Section
2.2
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a finite logical matrix of type
\mathcal{L}
and let
V_{\textsf{{A}}}:=\textsf{{A}}^{\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}}
(the set of all valuations in algebra
A
). We regard
|\textsf{{A}}|
as a finite topological space with discrete topology and
V_{\textsf{{A}}}
as the cartesian power of the space
|\textsf{{A}}|
relative to the product topology. According to Corollary
2.2.2.1
, the space
V_{\textsf{{A}}}
is compact.

For any formula
\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we denote:

V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha):=\{v\in V_{\textsf{{A}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ v[\alpha]\in D\}.

We aim to show that each
V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)
is open and closed in
V_{\textsf{{A}}}
. The idea is that, since an arbitrary formula
\alpha
contains finitely many sentential variables and the set
|\textsf{{A}}|
is finite, there are only finitely many restricted assignments that validate
\alpha
and there are finitely many restricted assignments that refute it in
M
.

We show that each
V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)
is an open set in the product topology. The closedness of it, that is the openness of its complement, is similar.

Given an arbitrary formula
\alpha
, we define a binary relation on
V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)
as follows:

v\equiv w\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ v(p)=w(p),\penalty\ \text{for any $p\in\mathcal{V}(\alpha)$}.

It is clear that the relation
\equiv
is an equivalence. For any
v\in V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)
, we denote:

|v|:=\{w\in V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)\penalty\ |\penalty\ w\equiv v\}.

It is well know that
\{|v|\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\in V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)\}
is a partition of
V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)
. If we show that each
|v|
is open in the product topology, so will be
V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)
.

We observe that

|v|=\!\!\!\prod_{\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}}Z_{p},

where

Z_{p}=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}\{v(p)\}&\text{if $p\in V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)$}\\
|\textsf{{A}}|&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\end{cases}

According to the description (
2.1
) of the product topology in Section
2.2
, this demonstrates that each
|v|
is open. In a similar manner, we prove that each

V_{\textsf{{A}}}\setminus V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)=\{v\in V_{\textsf{{A}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ v[\alpha]\notin D\}

is also open.

Now, assume that for a set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha
. This implies (is even equivalent to) that
\bigcap_{\beta\in X}V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\beta)\subseteq V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)
. The last inclusion in turn is equivalent to the following:

V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha)\cup\bigcup_{\beta\in X}(V_{\textsf{{A}}}\setminus V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\beta))=V_{\textsf{{A}}}.

(5.8)

This means that
\{V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha\}\cup\{(V_{\textsf{{A}}}\setminus V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\beta))\}_{\beta\in X}
is an open cover of
V_{\textsf{{A}}}
. Since the space

V_{\textsf{{A}}}
is compact, it has a finite subcover

\{V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\alpha\}\cup\{(V_{\textsf{{A}}}\setminus V_{\textsf{{A}}}(\beta))\}_{\beta\in Y}
, for some
Y\Subset X
. The latter is equivalent to
Y\models_{\textbf{M}}\alpha
.

Thus we have proved the following.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

A single-matrix consequence relative to a finite matrix is finitary.

Corollary 5.2.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a nonempty finite family of finite matrices of type
\mathcal{L}
. Then the
\mathcal{M}
-consequence is finitary.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
5.1
.
1
)

The next example shows that the converse of the statement of Proposition
5.2.1
in general is not true.

Example 5.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a propositional language with any set (perhaps finite) of propositional variables and a single binary connective
\circledast
. An abstract logic
\mathcal{S}^{\circledast}
is defined by the premiseless rule
\bm{\alpha}\circledast\bm{\alpha}
and modus ponens,
\bm{\alpha},\,\bm{\alpha}\circledast\bm{\beta}/\penalty\bm{\beta}
. We claim that
\mathcal{S}^{\circledast}
has no finite adequate matrix.

We will prove that
\mathcal{S}^{\circledast}
has no finite weakly adequate matrix; this will imply that it has no adequate matrix as well.

First of all, we note that the logic
\mathcal{S}^{\circledast}
is structural and finitary. Secondly, we notice the set of
\mathcal{S}^{\circledast}
-theses consists of the formulas of the form
\alpha\circledast\alpha
, for any
\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Now, for contradiction, assume that a matrix
\textsf{{M}}:=\langle\textsf{A};\circledast,D\rangle
is weakly adequate for
\mathcal{S}^{\circledast}
and has
n
elements. The observations above (about
\mathcal{S}^{\circledast}
-theses) implies that for any
a\in\textsf{A}
,
a\circledast a\in D
.

Let us definite an infinite sequence of terms as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}t^{1}(x)=x\circledast x\\
t^{n+1}(x)=t(t^{n}(x)).\end{array}

It must be clear that for an arbitrary
a\in\textsf{A}
, there is a natural number
k\geq 1
such that
t^{k}(a)=a
. Assume that
a=v[\alpha]
, for some formula
\alpha
at some valuation
v
in
A
. We note that
t^{k}(\alpha)
is a formula unequal to
\alpha
and hence
t^{k}(\alpha)\circledast\alpha
is not an
\mathcal{S}^{\circledast}
-theorem, though
v[t^{k}(\alpha)\circledast\alpha]\in D
. A contradiction.

Now we turn to a more general case.

Proposition 5.2.2

(Bloom-Zygmunt criterion)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the class of all models of an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{L}
. Then
\mathcal{S}
is finitary if, and only if, the class
\mathcal{M}
is closed with respect to ultraproducts.
1
1

1

Ultraproducts are understood in the sense of (
2.21
).

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{S}
is finitary. Then, according to Corollary
4.4.1.2
, there is a set
\mathcal{R}
of modus rules such that
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
. Thus,
\mathcal{M}
is the class of all
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
-models. Then, in virtue of Corollary
4.4.4.1
,
\mathcal{M}
is closed under ultraproducts.

Next, let the class
\mathcal{M}
be closed with respect to ultraproducts. For contradiction, assume that
\mathcal{S}
is not finitary. That is, for some set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
but for any
Y\Subset X
,
\alpha\notin Y
.

We denote:

I:=\{Y\penalty\ |\penalty\ Y\Subset X\}

and for any
Y\in I
,

\langle Y\rangle:=\{Z\in I\penalty\ |\penalty\ Y\subseteq Z\}.

We observe that for any
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\in I
,

\langle Y_{1}\rangle\cap\ldots\cap\langle Y_{n}\rangle=\langle Y_{1}\cap\ldots\cap Y_{n}\rangle.

That is, the set
\{\langle Y\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ Y\in I\}
has the finite intersection property. According to the Ultrafilter Theorem (
[
Chang and Keisler, 1990
]
, proposition 4.1.3), there is an ultrafilter
\nabla
over
I
such that

\{\langle Y\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ Y\in I\}\subseteq\nabla.

(5.9)

Given an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\beta\in X
, we note that

\langle\{\beta\}\rangle=\{Y\in I\penalty\ |\penalty\ \beta\in Y\}\subseteq\{Y\in I\penalty\ |\penalty\ \beta\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\}.

This, in view of (
5.9
), implies that for arbitrary formula
\beta\in X
,

\{Y\in I\penalty\ |\penalty\ \beta\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\}\in\nabla.

(5.10)

Using the notation
\mathbf{Lin}_{\,\mathcal{S}}[Y]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\rangle
(Section
4.3.4
), we define:

\textsf{{M}}:=\prod_{F}\mathbf{Lin}_{\,\mathcal{S}}[Y].

Thus

\textsf{{M}}=\langle(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}})^{I}/\penalty\nabla,D\rangle,

where a set
D\subseteq|(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}})^{I}/\penalty\nabla|
and such that for any
\theta\in|(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}})^{I}|
,

\theta/\penalty\nabla\in D\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \{Y\in I\penalty\ |\penalty\ \theta_{Y}\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\}\in\nabla.

(5.11)

For any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\beta
, we define:

\overline{\beta}:I\longrightarrow\{\beta\}

and then, for any
\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

v:\beta\mapsto\overline{\beta}/\penalty\nabla.

It must be clear that
v:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}})^{I}/\penalty\nabla
is a homomorphism and, hence, can be regarded as an assignment of the
\mathcal{L}
-formulas in
(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}})^{I}/\penalty\nabla
.

We aim to show that
v[X]\subseteq D
but
v[\alpha]\notin D
. This will imply that
M
is not an
\mathcal{S}
-model and hence does not belong to
\mathcal{M}
. The latter in turn will implies that
\mathcal{M}
is not closed under ultraproducts, which is contrary to the assumption.

Indeed, it follows from (
5.11
) and (
5.10
) that for any
\beta\in X
,

v[\beta]=\overline{\beta}/\penalty\nabla\in D.

On the other hand,

\{Y\in I\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y)\}=\varnothing.

Therefore, in view of (
5.11
),
v[\alpha]=\overline{\alpha}/\penalty\nabla\notin D
.
∎

Exercises
5.2

1.

Prove Corollary
5.2.1.1
.

2.

Show that the class of all models of
Cl
(Section
4.3.5
) is closed with respect to ultraproducts.

3.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of modus rules. Prove that the abstract logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{R}}
(Section
4.4.1
) is finitary.

5.3
The conception of separating tools

Considering an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
as a consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
, there are two main tasks: to confirm
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
or to refute it. Given a finitary abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, these two tasks can be regarded as one twofold problem (of extreme importance): Is there an algorithm that for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\Subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, decides whether
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
is true or false.

The importance of the second part of this dual-task was emphasized by J. Łukasiewicz; namely, he wrote:

Of two intellectual acts, to assert a proposition and to reject it,
2
2

2

[Łukasiewicz’s footnote]: “I owe this distinction to Franz Brentano, who describes the act of believing as
anerkennen
and
verwerfen
.”
only the first has been taken into account in modern formal logic. Gottlob Frege introduced into logic the idea of assertion, and the sign of assertion (
\bm{\vdash}
), accepted afterwards by the authors of
Principia Mathematica
. The idea of rejection, however, so far as I know, has been neglected up to the present day.
[
Łukasiewicz, 1957
]
, § 27

Trying to confirm
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, one can use rules of inference (sound with respect to
\mathcal{S}
) or a suitable adequate matrix or a set of matrices which determine
\mathcal{S}
. On the other hand, to refute
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, we need “means” that would separate
X
from
\alpha
, for any
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
or at least for any
X\cup\{\alpha\}\Subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. According to Corollary
4.3.13.1
, the Lindenbaum atlas for
\mathcal{S}
is a universal tool, for it works, at least in principal, for both tasks. However, it is too complicated and can be used mainly to prove very general theorems. For the refutation task, the Lindenbaum atlas is very inefficient. Nevertheless, in many cases, as we will see, it can be simplified to an extent to be useful.

A general view on the conception of
separating tools
was formulated by A. Kuznetsov
[
Kuznetsov, 1979
]
. According to him, if all formulas of a set
X
stand in a relation
R
to an object
\mathfrak{M}
, but a formula
\alpha
does not stand in
R
to
\mathfrak{M}
, we say that
\mathfrak{M}

separates

X
from
\alpha
with respect to
R
.
In this case, the character of the relation
R
must be such that from the separation of
X
from
\alpha
it follows that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
does not hold.
3
3

3

The conception of separating tools, proposed by Kuznetsov, was intended to be applied not only to the issue of derivability, but also to that of expressibility understood within the framework of a given calculus.
He also emphasized that the object
\mathfrak{M}
which was employed as a separating tools in the above sense has often been of algebraic nature.
4
4

4

One important exception is Kleene’s notion of realizability; see
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 82.

While the Lindenbaum atlas relative to an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
can play a role of universal separating tools, but for a particular pair
X
and
\alpha
, in case

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
does not hold, potentially any
\mathcal{S}
-matrix can be a separating tools. Sometimes a suitable separating model may not be the Lindenbaum matrix itself, but another matrix whose algebraic carrier is related to the algebraic carrier of the former. Here it is appropriate to recall the corelations (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) and the final remark about them in Section
4.3
.
Therefore, it would be interesting to find an algebraic connection between the Lindenbaum atlas or Lindenbaum matrix relative to
\mathcal{S}
and any
\mathcal{S}
-matrix. This will be the topic of the next chapter.

5.4
Historical notes

Although Proposition
5.1.1
, Proposition
5.1.2
and Proposition
5.1.3
were proven a bit longer than a half century ago, their history is somewhat complicated. Therefore, we break the statements of Proposition
5.1.1
and Proposition
5.1.2
into several part and add several new ones. This will allow us to see the logical structure of these statements, as well as a few more. Then, all statements will be analyzed from a historical point of view.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{A})&\text{an abstract logic $\mathcal{S}$ has an adequate matrix};\\
(\text{B})&\text{an abstract logic $\mathcal{S}$ is structural};\\
(\text{C})&\text{an abstract logic $\mathcal{S}$ is finitary};\\
(\text{D})&\text{an abstract logic $\mathcal{S}$ is uniform (Definition\penalty\ \ref{D:uniform-logic})};\\
(\text{E})&\text{an abstract logic $\mathcal{S}$ is couniform (Definition\penalty\ \ref{D:couniform-logic})};\\
(\text{F})&\text{an abstract logic $\mathcal{S}$ has the cancellation property (Definition\penalty\ \ref{D:cancellation-property})};\\
(\text{G})&\text{an abstract logic $\mathcal{S}$ is \emph{regular}
(see the definition of a regular logic}\\
&\text{in\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{wojcicki1969}{}{}]} or in\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{wojcicki1970}{}{}]}, section 1}.\\
(\text{H})&\text{an abstract logic $\mathcal{S}$ is \emph{absolutely separable}
(see the definition}\\
&\text{of an absolutely separable logic in\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{wojcicki1984}{}{}]}, section 34.6)}.\\
\end{array}

The original theorem of Lindenbaum, as was stated in
[
Łukasiewicz and Tarski, 1930
]
, theorem 3, concerned, using the formulation of
[
Zygmunt, 2012
]
, the “(weak) adequacy problem.” That is, as the aforementioned theorem 3 was clarified and proved in
[
Łoś, 1949
]
, theorem 10, given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, any
\mathcal{S}
-theory
X
is determined by a single logical matrix, namely
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]
. And, again, according to
[
Zygmunt, 2012
]
,
[
Łoś and Suszko, 1958
]
turned this problem into the “(strong) adequacy problem;” namely, they raised the question of which abstract logic can be determined using a single logical matrix.

Having introduced the notion of a uniform abstract logic, Łoś and Suszko then attempted to prove that a structural abstract logic defines a single-matrix consequence if, and only if, it is uniform.
The ‘only-if’ part of this criterion was stated in
[
Łoś and Suszko, 1958
]
, section 8, p. 181, while the ‘if’ part was presented as “the main theorem.” Thus, their combined statement was

(\text{A})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{B})\&(\text{D})

(5.12)

However, R. Wójcicki noticed in
[
Wójcicki, 1969
]
that, while

(\text{A})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{B})\&(\text{D})

is true, the converse,

(\text{B})\&(\text{D})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{A}),

in general is not. Since the implication

(\text{A})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{B})

(Lemma
5.1.1
) is true, the problem was with the implication

(\text{D})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{A}),

providing that the logic
\mathcal{S}
is structural. Thus, in the sequel, we always assume (B) as a premise.

Fixing a lacuna in Łoś and Suszko’s argumentation for (
5.12
), Wócicki
announced in
[
Wójcicki, 1969
]
, theorem 2, and proved in
[
Wójcicki, 1970
]
, theorem 2.2, that

(\text{B})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ ((\text{D})\&(\text{G})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{A})).

Also, he proved in
[
Wójcicki, 1970
]
that

(\text{B})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ ((\text{C})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{G}))

(assertion 1.3) and

(\text{B})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ ((\text{A})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{D})\&(\text{G})).

(assertion 2.1). Thus, it was proven therein that

(\text{B})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ ((\text{A})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{D})\&(\text{G})).

Later on, in
[
Wójcicki, 1984
]
, theorem 34.14, Wójcicki replaced regularity with absolute separability, i.e.

(\text{B})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ ((\text{A})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{D})\&(\text{H}))

and in
[
Wójcicki, 1988
]
, theorem 3.2.5, with finitariness, i.e.

(\text{B})\&(\text{C})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ ((\text{A})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{D})).

(Proposition
5.1.1
) which we call the
Łoś-Suszko-Wójcicki theorem
.

In
[
Wójcicki, 1988
]
, he introduced a couniform logic and proved (theorem 3.2.7) that

(\text{B})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ ((\text{A})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{D})\&(\text{E}))

(Proposition
5.1.2
) which we call the
Wójcicki theorem
.

In
[
Shoesmith and Smiley, 1971
]
, not referring to either
[
Łoś and Suszko, 1958
]
or
[
Wójcicki, 1969
,
Wójcicki, 1970
]
, D. J. Shoesmith and T. J. Smiley gave their version of the adequacy theorem: structural abstract logic has an adequate matrix if, and only if, it has the cancellation property, i.e.

(\text{B})\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ ((\text{A})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (\text{F}))

(Proposition
5.1.3
) which we call the
Shoesmith-Smiley theorem
.

Proposition
5.2.1
is due to
[
Łoś and Suszko, 1958
]
, section 8, p.181.

Example
5.2.1
is due to
[
McKinsey and Tarski, 1948
]
, section 2, Remark on p. 6.

The ‘only-if’ part of Proposition
5.2.2
was proved in
[
Zygmunt, 1974
]
, corollary; the full equivalence is due to
[
Bloom, 1975
]
, theorem 2.6.

Chapter 6
Unital Abstract Logics

6.1
Unital algebraic expansions

From the discussion in Section
5.3
, the following questions arise.

(1)

Is there a regular method of simplifying the Lindenbaum atlas (Lindenbaum matrix) relative to an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
to obtain an adequate atlas (or matrix), or at least weakly adequate matrix that would be a more working separating tool than
\mathbf{Lin}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}]
or
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}
?

(2)

Is there a regular method of simplifying the Lindenbaum atlas (Lindenbaum matrix) relative to an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
to have a connection between these simplified forms of
\mathbf{Lin}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}]
or
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}
and other
\mathcal{S}
-atlases and
\mathcal{S}
-matrices, respectively?

As to the question
(1)
, any finite matrix determines an abstract logic, the Lindenbaum matrix of which is always infinite, for the cardinality of any Lindenbaum matrix equals the cardinality of the set of formulas. Take, for instance, the matrix
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
of Section
3.2.1
. In Section
4.3.5
we have proved the equality
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}
. The latter corresponds to the abstract logic
Cl
(classical propositional logic). Even a rough comparison between
\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{Cl}}
and
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
demonstrates the difference between their complexities.

One thing is remarkable about the logical matrices discussed in Sections
3.2.1
–
3.2.5
. The logical filters of all of them consist of a single element. A logical matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
is called
unital
if
D
is a one-element set. We denote the designated element of a unital matrix by
\mathbf{1}
(perhaps with subscript). Thus, if
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\{\mathbf{1}\}\rangle
, we can consider
the algebra
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
as a semantics
for
\mathcal{L}
-formulas. We call
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
a
unital expansion
of
A
. Very often, such an expansion is not needed. Namely, when
D=\{c\}
, where
c
is a constant from the signature of
A
, we count that
\mathbf{1}=c
. In this event, we count
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
the same as
A
.

Thus, in case
M
is unital, instead of saying that an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\alpha
is valid in
M
, we say that
\alpha
is
valid
in
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
, meaning that for any valuation
v
in
A
,
v[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}
. Further, given an algebra
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
, for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we define:

X\models_{\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}v[X]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}\Rightarrow v[\alpha]=\mathbf{1},\penalty\ \text{for any valuation $v$ in $\textsf{{A}}$}.

The last definition allows us to adapt the definition of
\mathcal{S}
-model to algebras

\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
. Namely such an algebra is an
algebraic

\mathcal{S}
-
model
if for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\models_{\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\alpha.

Further, we say that an algebra
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
is
adequate
for an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
if for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\models_{\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\alpha;

and that
A
is
weakly adequate
for
\mathcal{S}
if for any
\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\text{for any valuation $v$ in $\textsf{{A}}$},v[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}.

The definition of
\mathcal{M}
-consequence (Section
4.3.4
) can be applied to the case when
\mathcal{M}=\{\langle\textsf{{A}}_{i},\mathbf{1}_{i}\rangle\}_{i\in I}
. Namely, for any (nonempty) such class
\mathcal{M}
, we define:

X\models_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ X\models_{\langle\textbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{1}_{i}\rangle}\alpha,\penalty\ \text{for each $\langle\textsf{{A}}_{i},\mathbf{1}_{i}\rangle$}.

An abstract logic which can be defined as an
\mathcal{M}
-consequence of this type is called
assertional
; cf
[
Font, 2016
]
, definition 3.5. Thus the assertional abstract logics are those which are determined by classes of their algebraic models.

It will be convenient to treat the validity of an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\alpha
in

\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
as the validity of a first-order formula in a first-order model.

Namely, given a sentential language
\mathcal{L}
, we define a first-order language
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
whose individual variables are the sentential variables of
\mathcal{L}
and the connectives of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
and the constants of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}}
are the functional symbols. (The constant symbols are treated as 0-ary functional symbols). In addition,
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
has a 0-ary functional symbol
\mathbf{1}
which is interpreted in the
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
-models by a constant
\mathbf{1}
. Also,
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
has symbol ‘
\approx
’ which is interpreted in the
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
-models as equality, the first-order logical connectives

\bm{\&}
(conjunction),
\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}
(implication) and universal quantifier
\boldsymbol{\forall}
.
The parentheses ‘(’ and ‘)’ are used in the usual way.

Thus each
\mathcal{L}
-formula becomes in
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
a term. In addition,
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
has terms like

\alpha(p,\ldots,\mathbf{1},\ldots,q)
obtained from
\alpha(p,\ldots,r,\ldots,q)
