by replacement of a variable
r
with the constant
\mathbf{1}
.

The atomic formulas of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
, called also
equalities
, are the expressions of the form:

\mathfrak{r}\approx\mathfrak{t},

where
\mathfrak{r}
and
\mathfrak{t}
are
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
-terms.

Thus
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
as a logical system is a first-order logic with equality.

With each expansion
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
we associate an
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
-model
\mathfrak{A}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1},\approx\rangle
so that,
talking about validity of identities and quasiidentities in
\mathfrak{A}
, we, referring to (
2.20
), will be writing

\mathfrak{A}\models\alpha(p,\mathbf{1},\ldots)\approx\beta(q,\mathbf{1},\ldots)\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathfrak{A}\models(\phi_{1}\bm{\&}\ldots\bm{\&}\phi_{n})\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\phi

instead of, respectively,
\mathfrak{A}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}(\alpha(p,\mathbf{1},\ldots)\approx\beta(q,\mathbf{1},\ldots))

and

\mathfrak{A}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}((\phi_{1}\bm{\&}\ldots\bm{\&}\phi_{n})\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\phi)
.
Thus, it must be clear that for any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\alpha
and any
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\ast}
-model

\mathfrak{A}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1},\approx\rangle
,

\models_{\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathfrak{A}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}(\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}).

(6.1)

Let
X:=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
. We denote:

X\approx\mathbf{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\alpha_{1}\approx\mathbf{1}\&\ldots\&\alpha_{n}\approx\mathbf{1}.

Thus, in this notation, for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\Subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we can observe that

X\models_{\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathfrak{A}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}(X\approx\mathbf{1}\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}).

(6.2)

(If
X=\varnothing
, we understand the last equivalence as (
6.1
).)

Proposition 6.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a finitary assertional abstract logic. Then all algebraic
\mathcal{S}
-models form a quasi-variety.

Proof.

Indeed, let
\left\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\right\rangle
be a unital algebraic expansion. Then, since
\mathcal{S}
is finitary,
\left\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\right\rangle
is an
\mathcal{S}
-model if, and only if,

\left\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1},\approx\right\rangle\models X\approx\mathbf{1}\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}
, for any
X\cup\{\alpha\}\Subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. ∎

Exercises
6.1

1.

Prove that if an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
has a finite unital adequate matrix, then the class of all algebraic
\mathcal{S}
-models is a quasi-variety.

2.

Show that the class of all algebraic
Cl
-models is a quasi-variety.

6.2
Unital abstract logics

Given a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we denote by
\theta(X)
the congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
, generated by the set
X\times X
. We will be interested in congruences
\theta(D)
, where
D
is a theory of an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, in particular in congruences
\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
.

The following observation will be useful in the sequel.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

A structural abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
is nontrivial if, and only if, it satisfies the following property: For any
p,q\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

p\neq q\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ p/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})\neq q/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}).

(6.3)

Proof.

To prove the ‘only-if’ part, we assume that
\mathcal{S}
is nontrivial. Then, since
\mathcal{S}
is structural,
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
does not contain sentential variables. Let
M
be the congruence class with respect to
\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
, which contains the variable
p
. Now, we define:
M_{1}:=\{p\}
and
M_{2}:=M\setminus\{p\}
. The new partition of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
induces the equivalence
\theta
.
It is obvious that
\theta\subset\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
. We will show that
\theta
is a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Let
F
be an arbitrary
n
-ary connective with
n\geq 1
. Assume that for formulas
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}
, each pair
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\in\theta
.
Then each pair
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\in\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
and hence

(F\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n},F\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{n})\in\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
. This means that either both
F\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}
and
F\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{n}
belong to
M_{2}
or they belong to a class of
\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
that is different from
M
and hence is a class of
\theta
.
This implies that
\{p\}
and
\{q\}
are two distinct congruence classes with respect to
\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
.

Conversely, suppose the implication (
6.3
) holds. Then, since one of the congruence classes with respect to
\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
contains
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
and there are more than two classes,

\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
∎

6.2.1
Definition and some properties

Definition 6.2.1

(unital abstract logic)
.

A structural abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
is called
unital
if any
\mathcal{S}
-theory
D
is a congruence class with respect to the congruence
\theta(D)
.

In the sequel (Chapter
9
), we also use the following notation:

\theta_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \theta(\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)).

where
\mathcal{S}
is a given abstract logic.

We need the following property in the sequel.

Proposition 6.2.2
.

Given a unital logic
\mathcal{S}
and a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the abstract logic
\mathcal{S}[X]
(Section
4.2
) is also unital.

Proof.

We observe that any
\mathcal{S}[X]
-theory is also an
\mathcal{S}
-theory.
∎

Since any congruence class cannot be empty, from Definition
6.2.1
and Proposition
6.2.1
, we immediately obtain the following.

Proposition 6.2.3
.

For any unital abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
,
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\varnothing
; in addition,
\mathcal{S}
is nontrivial if, and only if, (
6.3
) holds.

As we will see below (Corollary
6.2.4.2
), the unitality of an abstract logic is connected to the following concept.

Definition 6.2.2
.

Let
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
X\neq\varnothing
. A congruence
\theta
on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
is said to be
compatible
with
X

if for any
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta
,

\alpha\in X\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \beta\in X;

in other words,
X
is the union of congruence classes with respect to
\theta
.

It is obvious that

\theta_{0}:=\{(\alpha,\alpha)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\}

is a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
, which is compatible with any nonempty set
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proposition 6.2.4
.

Let
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
X\neq\varnothing
.
For any congruence
\theta
on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
X
is a congruence class with respect to
\theta
if, and only if, the following conditions are satisfied:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\theta(X)\subseteq\theta;\\
(\text{b})&\theta\penalty\ \textit{is compatible with}\penalty\ X.\end{array}

Proof.

Suppose
X
is a congruence class with respect to
\theta
. Then (a) and (b) are obviously true.

Conversely, suppose both (a) and (b) are fulfilled. Then, according to (a), all formulas in
X
are
\theta
-congruent to one another. In addition, in virtue of (b), each formula in
X
cannot be
\theta
-congruent to a formula beyond
X
.
∎

The next two corollaries follow immediately from Proposition
6.2.4
.

Corollary 6.2.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an abstract logic with
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\varnothing
and
D
be an
\mathcal{S}
-theory. Then
D
is a congruence class with respect to
\theta(D)
if, and only if,

\theta(D)
is compatible with
D
.

Corollary 6.2.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a structural abstract logic with
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\varnothing
. Then the following conditions are equivalent
\,:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\textit{$\mathcal{S}$ is unital};\\
(\text{b})&\textit{$\theta(D)$ is compatible with $D$, for each $\mathcal{S}$-theory $D$};\\
(\text{c})&\textit{for any $\mathcal{S}$-theory $D$, there is a congruence $\theta$ on $\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}$}\\
&\textit{such that $\theta(D)\subseteq\theta$ and $\theta$ is compatible with $D$}.\end{array}

6.2.2
Some subclasses of unital logics

In this subsection, we introduce several classes of abstract logics, which were considered in the literature and which turned out to be included in the class of unital logics.

Definition 6.2.3

(implicative logic)
.

A structural logic
\mathcal{S}
in a language
\mathcal{L}
is called
implicative
if
\mathcal{L}
contains a binary connective
\rightarrow
such that for any
\mathcal{S}
-theory
D
, the following conditions are satisfied:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\text{$\alpha\rightarrow\alpha\in D$, for any formula $\alpha$};\\
(\text{b})&\text{$\alpha\rightarrow\beta\in D$ and $\beta\rightarrow\gamma\in D$ imply $\alpha\rightarrow\gamma\in D$, for any $\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}$};\\
(\text{c})&\text{$\alpha\in D$ and $\alpha\rightarrow\beta\in D$ imply $\beta\in D$, for any $\alpha,\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}$};\\
(\text{d})&\text{$\beta\in D$ implies $\alpha\rightarrow\beta\in D$, for any $\alpha,\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}$};\\
(\text{e})&\text{if $\alpha_{i}\rightarrow\beta_{i}\in D$,
$\beta_{i}\rightarrow\alpha_{i}\in D$, $1\leq i\leq n$, and $F$ is any $n$-ary connective},\\
&\text{then $F\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}\rightarrow F\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{n}\in D$, for any formulas $\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}$}.\\
\end{array}

We note that if a logic
\mathcal{S}
is implicative, then
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\varnothing
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be (at least) an implicative logic and
D
be an
\mathcal{S}
-theory. We define:

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{D}\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \text{$\alpha\rightarrow\beta\in D$ and $\beta\rightarrow\alpha\in D$}.

Proposition 6.2.5
.

Let
D
be a theory of an implicative logic
\mathcal{S}
. Then
\theta_{D}
is a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}

and
D
is a congruence class with respect to
\theta_{D}
.

Proof.

That
\theta_{D}
is a congruence follows straightforwardly from (a), (b) and (e) of Definition
6.2.3
.

Next, we first note that, by premise,
D\neq\varnothing
. Secondly, if both

\alpha
and
\beta
belong to
D
, then, according to (d) of Definition
6.2.3
, both
\alpha\rightarrow\beta
and
\beta\rightarrow\alpha
belong to
D
, that is
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{D}
. And, thirdly, let
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{D}
and
\alpha\in D
. Then
\beta\in D
. In virtue of Proposition
6.2.4
, this implies that
D
is a congruence class with respect to
\theta_{D}
.
∎

Corollary 6.2.5.1
.

If an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
is implicative, then
\mathcal{S}
is unital.

Proof.

Let
D
be any
\mathcal{S}
-theory.
If
\alpha,\beta\in D
, then, in virtue of Proposition
6.2.5
,
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{D}
. Then means that
\theta(D)\subseteq\theta_{D}
. Further, by premise,
\theta_{D}
is compatible with
D
. In virtue of Corollary
6.2.4.2
,

\mathcal{S}
is unital.
∎

In Section
4.3.5
, we defined the calculus
Cl
(classical propositional logic) in the language
\mathcal{L}_{A}
, which is defined by the inference rules ax1–ax10 and (b-
iii
) (modus ponens) of Section
4.3.5
.
The calculus in
\mathcal{L}_{A}
, which is defined by the rules ax1–ax9, ax11 (Section
4.4.4
) and modus ponens, is called the
intuitionistic propositional logic
and denoted by
Int
.
Also, it will be convenient to consider another calculus in the language
\mathcal{L}_{A}
,
P
, which is defined by the inference rules ax1–ax8 and (b-
iii
) (modus ponens) of Section
4.3.5
. We call
P

positive logic
.

It must be clear that for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}

X\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha\penalty\ \textit{implies both}\penalty\ X\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ X\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\alpha.

(6.4)

Further, it can be easily shown that
P
,
Cl
and
Int
are implicative. (See Exercise
6.2
.
2
.)

Another class of abstract logics, the class of Fregean logics, stems from

Frege’s principle of compositionality
(FPC).

An abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
has the FPC if for for any set
X\cup\{\gamma(p,\ldots),\alpha,\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

\text{$X,\alpha\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta$ and $X,\beta\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha$ imply $X,\gamma(\alpha,\ldots)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\gamma(\beta,\ldots)$}.

This leads to the following (equivalent) definition.

Definition 6.2.4

(Frege relation, Fregean logic)
.

Given a structural logic
\mathcal{S}
and an
\mathcal{S}
-theory
D
, the relation on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
defined by the equivalence

(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda D\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \text{$D,\alpha\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta$ and $D,\beta\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha$}

is called a
Frege relation relative to

D
. A structural logic
is
Fregean
if for every
\mathcal{S}
-theory
D
,
\Lambda D
is a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

We leave the reader to prove that for any structural logic
\mathcal{S}
, the properties of satisfying the FPC and being Fregean are equivalent. (See Exercise
6.3
.
4
.)

Proposition 6.2.6
.

If
\mathcal{S}
is a Fregean logic with
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\varnothing
, then
\mathcal{S}
is unital.

Proof.

Let
D
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{S}
-theory. We observe that

\theta(D)\subseteq\Lambda D
. Also,
\Lambda D
is compatible with
D
.
Applying Corollary
6.2.4.2
, we obtain that
\mathcal{S}
is unital.
∎

Exercises
6.2

1.

Prove that the
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
- consequence and
\textsf{{\L}}_{3}
-consequence are unital.

2.

Prove that the abstract logics
P
,
Cl
and
Int
are implicative.

3.

Prove that the abstract logics
P
,
Cl
and
Int
are Fregean.

4.

Prove that a structural logic
\mathcal{S}
is Fregean if, and only if, it satisfies the FPC.

6.3
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras

6.3.1
Definition and properties

The concept of unital abstract logic in turn induces the following important notion.

Definition 6.3.1

(Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic and
D
be an
\mathcal{S}
-theory. The algebra

\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta(D),\mathbf{1}_{D}\rangle
, where
\mathbf{1}_{D}
is
D
understood as a congruence class with respect to
\theta(D)
,
(
or very often only the first component
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta(D)
)
is called a
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra

(
of
\mathcal{S}
)

relative to

D
. The algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}),\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}\rangle

(
or the first component
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
)
is called simply a
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra
of
\mathcal{S}
.

Proposition 6.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic and and let
D
and
D^{\prime}
be
\mathcal{S}
-theories. If
D\subseteq D^{\prime}
, then the algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D^{\prime}]
is a homomorphic image of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
. Hence, for any
\mathcal{S}
-theory
D
, the algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
is a homomorphic image of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

We observe that, given a unital logic
\mathcal{S}
and any
\mathcal{S}
-theories
D
and
D^{\prime}
, if
D^{\prime}\subseteq D
, then

\theta(D)\subseteq\theta(D^{\prime})
; see (
2.4
). Further, in virtue of Proposition
2.3.5
, this implies that
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
is a homomorphic image of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D^{\prime}]
. Thus, for any
\mathcal{S}
-theory
D
, the algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
is a homomorphic image of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
. ∎

Lemma 6.3.1
.

Let
\theta
be a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Then for any valuation
v
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta
, there is an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
such that for any formula
\alpha
,
v[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta
.

Proof.

Let
v
be any valuation in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta
; that is,
v
is a homomorphism of

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
to
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta
. Suppose
v[p]=\beta_{p}/\penalty\theta
, where
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Then we define a substitution as follows:

\sigma:p\mapsto\beta_{p},

for all
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, with any selection of
\beta_{p}
from the class
\beta_{p}/\penalty\theta
. Thus, we have:
v[p]=\sigma(p)/\penalty\theta
, for all
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Now let us take any formula
\alpha
and assume that

p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
are all variables that occur in
\alpha
. Then we have:

v[\alpha]=\alpha(v[p_{1}],\ldots,v[p_{n}])=\alpha(\sigma(p_{1})/\penalty\theta,\ldots,\sigma(p_{n})/\penalty\theta)=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta.

∎

Proposition 6.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic. Then each
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
is an algebraic
\mathcal{S}
-model.

Proof.

Suppose that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. Let us take any
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
and consider a valuation
v
in it. In virtue of Lemma
6.3.1
, there is a substitution
\sigma
such that for any formula
\beta
,
v[\beta]=\sigma(\beta)/\penalty\theta(D)
. Further, we note that, since
\mathcal{S}
is structural,
\sigma(X)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\sigma(\alpha)
.

Now, suppose that for any
\gamma\in X
,
v[\gamma]=\mathbf{1}_{D}
. Then, since
\mathcal{S}
is unital, for any
\gamma\in X
,
\sigma(\gamma)\in D
. This implies that
\sigma(\alpha)\in D
, that is
v[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}_{D}
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic. Then for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\Subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
and for any
\mathcal{S}
-theory
D
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\models X\approx\mathbf{1}_{D}\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}_{D}.

Proof.

We apply successively Proposition
6.3.2
and (
6.2
).
∎

Proposition 6.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic and let
D\!:=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
. Then for any formula
\alpha
,

\models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]}\alpha\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \models_{\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]}\alpha.

Hence,
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
is weakly adequate for
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

First, assume that
\models_{\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]}\alpha
; that is for any substitution
\sigma
,
\sigma(\alpha)\in D
.
Let
v
be a valuation in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
. By Lemma
6.3.1
, there is a substitution
\sigma_{v}
such that
v[\alpha]=\sigma_{v}(\alpha)/\penalty\theta(D)
. By premise, this implies that
v[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}_{D}
.

Conversely, suppose for some substitution
\sigma
,
\sigma(\alpha)\notin D
. We define a valuation in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
as follows:
v[p]:=\sigma(p)/\penalty\theta(D)
, for any
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Then
v[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta(D)\neq\mathbf{1}_{D}
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.3.1
.

Let
D
be an
\mathcal{S}
-theory of a unital logic
\mathcal{S}
. Then for any

\mathcal{S}
-thesis
\alpha
, the algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
validates
\alpha
.

Proof.

Having noticed that
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
is a homomorphic image of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
, we apply the last part of Proposition
6.3.3
.
∎

We recall that
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}
denotes the set of all
\mathcal{S}
-theories.

Corollary 6.3.3.2
.

Any unital logic
\mathcal{S}
is determined by the class
\{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\penalty\ |\penalty\ D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\}
. Hence, any unital abstract logic is assertional.

Proof.

Indeed, if
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, then, in virtue of Proposition
6.3.2
,
X\models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]}\alpha
, for each
D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}
.

On the other hand, if
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, then
\not\models_{\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]}\alpha
and hence, according to Proposition
6.3.3
,
\not\models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]}\alpha
, where
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic. If a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
is closed under arbitrary substitutions, then for any formula
\alpha
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]}\alpha,

where
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
.

Proof.

We have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\Longleftrightarrow&\models_{\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]}\alpha\penalty\ \penalty\ [\text{Lindenbaum's theorem (Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:lindenbaum-theorem})}]\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]}\alpha.\penalty\ \penalty\ [\text{Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:preLindenbaum-algebra}}]\\
\end{array}

∎

Now we define a special valuation in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
:

\bm{v}_{D}:p\mapsto p/\penalty\theta(D),

for any
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proposition 6.3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic and
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
.
Then for any formula
\alpha
, the following conditions are equivalent
\,:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha;\\
(\text{b})&X\models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]}\alpha;\\
(\text{c})&\bm{v}_{D}[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}_{D}.\end{array}

In particular, the following conditions are equivalent
\,:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a}^{\ast})&\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}};\\
(\text{b}^{\ast})&v[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}},\penalty\ \textit{for any valuation $v$ in}\penalty\ \mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}};\\
(\text{c}^{\ast})&\bm{v}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}.\end{array}

Proof.

The implication (
a
)
\Rightarrow
(
b
) follows from Corollary
6.3.2.1
.

Assume (b). Let us take any
\beta\in X
. We have:
\bm{v}_{D}[\beta]=\beta/\penalty\theta(D)=\mathbf{1}_{D}
. Hence (c).

Now assume (c), that is
\bm{v}_{D}[\alpha]=\mathbf{1}_{D}
. This means that
\alpha/\penalty\theta(D)=\mathbf{1}_{D}
, that is
\alpha\in D
. Hence (a).
∎

We aim to show that the Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras are good as separating tools (Section
5.3
); or, better to say, they are useful for creating separating tools. With this in mind, given a unital, and thus assertional (Corollary
6.3.3.2
), logic
\mathcal{S}
in a language
\mathcal{L}
, we define the following classes of unital expansions. These definitions are based on the conclusion of Corollary
6.3.3.2
that all unital logics are assertional.

•

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the class of all algebraic
\mathcal{S}
-models; according to Proposition
6.1.1
,
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a quasi-variety.

•

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the quasi-variety generated by the set
\{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\penalty\ |\penalty\ D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\}
. According to Proposition
6.3.2
,

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}.

(6.5)

Also, if
\mathcal{S}
is finitary, then, in virtue of Corollary
6.3.2.1
,
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
is determined by the quasiidentities
X\approx\mathbf{1}\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}
, for any
X\cup\{\alpha\}\Subset\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
.

•

\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the class of all unital expansions
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
such that

\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle\models\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}
, for any
\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. In view of (
6.1
), the class
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a variety. This, the second part of Proposition
6.3.4
and Proposition
6.3.1
imply that

\{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\penalty\ |\penalty\ D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}},

(6.6)

which in turn implies that

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}.

(6.7)

We conclude this subsection with the following.

Proposition 6.3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic. Then the following conditions are equivalent
\,:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha;\\
(\text{b})&\text{$X\models_{\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\alpha$, for any
$\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}$};\\
(\text{c})&\text{$X\models_{\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\alpha$, for any
$\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}$};\\
(\text{d})&\text{$X\models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]}\alpha$, for any $D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}$}.\end{array}

Proof.

The implication (a)
\Rightarrow
(b) is due to definition of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
; (b)
\Rightarrow
(c) is due to inclusion
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
; (c)
\Rightarrow
(d) is due to definition of
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
; and
(d)
\Rightarrow
(a) is due to Corollary
6.3.3.2
.
∎

Thus, we see that if
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, an algebra
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
separating
X
from
\alpha

can be found among the algebras of the class

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P_{r}}\{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\}_{D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}}
; cf.
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, § 11, theorem 4. As we will see in the next subsection, under a restriction on
\mathcal{S}
, separating algebras (in the above sense) can be reached as homomorphic images of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
. In the proof of this below, the class
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
plays a key role.

Corollary 6.3.5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic. Then
\mathcal{S}
has an adequate matrix.

Proof.

First we assume that
\mathcal{S}
is nontrivial.

Basing on the equivalence (a)
\Leftrightarrow
(c) of Proposition
6.3.5
, for any pair
(X,\alpha)
with
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, we define:

\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle_{(X,\alpha)}
is such an algebra
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
that
X\not\models_{\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\alpha
.

Further, we define:

\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{S}}:=\{\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle_{(X,\alpha)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ (X,\alpha)\in\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\}.

We observe that
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\varnothing
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
Next, we denote:

\langle\overline{\textsf{{A}}},\overline{\mathbf{1}}\rangle:=\prod\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{S}}.

It is obvious that
\langle\overline{\textsf{{A}}},\overline{\mathbf{1}}\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Further,
\langle\overline{\textsf{{A}}},\overline{\mathbf{1}}\rangle
is an adequate matrix for
\mathcal{S}
. Indeed, if
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, then, since
\langle\overline{\textsf{{A}}},\overline{\mathbf{1}}\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
, in virtue of the equivalence (a)
\Leftrightarrow
(c) of Proposition
6.3.5
,
X\models_{\langle\overline{\textsf{{A}}},\overline{\mathbf{1}}\rangle}\alpha
. On the other hand, if
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
, then for some
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
X\not\models_{\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\alpha
and, hence,

X\not\models_{\langle\overline{\textsf{{A}}},\overline{\mathbf{1}}\rangle}\alpha
.

If
\mathcal{S}
is trivial, a degenerated algebra in
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
is an adequate matrix.
∎

Corollary 6.3.5.2
.

Both
Cl
and
Int
have an adequate matrix. Therefore, both are uniform, couniform and have cancellation property.

6.3.2
Implicational unital logics

We aim to show that, under above restriction on
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a free algebra over
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Definition 6.3.2

(implicational abstract logic)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital abstract logic in a language
\mathcal{L}
. Assume that
there is an
\mathcal{L}
-formula of two sentential variables,
\bm{\iota}(p,q)
, such that for any
\mathcal{L}
-formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
, any algebra
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
and any valuation
v
in
A
,

v[\alpha]=v[\beta]\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ v[\bm{\iota}(\alpha,\beta)]=\mathbf{1}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ v[\bm{\iota}(\beta,\alpha)]=\mathbf{1}.

Then we call
\mathcal{S}

implicational
with respect to
\bm{\iota}(p,q)
.

The abstract logics
P
,
Cl
and
Int
are examples of implicational logics w.r.
p\rightarrow q
. (Exercise
6.3
.
1
)
This means that if
\mathcal{S}
is one of these abstract logics, then

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta.

The last observations leads to the following proposition.

Proposition 6.3.6

(replacement property)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be one of the abstract logics
P
,
Cl
or
Int
. Assume that a formula
\beta
is a subformula of a formula
\alpha
so that a designated occurrence of
\beta
in
\alpha
is denoted by
\alpha[\dots\beta\dots]
. Then

\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta\leftrightarrow\gamma\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha[\dots\beta\dots]\leftrightarrow\alpha[\dots\gamma\dots],

where
\alpha[\dots\gamma\dots]
is the formula obtained from
\alpha[\dots\beta\dots]
by replacement of the designated occurrence of
\beta
with
\gamma
.

Proof.

To prove we notice that if, by premise,
\beta/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})=\gamma/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
, then

\alpha[\dots\beta/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})\dots]=\alpha[\dots\gamma/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})\dots]
and hence
\alpha[\dots\beta\dots]/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})=\alpha[\dots\gamma\dots]/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})
. ∎

Below we will be considering implicational logics which are also unital. Since for such a logic
\mathcal{S}
,
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\varnothing
(Proposition
6.2.3
), we select an arbitrary
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\bigstar\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

In virtue of Proposition
6.3.4
, for any valuation
v
in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
,

v(\bigstar)=\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}.

(6.8)

(Exercise
6.3
.
2
)

We recall the signature of any unital expansion
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
is of type
\mathcal{L}
expanded by an additional constant symbol
\mathbf{1}
which is interpreted by the 0-ary operation
\mathbf{1}
in
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
(denoted by
\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}
in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
). We denote this extended type by
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
.

Let
\mathbf{t}
be a term of type
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
. The
conversion

of
\mathbf{t}
is the
\mathcal{L}
-formula (or term of type
\mathcal{L}
), denoted by
\mathbf{t}^{\star}
, which is obtained from
\mathbf{t}
by simultaneous replacement of all occurrences of
\mathbf{1}
, if any, in
\mathbf{t}
by
\bigstar
.

Consequently, any valuation
v
in an algebra
A
of type
\mathcal{L}
is extended to
the valuation
v^{\star}
in
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
such that
v^{\star}(p)=v(p)
, for any
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, and
v^{\star}(\mathbf{1})=\mathbf{1}
, where the first occurrence of ‘
\mathbf{1}
’ is a 0-ary constant of
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
, and the second is the 0-ary operation
\mathbf{1}
of
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
. We refer to
v^{\star}
as a
\star
-
extension
of
v
. We note that the valuation
v
can be restored from
v^{\star}
in the sense that if
w
is any valuation in
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
, there is a unique valuation
v
in
A
such that
v^{\star}=w
. (Exercise
6.3
.
3
)

Using (
6.8
), we observe that for any
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
-terms
\mathbf{t}
and
\mathbf{r}
and any valuation
v^{\star}
in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
,

v^{\star}(\mathbf{t})=v^{\star}(\mathbf{r})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ v(\mathbf{t}^{\star})=v(\mathbf{r}^{\star}).

(6.9)

(Exercise
6.3
.
4
)

For any terms
\mathbf{t}
and
\mathbf{r}
of type
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
, we denote:

\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\models\mathbf{t}\approx\mathbf{r}\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\

for any
\left\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\right\rangle\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
\models_{\langle\textbf{A},\mathbf{1}\rangle}\mathbf{t}\approx\mathbf{r}
.

The equivalence (
6.9
) can be generalized as follows.

Lemma 6.3.2
.

Let
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Then for any term
\mathbf{t}

of type
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
and any valuation
v^{\star}
in
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
,

v^{\star}(\mathbf{t})=v(\mathbf{t}^{\star})
.

Proof
can be carried out by induction on the number of functional symbols of the language
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
. We leave it to the reader. (Exercise
6.3
.
5
)

Proposition 6.3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be nontrivial unital logic which is implicational with respect to

\bm{\iota}(p,q)
. Then for terms
\mathbf{t}
and
\mathbf{r}
of type
\mathcal{L}^{\star}
,

\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\models\mathbf{t}\approx\mathbf{r}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \bm{v}_{0}^{\star}(\mathbf{t})=\bm{v}_{0}^{\star}(\mathbf{r}),

where
\bm{v}_{0}^{\star}
is the
\star
-extension of
\bm{v}_{0}
. Hence
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a free algebra of rank
\textit{card}(\mathcal{L})
over the variety
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
and also over the quasi-variety
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

We successively obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\models\mathbf{t}\approx\mathbf{r}&\Longleftrightarrow&\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\models\mathbf{t}^{\star}\approx\mathbf{r}^{\star}\quad[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:LT=free-algebra}}]\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\models\bm{\iota}(\mathbf{t}^{\star},\mathbf{r}^{\star})\approx\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\models\bm{\iota}(\mathbf{r}^{\star},\mathbf{t}^{\star})\approx\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}\\
&&[\text{for $\mathcal{S}$ is implicational with respect to $\bm{\iota}(p,q)$}]\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\bm{\iota}(\mathbf{t}^{\star},\mathbf{r}^{\star})\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \bm{\iota}(\mathbf{r}^{\star},\mathbf{t}^{\star})\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\\
&&[\text{since $\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}$ and Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:preLindenbaum-algebra}, part 2}]\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\bm{v}_{0}(\bm{\iota}(\mathbf{t}^{\star},\mathbf{r}^{\star}))=\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \bm{v}_{0}(im(\mathbf{r}^{\star},\mathbf{t}^{\star}))=\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}\\
&&[\text{Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:preLindenbaum-algebra}, part 2}]\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\bm{v}_{0}(\mathbf{t}^{\star})=\bm{v}_{0}(\mathbf{r}^{\star})\quad[\text{for $\mathcal{S}$ is implicational with respect to $\bm{\iota}(p,q)$}]\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\bm{v}_{0}^{\star}(\mathbf{t}^{\star})=\bm{v}_{0}^{\star}(\mathbf{r}^{\star})\quad[\text{in virtue of\penalty\ \eqref{E:LT-term-valuation}}].\end{array}

According to
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, § 12.2, theorem 1, this implies that
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a free algebra over the class
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. In virtue of Proposition
6.2.1
, the rank of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
equals
\textit{card}(\mathcal{L})
.

Since the quasi-variety
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
contains all algebras
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
and because of Corollary
6.3.3.2
,
\mathcal{S}
is determined by the algebraic expansions of

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
. It is obvious that
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a free algebra over
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
∎

6.3.3
Examples of Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a nontrivial unital logic in a language
\mathcal{L}
with
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}})=\kappa
. Then, according to (
6.3
),

\textit{card}(|\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}|)\geq\kappa
. In particular,
\textit{card}(|\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}|)=\textit{card}(|\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}|)=\aleph_{0}
.
It must be clear that
\{p/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\}
is the set of free generators of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
, if the latter can be considered as a free algebra of
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
For some questions like decision problem for the consequence relation or theoremhood of
\mathcal{S}
, it suffices to consider the formulas built of a limited set of variables. Thus, if
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
with
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V})=\kappa
and our consideration of
\mathcal{S}
is limited to the formulas built from
\mathcal{V}
, we denote by
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}(\kappa)
a subalgebra of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
, generated by the set
\{p/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}\}
. The notation is justified by the fact if
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
and

\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})=\textit{card}(\mathcal{V})
, then the subalgebras of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
, generated by

\{p/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}\}
and, respectively, by

\{p/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}})\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\}
are isomorphic; cf.
[
gra79
]
, §24, corollary 2. Since each such
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}(\kappa)
is a free algebra in
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
(or even in
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
), it is called the free algebra of rank
\kappa
. We note that, since
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
is always has at least one 0-ary operation,
\mathbf{1}
, even it makes sense to talk about a free algebra of rank 0.

We consider the free algebras
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(0)
,
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(0)
,
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(1)

\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(2)
and
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
, in order. The first two algebras should be understood as subalgebras of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}
and
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}
, generated by their unit,
\mathbf{1}
. The resulting algebras are isomorphic (see Section
2.3.8
and Section
2.3.9
) and can be depicted by the diagram:

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\mathbf{1}}}

Figure 6.1:
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(0)
, or equivalently
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(0)

Before discussing the structure of the other just mentioned Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras, we first consider the varieties

\mathbb{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\textsf{Int}}
. Namely, we aim to show that these varieties coincide with the varieties of Boolean and Heyting algebras, respectively.

Since the classes
\mathbb{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\textsf{Int}}
consist of the algebras validating the theses of
Cl
and
Int
, respectively, (defined in Section
6.2.2
) we take a look at these calculi more closely.

Technically, it is convenient to have a collective name for the calculi
\textsf{P},\textsf{Int}
and
Cl
. Refereeing to any of these calculi, we sometimes use the latter
C
.

Lemma 6.3.3
.

In addition to modus ponens, the inference rules (
a
–
i
)–(
a
–
iii
) and
(
b
–
i
)–(
b
–
ii
) of Section
4.3.5
are sound with respect to
P
,
Int
and
Cl
.

Proof.

To prove the soundness of the rules (a–
i
)–(a–
iii
), we have to show that for any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
, the following hold:

\alpha,\beta\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\wedge\beta,\quad\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\vee\beta,\quad\beta\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\vee\beta,\quad\alpha\wedge\beta\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \alpha\wedge\beta\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\beta;

and then, for the soundness of the rules (b–
i
)–(b–
ii
):

\alpha\land\beta\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \alpha\land\beta\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\beta.

We leave this task to the reader. (Exercise
6.3
.
6
)
∎

The next lemma is known as the deduction theorem.

Lemma 6.3.4
.

The hyperrule (
c
–
i
) of Section
4.3.5
is sound with respect to
P
,
Int
and
Cl
.

Proof.

First of all, we notice that for any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
,

\begin{array}[]{cll}1.&\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\rightarrow(\beta\rightarrow\alpha)&[\text{an instance of ax1}]\\
2.&\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\rightarrow((\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\alpha)&[\text{an instance of ax1}]\\
3.&\vdash_{\textsf{C}}(\alpha\rightarrow(\beta\rightarrow\alpha))\rightarrow((\alpha\rightarrow((\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\alpha))\rightarrow(\alpha\rightarrow\alpha))&[\text{an instance of ax2}]\\
4.&\vdash_{\textsf{C}}(\alpha\rightarrow((\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\alpha))\rightarrow(\alpha\rightarrow\alpha)&[\text{from 1 and 3 by (b--$iii$)}]\\
5.&\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\rightarrow\alpha.&[\text{from 2 and 4 by (b--$iii$)}]\end{array}

Thus for any formula
\alpha
,

\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\rightarrow\alpha.

Now assume that
X,\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\beta
. That is, there is a
C
-derivation of
\beta
from
X,\alpha
:

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\beta.

Let us form a finite sequence:

\alpha\rightarrow\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha\rightarrow\beta

and denote:

\gamma_{i}:=\alpha\rightarrow\alpha_{i}
, where
1\leq i\leq n
; so that
\gamma_{n}:=\alpha\rightarrow\beta
. By induction on the number
n
of the formulas of
(\ast\ast)
, we prove that
\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\gamma_{n}
.

If
n=1
, then either
\beta=\alpha
or
\beta\in X
. If the former is the case, then we apply (
\ast
). If
\beta\in X
, then we successively obtain:

\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\beta\rightarrow(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)
and
X\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta
.

Next, assume that
\beta
is obtained from
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{i}\rightarrow\beta
. By induction hypothesis,

\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\rightarrow\alpha_{i}
and

\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\rightarrow(\alpha_{i}\rightarrow\beta)
. Using ax2 and
(b–
iii
) twice, we receive that
\vdash_{\textsf{C}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta
.
∎

Lemma 6.3.5
.

The hyperrules (c–
ii
) and (c–
iii
) of Section
4.3.5
are sound with respect to
Int
and
Cl
.
Hence the rule (a–
iv
) is sound with respect to
Int
and
Cl
.

Proof.

The proof of the first part is straightforward and is left to the reader. (Exercise
6.3
.
7
)

The proof of the second part is as follows. Let
\mathcal{S}
be either
Int
or
Cl
. Since
\alpha,\neg\alpha\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
and

\alpha,\neg\alpha\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\neg\alpha
, by (c-
ii
), we conclude that
\alpha\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\neg\neg\alpha
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.7.1
.

The logic
Cl
is an extension of
Int
.

Proof.

We show that
\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\text{ax11}
, that is, for any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
,

\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\beta\rightarrow(\neg\beta\rightarrow\alpha).

(6.10)

Indeed, since
\beta,\neg\beta,\neg\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\beta
and

\beta,\neg\beta,\neg\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\neg\beta
, by the hyperrrule (c–
ii
), we derive that
\beta,\neg\beta\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\neg\neg\alpha
.
Also, since, in virtue of Lemma
6.3.5
,
\neg\neg\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\alpha
, by cut, we obtain that

\beta,\neg\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\alpha
. Then, we apply twice the hyperrule
(c–
i
).
∎

Lemma 6.3.6
.

For any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, the following formulas and their converses are theses of
P
:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&(\alpha\wedge\beta)\rightarrow(\beta\wedge\alpha),\\
(\text{b})&(\alpha\wedge(\beta\wedge\gamma))\rightarrow((\alpha\wedge\beta)\wedge\gamma),\\
(\text{c})&((\alpha\wedge\beta)\wedge\gamma)\rightarrow(\alpha\wedge(\beta\wedge\gamma)),\\
(\text{d})&((\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee\beta)\rightarrow\beta,\\
(\text{e})&(\alpha\vee\beta)\rightarrow(\beta\vee\alpha),\\
(\text{f})&(\alpha\vee(\beta\vee\gamma))\rightarrow((\alpha\vee\beta)\vee\gamma),\\
(\text{g})&((\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee\beta)\rightarrow\beta,\\
(\text{h})&\alpha\wedge(\alpha\vee\beta)\rightarrow\alpha,\\
(\text{j})&(\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma))\rightarrow((\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)),\\
(\text{i})&(\alpha\vee(\beta\wedge\gamma))\rightarrow((\alpha\vee\beta)\wedge(\alpha\vee\gamma)).\end{array}

Proof.

We show about one of these formulas, namely (j), that it and its converse are theses of
P
. First, we prove that

\vdash_{\textsf{P}}(\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma))\rightarrow((\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)).

We obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rll}1a.&\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma)\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (b--$i$)}]\\
2a.&\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma)\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\beta\vee\gamma&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (b--$ii$)}]\\
3a.&\alpha,\beta\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha\wedge\beta&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (a--$i$)}]\\
4a.&\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma),\beta\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha\wedge\beta&[\text{from 1$a$ and 3$a$ by cut (Section\penalty\ \ref{section:consequence-relation})}]\\
5a.&\alpha\wedge\beta\vdash_{\textsf{P}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (a--$ii$)}]\\
6a.&\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma),\beta\vdash_{\textsf{P}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)&[\text{from 4$a$ and 5$a$ by cut}]\\
7a.&\alpha,\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha\wedge\gamma&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (a--$i$)}]\\
8a.&\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma),\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha\wedge\gamma&[\text{from 1$a$ and 7$a$ by cut}]\\
9a.&\alpha\wedge\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (a--$iii$)}]\\
10a.&\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma),\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)&[\text{from 8$a$ and 9$a$ by cut}]\\
11a.&\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma),\beta\vee\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)&[\text{from 6$a$ and 10$a$ by hyperrule (c--$iii$)}]\\
12a.&\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma)\vdash_{\textsf{P}}(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)&[\text{from 2$a$ and 11$a$ by cut}]\\
13a.&\vdash_{\textsf{P}}(\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma))\rightarrow((\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)).&[\text{from 12$a$ by hyperrule (c--$i$)}]\end{array}

Further, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rll}1b.&\alpha\wedge\beta\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (b--$i$)}]\\
2b.&\alpha\wedge\beta\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\beta&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (b--$ii$)}]\\
3b.&\beta\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\beta\vee\gamma&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (a--$ii$)}]\\
4b.&\alpha\wedge\beta\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\beta\vee\gamma&[\text{from 2$b$ and 3$b$ by cut}]\\
5b.&\alpha\wedge\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (b--$i$)}]\\
6b.&\alpha\wedge\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\gamma&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (b--$ii$)}]\\
7b.&\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\beta\vee\gamma&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (a--$iii$)}]\\
8b.&\alpha\wedge\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\beta\vee\gamma&[\text{from 6$b$ and 7$b$ by cut}]\\
9b.&(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\beta\vee\gamma&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:hyperrules-c_ii-c_iii} and hyperrule (c--$iii$)}]\\
10b.&(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:hyperrules-c_ii-c_iii} and hyperrule (c--$iii$)}]\\
11b.&\alpha,\beta\vee\gamma\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma)&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl} and rule (a--$i$)}]\\
12b.&(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma),\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma)&[\text{from 9$b$ and 11$b$ by cut}]\\
13b.&(\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma)\vdash_{\textsf{P}}\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma)&[\text{from 10$b$ and 12$b$ by cut}]\\
14b.&\vdash_{\textsf{P}}((\alpha\wedge\beta)\vee(\alpha\wedge\gamma))\rightarrow(\alpha\wedge(\beta\vee\gamma)).&\text{from 13$b$ by hyperrule (c--$i$)}\end{array}

We leave for the reader to complete the proof of this lemma. (Exercise
6.3
.
8
)
∎

Lemma 6.3.7
.

For any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
, the following formulas and their converses are theses of
Cl
:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{k})&((\alpha\wedge\neg\alpha)\vee\beta)\rightarrow\beta,\\
(\text{l})&((\alpha\vee\neg\alpha)\wedge\beta)\rightarrow\beta.\end{array}

Proof.

We prove that (k) and its converse are theses of
Cl
and leave the rest to the reader. (Exercise
6.3
.
9
)

First, we prove that

\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}((\alpha\wedge\neg\alpha)\vee\beta)\rightarrow\beta.

Indeed, we have:

\begin{array}[]{cll}1a.&\alpha\wedge\neg\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\beta&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl}, rules (b-$i$), (b--$ii$), \eqref{E:Int-axiom-valid-in-Cl} and cut}]\\
2a.&\beta\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\beta\\
3a.&(\alpha\wedge\neg\alpha)\vee\beta\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\beta&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:hyperrules-c_ii-c_iii} and hyperule (c--$iii$)}]\\
4a.&\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}((\alpha\wedge\neg\alpha)\vee\beta)\rightarrow\beta.&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:hyperrules-c_ii-c_iii} and hyperule (c--$i$)}]\end{array}

For the converse, we have:

\begin{array}[]{cll}1b.&\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\beta\rightarrow((\alpha\wedge\neg\alpha)\vee\beta).&[\text{rule ax7}]\end{array}

∎

Proposition 6.3.8
.

\mathbb{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
coincides with the class of all Boolean algebras.

Proof.

Let
\textsf{{B}}=\left\langle\textsf{B};\wedge,\vee,\neg,\mathbf{1}\right\rangle
be a Boolean algebra. Expanding this algebra with an operation

x\rightarrow y:=\neg x\vee y,

according to Proposition
2.3.23
, we obtain a Heyting algebra
\textsf{{H}}=\left\langle\textsf{B};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\right\rangle
. In virtue of
Proposition
2.3.24
, the rules ax1–ax9 are valid in
H
and hence in
B
. In view of Corollary
2.3.23.1
, the rule ax10 is also valid in
B
. And, because of the property
(\text{h}_{2})

(Section
2.3.9
), modus ponens preserves this validity over any
Cl
-derivation.

Now, assume that an algebra
\textsf{{A}}=\left\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\neg,\mathbf{1}\right\rangle\in\mathbb{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
. That is, for any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
,

\textsf{{A}}\models\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}
. This means that for any
\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
and any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-valuation
v
in
A
,
v(\alpha)=\mathbf{1}
. Therefore, in virtue of Lemma
6.3.6
and the fact that
P
is implicational w.r.t
\rightarrow
, the properties
(\text{l}_{1})
–
(\text{l}_{4})
are valid in
A
. Namely, we have:
(a) and its converse prove the validity of (
\text{l}_{1}
–
i
); (b) and its converse prove the validity of (
\text{l}_{1}
–
ii
); (c) and its converse prove the validity of (
\text{l}_{2}
–
i
);
(f) and its converse prove the validity of (
\text{l}_{2}
–
ii
);
(g) and its converse prove the validity of (
\text{l}_{3}
–
i
);
(h) and its converse prove the validity of (
\text{l}_{3}
–
ii
);
(j) and its converse prove the validity of (
\text{l}_{4}
–
i
);
(i) and its converse prove the validity of (
\text{l}_{4}
–
ii
).
Thus
\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee\rangle
is a distributive lattice.

Further, if
\bigstar
is an arbitrary
Cl
-thesis and
p
is an arbitrary variable, then

\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}p\rightarrow\bigstar.

(6.11)

This implies that for any element
x\in\textsf{A}
,
x\leq\mathbf{1}
, that is
the equalities
(\text{b}_{1})
hold in
\langle\textsf{A};\wedge,\vee,\mathbf{1}\rangle
,
where
\mathbf{1}
turns to be a greatest element in the sense of
\leq
(Section
2.3.8
).

Finally, we use Lemma
6.3.7
and the fact that
Cl
is and implicational abstract logic with respect to
\rightarrow
to conclude that
A
is a Boolean algebra.
∎

Corollary 6.3.8.1
.

\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}
is a free Boolean algebra of rank
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}})
.
Hence,
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(\kappa)
, where
\kappa\leq\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}})
, is a free Boolean algebras of rank
\kappa
.

According to Corollary
6.3.8.1
and Proposition
2.3.21
, any
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(\kappa)
of a finite rank
\kappa
is finite. Let us take
\kappa=1
, assuming that
\mathcal{V}=\{p\}
. For simplicity, we denote

[p]:=p/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}).

It is not difficult to show that the subalgebra of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}
generated by
[p]
consists of the four pairwise distinct elements:
[p]
,
[\neg p]
,
[p\vee\neg p]
and
[p\wedge\neg p]
. (Exercise
6.3.3
.
10
)This subalgebra is
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(1)
. The relation
\leq
between
[\alpha]
and
[\beta]
of this algebra is defined, according to (
2.8
), as follows:

[\alpha]\leq[\beta]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}[\alpha]\wedge[\beta]=[\alpha].

Thus, we arrive at the following diagram:

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle[p]}}

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle[\neg p]}}

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\mathbf{1}=[p\vee\neg p]}}

{{\displaystyle\mathbf{0}=[p\wedge\neg p]}}

Figure 6.2:
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(1)

If
\mathcal{V}=\{p,q\}
, we obtain
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(2)
as a subalgebra of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}
generated by the classes
[p]
and
[q]
. If we identify any congruence class of this subalgebra with its representative, we can depict
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(2)
by the following diagram:

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle p}}

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle p\land q}}

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle(p\land q)\lor(\neg p\land\neg q)}}

{{\displaystyle\bullet}}

{{\displaystyle\neg q}}

Figure 6.3:
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}(2)

Now we turn to Heyting algebras.

Lemma 6.3.8
.

Let
\bigstar
be an arbitrary
Int
-thesis. For any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, the following implications and their converses are
Int
-theses:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&(\alpha\wedge(\alpha\rightarrow\beta))\rightarrow(\alpha\wedge\beta),\\
(\text{b})&((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\wedge\beta)\rightarrow\beta,\\
(\text{c})&((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\wedge(\alpha\rightarrow\gamma)\rightarrow(\alpha\rightarrow(\beta\wedge\gamma))\\
(\text{d})&(\alpha\wedge(\beta\rightarrow\beta))\rightarrow\alpha,\\
(\text{e})&(\neg\bigstar\vee\alpha)\rightarrow\alpha,\\
(\text{f})&\neg\alpha\rightarrow(\alpha\rightarrow\neg\bigstar).\end{array}

Proof.

We prove that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\neg\bigstar\vee\alpha)\rightarrow\alpha

and
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\rightarrow(\neg\bigstar\vee\alpha)
and leave for the reader to complete this proof. (Exercise
6.3
.
11
)

Indeed, we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{cll}1.&\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\bigstar&[\text{by premise $\bigstar$ is an {{Int}}-thesis}]\\
2.&\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\bigstar\rightarrow(\neg\bigstar\rightarrow\alpha)&[\text{rule ax11}]\\
3.&\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\neg\bigstar\rightarrow\alpha&[\text{from $1$ and $2$ by modus ponens]}\\
4.&\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\rightarrow\alpha&[\text{because of $(\ast)$ in the proof of Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:deduction-theorem}}]\\
5.&\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\neg\bigstar\vee\alpha)\rightarrow\alpha.&[\text{from ax8, $3$ and $4$ by modus ponens}]\end{array}

Further,
\alpha\rightarrow(\neg\bigstar\vee\alpha)
is an instantiation of ax7.
∎

Proposition 6.3.9
.

\mathbb{T}_{\textsf{Int}}
coincides with the class of all Heyting algebras.

Proof.

Let
\textsf{{H}}=\langle\textsf{H};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle

be a Heyting algebra. In virtue of
Proposition
2.3.24
, the rules ax1–ax9 are valid in
H
.
In virtue of the properties (
2.16
), (
2.17
) and (
2.18
), we receive that ax11 is also valid in
H
. Noticing, that modus ponens preserves validity in any Heyting algebra, we conclude that if
\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}}
, then
\textsf{{H}}\models\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}
.

Next, assume that an algebra
\textsf{{H}}=\langle\textsf{H};\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle\in\mathbb{T}_{\textsf{Int}}
. That is, for any
\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}}
and any
valuation
v
in
H
,
v(\alpha)=\mathbf{1}
. We show that the properties

(\text{l}_{1})
–
(\text{l}_{4})
,
(\text{b}_{1})
, and
(\text{h}_{1})
–
(\text{h}_{6})
are valid in
H
.

In virtue of Lemma
6.3.6
and the fact that
P
is implicational w.r.t
\rightarrow
, the properties
(\text{l}_{1})
–
(\text{l}_{4})
are valid in
H
. Thus
\langle\textsf{H};\wedge,\vee\rangle
is a distributive lattice.

Further, similarly to (
6.11
), we derive that

\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p\rightarrow\bigstar,

for any variable
p
and
Int
-thesis
\bigstar
. This implies that the equalities
(\text{b}_{1})
are true in
H
.

To prove the properties
(\text{h}_{1})
–
(\text{h}_{6})
, we apply Lemma
6.3.8
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.9.1
.

\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}
is a free Heyting algebra of rank
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}})
.
Hence,
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(\kappa)
, where
\kappa\leq\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}})
, is a free Heyting algebras of rank
\kappa
.

Below we give a description of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
. For this, we consider the following formulas of an arbitrary variable
p
.

First, we define:

p^{0}:=p\land\neg p,\qquad p^{1}:=\neg p,\qquad p^{2}:=p,\qquad p^{\omega}:=p\to p.

Then for any
n\geq 0
, we define:

\begin{array}[]{ll}i)\quad p^{2n+3}:=p^{2n+1}\to p^{2n}&ii)\quad p^{2n+4}:=p^{2n+1}\lor p^{2n+2}.\end{array}

(6.12)

Thus ‘
m
’ in ‘
p^{m}
’ can take the value of any ordinal less than or equal to the first infinite ordinal, symbolically
m\leq\omega
.

We conclude with the following observation, due to I. Nishimura
[
Nishimura, 1960
]
; see also
[
Rieger, 1957
]
.

A description of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
can be outlined in terms of the following relations on the set of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}
-formulas.

\begin{array}[]{cl}\bullet&\alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta;\\
\bullet&\alpha\sim\beta\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\beta\rightarrow\alpha;\\
\bullet&\alpha\prec\beta\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\penalty\ \text{and not}\penalty\ \alpha\sim\beta;\\
\end{array}

(6.13)

It should be clear that

\alpha\sim\beta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}})=\beta/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}});

and, since

\alpha\sim\beta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \beta\preccurlyeq\alpha,

\alpha/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}})\leq\beta/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}})\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\preccurlyeq\beta,

where
\leq
is the (ordinary) partial order in the Heyting algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}
(Corollary
6.3.9.1
). Below we treat
\sim
as a congruence on
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}
.

The above equivalences will be used without reference. In particular, in the description of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
as a diagram below, we depict each congruence class by its representative. Thus if a formula
\alpha
can be connected by an upward path with a formula
\beta
, which means that
\alpha/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}})\leq\beta/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}})
, then
\alpha\preccurlyeq\beta
. Also,
\alpha/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}})<\beta/\penalty\theta(\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}})
if, and only if,
\alpha\prec\beta
.

In accordance with this agreement, we have:

\mathbf{0}:=p^{0}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{1}:=p^{\omega}.

It should be clear that for any
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}}
-formula
\alpha
,

\mathbf{0}\preccurlyeq\alpha\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \alpha\preccurlyeq\mathbf{1}.

Thus the elements
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{1}
are a least and greatest elements, respectively, of any Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra of
Int
and hence are those of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
.

Proposition 6.3.10
.

Every
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula of one variable
p
is equivalent in
Int
to one and only one formula
p^{i}
, which are not equivalent to each other
(
in the sense of
\sim
)
and related to each other
(
in the sense of
\preccurlyeq
)
as shown in the diagram of Figure
6.4
.
