Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)

We call the above diagram
Rieger-Nishimura algebra
; other names in the literature are
Rieger-Nishimura ladder
and
Rieger-Nishimura lattice
.
The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
6.3.10
.

6.3.4
Rieger-Nishimura algebra

In the proofs of propositions and lemmas below in this subsection we use Proposition
6.3.6
and transitivity of
\sim
without reference.
Also, we use rules of (a) and (b) and the hyperrules of (c) (Section
4.3.5
), as needed. The applications of those rules are guaranteed by Lemma
6.3.3
, Lemma
6.3.4
and Lemma
6.3.5
.

At one point or another, we will need to use the
Int
-theses that we have collected in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3.9
.

For arbitrary
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, the following properties hold:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&(\alpha\rightarrow\neg\beta)\sim\neg(\alpha\land\beta),\\
(\text{b})&(\neg\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\preccurlyeq\neg\neg(\alpha\lor\beta),\\
(\text{c})&(\alpha\rightarrow(\beta\rightarrow\gamma))\sim((\alpha\land\beta)\rightarrow\gamma),\\
(\text{d})&((\alpha\lor\beta)\rightarrow\gamma)\sim((\alpha\rightarrow\gamma)\land(\beta\rightarrow\gamma)),\\
(\text{e})&(\alpha\rightarrow(\beta\land\gamma))\sim((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\land(\alpha\rightarrow\gamma)),\\
(\text{f})&(\alpha\rightarrow\neg\alpha)\sim\neg\alpha,\\
(\text{g})&(\neg\alpha\rightarrow\alpha)\sim\neg\neg\alpha,\\
(\text{h})&(\neg\alpha\rightarrow\neg\neg\alpha)\sim\neg\neg\alpha,\\
(\text{i})&(\neg\neg\alpha\rightarrow\neg\alpha)\sim\neg\alpha,\\
(\text{j})&\neg\alpha\sim\alpha\rightarrow(p\land\neg p),\\
(\text{k})&\neg\neg\neg\alpha\sim\neg\alpha;\\
(\text{l})&(\neg\alpha\land\alpha)\sim p^{0};\\
(\text{m})&((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\land(\beta\rightarrow\gamma))\rightarrow(\alpha\rightarrow\gamma);\\
(\text{n})&\alpha\land(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\sim\alpha\land\beta.\end{array}

Proof.

We prove (a) and leave the rest to the reader as an exercise. (Exercise
6.3
.
12
)

With the help of rules (b–
i
) (b–
iii
) and the definition of
Int
, we obtain:

\alpha\rightarrow\neg\beta,\alpha\land\beta\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha;\penalty\ \alpha\rightarrow\neg\beta,\alpha\land\beta\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\beta;\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \alpha\rightarrow\neg\beta,\alpha\land\beta,\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\neg\beta.

Hence we have:

\alpha\rightarrow\neg\beta,\alpha\land\beta\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\beta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \alpha\rightarrow\neg\beta,\alpha\land\beta\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\neg\beta.

By hyperrules (c–
ii
) and (c–
i
), we successively derive:

\alpha\rightarrow\neg\beta\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\neg(\alpha\land\beta)\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\alpha\rightarrow\neg\beta)\rightarrow\neg(\alpha\land\beta).

Conversely, applying rule (a–
i
), we derive:

\neg(\alpha\land\beta),\alpha,\beta\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\land\beta.

Hence, by hyperrule (c–
ii
),

\neg(\alpha\land\beta),\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\neg\beta.

Applying hyperrule (c–
i
) twice, we conclude that

\neg(\alpha\land\beta)\rightarrow(\alpha\rightarrow\neg\beta)
is an
Int
-thesis.

Then, it remains to apply rule (a–
i
).
∎

Further, we add to the relations (
6.13
) one more, namely we define:

\alpha\ll\beta\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\beta.

It should be clear that Proposition
6.3.10
will be proven when we prove Corollary
6.3.11.1
and Corollary
6.3.13.1
below. These corollaries follow from Proposition
6.3.11
and, respectively, Proposition
6.3.13
. Thus, our argument consists of two almost separate paths. Both paths, however, pass through Lemma
6.3.11
which is about properties of the relations
\preccurlyeq
,
\sim
and
\ll
for concrete formulas, or say it better, for concrete elements of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
. But we start with general properties of these relations.

Lemma 6.3.10
.

For arbitrary
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, the following conditionals and equivalences hold:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&(\alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \beta\preccurlyeq\gamma)\Longrightarrow\alpha\preccurlyeq\gamma;\\
(\text{b})&\alpha\ll\beta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\land((\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\alpha);\\
(\text{c})&(\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\sim\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\alpha;\\
(\text{d})&\alpha\ll\beta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \beta\rightarrow\alpha\sim\alpha;\\
(\text{e})&((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\sim\beta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \beta\preccurlyeq\gamma)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\rightarrow\gamma\sim\gamma;\\
(\text{f})&(\alpha\ll\beta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \beta\preccurlyeq\gamma)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\ll\gamma;\\
(\text{g})&(\alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \beta\ll\gamma)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\ll\gamma;\\
(\text{h})&(\alpha\sim\beta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \beta\sim\gamma)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\sim\gamma;\\
(\text{i})&\alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\land\beta\sim\alpha;\\
(\text{j})&\alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\lor\beta\sim\beta;\\
(\text{k})&\alpha\lor\beta\preccurlyeq\gamma\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\preccurlyeq\gamma\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \beta\preccurlyeq\gamma;\\
(\text{l})&\alpha\land\beta\preccurlyeq\gamma\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\rightarrow\gamma.\end{array}

Proof.

We prove (d) and leave other properties for the reader to prove as exercises. (Exercise
6.3
.
13
)

Suppose that
\alpha\ll\beta
, that is,
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\beta
. This means that there is an
Int
-derivation without any premise:

\ldots,\penalty\ (\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\beta,

which can be continued as an
Int
-derivation with the premise
\beta\rightarrow\alpha
:

\underbrace{\ldots,\penalty\ (\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\beta}_{\text{derivation (d--$\ast$)}},\penalty\ \underbrace{\beta\rightarrow\alpha}_{\text{premise}},\beta,\alpha.

Thus, we have:
\beta\rightarrow\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha
. Applying
hyperrule (c–
i
), we obtain that
\beta\rightarrow\alpha\preccurlyeq\alpha
.
On the other hand, it is obvious that
\alpha\preccurlyeq\beta\rightarrow\alpha
. Applying rule (a–
i
), we get the equivalence

\beta\rightarrow\alpha\sim\alpha
. The latter allows us to replace
\beta\rightarrow\alpha
with
\alpha
in
(\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\beta
and obtain that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta
, that is
\alpha\preccurlyeq\beta
.

Conversely, assume that
\alpha\preccurlyeq\beta
and
\beta\rightarrow\alpha\sim\alpha
. Applying rule (b–
ii
) to the second assumption and then (b–
iii
)to the result, we get that
\beta\rightarrow\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha
. Hence, along with the first assumption, we have:
\beta\rightarrow\alpha\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\beta
.
Applying hyperrule (c–
i
), we get
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\beta
, that is
\alpha\ll\beta
.
∎

Before moving on to the next lemma, recall the following relation, namely,

p^{0}\preccurlyeq p^{n},

which will be used without reference.

Lemma 6.3.11
.

For any
n,i<\omega
, the following properties hold:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&p^{2n}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+i};\\
(\text{b})&p^{2n+1}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+4+i};\\
(\text{c})&p^{n}\preccurlyeq p^{n+3+i};\\
(\text{d})&p^{2n+1}\lor p^{2n+3}\sim p^{2n+6};\\
(\text{e})&p^{2n+1}\land p^{2n+3}\sim p^{2n};\\
(\text{f})&p^{2n+1}\land p^{2n+2}\sim p^{2n};\\
(\text{g})&p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n+3}\sim p^{2n+3};\\
(\text{h})&p^{2n+3}\rightarrow p^{2n+1}\sim p^{2n+1};\\
(\text{i})&p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n+2}\sim p^{2n+3};\\
(\text{j})&p^{2n+2}\rightarrow p^{2n+1}\sim p^{2n+1};\\
(\text{k})&p^{2n+2}\rightarrow p^{2n}\sim p^{2n+1};\\
(\text{l})&p^{k}\ll p^{n},\penalty\ \text{providing that $k,n<\omega$ and $n-k\geq 4$};\\
(\text{m})&(p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{n})\rightarrow p^{n}\sim p^{2n+1}.\\
\end{array}

Proof.

We prove successively each statement of (a)–(m).

Proof of
(a). We observe that for any
n\geq 0
,

p^{2n}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+2}

Indeed, it is obvious if
n=0
.
Now, assuming that
n=l+1
, we have
p^{2(l+1)+2}=p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+2}
.

Now we observe that for any
n\geq 0
,

p^{2n+1}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+5}

Indeed, we successively obtain the following:

\begin{array}[]{cll}(\text{a})&p^{2n+1},\penalty\ p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n}&[\text{by rule (b-$iii$)}]\\
(\text{b})&p^{2n+1},\penalty\ p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+2}&[\text{using (e--$\ast$)}]\\
(\text{c})&p^{2n+1},\penalty\ p^{2n+3}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+2}&[\text{by definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$i$)}]\\
(\text{d})&p^{2n+1}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+3}\rightarrow p^{2n+2}&[\text{by hyperrule (c--$i$)}]\\
(\text{e})&p^{2n+1}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+5}&[\text{by definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$i$)}]\\
(\text{f})&\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n+5}&[\text{by hyperrule (c--$i$)}].\end{array}

Next we observe that for any
n\geq 0
,

p^{2n}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+1}.

Omitting the obvious case when
n=0
, we notice that, when
n=1
,
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p\rightarrow p^{3}
, since
p^{3}\sim\neg\neg p
. Now, assuming that
n=l+2
, we, on the one hand, have:
p^{2n}=p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+2}
and, on the other hand,
p^{2n+1}=p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2l+2}
. It must be obvious that
p^{2l+2}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+3}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)}
. On the other hand, in the light of the rule (b–
iii
),
p^{2l+1},\penalty\ p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2l}
and hence, in virtue of (a–
\ast\ast\ast
),
p^{2l+1},\penalty\ p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2l+2}
. Applying the hyperrule (c–
i
), we obtain that
p^{2l+1}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+3}\rightarrow p^{2l+2}
. Then, we apply Lemma
6.3.10
–k.

Now, we begin the proof of (a) by induction on
i
. The case when
i=0
is obvious. The case when
i=1
follows from (a–
\ast\ast\ast
) and the case when
i=2
follows from (a–
\ast\ast
).

By induction hypothesis, assume that

p^{2n}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+k},

for any
k
with
0\leq k<i
, where
i\geq 2
. Thus
0\leq k\leq i-2
.

We consider two cases:
2n+(i-1)
is even and
2n+(i-1)
is odd.

If
2n+(i-1)
is even, then, in virtue of (a–
\ast\ast\ast
),

p^{2n+(i-1)}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+i}
and hence, according Lemma
6.3.10
–k,
p^{2n}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+i}
.

Now let
2n+(i-1)=2l+1
. This implies that
2n+i=2l+2
. By induction hypothesis,
p^{2n}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+(i-2)}=p^{2l}
. Applying (a–
\ast
), we receive that
p^{2l}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+2}=p^{2n+i}
.
Then, we apply Lemma
6.3.10
–k.

Proof of
(b). Since
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
is a Heyting algebra (Corollary
6.3.9.1
) and by definition (
6.12
–
ii
),
p^{2n+1}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+1}\lor p^{2n+2}=p^{2n+4}
. Further, in virtue of (a),

p^{2n+4}=2^{2(n+2)}\preccurlyeq 2^{2(n+2)+i}
. Then, we apply Lemma
6.3.10
–a.

Proof of
(c). Considering two cases, whether
n
is even or odd, we get:
p^{2l}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+3+i}
, by (a), and
p^{2l+1}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+1+3+i}
, by (b).

Proof of
(d). We successively obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rl}p^{2n+6}=p^{2(n+1)+4}=p^{2(n+1)+1}\lor p^{2^{2(n+1)+2}}&[\text{definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$ii$)}]\\
=p^{2n+3}\lor p^{2n+4}=p^{2n+3}\lor(p^{2n+1}\lor p^{2n+2})&[\text{definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$ii$)}]\\
\sim p^{2n+1}\lor(p^{2n+2}\lor p^{2n+3})&[\text{associative law for $\lor$ in $\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)$}]\\
\sim p^{2n+1}\lor p^{2n+3}&[\text{by (a)}].\end{array}

Proof of
(e). Since
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
is a Heyting algebra (Corollary
6.3.9.1
) and by definition, we have:
p^{2n+1}\land p^{2n+3}\sim p^{2n+1}\land p^{2n}
. Then, we apply (a–
\ast\ast\ast
).

Proof of
(f). First, we notice that, in view of (a),

p^{2n}\preccurlyeq p^{2n+1}\land p^{2n+2}.

Then, in virtue of Lemma
6.3.9
–c,

(p^{2n+1}\land p^{2n+2})\rightarrow p^{2n}\sim p^{2n+2}\rightarrow(p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n})\sim p^{2n+2}\rightarrow p^{2n+3}.

Applying (a), we conclude that
p^{2n+1}\land p^{2n+2}\preccurlyeq p^{2n}
.

Proof of
(g). Using definition (
6.12
–
i
) and Lemma
6.3.9
–c, we have:

p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n+3}=p^{2n+1}\rightarrow(p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n})\sim(p^{2n+1}\land p^{2n+1})\rightarrow p^{2n}\sim(p^{2n+1})\rightarrow p^{2n}=p^{2n+3}.

Proof of
(h). If
n=0
, according to Lemma
6.3.9
–i, we have:

p^{3}\rightarrow p^{1}\sim\neg\neg p\rightarrow\neg p\sim\neg p=p^{1}.

And for any
n=l+1
, applying Lemma
6.3.9
–c, (a–
\ast\ast
) and definition (
6.12
–
i
),
we have:

\begin{array}[]{r}p^{2(l+1)+3}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)+1}\sim p^{2l+5}\rightarrow(p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l})\sim(p^{2l+5}\land p^{2l+1})\rightarrow p^{2l}\\
\sim p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l}\sim p^{2l+3}.\end{array}

Proof of
(i). On the one hand, in virtue of (
6.12
–
i
), (a–
\ast
) and Lemma
6.3.9
–m, we have

p^{2n+3}\sim(p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n})\land(p^{2n}\rightarrow p^{2n+2})\preccurlyeq p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n+2}.

On the other hand, in virtue of Lemma
6.3.9
–n and (f), we obtain:

p^{2n+1}\land(p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n+2})\sim p^{2n+1}\land p^{2n+2}\sim p^{2n}.

In particular,

(p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n+2})\land p^{2n+1}\preccurlyeq p^{2n},

which, according to Lemma
6.3.10
–l, is equivalent to

p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n+2}\preccurlyeq\underbrace{p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n}}_{p^{2n+3}}.

Proof of
(j). If
n=0
, then, using Lemma
6.3.9
–f, we have:

p^{2}\rightarrow p^{1}\sim\neg p=p^{1}.

Now let
n=l+1
. Then we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rl}p^{2l+4}\rightarrow p^{2l+3}=(p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+2})\rightarrow p^{2l+3}&[\text{by definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$ii$)}]\\
\sim(p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l+3})\land(p^{2l+2}\rightarrow p^{2l+3})&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:Int-theses}--d}]\\
\sim p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l+3}&[\text{by (a--$\ast\ast\ast$)}]\\
\sim p^{2l+1}\rightarrow(p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l})&[\text{by definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$i$)}]\\
\sim p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l}&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:Int-theses}--c}]\\
\sim p^{2l+3}=p^{2n+1}&[\text{definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$i$)}].\end{array}

Proof of
(k). If
n=0
, according to Lemma
6.3.9
–j, we have:

p^{2}\rightarrow p^{0}\sim\neg p=p^{1}.

Now assume that
n=l+1
. Then we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rl}p^{2l+4}\rightarrow p^{2l+2}\sim(p^{2l+2}\lor p^{2l+1})\rightarrow p^{2l+2}&[\text{definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$ii$)}]\\
\sim(p^{2l+2}\rightarrow p^{2l+2})\land(p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l+2})&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:Int-theses}--d}]\\
\sim p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l+2}&[\text{($\ast$) in the proof of Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:deduction-theorem}}]\\
\sim p^{2l+3}&[\text{by (i)}].\end{array}

Proof of
(l). We distinguish two cases:
k
is even and
k
is odd.

Suppose
k=2l
; then
n=2l+4+i
. We notice that, on the one hand, according to (a),
p^{2l}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+2}
. On the other hand, we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rl}p^{2l+4}\rightarrow p^{2l}=(p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+2})\rightarrow p^{2l}&[\text{by definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$ii$)}]\\
\sim(p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l})\land(p^{2l+2}\rightarrow p^{2l})&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:Int-theses}--d}]\\
=p^{2l+3}\land(p^{2l+2}\rightarrow p^{2l})&[\text{by definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$ii$)}]\\
\sim p^{2l+3}\land p^{2l+1}&[\text{by (k)}]\\
\sim p^{2l}&[\text{by (e)}].\end{array}

Applying Lemma
6.3.10
–d, we receive that
p^{2l}\ll p^{2l+4}
. And since, in virtue of (a),
p^{2l+4}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+4+i}
, we, applying Lemma
6.3.10
–f, derive that
p^{2l}\ll p^{2l+4+i}
.

Now assume that
k=2l+1
; then
n=2l+5+i
. First, we prove that
p^{2l+1}\ll p^{2l+4}
.

Indeed, on the one hand, in virtue of definition (
6.12
–
ii
),
p^{2l+1}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+2}=p^{2l+4}
. On the other hand, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}p^{2l+4}\rightarrow p^{2l+1}=(p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+2})\rightarrow p^{2l+1}&[\text{by definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$ii$)}]\\
\sim(p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l+1})\land(p^{2l+2}\rightarrow p^{2l+1})&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:Int-theses}--d}]\\
\sim p^{2l+2}\rightarrow p^{2l+1}\sim p^{2l+1}&[\text{by (j)}].\end{array}

Then, applying Lemma
6.3.10
–d, we get that

p^{2l+1}\ll p^{2l+4}
.

Now, since, in virtue of (a),
p^{2l+4}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+4+i}
, according to
Lemma
6.3.10
–f, we conclude that

p^{2l+1}\ll p^{2l+4+i}
, in particular,
p^{2l+1}\ll p^{2l+5+i}
.

Proof of
(m). If
n=0
, then we have:

(p^{1}\rightarrow p^{0})\rightarrow p^{0}\sim\neg\neg\neg p\sim\neg p

(Lemma
6.3.9
, (g) twice and (h)).

Next we take
n=1
. Then we have:

(p^{3}\rightarrow p^{2})\rightarrow p^{2}\sim(\neg\neg p\rightarrow p)\rightarrow p.

First, we prove that

(\neg\neg p\rightarrow p)\rightarrow p\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\neg\neg p

and, then, it will remain to apply the hyperrule (c–
i
) (Lemma
6.3.4
).

We have:

\begin{array}[]{cl}1.&(\neg\neg p\rightarrow p)\rightarrow p\quad[\text{premise}]\\
2.&\neg p\quad[\text{assumption}]\\
3.&\neg p\rightarrow(\neg\neg p\rightarrow p)\quad[\text{an instance of ax11 (Section\penalty\ \ref{section:modus-rules})}]\\
4.&\neg\neg p\rightarrow p\quad[\text{from 2 and 3 by (b--$iii$)}]\\
5.&p\quad[\text{from 1 and 4 by (b--$iii$)}].\end{array}

Then, we apply (c–
ii
) to obtain (m–
\ast
) (Lemma
6.3.5
).

On the other hand, applying the rule (b–
iii
) and hyperrule (c–
i
), we derive:

\neg\neg p\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\neg\neg p\rightarrow p)\rightarrow p.

Next we take
n=l+2
and prove that

(\underbrace{p^{2(l+2)+1}}_{p^{2(l+1)+3}}\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)}\sim p^{2(l+2)+1},

that is,

((p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)}\sim p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)}.

First, we prove that

((p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)},p^{2(l+1)+1}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2(l+1)}

and then apply the hyperrule (c–
i
).

To prove (m–
\ast\ast
) we first obtain:

\begin{array}[]{cl}1.&((p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)}\quad[\text{premise}]\\
2.&p^{2(l+1)+1}\quad[\text{premise}]\\
3.&p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)}\quad[\text{assumption}]\\
4.&p^{2(l+1)}\quad[\text{from 2 and 3 by (b--$iii$)}]\\
5.&\underbrace{p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2(l+1)}}_{p^{2(l+2)}}\quad[\text{from 3 by (a--$iii$); see (Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:rules-sound-wrt-P-Int-Cl})}].\\
\end{array}

Applying the hyperrule (c–
i
), we obtain:

((p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)},p^{2(l+1)+1}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)}

and hence

((p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+2)},\underbrace{p^{2(l+1)+1}}_{p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l}}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\underbrace{p^{2(l+2)}}_{p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2(l+1)}}.

We notice that
p^{2l+1},p^{2(l+1)+1}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2l}
and hence, in virtue of (a),

p^{2l+1},p^{2(l+1)+1}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2(l+1)}.

On the other hand,
p^{2(l+1)}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2(l+1)}
. Applying the hyperrule (c–
iii
), we conclude that

p^{2(l+1)+1},p^{2(l+2)}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2(l+1)}.

Using this and (m–
\ast\ast
), we obtain (m–
\ast\ast\ast
).

The converse, that is

p^{2n+1},p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n},

is obvious. Two applications of the hyperrule (–
i
) leads to the conclusion:

p^{2n+1}\rightarrow((p^{2n+1}\rightarrow p^{2n})\rightarrow p^{2n})
is an
Int
-thesis.
∎

Proposition 6.3.11
.

For any
n,k\leq\omega
, there is
m\leq\omega
, individually its own for each of the statements (a)–(d) below, such that

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\neg(p^{n})\sim p^{m};\\
(\text{b})&p^{n}\land p^{k}\sim p^{m};\\
(\text{c})&p^{n}\lor p^{k}\sim p^{m}\\
(\text{d})&p^{n}\rightarrow p^{k}\sim p^{m}.\\
\end{array}

Proof.

Case (a) follows from (d) because of Lemma
6.3.9
–j and Lemma
6.3.10
–h.

Case (b). Suppose that
n=k
, then, obviously,
p^{n}\land p^{k}\sim p^{n}
. Assume that
n<k
. If
n
is even, then, in virtue of Lemma
6.3.11
–a,
p^{n}\preccurlyeq p^{k}
and, hence (Lemma
6.3.10
–i),
p^{n}\land p^{k}\sim p^{n}
.

Now assume that
n=2l+1
. Then
p^{n}\land p^{n+1}\sim p^{2l}
(Lemma
6.3.11
–f),
p^{n}\land p^{n+2}\sim p^{2l}

(Lemma
6.3.11
–e) and
p^{n}\land p^{n+3+i}\sim p^{n}
(Lemma
6.3.11
–c).

Case (c). If
n=k
, then, obviously,
p^{n}\lor p^{k}\sim p^{n}
. Thus we assume that
n<k
. If
n
is even, then, in virtue of Lemma
6.3.11
–a and Lemma
6.3.10
–j,
p^{n}\lor p^{k}\sim p^{k}
.

Let
n=2l+1
. Then, by definition (
6.12
–
ii
),
p^{n}\lor p^{n+1}\sim p^{n+3}
. Also,
p^{n}\lor p^{n+2}\sim p^{n+5}
. Indeed, since
p^{2l+2}\preccurlyeq p^{2l+3}
(Lemma
6.3.11
–a),
p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+3}\sim p^{2l+1}\lor(p^{2l+2}\lor p^{2l+3})
. However, in virtue of lattice laws true for
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
,
p^{2l+1}\lor(p^{2l+2}\lor p^{2l+3})\sim(p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+2})\lor p^{2l+3}
. Hence, by definition (
6.12
–
ii
),

p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+3}\sim p^{2l+4}\lor p^{2l+3}
. However, also by definition (
6.12
–
ii
),

p^{2l+3}\lor p^{2l+4}=p^{2l+6}
.

Finally (in this case), in virtue of Lemma
6.3.11
–c and Lemma
6.3.10
–j,
p^{n}\lor p^{k}\sim p^{k}
, for any
k\geq n+3
.

Case (d). The cases when
n=k
or
n=0
or
n=\omega
or
k=\omega
are obvious. So, first we suppose that

0<n<k<\omega
.

If
n
is even, then
p^{n}\rightarrow p^{k}\sim p^{\omega}
(Lemma
6.3.11
–a).

Now let
n
be odd, that is,
n=2l+1
, for some
l\geq 0
. Then we observe:

\begin{array}[]{rl}p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l+2}\sim p^{2l}&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:relations-specific-properties}--h}];\\
p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l+3}\sim p^{2l+3}&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:relations-specific-properties}--g}];\\
p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{2l+4+i}\sim p^{\omega}&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:relations-specific-properties}--b}].\\
\end{array}

Now we turn to the case when
0\leq k<n<\omega
. We will employ course-of-value induction on
n
. As the base of induction, we observe the following specifications:

\begin{array}[]{rl}p^{1}\rightarrow p^{0}=p^{3}&[\text{by definition}];\\
p^{2}\rightarrow p^{0}\sim p^{1}&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:Int-theses}--j}];\\
p^{2}\rightarrow p^{1}\sim p^{1}&\text{[Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:relations-specific-properties}--k]};\\
p^{3}\rightarrow p^{0}=(p^{1}\rightarrow p^{0})\rightarrow p^{0}\sim p^{1}&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:relations-specific-properties}--m}];\\
p^{3}\rightarrow p^{1}\sim p^{1}&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:relations-specific-properties}--h}];\\
p^{3}\rightarrow p^{2}=p^{5}&\text{[by definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$i$)]}.\\
\end{array}

Next, assume that
n=2l+4
. Then we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}p^{2l+4}\rightarrow p^{k}\sim(p^{2l+1}\lor p^{2l+2})\rightarrow p^{k}&[\text{by definition (\ref{E:powers_p^n}--$ii$)}]\\
\sim(p^{2l+1}\rightarrow p^{k})\land(p^{2l+2}\rightarrow p^{k})&[\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{L:Int-theses}--d}].\\
\end{array}

One of the following cases are possible:
k\geq 2l+2
,
2l+1\leq k<2l+2
and
k<2l+1
. In any event, either by the case which have been considered above when
n\leq k
or by induction, there are
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
such that

p^{2l+4}\rightarrow p^{k}\sim p^{m_{1}}\land p^{m_{2}}.

This, according to the case (b) above, implies that there is an
m
such that

p^{2l+4}\rightarrow p^{k}\sim p^{m}.

Now suppose that
n=2l+5
and
0\leq k\leq 2l+1
.

If
k=2l+4
, then, by definition,
p^{n}\rightarrow p^{k}\sim p^{2(l+2)+3}
. If
k=2l+3
, then
p^{n}\rightarrow p^{k}=p^{2(l+1)+3}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)+1}\sim p^{2(l+1)+1}
(Lemma
6.3.11
–h). And if
k=2l+2
, then
p^{n}\rightarrow p^{k}=p^{2(l+1)+3}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)}=(p^{2(l+1)+1}\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)})\rightarrow p^{2(l+1)}\sim p^{2(l+1)+1}

(Lemma
6.3.11
–m).

Thus it remains to consider the case when
n=2l+5
and
k\leq 2l+1
. But then
n-k\geq 4
and, in virtue Lemma
6.3.11
–l, we conclude that
p^{k}\ll p^{n}
. This, in virtue of Lemma
6.3.10
–d, implies that
p^{n}\rightarrow p^{k}\sim p^{k}
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.11.1
.

For any formula
\alpha(p)
with a single variable
p
, there is
n\leq\omega
such that
\alpha(p)\sim p^{n}
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
6.3.11
, the proof can be carried out by induction on the number of logical connectives occurring in
\alpha(p)
. We leave this to the reader as an exercise. (Exercise
6.3
.
14
) ∎

Now we turn to the other path of our argument. Since to prove Proposition
6.3.13
, we have to separate the set of
Int
-theses from the formulas
p^{n}
, for any
n<\omega
. If some initial powers of
p
can be refuted in relatively simple Heyting algebras, in order to carry out proof by induction, we will need a o more transparent presentation of
Int
-theses.
\Mapsto

For our purposes, we borrow a sequential calculus G3 from
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, chapter XV. The main formal objects are
sequents
which are pairs of finite sets of
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas, say
\Gamma
and
\Theta
, divided by the symbol
\Mapsto
—
\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta
, where
\Theta
is allowed to consist of not more than one formula. The system G3 is formulated in terms of metavariables for
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas and variables
\Gamma
and
\Theta
for finite sets of formulas (with the aforementioned restriction on
\Theta
) as follows.

Axiom schema:
\Gamma,\bm{\alpha}\Mapsto\bm{\alpha}
.

Rules of inference:

\begin{array}[]{llrl}(\text{$\rightarrow$-1})&\dfrac{\bm{\alpha},\Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\beta}}{\Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta}}&\quad(\text{$\rightarrow$-2})&\dfrac{\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta},\Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\alpha}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \bm{\beta},\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta},\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta}{\bm{\alpha}\rightarrow\bm{\beta},\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta}\\
\\
(\text{$\land$-1})&\dfrac{\Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\alpha}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\beta}}{\Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\alpha}\land\bm{\beta}}&\quad(\text{$\land$-2})&\dfrac{\bm{\alpha},\bm{\alpha}\land\bm{\beta},\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta\penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \bm{\beta},\bm{\alpha}\land\bm{\beta},\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta}{\bm{\alpha}\land\bm{\beta},\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta}\\
\\
(\text{$\lor$-1})&\dfrac{\Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\alpha}\penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\beta}}{\Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\alpha}\lor\bm{\beta}}&\quad(\text{$\lor$-2})&\dfrac{\bm{\alpha},\bm{\alpha}\lor\bm{\beta},\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \bm{\beta},\bm{\alpha}\lor\bm{\beta},\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta}{\bm{\alpha}\lor\bm{\beta},\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta}\\
\\
(\text{$\neg$-1})&\dfrac{\bm{\alpha},\Gamma\Mapsto}{\Gamma\Mapsto\neg\bm{\alpha}}&\quad(\text{$\neg$-2})&\dfrac{\neg\bm{\alpha},\Gamma\Mapsto\bm{\alpha}}{\neg\bm{\alpha},\Gamma\Mapsto\Theta}\end{array}

A derivation in G3 is defined in the form of a tree where each node is labeled with a sequent; the top sequents of the tree are instances of the axiom schema and the root is a sequent that is intended to be proved. (Cf.
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, the end of § 24.) The following proposition we take for granted.

Proposition 6.3.12

(
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 80)
.

For any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\alpha
,
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha
if, and only if, the sequent
\Mapsto\alpha
is derived in G3.

We use the last proposition to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3.12
.

For any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, if
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow(\alpha\lor\gamma)
, then either
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow\alpha
or

\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow\gamma
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow(\alpha\lor\gamma)
. This, in virtue of Proposition
6.3.12
, means that the sequent
\Mapsto(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow(\alpha\lor\gamma)
is derivable in G3. According to the rules of G3, this sequent can only be obtained from the sequent
\alpha\rightarrow\beta\Mapsto\alpha\lor\gamma
by the rule (
\rightarrow
-1). Regarding the last sequent, we have several options. Either it is obtained from the sequent
\alpha\rightarrow\beta\Mapsto\alpha
or from the sequent
\alpha\rightarrow\beta\Mapsto\gamma
by one of the two variants of the rule (
\lor
-1). In event of each of theses cases, we arrive at a desirable conclusion, applying the rule (
\rightarrow
-1) and then using Proposition
6.3.12
.

Now we consider the case:
\alpha\rightarrow\beta\Mapsto\alpha\lor\gamma
is obtained from
\alpha\rightarrow\beta\Mapsto\alpha
and
\beta,\alpha\rightarrow\beta\Mapsto\alpha\lor\gamma
by the rule (
\rightarrow
-2). Thus we have that it is necessary that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow\alpha
(Proposition
6.3.12
).
∎

Corollary 6.3.12.1

(disjunction property for
Int
)
.

If
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\lor\beta
, then either
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha
or
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\beta
.

Proof.

Let
\star
be an arbitrary
Int
-thesis, that is,
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\star
. Assume that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\lor\beta
. Then both
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha\rightarrow\star
and
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\alpha\rightarrow\star)\rightarrow(\alpha\lor\beta)
hold. In virtue of Lemma
6.3.12
, the latter implies that either
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(\alpha\rightarrow\star)\rightarrow\alpha
or

(\alpha\rightarrow\star)\rightarrow\beta
, which in turn implies that either

\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\alpha
or
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\beta
.
∎

Lemma 6.3.13
.

For every natural number
n
, if
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+7}
, then either
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+3}
or
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+1}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+7}
. According to Lemma
6.3.11
, (i) and (d), we respectively have

\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+5}\rightarrow p^{2n+6}
and
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+5}\rightarrow(p^{2n+1}\lor p^{2n+3})
. Then, by definition, we have:
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(p^{2n+3}\rightarrow p^{2n+2})\rightarrow(p^{2n+1}\lor p^{2n+3})
. By Lemma
6.3.13
, this implies that either

\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(p^{2n+3}\rightarrow p^{2n+2})\rightarrow p^{2n+3}

or
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}(p^{2n+3}\rightarrow p^{2n+2})\rightarrow p^{2n+1}
; that is, by definition,
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+5}\rightarrow p^{2n+3}
or

\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+5}\rightarrow p^{2n+1}
. Applying Lemma
6.3.11
–h, we obtain that either

\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+3}
or
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+1}
.
∎

Proposition 6.3.13
.

For every
n<\omega
,
p^{n}
is not an
Int
-thesis; in other words,
\not\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{n}
, for any natural number
n
.

Proof.

We prove by induction on
n
. First, we notice that neither of the following formulas is an
Int
-thesis:

\underbrace{p\land\neg p}_{p^{0}},\penalty\ \underbrace{\neg p}_{p^{1}},\penalty\ \underbrace{p}_{p^{2}},\penalty\ \underbrace{\neg\neg p}_{p^{3}},\penalty\ \underbrace{p\lor\neg p}_{p^{4}},\penalty\ \underbrace{\neg\neg p\rightarrow p}_{p^{5}},

which are equal or equivalent (in the sense of
\sim
) respectively to the formulas
p^{0},\penalty\ \penalty\ p^{1},\penalty\ \penalty\ p^{2},\penalty\ \penalty\ p^{3},\penalty\ \penalty\ p^{4},\penalty\ \penalty\ p^{5}
.

Indeed, the first four are even not classical tautologies, since can be refuted in
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
, and the last two, though are classical tautologies, are refuted in the Heyting algebra
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
.

Further, for contradiction, we assume that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{n}
, where
n\geq 6
. Next we consider the following two cases.

Case:
n
is even, that is,
n=2l+6
. By definition, we have that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2l+4}\lor p^{2l+1}
. This, in virtue of Corollary
6.3.12.1
, either
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2l+4}
or
p^{2l+1}
. Both options contradict the induction hypothesis.

Case:
n
is odd, that is,
n=p^{2l+7}
. According to Lemma
6.3.13
, the assumption that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2l+7}
implies that either
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2l+3}
or
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2l+1}
. Again, both options contradict the induction hypothesis.

Therefore,
\not\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{n}
. ∎

Remark 6.3.1
.

In the light of Proposition
6.3.13
, the conditional in Lemma
6.3.13
should be understood as counterfactual rather than implication. That is, it would be more appropriate to formulas it as follows: If it were the case that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+7}
, then it would be that either
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+3}
or
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+1}
.

Corollary 6.3.13.1
.

For any
n,k\leq\omega
,

p^{n}\sim p^{k}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ n=k.

Proof.

The
\Leftarrow
-implication is obvious. Now, for definiteness, suppose that
n<k
. If it were that
p^{n}\sim p^{k}
, then it would imply that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{k}\rightarrow p^{n}
. This in turn would imply that there is a natural number
m
such that
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{m}
. (Proposition
6.3.11
–d). A contradiction. ∎

Exercises
6.3

Show that the abstract logics
P
,
Cl
and
Int
are implicational with respect to
p\rightarrow q
.

2.

Prove (
6.8
).

3.

Prove that for any valuation
w
in
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
, there is a unique valuation
v
in
A
such that
v^{\star}=w
.

4.

Prove (
6.9
).

Prove Lemma
6.3.2
.

Complete the proof of Lemma
6.3.3
.

7.

Show that the hyperrules (c–
ii
) and (c–

) are sound with respect to
Cl
and
Int
.

Complete the proof of Lemma
6.3.6
.

9.

Complete the proof of Lemma
6.3.7
.

10.

Show that the elements
[p]
,
[\neg p]
,
[p\vee\neg p]
and

are pairwise distinct in
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}
.
(Hint: prove that the matrix
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
is a
Cl
-model.)

.

. (Hint: consult

.

.

6.4
Examples of application of Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras

In this section, we discuss several examples where the notion of Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra plays a key role.

6.4.1
Glivenko’s theorem

The following theorem was established by Glivenko
[
Glivenko, 1929
]
: For any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\alpha
,

\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \neg\neg\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}}.

Sketch of proof
. Let us fix an
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\alpha
.

First, we notice that
\neg\neg\alpha\rightarrow\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
and
\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}}\subseteq\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
. Taking into account that
\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
is closed under detachment rule, we obtain the implication:

\neg\neg\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}}\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}.

Conversely, assume that
\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
. According to the second part of Proposition
6.3.4
,
\alpha
is valid in
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}
and hence (Corollary
6.3.8.1
)
\alpha
is valid in any Boolean algebra.

Now, assume that
\neg\neg\alpha\notin\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}}
. This implies that
v[\neg\neg\alpha]
is invalid in
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}
at
v
, where
v
is the Lindenbaum valuation. This in turn implies that the element
v[\alpha]
is not dense in the Heyting algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}
. Thus,
\alpha
will be invalid in the homomorphic image with respect to the homomorphism determined by the filter of all dense elements of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}
, which, according Rasiowa and Sikorski
[
Rasiowa and Sikorski, 1970
]
, chapter IV, theorem 5.8, is a Boolean algebra. Then, in virtue of Corollary
6.3.8.1
,
\alpha
is invalid in
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}
and hence (Proposition
6.3.4
)
\alpha\notin\bm{T}_{\textsf{Cl}}
.

6.4.2
Completeness

In this subsection by ‘completeness’ we understand the following. A unital logic
\mathcal{S}
is complete with respect to that class
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
(in this narrow sense) if the equivalence

\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \alpha\penalty\ \text{is valid in all algebras of}\penalty\ \mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}},

holds for all formulas
\alpha
.

The above equivalence, in particular, is true if the conditions of Proposition
6.3.7
are fulfilled. This allowed Rasiowa and Sikorski to prove in
[
Rasiowa and Sikorski, 1970
]
that for any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\alpha
,

\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \text{$\alpha$ is valid in all Heyting algebras}.

However, the first result of this kind was obtained in
[
McKinsey, 1941
]
in relation to the Lewis systems S2 and S4.
But it was A. Tarski who opened the door towards this direction, proving in
[
Tarski, 1935
]
, theorem 4, that
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Cl}}
is a Boolean algebra.

6.4.3
Admissible and derivable structural inference rules

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic. Since the logic
\mathcal{S}
is structural, any modus rule sound with respect to
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
can be written in terms of a pair
\langle X,\alpha\rangle
, where
X=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
is a finite (perhaps empty) set of formulas, not metaformulas as we formulated in Section
4.3.5
in general. According to this definition, for any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
,
\sigma(X)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\sigma(\alpha)
; and, according to Proposition
6.3.3
and Proposition
6.3.4
,
X\models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[X]}\alpha
, which, in virtue of (
6.2
), implies that

\langle\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[X],\approx\rangle\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\,(\alpha_{1}\approx\mathbf{1}_{D}\&\ldots\&\alpha_{n}\approx\mathbf{1}_{D}\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}_{D}),

where
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
. The latter is especially interesting when we investigate
admissible rules
of a unital calculus, that is, those rules that, being added as postulated rules, do not change the set of theses of the calculus. Then, a modus rule
\langle X,\alpha\rangle
is admissible in a unital calculus
\mathcal{S}
if, and only if,

\langle\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}},\approx\rangle\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\ldots\boldsymbol{\forall}\,(\alpha_{1}\approx\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}\&\ldots\&\alpha_{n}\approx\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}),

The first to pay attention to this equivalence with respect to
Int
was A. Citkin; cf.
[
Citkin, 1977
]
. This idea was later picked up by other researches, which led to a correspondence between finitary structural abstract logics and quasi-varieties; for more information see
[
Rybakov, 1997
]
, section 1.4, and
[
Gorbunov, 1998
]
, section 1.5.2.

Given a consequence relation
\vdash
and a set of rules
\mathcal{R}
, one can construct a new, stronger, consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
: for any set of formulas
\Gamma
and a formula
\alpha
,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\alpha
if, and only if, there is a sequence of formulas
\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n}
such that
\alpha_{n}
=
\alpha
and for each
i<n
,
\alpha_{i}\in\Gamma
, or
\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{i-1}\vdash\alpha_{i}
, or
\alpha_{i}
can be derived from the preceding formulas by application of an instance of one of the rules from
R
. Such a sequence is called an
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
-
inference
.

It is clear that if each rule of
\mathcal{R}
is admissible in a consequence relation
\vdash
, then consequence relations
\vdash
and
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
have the same sets of theorems.

Example.
According to Lemma
6.3.13
, for any
n\geq 0
,
if

\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+7}
, either
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n^{3}}
or

\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}p^{2n+1}
. Hence, for each
n\geq 0
, the rule

R_{n}:=p^{2n+7}/p^{2n^{3}}\lor p^{2n^{1}}

(6.14)

is admissible for
\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}
and therefore, for any set
N
of natural numbers, if
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{n}\ |\ n\in N\}
, the set of the theorems of the consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}
coincides with
\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}}
.

A rule
R=\Gamma/\alpha
is said to be
derivable from a set of rules

\mathcal{R}
relative to a consequence relation
\vdash
(in symbols
\mathcal{R}\vdash R
) if

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{R}}\alpha.

(6.15)

Sets of rules
\mathcal{R}_{0}
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}
are said to be
equivalent relative to a consequence relation

\vdash
(in symbols
\mathcal{R}_{0}\approx_{\vdash}\mathcal{R}_{1}
) if
\mathcal{R}_{0}\vdash R
for each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}\vdash R
for each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
.

Proposition 6.4.1
.

Let
\vdash
be a consequence relation and let
\mathcal{R}_{0}
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}
be equivalent sets of rules relative to
\vdash
. Then for each rule
R
,

\mathcal{R}_{0}\vdash R
entails
\mathcal{R}_{1}\vdash R
, and conversely.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{R}_{0}\vdash R
, where
R=\Gamma/\alpha
. Then,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\alpha
, that is, there is a
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
-inference of
\alpha
from
\Gamma
. The proof can be carried out by induction by induction on the minimal number of the applications of the rules from
R_{0}
in the
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
-inferences of
\alpha
from
\Gamma
. The basis is obvious, and the proof of the induction step fis based on the observation that in the
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
-inference of
\alpha
from
\Gamma
the last application of rule from
\mathcal{R}_{0}
, let say
\Delta/\beta
, can be replaced by an
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
-inference of
\beta
from
\Delta
. A more detailed sketch of the proof of the induction step is the following.

Suppose that in a
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
-inference
\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n}=\alpha
of
\alpha
from
\Gamma
, formula
\alpha_{i}
is obtained by application of an instance
\sigma(\Delta)/\sigma(\beta)
of the rule
\Delta/\beta\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
. By assumption,
\mathcal{R}_{1}\vdash\Delta/\beta
, that is, by the definition,
\Delta\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\beta
. Hence, there is a
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
-inference
\beta_{0},\dots,\beta_{m}=\beta
of
\beta
from
\Delta
. Thus,
\sigma(\beta_{0}),\dots,\sigma(\beta_{m})
is a
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
-inference of
\sigma(\beta)
from
\sigma(\Delta)
. Now we observe that we can replace the application of
\sigma(\Delta)/\sigma(\beta)
with
\sigma(\beta_{0}),\dots,\sigma(\beta_{m})
, and use the induction assumption to obtain a
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
-inference of
\alpha
from
\Gamma
.
∎

Corollary 6.4.1.1
.

Let
\vdash
be a consequence relation and let
\mathcal{R}_{0}
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}
be sets of rules. Then

\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}=\penalty\ \vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathcal{R}_{0}\approx_{\vdash}\mathcal{R}_{1}.

(6.16)

Proof.

Suppose that
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}=\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
. Let
r=\Gamma/\alpha
and
R\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
. It is clear that
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\alpha
. Hence, by the assumption,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\alpha
, which means that
\mathcal{R}_{1}\vdash R
. Thus, for each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
,
R_{1}\vdash R
. By the same argument,
\mathcal{R}_{0}\vdash R
for each
R\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
, that is,
\mathcal{R}_{0}\approx_{\vdash}\mathcal{R}_{1}
.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathcal{R}_{0}\approx_{\vdash}\mathcal{R}_{1}
. Let
\Gamma
be a set of formulas and
\alpha
be a formula. Let us consider the rule
R:=\Gamma/\alpha
. If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\alpha
, then by (
6.15
),
\mathcal{R}_{0}\vdash R
and by Proposition
6.4.1
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}\vdash R
, which means that
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\alpha
. Thus,
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\ \subseteq\ \vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
. By the same argument,
\vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\ \subseteq\ \vdash_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
. ∎

Let
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{n}\ |\ n\geq 0\}
, where
R_{n}
are the rules defined in (
6.14
), and
\mathcal{R}_{n}:=\mathcal{R}\setminus\{R_{n}\penalty\ |\penalty\ n\geq 0\}
. It was observed in
[
Citkin, 1977
]
that
\mathcal{R}_{n}\not\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}R_{n}
.
Hence, by Corollary
6.4.1.1
, for any
\mathcal{R}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
, if
\mathcal{R}_{0}\not\approx_{\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}}\mathcal{R}_{1}
, then,
\vdash_{R_{0}}\ \neq\ \vdash_{R_{1}}
.
Consequently, the set of consequence relations each having the set of its theorems equal to
\bm{T}_{\textsf{Int}}
, is not countable.

6.4.4
Classes of algebras corresponding and fully corresponding to a calculus

Definition 6.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital calculus. A unital logic
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is said to be a
normal extension of

\mathcal{S}
if
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\varnothing)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(\varnothing)

and
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(\varnothing)
is closed under all rules of inference that are postulated in the calculus
\mathcal{S}
.

For example, both
Cl
and
LC
are normal extension of
Int
.

Definition 6.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic and
\mathcal{K}
be a class of unital expansions. We say that
\mathcal{K}

corresponds to

\mathcal{S}
if for any formula
\alpha
,

\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\varnothing)\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (\textsf{{A}}\models\alpha,\penalty\ \text{for any $\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{K}$});

and
\mathcal{K}

fully corresponds to

\mathcal{S}
if for any

X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (\textsf{{A}}\models X\Longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}\models\alpha,\text{for any $\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{K}$});

Proposition 6.4.2
.

Given a unital logic
\mathcal{S}
, the class
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the largest such a class.

Proof.

In virtue of (
6.6
),
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Then, we apply the second part of Proposition
6.3.4
.

The second part is obvious.
∎

It must be clear that

If a class
\mathcal{K}
fully corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{K}
also corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
.

(6.17)

The converse is not always true.
2
2

2

See examples in
[
Muravitsky, 2014b
]
, section 7.3.

Next we define: For any unital calculus
\mathcal{S}
,

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}:=\{\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{S}[L\textsf{{A}}]\penalty\ \text{is a normal extension of $\mathcal{S}$}\}.

Lemma 6.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a unital logic and
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}
be the sett of all
\mathcal{S}
-theories. Then
\{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\penalty\ |\penalty\ D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for any instance
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}/\penalty\beta
of an inference rule of the calculus
\mathcal{S}
,

\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\models\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\models\beta,

for any
D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}
. So, suppose
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\models\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
. Since
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
, in virtue of the first part of Proposition
6.3.4
,
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\models\beta
.
∎

Proposition 6.4.3
.

Given a unital calculus
\mathcal{S}
, the class
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
fully corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
. And if a class
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
fully corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
, then
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
.

Proof.

Let
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
and suppose that
\textsf{{A}}\models X
, where
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
. Since
A
is an algebraic
\mathcal{S}
-model,

\textsf{{A}}\models\alpha
.

Next, assume that
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha
. We note that, in virtue of Proposition
6.3.4
,
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\models X
and
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\not\models\alpha
, where
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
. However, according to Proposition
6.3.5
,
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
. It remains to show that
L\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
is closed under any inference rule postulated in
\mathcal{S}
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}/\penalty\beta
be an instance of such a rule and
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\models\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
. In virtue of Proposition
6.3.4
,
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}\subseteq\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. By premise, this implies that
\beta\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
and hence (Proposition
6.3.4
)

\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\models\beta
, that is,
L\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]
is closed under the given rule of inference. Thus
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}[D]\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
.

This completes the proof that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}
fully corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
.

Now, suppose that a class
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
fully corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
and
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{K}
. We need to show
L\textsf{{A}}
is closed under any inference rule postulated in
\mathcal{S}
, since
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}[L\textsf{{A}}]}(\varnothing)=L\textsf{{A}}
.

Indeed, let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}/\penalty\beta
be an instance of such a rule. This implies that
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta
.
Thus, if
\textsf{{A}}\models\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
, then, by premise,
\textsf{{A}}\models\beta
.
∎

Corollary 6.4.3.1
.

Given a unital calculus
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

We prove the inclusion in question, by using Proposition
6.4.3
, (
6.17
) and Proposition
6.4.2
. ∎

Proposition 6.4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
be an equational class defined by identities of the form
\gamma\approx\mathbf{1}
. If
\mathcal{K}
fully corresponds to a unital calculus
\mathcal{S}
, then
\mathcal{K}=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

In virtue of Proposition
6.4.3
and Corollary
6.4.3.1
, we have:
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. For contradiction, we assume that
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. This implies that there is an identity
\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}
that belongs the equational theory of
\mathcal{K}
but
\alpha\notin\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Since, according to (
6.17
),
\mathcal{K}
corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
, there is an algebra
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{K}
such that
\textsf{{A}}\not\models\alpha
; that is,
\alpha\approx\mathbf{1}
does not belong to the equational theory of
\mathcal{K}
. A contradiction.
∎

6.5
An alternative approach to the concept of Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra

The approach to the concept of Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra, which we present concisely below, is not supposed to be used in the search of convenient separating tools in the sense of Section
5.3
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a logical matrix. It is not difficult to see that a congruence
\theta
on
A
is compatible with
D
if, and only if

D=\bigcup\{x/\penalty\theta\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in D\}.

The set of all congruences compatible with
D
has a least element, the equality, and, as was proved in
[
Blok and Pigozzi, 1989
]
, has also a largest congruence with respect to
\subseteq
. This congruence is called the
Leibniz congruence
of
M
; it is denoted by
\Omega_{\textsf{{A}}}D
and can be defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\Omega_{\textsf{{A}}}D=&\{(a,b)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall\alpha(p,p_{0},\ldots,p_{n})\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\forall c_{0},\ldots,c_{n}\in|\textsf{{A}}|.\\
&\alpha(a,c_{0},\ldots,c_{n})\in D\Leftrightarrow\alpha(b,c_{0},\ldots,c_{n})\in D\}.\end{array}

If the matrix in question is a Lindenbaum matrix
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},D_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
, where
D_{\mathcal{S}}
is an
\mathcal{S}
-theory, then an example of a compatible equivalence on this matrix is the Frege relation relative to
D_{\mathcal{S}}
(Definition
6.2.4
).

Another example of a compatible relation on
\langle\textsf{{A}},D_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
is the largest congruence of
A
contained in
\Lambda D_{\mathcal{S}}
, which is referred to as a
Suszko congruence
:

\begin{array}[]{rr}(\alpha,\beta)\in\tilde{\Omega}D_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow&\text{for every $\gamma(p)$, $D_{\mathcal{S}},\gamma(\alpha/p)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\gamma(\beta/p)$}\\
&\text{and $D_{\mathcal{S}},\gamma(\beta/p)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\gamma(\alpha/p)$}.\end{array}

Obviously, a system
\mathcal{S}
is Fregean if, and only if,
\Lambda D_{\mathcal{S}}=\tilde{\Omega}D_{\mathcal{S}}
, for all
D_{\mathcal{S}}
.

The Leibniz congruence of a matrix
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},D_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
is referred to as
Leinbniz congruence relative to

D_{\mathcal{S}}
. It turns out that

\Omega D_{\mathcal{S}}=\bigcap\{\tilde{\Omega}D^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ D_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq D^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}}\}

and, therefore, each Suszko congruence
\tilde{\Omega}D_{\mathcal{S}}
is compatible with
D_{\mathcal{S}}
. Also, given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
, one defines

\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathcal{S}}=\bigcap\{\tilde{\Omega}D_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ D_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ \text{is an $\mathcal{S}$-theory}\}.

Thus we have:

\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\tilde{\Omega}D_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\Lambda D_{\mathcal{S}}\cap\Omega D_{\mathcal{S}}.

Suszko, Leibniz and Tarski congruences give rise to the
\mathcal{S}
-matrices
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\Omega D_{\mathcal{S}},D_{\mathcal{S}}/\Omega D_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
,
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\tilde{\Omega}D_{\mathcal{S}},D_{\mathcal{S}}/\tilde{\Omega}D_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
, and the atlas
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathcal{S}},\{D_{\mathcal{S}}/\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ D_{\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ \text{is an $\mathcal{S}$-theory}\}\rangle
, whose first components,
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\Omega D_{\mathcal{S}}
,

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\tilde{\Omega}D_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathcal{S}}
, in
Algebraic abstract logic
are referred to as
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras
. (See
[
Font et al., 2003
]
and
[
Font and Jansana, 2009
]
with update in
[
Font et al., 2009
]
for comprehensive surveys.)

6.6
Historical notes

The notion of a unital matrix is ​​a natural bridge from (ordinary) logical matrices to algebras. Such a transition makes it possible to employ more methods of universal algebra than methods of model theory. This notion was formally introduced in
[
Czelakowski, 2001
]
and extensively used therein, although many of the firs logical matrices in the history of many-valued logic were unital. Thus, by 2010, when Czelakowski’s book was published, the concept, not the term, had been well known.

The class of unital logics was defined in
[
Muravitsky, 2014a
]
, although the name for this class of logics was suggested by an anonymous referee of this paper. In fact, this notion is rooted in Definition 4 of
[
Muravitsky, 2014b
]
, where “a calculus
C
[that] admits the Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra” was defined, which is somewhat weaker than the notion of a unital logic.

It was established in
[
Muravitsky, 2014a
]
that the class of implicative logics (studied in
[
Rasiowa, 1974
]
) and that of Freagean logics (studied in
[
Czelakowski and Pigozzi, 2004
]
) are properly included in the class of unital logics.

In
Algebraic Abstract Logic
, a different approach to the notion of a Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra has been developed. See
[
Font, 2016
]
and references therein for detail; a brief outline of this approach can be found in
[
Citkin and Muravitsky (originators), 2013
]
. Unlike ours, this approach does not focus on the Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra as a source for constructing separating tools.

The second part of Corollary
6.3.5.2
was proved in
[
Shoesmith and Smiley, 1971
]
, theorem 5.

Corollary
6.3.9.1
was established in
[
Rieger, 1949
]
, theorem 4.4.

The diagram in Figure
6.4
was discovered by L. Rieger in
[
Rieger, 1957
]
and I. Nishimura in
[
Nishimura, 1960
]
. However, earlier in
[
Rieger, 1949
]
, theorem 4.6, Rieger, as well as Nishimura in
[
Nishimura, 1960
]
, § 3, gave the definition of the elements of
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{Int}}(1)
similar to definition (
6.12
). Both researchers merely sketched their proofs. Referring to theorem 4.6, Rieger wrote:

“The proof is essentially not difficult but somewhat labourious.”
[
Rieger, 1949
]
, p. 33

For his part, referring to the diagram, Nishimura wrote:

“We can investigate inclusion, noninclusion and equivalence relationships between these logics […]”
[
Nishimura, 1960
]
, § 3

Both researchers only sketched the proof of Proposition
6.3.11
in order to obtain with its help Corollary
6.3.11.1
. However, they failed to notice that Corollary
6.3.13.1
is also important.

For the treatment of
Int
-theses (and, in fact, for a broader framework), G. Gentzen introduced in
[
Gentzen, 1969b
]
a sequential calculus LJ.
3
3

3

An English translation of
[
Gentzen, 1969b
]
can be found in
[
Gentzen, 1969a
]
, pp. 68–131.
Kleene calls in
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 77, this calculus G1 for the intuitionistic system.
4
4

4

The difference between the classical and intuitionistic systems G1 consists in one restriction for two postulates of G1. See
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 77 for detail.
Kleene’s sequential calculus G3 (see
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 80) was proved equivalent to G1 (in the sense of
[
Kleene, 1952
]
, § 80, Theorem 56). The treatment of
Int
-theses in the framework of G3 is carried out through Proposition
6.3.12
.

Chapter 7
Equational Consequence

The type of consequence we are going to discuss in this chapter is perhaps one of the earliest kinds of reasoning found in mathematical practice. Indeed, suppose we want to solve an equation
x+a=b
. Assuming that
c
is a solution to this equation, we, then, proceed as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rl}c+a=b&\Longrightarrow c=b-a.\end{array}

(7.1)

Thus we obtain a solution under the assumption that a solution exists. To see that a solution does exist, we observe that the step indicated by (
7.1
), in fact, is invertible, that is, actually, the equivalence

\begin{array}[]{rl}c+a=b&\Longleftrightarrow c=b-a\end{array}

(7.2)

holds. As we will see below, (
7.2
) is the exception rather than the rule. Therefore, we focus on the type of consequence exemplified by (
7.1
).

Also, we would like to note that in the antecedent ‘
c+a=b
’ of (
7.1
) we only
assume
that it is true. In other words, we will be treating equalities rather as
formal
messages which can be true or false. This viewpoint requires a special formal language which we introduce in the next section.

7.1
Language for equalities and its semantics

Our language for equalities has the same schematic character as the language
\mathcal{L}
of Chapter
3
. Actually, the new language is based on
\mathcal{L}
but is not its extension (in the sense of Section
3.1
). What is called
\mathcal{L}
-formulas in Definition
3.1.1
, in this chapter we call
\mathcal{L}
-
terms
; accordingly, sentential (or propositional) variables we call
term variables
and subformulas now will be called
subterms
. The set of all (term) variables is denoted by
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
(or simply by
\mathcal{V}
), and the set of variables occurring in the formulas of a set
X
by
\mathcal{V}(X)
; in particular, given an equality
\epsilon
,
\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)
denotes the set of all variables occurring in
\epsilon
. If language
\mathcal{L}
is specified as a language
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime},\ldots
, accordingly, we have
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-terms,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}
-terms and so on.

For formulas, the language
\mathcal{L}
is extended by one predicate symbol,
\approx
, which will be used in the infix notation; that is for any
\mathcal{L}
-terms
\alpha
and
\beta
,
\alpha\approx\beta
is a
formula
. The formulas are only those words that can be obtained in this way. The formulas are also called
\mathcal{L}
-
equalities
(or simply
equalities
). The set of all equalities of
\mathcal{L}
-terms is denoted by
\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Slightly abusing notation, we denote the new formal language also by
\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

In accordance with this change of terminology, in this chapter we call the set
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
of Section
3.1
the
set of
\mathcal{L}
-terms
and the algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
the
algebra of
\mathcal{L}
-terms
.

The notion of substitution defined for
\mathcal{L}
-formulas in Definition
3.1.3
now is renamed as
substitution of

\mathcal{L}
-
terms

for term variables
.
(We continue calling it
\mathcal{L}
-
substitution
.) And the latter is extended to the equalities, so that if
\epsilon=\alpha\approx\beta
and
\sigma
is an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution, then

\sigma(\epsilon):=\sigma(\alpha)\approx\sigma(\beta).

As before, the
unit substitution
is denoted by
\iota
; that is

\iota(\alpha\approx\beta):=\alpha\approx\beta.

Similar to what we defined for
\mathcal{L}
-formulas in Section
3.1
, the last definition is extended to any set
X
of equalities as follows:

\sigma(X):=\{\sigma(\epsilon)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \epsilon\in X\}.

In parallel, we treat
\mathcal{L}
-metaformulas of Definition
3.1.2
as

\mathcal{L}
-
metaterms
. Then, given
\mathcal{L}
-metaterms
\phi
and
\psi
, we call a
metaequality
the following expression:

\phi\approx\psi.

If
\bm{\epsilon}
is metaequality, say

\bm{\epsilon}:=\phi\approx\psi,

in which some occurrences of a metavariable
\bm{\alpha}
are designated, symbolically
\bm{\epsilon}[\bm{\alpha}]:=\phi[\bm{\alpha}]\approx\psi[\bm{\alpha}]
, (
\bm{\alpha}
may occur in
\phi
or in
\psi
or in both), then we denote by

\bm{\epsilon}[\bm{\beta}]:=\phi[\bm{\beta}]\approx\psi[\bm{\beta}]

the result of replacement of the designated occurrences of
\bm{\alpha}
with a metavariable
\bm{\beta}
.
We will need metaequalities, when we discuss rules of inference for equational consequence.

Let
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\textsf{A},\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C}\rangle
be a (nontrivial) algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
and
D(x,y)
be a binary predicate on
|\textsf{{A}}|
. We call the structure
\langle\textsf{{A}},D(a,y)\rangle
a
binary logical matrix
. Instead of predicate
D(x,y)
, we will also be using a binary relation

D\subseteq\textsf{A}\times\textsf{A}
, in which case a binary logical matrix will be denoted in the form
\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
. We continue to call
D
a
logical filter
.

A binary logical matrix
\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
is called an
equational matrix
(or an E-
matrix
for short) if
D=\varDelta
, where
\varDelta
is the diagonal of
\textsf{A}\times\textsf{A}
.

We recall that, working with
\varDelta
as a binary predicate, we have:

(x,y)\in\varDelta\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ x=y,

for any
x,y\in|\textsf{{A}}|
. We note that, given equational matrices
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
and
\textsf{{N}}=\langle\textsf{{B}},\varDelta\rangle
, the first occurrence of
\varDelta
is the diagonal of
|\textsf{{A}}|\times|\textsf{{A}}|
and the second on
|\textsf{{B}}|\times|\textsf{{B}}|
. It should not cause any confusion, since we will be using
\varDelta
always as the diagonal of the Cartesian square of the carrier of an indicated algebra. However, sometimes, when an algebra
A
is introduced and we need to refer to the diagonal of
|\textsf{{A}}|\times|\textsf{{A}}|
, we will use
\varDelta
with a subscript —
\varDelta_{\textsf{A}}
.

Given E- matrices
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
and
\textsf{{N}}=\langle\textsf{{B}},\varDelta\rangle
, any homomorphism of
A
to
B
we treat as a homomorphism of
M
to
N
; and we say
M
and
N
are
isomorphic
if so are
A
and
B
.

Given an equality
\epsilon=\alpha\approx\beta
and a valuation
v
in algebra
A
, by a
value of an equality

\epsilon
in
A

under

v
we understand the pair
v[\epsilon]=(v[\alpha],v[\beta])
,
where
v[\alpha]
and
v[\beta]
are the corresponding values of
\alpha
and
\beta
; see Definition
3.2.3
.

7.2
Equational consequence

We begin with the following definition.

Definition 7.2.1

(E-matrix semantics)
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a binary matrix. We say that an equality
\alpha\approx\beta
is
satisfiable
in
M
if there is a valuation
v
in
A
such that
(v[\alpha],v[\beta])\in D
, in which case we say that
\alpha\approx\beta
is
satisfied at
v
, or
v

satisfies

\alpha\approx\beta

in

M
. A set
X
of equalities is
satisfiable
in
M
if there is a valuation
v
such that all equalities of
X
are satisfied at
v
. An equality
\alpha\approx\beta
is
valid
in
M
if it is satisfied at any valuation
v
in
A
. Accordingly, a set
X
of equalities is
valid
in
M
if each equality of
X
is valid.
We denote the satisfiability of
X
in
M
at
v
by

\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X

and validity by

\textsf{{M}}\models X.

This suggests to extend the definition (
4.17
) of matrix consequence to comprise the new notion of formula (equality) and new notion of logical matrix (binary logical matrix).

Thus for a (nonempty) class
\mathcal{M}
of binary matrices, we define a relation
\models_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}X\models_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha\approx\beta&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}&\text{for any matrix $\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{M}$ and any valuation}\\
&&\text{$v$ in $\textsf{{A}}$, $\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X$ implies $\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\alpha\approx\beta$}.\end{array}

We write simply

X\models_{\textsf{{M}}}\alpha\approx\beta

if
\mathcal{M}=\{\textsf{{M}}\}
.

Proposition 7.2.1
.

The relation
\models_{\mathcal{M}}
as defined in (
7.2
) is a structural consequence relation.

Proof.

That
\models_{\mathcal{M}}
is a consequence relation we obtain with the help of Proposition
4.3.8
. We leave to the reader to show that this consequence is structural. (See Exercise
7.2
.
1
) ∎

The definition (
7.2
) and Proposition
7.2.1
give rise to the following concept.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a nonempty class of E-matrices.
The matrices of
\mathcal{E}
are called
\mathcal{E}
-
matrices
.

We define a relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}})\times\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}&\text{for any $\mathcal{E}$-matrix {$\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle$} and any}\\
&&\text{valuation $v$ in $\textsf{{A}}$, $\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X$ implies $\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\alpha\approx\beta$}.\end{array}

We call any such a relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}
an
equational consequence
. If a set
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices is specified, we call the corresponding equational consequence
\mathcal{E}
-
consequence

or
equational
(
abstract
)
logic

\mathcal{E}
. Thus will be using the notation
\mathcal{E}
as a class of E-matrices and as the equational abstract logic this class generates.

Applying Proposition
7.2.1
to
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}
, we obtain straightforwardly the following.

Corollary 7.2.1.1
.

Any
\mathcal{E}
-consequence is a structural consequence relation.

Remark 7.2.1
.

To the question of whether any equational consequence can be given by a single E-matrix, we can answer as follows.
Since any equational consequence is structural, although in a new sense that differs from the old one in that instead of substitution instances of terms we use substitution instances of equalities, many, if not all, of the properties of structural abstract logic can be extended to equational consequence. In particular, this is true about Łoś-Suszko-Wójcicki and Wójcicki theorems
(
Proposition
5.1.1
and Proposition
5.1.2
, respectively
)
understood in the context of equational consequence.

We leave the task mentioned in Remark
7.2.1
to the reader. (Exercise
7.2
.
2
)

The following property is rather technical; it will be needed in the sequel.

Proposition 7.2.2
.

Let a language
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
be a primitive extension of
\mathcal{L}
. Also, let
f
be a function from
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
into
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
Assume that
X
is a set of
\mathcal{L}
-equalities and a set
Y
is obtained by adding to
X
some or all equalities of the set
\{q\approx f(q)\penalty\ |\penalty\ q\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\}
. Then for any equality
\epsilon
with
\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, if
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
, then
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
be a matrix of
\mathcal{E}
. Assume that
v[X]\subseteq\varDelta
, where
v
is a valuation of
\mathcal{L}
-terms in
A
. Now we define:

\begin{array}[]{cl}v^{\prime}[q]:=\begin{cases}v[q]&\text{if $q\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$}\\
v[f(q)]&\text{if $q\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$}.\end{cases}\end{array}

It must be clear that for any
\mathcal{L}
-term
\alpha
,

v^{\prime}[\alpha]=v[\alpha].

This implies that
v^{\prime}[Y]\subseteq\varDelta
and hence, by premise,

v^{\prime}[\epsilon]\in\varDelta
. Applying (
7.2.2
–
\ast
) again, we obtain that
v[\epsilon]\in\varDelta
.
∎

Definition 7.2.2

(abstract logics
\mathcal{E}
and
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
)
.

The abstract logic and consequence operator corresponding to
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}
we denote also by
\mathcal{E}
and by
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}
, respectively. If
\mathcal{E}
is the class of all E-matrices of type
\mathcal{L}
, we denote the corresponding abstract logic by
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
.

We immediately observe that
\textbf{Cn}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\varnothing)\neq\varnothing
, for
\varnothing\vdash_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}\alpha\!\approx\!\alpha
, that is
\alpha\!\approx\!\alpha\in\textbf{Cn}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\varnothing)
, for any
\mathcal{L}
-term
\alpha
. In fact,
\textbf{Cn}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\varnothing)
does not contain any other formulas. Indeed, let
\alpha
and
\beta
be two distinct
\mathcal{L}
-terms. Consider the equational matrix
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\varDelta\rangle
. We note that all valuations in this matrix are substitutions. Let us take the identity substitution
\iota
. Since
\iota(\alpha)\neq\iota(\beta)
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
, according to (
7.2
),
\varnothing\not\vdash_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}\alpha\approx\beta
, that is
\alpha\approx\beta\notin\textbf{Cn}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\varnothing)
.

Thus we have proved the following.

Proposition 7.2.3
.

\textbf{Cn}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\varnothing)=\{\alpha\approx\alpha\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\}
.

In the sequel, we will find the following remark useful.

Corollary 7.2.3.1
.

The class
\textbf{Cn}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\varnothing)
is closed under any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
7.2
.
3
)

To prove the next proposition we introduce a quantifier-free firs-order language,
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{V}]}
, in which the terms are built from individual variables, the individual constants are taken from the set
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
is a set of functional constants. (Thus the set of individual constants of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{V}]}
is never empty.) The only elementary formulas are equalities of
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{V}]}
-terms. Thus, the closed elementary
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{V}]}
-formulas are
\mathcal{L}
-equalities and only them.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
be an E-matrix. We extend the signature of
A
by the set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
of
0
-ary operations and denote so obtained algebra by
\textsf{{A}}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}]
. The structure
\textsf{{M}}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}]:=\langle\textsf{{A}}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}],\varDelta\rangle
is a
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{V}]}
-
structure
.

Thus, given an
\mathcal{E}
-matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
of type
\mathcal{L}
and a valuation
v
in
A
, we can extend the interpreting function associated with
M
over the
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{V}]}
-constants of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
by means of the valuation
v
. This leads to the understanding of an
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{V}]}
-structure
\textsf{{M}}[\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}]
as a pair
(\textsf{{M}},v)
, where
\textsf{{M}}:=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
is an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix and
v
is a valuation in
A
. Employing the usual definition of
satisfiability
in first-order models (see Section
2.4
), we observe that, given
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{V}]}
-model
(\textsf{{M}},v)
, for any
\mathcal{L}
-equality
\epsilon
,
\epsilon
is true in
(\textsf{{M}},v)
if, and only if,
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\epsilon
, in symbols

(\textsf{{M}},v)\models\epsilon\Longleftrightarrow\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\epsilon.

(7.9)

A direct consequence of (
7.9
) is the following observation.

Proposition 7.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a nonempty class of E-matrices such that the class
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
is closed under ultraproducts. Then the abstract logic
\mathcal{E}
is finitary.

Proof.

Let
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Regarding each E-matrices of
\mathcal{E}
as a
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{V}]}
-model, we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon&\Longleftrightarrow&\text{for any E-matrix $\textsf{{M}}$ and any valuation $v$, $\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X$ implies $\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\epsilon$};\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\text{the set $X\cup\{\boldsymbol{\neg}\epsilon\}$ is not satisfiable in $\mathcal{E}$};\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\text{for some $X_{0}\Subset X$, the set $X_{0}\cup\{\boldsymbol{\neg}\epsilon\}$ is not satisfiable}\\
&&\text{in $\mathcal{E}$ [by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:compactness-thm-refined}]};\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\text{there is $X_{0}\Subset X$ such that for any $\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}$ and any valuation $v$,}\\
&&\text{$\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X$ implies $\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\epsilon$};\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\text{there is $X_{0}\Subset X$ such that
$X_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon$}.\end{array}

∎

The next corollary is straightforwardly derived from the last propostion.

Corollary 7.2.4.1
.

The abstract logic
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
is finitary.

In the sequel (Section
7.6
), we will see that Proposition
7.2.3
is not true for some abstract logics
\mathcal{E}
. Now we show that Proposition
7.2.4
also is not true for some abstract logics
\mathcal{E}
.

Indeed, let
\textsf{{M}}:=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
be an E-matrix, where
A
is the algebra of the matrix
\textsf{{LC}}^{\ast}
of Section
3.2.5
, extended with an additional 0-ary operation
\mathbf{1}
(the greatest element of
|\textsf{{A}}|
). And
\mathcal{E}
be the abstract logic determined by this matrix.

We remind that
A
is an algebra of type
\mathcal{L}_{A}
extended with an additional constant
\top
which is interpreted in
A
by
\mathbf{1}
.
Now we define:

X:=\{(p_{i}\leftrightarrow p_{j})\rightarrow p_{0}\approx\top\penalty\ |\penalty\ 0<i<j<\omega\}.

We aim to show that

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}p_{0}\approx\top.

Let
v
be an arbitrary valuation in
A
. By premise, for any
i
and
j
with
0<i<j<\omega
,

v[p_{i}]\leftrightarrow v[p_{j}]\leq v[p_{0}].

If for at least one pair
i
and
j
,
v[p_{i}]=v[p_{j}]
, then obviously
v[p_{0}]=\mathbf{1}
, that is
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}p_{0}\approx\top
.

Now assume that for pairs
i
and
j
with
0<i<j<\omega
,
v[p_{i}]\neq v[p_{j}]
.
Then the set
\{v[p_{i}]\penalty\ |\penalty\ 0<i<\omega\}
is infinite and can be arranged as an increasing chain with respect to
<
. We observe that
v[p_{0}]
is an upper bound of this set. Since there only one upper bound of any infinite set in
|\textsf{{A}}|
, namely
\mathbf{1}
,
v[p_{0}]=\mathbf{1}
.

On the other hand, it is obvious that

\varnothing\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}p_{0}\approx\top.

Further, let
X_{0}
be any nonempty finite subset of
X
; that is,
X_{0}
is included in the set

X_{1}:=\{(p_{i}\leftrightarrow p_{j})\rightarrow p_{0}\approx\top\penalty\ |\penalty\ 0<i<j<n\},

for some positive integer
n
. We prove that

X_{1}\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}p_{0}\approx\top,

which, by monotonicity, implies that

X_{0}\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}p_{0}\approx\top

either.

Let us take any valuation
v
in
A
such that for any
i
with

0<i<n
,
v[p_{i}]
equals the
i
th element with respect to the linear order
\leq
in
A
and
v[p_{0}]
equals the
n
th element. It is obvious that

\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X_{1}
and
\textsf{{M}}\not\models_{v}p_{0}\approx\top
.

Given a nonempty class
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices, we denote by
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{E}}
the set of
\mathcal{E}
-theorems; that is

\bm{T}_{\mathcal{E}}=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(\varnothing).

Since, by definition, the logical filter of a binary matrix is a binary relation, we need the following adjuster:

\textbf{t}(\alpha\approx\beta):=(\alpha,\beta);

and for any
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X^{\textbf{t}}:=\{\textbf{t}(\epsilon)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \epsilon\in X\}.

Sometimes it will be convenient to write
[X]^{\textbf{t}}
instead of
X^{\textbf{t}}
.

Using this notation, we may seem somewhat pedantic when we note:

\textbf{t}(\epsilon)\in X^{\textbf{t}}\Longleftrightarrow\epsilon\in X.

By means of the above adjuster, we define
Lindenbaum matrices
as follows:

\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},[\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)]^{\textbf{t}}\rangle,

(7.10)

for any
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
; respectively, we denote:

\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{E}}:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},[\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(\varnothing)]^{\textbf{t}}\rangle.

We note that, in virtue of Proposition
7.2.3
, the matrix
\mathbf{Lin}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}
is equational. On the other hand, the matrix
\mathbf{Lin}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}[X]
is equational if, and only if,
X\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\varnothing)
; thus, for
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
, the only Lindenbaum matrix which is equational (up to isomorphism) is
\mathbf{Lin}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}
.

Further, we observe that any valuation in
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
is an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution. This observation leads to an analogue of Lindenbaum’s theorem (Proposition
4.3.12
).

Proposition 7.2.5
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X\models_{\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]}\epsilon;\\
(\text{c})&\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]\models_{\iota}\epsilon.\end{array}

Consequently,

\epsilon\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{E}}\Longleftrightarrow\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{E}}\models\epsilon.

Proof.

We will prove the following implications: (a)
\Rightarrow
(b)
\Rightarrow
(c)
\Rightarrow
(a).

Case: (a)
\Rightarrow
(b). Assume that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
and let
\sigma
be any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution. If
\sigma(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
, then, first,

\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(\sigma(X))\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
and, second, since
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}
is structural
(Corollary
7.2.1.1
),
\sigma(\epsilon)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(\sigma(X))
and hence
\sigma(\epsilon)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
.

Case: (b)
\Rightarrow
(c) is obvious, for
\iota(X)=X\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
.

Case: (c)
\Rightarrow
(a). Assume that
\iota(\epsilon)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
, that is
\epsilon\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
, which in turn means that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
.
∎

A set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
is called an
\mathcal{E}
-
theory
if

\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=X
.
We denote:

\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}}:=\{X\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\penalty\ \text{is an $\mathcal{E}$-theory}\}

and

[\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}}]^{\textbf{t}}:=\{X^{\textbf{t}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\penalty\ \text{is an $\mathcal{E}$-theory}\}

The last notion initiates the following definition.

Definition 7.2.3

(Lindenbaum
\mathcal{E}
-bundle/
\mathcal{E}
-atlas)
.

The bundle
\{\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},D^{\textbf{t}}\rangle\}_{D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}}}
is called a
Lindenbaum

\mathcal{E}
-
bundle
and the atlas
\mathbf{Lin}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}}]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}},[\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}}]^{\textbf{t}}\rangle
is a
Lindenbaum

\mathcal{E}
-
atlas
.

As earlier, we define for any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
:
