\begin{array}[]{rl}X\models_{\textsf{{Lin}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}]}\epsilon\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}&\!\!\!\textit{for any $\mathcal{L}$-substitution $\sigma$ and any $D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}}$},\\
&\!\!\!\textit{$\sigma(X)\subseteq D$ implies $\sigma(\epsilon)\in D$}.\end{array}

(7.11)

Proposition 7.2.6
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\Longleftrightarrow X\models_{\textsf{{Lin}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}}]}\epsilon.

Proof.

Suppose
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
. Let
D
be an
\mathcal{E}
-theory. Now assume that, given a substitution
\sigma
,
\sigma(X)\subseteq D
. This implies that
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(\sigma(X))\subseteq D
as well. Also, since
\mathcal{E}
is structural,

\sigma(X)\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\sigma(\epsilon)
. Hence,
\sigma(\epsilon)\in D
.

Conversely, suppose that
X\models_{\textsf{{Lin}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}}]}\epsilon
. This implies that for the unit substitution
\iota
and the theory
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
, if
\iota(X)\subseteq D
, then
\iota(\epsilon)\in D
. Hence
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
.
∎

Exercises
7.2

1.

Complete the proof of Proposition
7.2.1
.

2.

Prove Proposition
5.1.1
and Proposition
5.1.2
in the context of equational consequence.

3.

Prove Corollary
7.2.3.1
.

7.3
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
as a deductive system

In this section we will show, how the abstract logic
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
can be defined via inference rules. Below we define three deductive systems. It turns out that each of them defines a consequence relation equal to
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
. In our exposition we employ the procedure of replacement that was defined in Section
3.1
.

System E1:

\begin{array}[]{rllll}\text{Axiom:}&\bm{\alpha}\approx\bm{\alpha}.\\
\\
\text{Rules of inference:}&a)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha}\,\approx\,\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\beta}\,\approx\,\bm{\alpha}}&b)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha}\,\approx\,\bm{\beta},\penalty\ \bm{\beta}\,\approx\,\bm{\gamma}}{\bm{\alpha}\,\approx\,\bm{\gamma}}&c)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha}_{1}\,\approx\,\bm{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\penalty\ \bm{\alpha}_{n}\,\approx\,\bm{\beta}_{n}}{F\bm{\alpha}_{1}\ldots\bm{\alpha}_{n}\,\approx\,F\bm{\beta}_{1}\ldots\bm{\beta}_{n}},\\
\\
&&&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for any $F\in\mathcal{F}$ of arity $n>0$}.\end{array}

System E2:

\begin{array}[]{rll}\text{Axiom:}&\bm{\alpha}\approx\bm{\alpha}.\\
\\
\text{Rules of inference:}&a)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha}\,\approx\,\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\beta}\,\approx\,\bm{\alpha}}&b)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\epsilon}[\bm{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\alpha}_{n}],\penalty\ \bm{\alpha}_{1}\,\approx\,\bm{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\penalty\ \bm{\alpha}_{n}\,\approx\,\bm{\beta}_{n}}{\bm{\epsilon}[\bm{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\beta}_{n}]},\\
\\
&&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for any metaequality $\bm{\epsilon}[\bm{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\alpha}_{n}]$}.\end{array}

System E3:

\begin{array}[]{rll}\text{Axiom:}&\bm{\alpha}\approx\bm{\alpha}.\\
\\
\text{Rules of inference:}&a)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\alpha}\,\approx\,\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\beta}\,\approx\,\bm{\alpha}}&b)\penalty\ \dfrac{\bm{\epsilon}[\bm{\alpha}],\penalty\ \bm{\alpha}\,\approx\,\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\epsilon}[\bm{\beta}]},\\
\\
&&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for any metaequality $\bm{\epsilon}[\bm{\alpha}]$}.\end{array}

The notion of formal derivation is similar for all three E1–E3. Namely, let
\text{E}^{\ast}
stand for any of E1–E3. A finite list of equalities

\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}

(7.12)

is a
formal derivation
(or simply a
derivation
) in
\text{E}^{\ast}
from a set
X
of equalities if each
\epsilon_{i}
is either an instantiation of the axiom of
\text{E}^{\ast}
or belongs to
X
or can be obtained from any suitable group of the preceding equalities
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{i-1}
, by one of the inference rules of
\text{E}^{\ast}
. We say that an equality
\epsilon
is (
formally
)
derivable from

X
, in symbols
X\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}}\epsilon
, if there is a derivation (
7.12
), where
\epsilon=\epsilon_{n}
.
Given a derivation, the number of the formulas in it is called the
length
of this derivation.

Remark 7.3.1
.

Prior to Proposition
7.3.5
, we do not claim that the relations ‘
\vdash_{\text{E}1}
’, ‘
\vdash_{\text{E2}}
’ and‘
\vdash_{\text{E3}}
’ are examples of equational consequence, although Proposition
7.3.5
claims that they are.

Proposition 7.3.1

(variables preservation property)
.

Let
X\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}}\epsilon
. Then

\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
if, and only if, there is a derivation of
\epsilon
from
X
in which any instantiation of the axiom,
\alpha\approx\alpha
, satisfies the condition
\mathcal{V}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
7.3
.
1
)

Proposition 7.3.2
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent.

\begin{array}[]{rl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\text{E1}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X\vdash_{\text{E2}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{c})&X\vdash_{\text{E3}}\epsilon.\end{array}

Proof.

We prove in the following order: (a)
\Rightarrow
(b)
\Rightarrow
(c)
\Rightarrow
(a).

Case: (a)
\Rightarrow
(b). Suppose that the sequence (
7.12
) is a derivation E1. Assume that one of the equalities of this sequence,
\alpha\approx\gamma
, is obtained from equalities
\alpha\approx\beta
and
\beta\approx\gamma
, preceding it in (
7.12
), by the rule
(b)
. Then we treat
\alpha\approx\beta
as
\epsilon[\beta]
and obtain
\alpha\approx\gamma
by
replacement
\epsilon[\beta],\beta\approx\gamma/\penalty\epsilon[\gamma]
, which is an instance of the rule (E2–
b
).

Now suppose (
7.12
) has an application of the rule (E1–
c
). And assume that

\dfrac{\alpha_{1}\,\approx\,\beta_{1},\ldots,\penalty\ \alpha_{n}\,\approx\,\beta_{n}}{F\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}\,\approx\,F\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{n}}

is the first such application. Then we insert the equality
F\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}\,\approx\,F\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}
, which is an instance of the axiom, right before the equality
F\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}\,\approx\,F\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{n}
. Now we treat the former equality as
\epsilon[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}]
, where the designated occurrences of terms appear after the equality sign
\approx
. Next we apply the rule (E2–
c
) to obtain
F\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}\,\approx\,F\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{n}
. Since we may only insert new equalities in (
7.12
), the resulting sequence will be a derivation of
\epsilon_{n}
from
X
in E2.

Case: (b)
\Rightarrow
(c) is obvious, for (E2–
b
) is a generalization of (E3–
b
).

Case: (c)
\Rightarrow
(a). Now we assume that (
7.12
) is a derivation in E3. And suppose

\dfrac{\epsilon[\alpha],\alpha\approx\beta}{\epsilon[\beta]}

is the first application of this rule in (
7.12
). Now, by induction on the degree of the complexity of
\epsilon
, we prove that this step can be replaced with the axiom and rules of E1. Indeed, assume that

\epsilon[\alpha]:=\alpha\approx\gamma
. Then, using (E1–
a
) and (E1–
b
), we obtain:

\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\alpha\approx\gamma,\ldots,\alpha\approx\beta,\beta\approx\alpha,\beta\approx\gamma,

which is a derivation in E1. The case, when
\epsilon[\alpha]:=\gamma\approx\alpha
, is considered analogously.

Next, we assume that

\epsilon[\alpha]:=F\zeta_{1}[\alpha]\ldots\zeta_{m}[\alpha]\approx G\eta_{1}[\alpha]\ldots\eta_{k}[\alpha].

Then, before the first application of (E3-
b
) in (
7.12
), we insert the sequence:

\zeta_{1}[\alpha]\approx\zeta_{1}[\alpha],\zeta_{1}[\alpha]\approx\zeta_{1}[\beta],\ldots,\zeta_{m}[\alpha]\approx\zeta_{m}[\alpha],\zeta_{m}[\alpha]\approx\zeta_{m}[\beta]

and

\eta_{1}[\alpha]\approx\eta_{1}[\alpha],\eta_{1}[\alpha]\approx\eta_{1}[\beta],\ldots,\eta_{k}[\alpha]\approx\eta_{k}[\alpha],\eta_{k}[\alpha]\approx\eta_{k}[\beta].

Let us focus on the sequence:

\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\alpha\approx\beta,\ldots,\zeta_{i}[\alpha]\approx\zeta_{i}[\alpha],\zeta_{i}[\alpha]\approx\zeta_{i}[\beta],

which is an initial subsequence of the sequence obtained from (
7.12
) by such an insertion.
We see that it is an E3-derivation, where the last equality is obtained from

\alpha\approx\beta
and
\zeta_{i}[\alpha]\approx\zeta_{i}[\alpha]
by (E3–
b
).
By induction hypothesis, this application of (E3–
b
) can be transformed to an E1-derivation. Applying this argument to all equalities
\zeta_{i}[\alpha]\approx\zeta_{i}[\beta]
and all equalities
\eta_{i}[\alpha]\approx\eta_{i}[\beta]
, we, then, apply (E1-
c
) to obtain that

X\vdash_{\text{E}1}F\zeta_{1}[\alpha]\ldots\zeta_{m}[\alpha]\approx F\zeta_{1}[\beta]\ldots\zeta_{m}[\beta]

and

X\vdash_{\text{E}1}G\eta_{1}[\alpha]\ldots\eta_{k}[\alpha]\approx G\eta_{1}[\beta]\ldots\eta_{k}[\beta].

Finally, by means of application of (E1–
a
) and (E1–
b
), we obtain:

X\vdash_{\text{E}1}F\zeta_{1}[\beta]\ldots\zeta_{m}[\beta]\approx G\eta_{1}[\beta]\ldots\eta_{k}[\beta].

∎

Proposition 7.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be any nonempty class of E-matrices.
Suppose
\dfrac{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}}{\epsilon}
, where
n\geq 1
, is one of the inference rules of any of E1–E3. Then
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
, whenever
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon_{1},\ldots,X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon_{n}
. Consequently,

X\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}}\epsilon\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
7.3
.
2
)

Given a set
X\cup\{\alpha\approx\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we define:

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X)\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ X\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}}\alpha\approx\beta.

(7.13)

Proposition 7.3.4
.

Given a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the relation
\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X)
is a congruence on
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

To prove this proposition we apply Proposition
7.3.2
and the definition of E1.
∎

We aim to prove the following.

Proposition 7.3.5

(soundness and completeness)
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}\epsilon.

Proof.

The
\Rightarrow
-implication (soundness) is the second part of Proposition
7.3.3
.

Now we prove the
\Leftarrow
-implication (completeness). Let

\epsilon:=\alpha\approx\beta

and
X\not\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}}\alpha\approx\beta
. Then
(\alpha,\beta)\notin\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X)
and, in virtue of Proposition
7.3.4
,
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X)\neq\beta/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X)
in the algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X)
.

For convenience, we denote

\textsf{{A}}_{0}:=\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X).

Let us consider a valuation

v:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X):\penalty\ p\mapsto p/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X),

for any
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

It must be clear that for any
\epsilon^{\prime}\in X
,
v(\epsilon^{\prime})\in\varDelta_{\textsf{A}_{0}}
, but
(\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X),\beta/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X))\!\notin\!\varDelta_{\textsf{A}_{0}}
. Hence
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
.
∎

Corollary 7.3.5.1
.

Given a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, for any equality
\alpha\approx\beta
,

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X)\Longleftrightarrow\alpha\approx\beta\in\textbf{Cn}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X).

Proof
follows immediately from Proposition
7.3.5
.

We note that Corollary
7.2.4.1
also follows from Proposition
7.3.5
.

Exercises
7.3

1.

Prove Proposition
7.3.1
by induction on the length of derivation in
\text{E}^{\ast}
.

2.

Prove Proposition
7.3.3
.

7.4
Lindenbaum-Tarski matrices for logic
\mathcal{E}

We draw the reader’s attention to the fact that Lindenbaum matrices of the definition (
7.10
) are not necessarily E-matrices. On the other hand, we remind the reader that the concept of Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra, which was introduced for the unital abstract logics in
Section
6.3
, turned out to be a convenient tool for constructing separating tools. (The latter conception was discussed in Section
5.3
.) In this section we implement the same idea in relation to equational consequence.

For an arbitrary equational logic
\mathcal{E}
, we define:

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta,

(7.14)

for any set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
; in case
X=\varnothing
, we denote

\theta_{\mathcal{E}}:=\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(\varnothing).

We note that for any
X\cup Y\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\subseteq Y\Longrightarrow\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\subseteq\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(Y);

(7.15)

in particular, it is obvious that

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\mathcal{E}}\Longrightarrow(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X).

(7.16)

According to Proposition
7.3.3
, each
\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
is a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Definition 7.4.1

(Lindenbaum-Tarski matrices for
\mathcal{E}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an equational logic. For an arbitrary set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the E-matrix
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X),\varDelta\rangle
is called a
Lindenbaum-Tarski matrix for
\mathcal{E}
relative to

X
. We denote
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}:=\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[\varnothing]
and call the latter simply a
Lindenbaum-Tarski matrix for
\mathcal{E}
.

We note that, in virtue of Proposition
7.2.3
, the map
\alpha\mapsto\{\alpha\}
is an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
onto
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}
-algebra. (See Exercise
7.4
.
1
.)

Proposition 7.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an equational logic and
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Then the map
p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}\mapsto p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
, where
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, can be uniquely extended to an epimorphism of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}
onto
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
.

Proof.

We use (
7.16
) and Proposition
2.3.5
. (See Exercise
7.4
.
2
.)
∎

The following lemma is an adaptation of Proposition
3.2.5
.

Lemma 7.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an equational logic and
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. For any valuation
v
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
, there is an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
such that for any term
\alpha
,

v[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. Conversely, for any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
, there is a unique valuation
v_{\sigma}
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
such that for any term
\alpha
,
v_{\sigma}[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
.

Now we define a valuation in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
:

v_{X}[p]:=p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X),\penalty\ \text{for any $p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$}.

Extending
v_{X}
on all
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we get:

v_{X}[\alpha]=\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X),\penalty\ \text{for any $\alpha\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}$}.

Proposition 7.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an equational logic. For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X\models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]}\epsilon;\\
(\text{c})&\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]\models_{v_{X}}\epsilon.\end{array}

Proof.

We prove the desired equivalence in the following order: (a)
\Rightarrow
(b)
\Rightarrow
(c)
\Rightarrow
(a).

Suppose that
\epsilon=\alpha\approx\beta
.

Case: (a)
\Rightarrow
(b). Assume that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
. Let us take any valuation
v
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. Now we take any valuation
v
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that for any
\alpha^{\prime}\approx\beta^{\prime}\in X
,
v[\alpha^{\prime}]=v[\beta^{\prime}]
. In virtue of Lemma
7.4.1
, there is a substitution
\sigma
such that
\sigma_{v}(\alpha^{\prime})/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=\sigma(\beta^{\prime})/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
, that is
\sigma(\alpha^{\prime}\approx\beta^{\prime})\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. This implies that
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(\sigma(X))\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. Since
\mathcal{E}
is structural
(Corollary
7.2.1.1
),

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\sigma(\alpha)\approx\sigma(\beta)
. This in turn implies that

v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
.

Case: (b)
\Rightarrow
(c). It must be clear that for any term
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
,
v_{X}[\alpha^{\prime\prime}]=\alpha^{\prime\prime}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. Since for any
\alpha^{\prime}\approx\beta^{\prime}\in X
, in virtue of Proposition
7.3.5
and (
7.13
),

v_{X}[\alpha^{\prime}]=v_{X}[\beta^{\prime}]
, by means of (b), we conclude that

v_{X}[\alpha]=v_{X}[\beta]
.

Case: (c)
\Rightarrow
(a). Suppose that
v_{X}[\alpha]=v_{X}[\beta]
, that is

\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. According to (
7.14
),
this implies that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
.
∎

Proposition 7.4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an equational logic; also, let
X
be a set of equalities and
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
be an E-matrix. Given a valuation
v
in
A
,
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X
if, and only if, the map

f:p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\mapsto v[p]
, for any
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, can be extended to a homomorphism from
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
to
A
.

Proof.

Suppose
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X
. Let us define an extension of
f
to all elements of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
as follows:

f^{\ast}(\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)):=v[\alpha],\penalty\ \text{for any term $\alpha$}.

(7.17)

This map is a homomorphism if the above definition is correct. To show correctness, we assume that
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
.
According to (
7.14
),
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
.
This, by premise, implies that
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
.

Conversely, assume that
f:p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\mapsto v[p]
can be extended to a homomorphism. This means that (
7.17
) holds. Now let us take any equality
\alpha\approx\beta\in X
. Since
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
, applying again the definition (
7.14
), we derive
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. Then, by means of (
7.17
), we conclude that
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
.
∎

So far, we have dealt with individual matrix algebras. Now we want to consider them as a class.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a nonempty class of E-matrices of type
\mathcal{L}
. We denote:

\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
is the class of all matrix algebras of the class
\mathcal{E}
.

Given a nonempty class of E-matrices
\mathcal{E}
, for every
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we define:

\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}(X):=\{\theta\in\textbf{Con}\,\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ X^{\textbf{t}}\subseteq\theta\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta\in\mathbf{S}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\}.

Lemma 7.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an equational logic and
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. If
\mathcal{E}
contains a matrix that satisfies all equalities of
X
, then
\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an equational logic and a matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
of
\mathcal{E}
satisfies all equalities of
X
. According to Proposition
7.4.3
, there is a homomorphism of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
onto a subalgebra
B
of
A
(Proposition
2.3.3
). In virtue of Proposition
2.3.7
, there is a congruence
\theta\in\textbf{Con}\,\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that
\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\subseteq\theta
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta
is isomorphic to
B
; that is
\theta\in\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
.
∎

Next we define:

\theta_{\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]}(X):=\bigcap\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}(X).

Lemma 7.4.3
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is an equational logic and
\mathcal{E}
contains a matrix that satisfies all equalities of
X
, then
\theta_{\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]}(X)=\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
.

Proof.

We need the condition of the lemma to make sure that
\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}
is not empty.

First we assume that
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. Let us take any
\theta\in\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
and define a valuation
v
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta
as follows:

v[p]:=p/\penalty\theta,\penalty\ \text{for any $p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$}.

Now we take any equality
\gamma\approx\gamma^{\prime}\in X
. Since
(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\in\theta
, we conclude that
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta,\varDelta\rangle\models_{v}X
. By definition, there is an algebra
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
such that
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta
is a subalgebra of
A
(Proposition
2.3.3
). It must be clear that

\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle\models_{v}X
and hence, by premise,

\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle\models_{v}\alpha\approx\beta
. Since
v[\alpha]
and
v[\beta]
belong to
|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta|
,
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta
. This allows us to conclude that
\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\subseteq\theta_{\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]}(X)
.

Conversely, suppose that
(\alpha,\beta)\notin\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
.
This means that for some matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
of
\mathcal{E}
and a valuation
v
in
A
,
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X
and
\textsf{{M}}\not\models_{v}\alpha\approx\beta
, that is
v[\alpha]\neq v[\beta]
in
A
. By Proposition
7.4.3
, there is a homomorphism
f^{\ast}
of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
onto a subalgebra
B
of
A
such that
f^{\ast}(\gamma/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X))=v[\gamma]
, for any
\mathcal{L}
-term
\gamma
. This implies that there is a congruence
\theta
such that
\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\subseteq\theta
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta
is isomorphic
B
(Proposition
2.3.7
). It is obvious that
\alpha/\penalty\theta\neq\beta/\penalty\theta
. That is,
\theta\in\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}
and
(\alpha,\beta)\notin\theta
. This implies that
(\alpha,\beta)\notin\theta_{\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]}
.
∎

Proposition 7.4.4

(Birkhoff’s theorem generalized)
.

Let
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
and a class
\mathcal{E}
contain a matrix which satisfies all equalities of
X
. Then
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
-algebra belongs to
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{P}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
. Thus if
\mathcal{E}
contains a trivial matrix
and
\mathbf{I}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\subseteq\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
,
\mathbf{S}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\subseteq\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
, and
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\subseteq\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
, in particular if
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
is a quasi-variety, then
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
-algebra belongs to
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
.

Proof.

Taking into account Lemma
7.4.3
, we then employ Proposition
2.3.19
directly.
∎

Exercises
7.4

1.

Prove that the map
\alpha\mapsto\{\alpha\}
is an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
onto
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}
-algebra.

2.

Give a detailed proof of Proposition
7.4.1
.

7.5
Mal’cev matrices

According to Proposition
7.4.2
, given a set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}
of equalities, the matrix
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
is an adequate tool to determine whether
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
or not. In case
\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
, there is a more effective tool, especially when the set
\mathcal{V}(X)
is finite. This tool is a refinement of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
in the form of
Mal’cev matrix
,
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
(see Definition
7.5.1
below). Moreover, as Proposition
7.4.4
and Proposition
7.5.9
show, given a class
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices,
if
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
satisfies certain closure conditions, both

\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
and
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
belong to
\mathcal{E}
.

Given a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we denote by
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{V}(X)]/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{M}]}(X)
which is generated by the elements of the set
\{p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}(X)\}
.

Definition 7.5.1

(Mal’cev matrices)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a nonempty class of E-matrices.
For any set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we define
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{V}(X)]/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X),\varDelta\rangle
, and call this matrix a
Mal’cev

matrix relative to

X
. We denote
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}:=\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[\varnothing]
.

We note that
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}=\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}
. Hence,
\mathbf{Mal}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}
is is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
; see Exercise
7.4
.
1
.

Taking into account Proposition
7.4.2
, we derive the following.

Proposition 7.5.1
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
with
\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
, the following conditions are equivalent.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X\models_{\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]}\epsilon;\\
(\text{c})&\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]\models_{v^{\prime}}\epsilon,\penalty\ \text{providing that $v^{\prime}[p]=v_{X}(p)$, for any $p\in\mathcal{V}(X)$}.\end{array}

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
7.5
.
1
)

Definition 7.5.2
.

A set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
is called a
defining set for
a matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle

(
or
X

defines

M
)

relative to

\mathcal{E}
if there is a valuation
v
in
A
such that

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\text{$\textsf{{A}}$ is generated by the elements of the set $\{v[p]\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}(X)\}$};\\
(\text{b})&\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X;\\
(\text{c})&\text{for any equality $\epsilon$ with $\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)$, if $\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\epsilon$,
then $X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon$}.\end{array}

We also say that
X
is a
defining set for

M

(
or
X

defines

M
)

with respect to

v
.

Proposition 7.5.2
.

For any set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
X
defines
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
relative to
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

Let
v^{\prime}
be any valuation in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{V}(X)]/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
with

v^{\prime}[p]=v_{X}[p],\penalty\ \text{for any $p\in\mathcal{V}(X)$}.

Then, according to Proposition
7.5.1
,
X
is a defining set for
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
with respect to
v^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 7.5.3
.

Let
X
be a defining set for
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
relative to
\mathcal{E}
with respect to a valuation
v
. Then the maps
f:\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\mapsto v[\alpha]
and
g:v[\alpha]\mapsto:\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
, where
\mathcal{V}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
, are mutually invertible isomorphisms from
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{V}(X)]/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
onto
A
and from
A
onto
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{V}(X)]/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
, respectively. Hence,
M
and
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
are isomorphic.

Proof.

According to Proposition
7.4.3
, the map defined in (
7.17
), being restricted to the terms built from
\mathcal{V}(X)
, is an epimorphism of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{V}(X)]/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
onto
A
and is coincident with
f
.

On the other hand, if
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
, then
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\alpha\approx\beta
. This, by premise, implies that

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
. Hence, by the definition (
7.14
),
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. This shows that
g
is defined correctly. It is a routine check that
g
is an epimorphism. It is also obvious that
f
and
g
are inverses of one another.
∎

Corollary 7.5.3.1
.

All the E-matrices, for which
X
is a defining set relative to
\mathcal{E}
, are isomorphic.

Proposition 7.5.4

(Mal’cev’s first theorem)
.

Let
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
be an E-matrix satisfying the conditions (a) and (b) of Definition
7.5.2
with respect to a valuation
v
in
A
.
Then
X
is a defining set for
M
relative to
\mathcal{E}
with respect to
v
if, and only if, for any E-matrix
\textsf{{N}}=\langle\textsf{{B}},\varDelta\rangle
, if
\textsf{{N}}\models_{w}X
, for some valuation
w
in
B
, then the map

h:v[p]\mapsto w[p]
, for any
p\in\mathcal{V}(X)
, can be extended to a homomorphism
of
A
to
B
.

Proof.

First, we suppose that
X
is a defining set for
M
with respect to
v
. Because of Proposition
7.5.3
, without loss of generality, we can identify
M
with
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
and
v
with any valuation
v^{\prime}_{X}
that is coincident with
v_{X}
for any
p\in\mathcal{V}(X)
. Accordingly, we will have that
h:p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\mapsto w[p]
. Now, we extend
h
according to the rule

h^{\ast}(\alpha)=w[\alpha],\penalty\ \text{for any $\alpha$ with $\mathcal{V}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)$}.

The correctness of
h^{\ast}
is proved as follows. Suppose
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. This means that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
. Then, by premise,
w[\alpha]=w[\beta]
.

That
h^{\ast}
is a homomorphism is obvious. Since the conditions (a) and (b) of Definition
7.5.2
are fulfilled, we have to show that the condition (c) is also true.

Now we prove the ‘if’-part.
For contradiction, assume that
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\alpha\approx\beta
, but
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
. The latter means that there is an E-matrix

\textsf{{N}}=\langle\textsf{{B}},\varDelta\rangle
and a valuation
w
in
B
such that
\textsf{{N}}\models_{w}X
and
w[\alpha]\neq w[\beta]
. Using the homomorphism
h
which exists by premise, we obtain:
h(v[\alpha])=w[\beta]

and
h(v[\beta])=w[\beta]
. This implies that
v[\alpha]\neq v[\beta]
. A contradiction.
∎

Proposition 7.5.5
.

Given an E-matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
and an epimorphism

g:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{V}(X)]/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\longrightarrow\textsf{{A}}
, there is a valuation
v
in
A
such that the conditions (a) and (b) of Definition
7.5.2
are satisfied.

Proof.

Let
a
be an element of
|\textsf{{A}}|
. Then, we define:

v[p]:=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}g(p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X))&\text{if $p\in\mathcal{V}(X)$}\\
a&\text{if otherwise},\end{array}\end{cases}

for any
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

It must be clear that the condition (a) is fulfilled. According to Proposition
7.5.1
,
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]\models_{v^{\prime}_{X}}X
,
where
v^{\prime}_{X}[p]=v_{X}[p]
, for any
p\in\mathcal{V}(X)
. Since
A
is a homomorphic image of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{V}(X)]/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
with respect to
g
,
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}X
. (See Exercise
7.5
.
2
.)
∎

Proposition 7.5.6

(Dyck’s theorem)
.

Let
X\cup Y\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
with
\mathcal{V}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(Y)
. If for every
\epsilon\in X
,
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
, then the map
f:p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\mapsto p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(Y)
can be extended to an epimorphism of
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
-algebra onto
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y]
-algebra. Moreover, if
\mathcal{V}(X)=\mathcal{V}(Y)
and for any
\epsilon
with
\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
, the equivalence

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\Longleftrightarrow Y\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon

holds, then the above epimorphism is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We extend the map
f
according to the rule:

f^{\ast}(\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)):=\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(Y),

for any term
\alpha
with
\mathcal{V}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
. The proof of the correctness of this definition is standard. Namely, assume that for terms
\alpha
and
\beta
, where
\mathcal{V}(\alpha)\cup\mathcal{V}(\beta)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
,

\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. This means that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
. By transitivity of
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}
,
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
; that is
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(Y)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(Y)
.

It is easy to see that
f^{\ast}
is a homomorphism. (Exercise
7.5
.
3
)

The proof of the second part of the proposition is also left to the reader. (See Exercise
7.5
.
4
.)
∎

Proposition 7.5.7

(Tietze’s theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a nonempty class of E-matrices.
Also, let a language
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of terms be a primitive extension of a language
\mathcal{L}
and let
X
be a set of equalities with
\mathcal{V}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Now we obtain a set
Y
by adding to
X
the set of equalities in the language
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
that are formed in the following way: for any term variable
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we select at random an
\mathcal{L}
-term
\alpha_{p}
and form an equality
p\approx\alpha_{p}
. Then
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
and
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y]
are isomorphic.

Proof.

We note that the signatures of the languages
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are the same.

We define a map
f^{\ast}
from
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y]
-algebra to
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
-algebra as follows. First, we define:

f(p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(Y)):=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)&\text{if $p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$}\\
\alpha_{p}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)&\text{if $p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$}.\end{array}\end{cases}

Then, we extend
f
in the following way:

f^{\ast}(\alpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(Y)):=\alpha(f(p_{1}),\ldots,f(p_{n})),

where
\alpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
is any
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-term and

\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}=\mathcal{V}(\alpha)
.

First, we show that
f^{\ast}
is defined correctly. For this, we take any two

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-terms
\alpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q_{1},\ldots,q_{l})
and

\beta(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q_{1},\ldots,q_{l})
, where
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
,
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{l}\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}

and
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q_{1},\ldots,q_{l}\}=\mathcal{V}(\alpha)\cup\mathcal{V}(\beta)
.

Now assume that
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(Y)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(Y)
, that is
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
. Since, by definition,

Y\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}q_{1}\approx\alpha_{q_{1}},\ldots,Y\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}q_{l}\approx\alpha_{q_{l}},

in virtue of (the first part of) Proposition
7.3.3
, we obtain that

Y\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},\alpha_{q_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{q_{l}})\approx\beta(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},\alpha_{q_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{q_{l}}).

This, in virtue of Proposition
7.2.2
, implies that

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},\alpha_{q_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{q_{l}})\approx\beta(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},\alpha_{q_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{q_{l}}).

Hence,

\alpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},\alpha_{q_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{q_{l}})/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=\beta(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},\alpha_{q_{1}},\ldots,\alpha_{q_{l}})/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X).

This completes the proof of the correctness of the definition of
f^{\ast}
.

It is easily seen that
f^{\ast}
is a homomorphism of

\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y]
onto
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
. We leave for the reader to show that
f^{\ast}
, in fact, is an isomorphism. (Exercise
7.5
.
5
)
∎

The following proposition is useful, if one wants to move from one set of equalities to another one that is expressed in terms of different term variables so that both sets would define the same E-matrix relative to a class
\mathcal{E}
.

Proposition 7.5.8
.

Let
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
with
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{V}(X)=\varnothing
.
Also, let
X
define an E-matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
with respect to a valuation
v
.
Assume that there is a valuation
w
in
A
such that the set
\{w(q)\penalty\ |\penalty\ q\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\}
generates
A
, that is, there is a map
f:\mathcal{V}(X)\longrightarrow\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
with

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\bigcup\{\mathcal{V}(f(p))\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}(X)\}
such that for any
p\in\mathcal{V}(X)
,

v[p]=w[f(p)]
. Now let a set
Y
be obtained from
X
by the simultaneous replacement of every occurrence of
p\in\mathcal{V}(X)
in each equality of
X
by the term
f(p)
. Then
Y
defines
M
with respect to
w
.

Proof.

We define:

Y^{\prime}:=Y\cup\{p\approx f(p)\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}(X)\}.

In virtue of Tietze’s theorem (Proposition
7.5.7
), the matrices
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y]
and
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y^{\prime}]
are isomorphic.

Now we define a valuation in
A
:

v^{\ast}:=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}v[q]&\text{if $q\in\mathcal{V}(X)$}\\
w[q]&\text{if $q\notin\mathcal{V}(X)$}.\end{array}\end{cases}

We note that for any equality
\epsilon
with
\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
,

\textsf{{M}}\models_{v^{\ast}}\epsilon\Longleftrightarrow\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\epsilon.

This implies that
X
defines
M
with respect to
v^{\ast}
. In particular,
for any equality
\epsilon
with
\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
,

\textsf{{M}}\models_{v^{\ast}}\epsilon\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon.

Next we define:

X^{\prime}:=X\cup\{p\approx f(p)\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}(X)\}.

We notice that
\mathcal{V}(X^{\prime})=\mathcal{V}(Y^{\prime})=\mathcal{V}(X)\cup\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
Also, it should be clear that, using Proposition
7.2.2
, for any
\epsilon
with
\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)\cup\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
,

Y^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\Longleftrightarrow X^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon.

(See Exercise
7.5
.
6
.) In virtue of Dyck’s theorem (Proposition
7.5.6
), the matrices

\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y^{\prime}]
and
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X^{\prime}]
are isomorphic and, hence, the matrices

\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y]
and
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X^{\prime}]
are also isomorphic. It remains to show that the matrices
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X^{\prime}]
and
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
are isomorphic.

First, we note that
\mathcal{V}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X^{\prime})
. Second, we observe that for any term
\epsilon
with
\mathcal{V}(\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
implies
X^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
. According to the proof of Dyck’s theorem
(Proposition
7.5.6
), this means that the map

g:\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)\mapsto\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X^{\prime}),\penalty\ \text{for any term $\alpha$ with $\mathcal{V}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)$},

is an epimorphism of
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
-algebra onto
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X^{\prime}]
-algebra.
We will show that
g
is, in fact, an isomorphism.

Indeed, assume that for arbitrary terms
\alpha
and
\beta
with
\mathcal{V}(\alpha)\cup\mathcal{V}(\beta)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(X)
,
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X^{\prime})=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X^{\prime})
, that is
X^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
.
We observe that
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v^{\ast}}X^{\prime}
and hence
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v^{\ast}}\alpha\approx\beta
. Since
X
defines
M
with respect to
v^{\ast}
,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\alpha\approx\beta
, that is
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}}(X)
. Thus, we conclude that
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[x]
and
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X^{\prime}]
are isomorphic. Hence
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
,
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y]
and
M
are also isomorphic;
that is
Y
is a defining set for
M
.

What remains to finish the proof is to show that
Y
defines
M
with respect to
w
.
We leave for the reader to complete the proof. (See Exercise
7.5
.
7
.)
∎

Remark 7.5.1
.

We note that Proposition
7.5.8
is true, even when
\bigcup\{\mathcal{V}(f(p))\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}(X)\}\subset\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. In this case, we, having selected a term variable
p_{0}\in\mathcal{V}(X)
, add to
Y
the set
\{p_{0}\approx q\penalty\ |\penalty\ q\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\setminus\bigcup\{\mathcal{V}(f(p))\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}(X)\}\}
. (Exercise
7.5
.
8
)

Proposition 7.5.9

(Mal’cev’s second theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a class of E-matrices which contains a trivial matrix. Then if
\mathbf{I}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\subseteq\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
,
\mathbf{S}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\subseteq\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
, and
\mathbf{P}\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\subseteq\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
, in particular if
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
is a quasi-variety, then each
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
belongs to
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
.

Proof.

We recall that
\mathbf{Mal}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
-algebra is a subalgebra of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}}[X]
-algebra. Then, we apply Proposition
7.4.4
.
∎

\ast
), prove that for any equality

.

7.6
Equational consequence based on

implicational logic

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an implicational logic with respect to
\bm{\iota}(p,q)
in a language
\mathcal{L}
.

In this section, we discuss equational consequence based on implicational assertional consequence. For any equation
\alpha\approx\beta
in
\mathcal{L}
, we define the following transforming function:

\textbf{i}(\alpha\approx\beta):=\{\bm{\iota}(\alpha,\beta),\bm{\iota}(\beta,\alpha)\};

and for any
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X^{\textbf{i}}:=\bigcup\{\textbf{i}(\epsilon)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \epsilon\in X\}.

To discuss equational consequence based on abstract logics that are both implicative and implicational with respect to
p\rightarrow q
, we define a transforming function:

\textbf{s}(\alpha\approx\beta):\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta,

where

\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta:=(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\land(\beta\rightarrow\alpha).

The function
s
is extended to an arbitrary set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
as follows:

X^{\textbf{s}}:=\{\textbf{s}(\epsilon)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \epsilon\in X\}.

We aim to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 7.6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an implicational logic with respect to
\bm{\iota}(p,q)
in
\mathcal{L}
. Also, let
\mathcal{E}
be an equational logic with
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
such that
\{\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\}=\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Then for any
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \textbf{i}(\epsilon)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X^{\textbf{i}}).

Further, if
\mathcal{S}
is implicative and implicational with respect to
p\rightarrow q
, and, in addition, if
\mathcal{S}
is sound with respect to the inferences rules (
a
–
i
) and (
b
–
i
)
(
Section
4.3.5

)
, then for
any
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon).

(7.18)

Proof.

Let
\epsilon:=\alpha\approx\beta
. Also, we denote by
\textsf{{A}}^{\ast}:=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
, an algebraic expansions of algebra
A
of type
\mathcal{L}
. We use this notation to refer to algebras of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
1
1

1

We remind the reader that, if the constant
\mathbf{1}
is already present in the signature of
A
, we count
\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle
the same as
A
; see Section
6.1
.

By premise, the logic
\mathcal{S}
is assertional. We note that, according to Proposition
6.3.5
,
\mathcal{S}
is determined by
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Assume that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
. Let
v
be a valuation in
A
such that
\textsf{{A}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Suppose that
v[X^{\textbf{i}}]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
. This implies that for each equality
\alpha^{\prime}\approx\beta^{\prime}\in X
,
\{v[\bm{\iota}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})],v[\bm{\iota}(\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})]\}\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
. Taking into account (
6.7
), by Definition
6.3.2
,
v[\alpha^{\prime}]=v[\beta^{\prime}]
. This means that
v[X]\subseteq\varDelta
. By premise, we obtain that
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
. Using Definition
6.3.2
again, we derive that
v[\textbf{i}(\epsilon)]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
.

Conversely, suppose that
\textbf{i}(\epsilon)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(X^{\textbf{i}})

that
v[X]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
, for any valuation
v
in
A
with
\textsf{{A}}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
. According to Definition
6.3.2
,

v[X^{\textbf{i}}]\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is assertional,
v[\bm{\iota}(\alpha,\beta)],v[\bm{\iota}(\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})]\}\subseteq\{\mathbf{1}\}
. Applying Definition
6.3.2
one more time, we get that
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
. This completes the proof of the first part.

We leave for the reader to prove the second claim of the proposition. (Exercise
7.6
.
1
)
∎

The next proposition is a refinement of the last one.

Proposition 7.6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a finitary logic whose language contains binary connectives
\land
and
\rightarrow
such that
\mathcal{S}
is implicative and implicational with respect to
p\rightarrow q
and sound with respect to the hyperrule (
c
–
i
) and the inferences rules (
a
–
i
) and (
b
–
i
)
(
Section
4.3.5
)
. Also, we assume that the restriction of any algebraic
\mathcal{S}
-model to the signature
\langle\land,\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a Brouwerian semilattice, or at least satisfies the properties
(\text{d}_{4})
, (
2.6
) and (
2.10
)
(
Section
2.3
)
. Further, let
\mathcal{E}
be an equational logic determined by a class
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
such that
\{\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\}=\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
be an equational logic determined by a class
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}]
with
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\{\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}]\}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Then for any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent
\,:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{c})&X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon).\end{array}

Proof.

The implication (a)
\Rightarrow
(b) is supported by the inclusion (
6.7
). The implication (b)
\Rightarrow
(c)
is the
\Rightarrow
-part of the equivalence (
8.14
). Thus, it remains to prove the implication (c)
\Rightarrow
(a).

Assume that (c) holds. Since
\mathcal{S}
is finitary, for some set
X_{0}\Subset X
,
X_{0}^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon)
.
For the logic
\mathcal{S}
is sound with respect to the rules (
a
–
i
) and (
b
–
i
) and the hyperrule (
c
–
i
), we derive that

\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\bigwedge X_{0}^{\textbf{s}}\rightarrow\textbf{s}(\epsilon)
, that is,
X_{0}^{\textbf{s}}\rightarrow\textbf{s}(\epsilon)\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Since the logic
\mathcal{S}
is unital, in virtue of the second part of Proposition
6.3.4
, at the valuation
\bm{v}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}
in the algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
\bm{v}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}[\bigwedge X_{0}^{\textbf{s}}\rightarrow\textbf{s}(\epsilon)]=\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}
, that is,

\bm{v}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}[\bigwedge X_{0}^{\textbf{s}}]\rightarrow\bm{v}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}[\textbf{s}(\epsilon)]=\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}.

Now let
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}]
and
v
be a valuation in
A
such that
v[X]\subseteq\varDelta
. The latter implies that

v[X_{0}]\subseteq\varDelta
. Using successively (
\text{d}_{4}
) and (
2.10
), this implies that
v[\bigwedge X_{0}^{\textbf{s}}]=\mathbf{1}
.

Next, we define a map
f:\bm{v}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}}[\alpha]\mapsto v[\alpha]
, for any
\mathcal{L}
-term
\alpha
. Since
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a free algebra of rank
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
over
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{S}}
(Proposition
6.3.7
), the map
f
is a homomorphism of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}}
into
A
. Using (
7.6.2
–
\ast
), we derive that

v[\bigwedge X_{0}^{\textbf{s}}]\rightarrow v[\textbf{s}(\epsilon)]=\mathbf{1}.

Then, with the help of (
2.6
), we conclude that
v[\textbf{s}(\epsilon)]=\mathbf{1}
, that is, in virtue of (
2.10
) and (
\text{d}_{4}
),
v[\epsilon]\in\varDelta
.
∎

Remark 7.6.1
.

Besides applications of Proposition
7.6.2
which are demonstrated below, the usefulness of this proposition is seen in the following. In order to prove
X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon)
we have to examine the class
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
, but for refuting the deducibility assertion, namely for
X^{\textbf{s}}\not\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon)
, we have at our disposal a larger class of algebraic separating tools.

Below we consider two applications of Proposition
7.6.2
.

7.6.1
Abstract logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}
be the class of all E-matrices whose algebras are Boolean algebras.
Since the class
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}]
is a variety and, therefore, is closed under ultraproducts, in virtue of Proposition
7.2.4
, the abstract logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}
is finitary. A more complete characterization of it is obtained below.
For this characterization, we employ the matrix consequence

\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
, that is the matrix consequence with respect to the logical matrix
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
(Section
3.2.1
), and the classical propositional logic

Cl
(Section
4.3.5
).

Proposition 7.6.3
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X^{\textbf{s}}\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon);\\
(\text{c})&X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon).\\
\end{array}

Proof.

The implication (a)
\Rightarrow
(b) follows from (
8.14
) and the fact that
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
-algebra is a simplest nontrivial Boolean algebra.

To prove the implication (b)
\Rightarrow
(c) we recall that, in virtue of Proposition
5.2.1
, the consequence
\models_{\textbf{B}_{2}}
is finitary. Then, we apply the equality (
4.23
).

To prove the implication (c)
\Rightarrow
(a) we apply Proposition
7.6.2
.
∎

Corollary 7.6.3.1
.

\{\alpha\approx\alpha\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\}\subset\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}}(\varnothing)
.

Proof.

Indeed, we notice that
\varnothing\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}}p\land(q\lor r)\leftrightarrow(p\land q)\lor(p\land r)
. Then, we apply Proposition
7.6.3
.
∎

7.6.2
Abstract logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{H}}

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\text{H}}
be the class of all E-matrices whose algebras are Heyting algebras.
Since the class of all Heyting algebras is a variety, the abstract logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{H}}
is finitary. (See Exercise
7.6
.
2
.) We learn more about it in the following proposition.

Proposition 7.6.4
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{H}}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon).\\
\end{array}

Proof.

Using Proposition
6.3.9
and Proposition
7.6.2
, we obtain the equivalence in question. ∎

Similarly to Corollary
7.6.3.1
, we obtain:

Corollary 7.6.4.1
.

\{\alpha\approx\alpha\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\}\subset\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{H}}}(\varnothing)
.

Exercises
7.6

1.

Prove that if
\mathcal{S}
is implicative and implicational with respect to
p\rightarrow q
, and, in addition,
\mathcal{S}
is sound with respect to the inferences rules (
a
–
i
) and (
b
–
i
) (Section
4.3.5
), then for
any
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon).

Show that the equational logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{H}}
is finitary.

7.7
Philosophical and historical notes

The question about the nature of equality had been challenging in philosophy of logic, at least until the beginning of the twentieth century. To illustrate the core of the problem, we quote A. N. Whitehead.

“Identity may be conceived as a special limiting case of equivalence. For instance in arithmetic we write,
2+3=3+2
. This means that, in so far as the total number of objects mentioned,
2+3
and
3+2
come to the same number, namely
5
. But
2+3
and
3+2
are not identical: the order of the symbols is different in the two combinations, and this difference of order directs different processes of thought. The importance of the equation arises from its assertion that these different processes of thought are identical as far as the total number of things thought of is concerned.”
[
Whitehead, 2009
]
, section I.3

In the above excerpt, it is clear that Whitehead, on the one hand, points at the difference between the terms ‘
2+3
’ and ‘
3+2
’ and on the other hand, indicates the sameness of what the two thought processes associated with these terms,
lead, namely, the number
5
. Thus, Whitehead makes it clear, though there may be confusion in the way he uses the terminology, that there is the equality between the terms ‘
2+3
’ and ‘
3+2
’ (he uses the term
equivalence
), at the same time pointing at their non-identity.

The distinction between the concepts of
equality
and
identity
, as we will see below, was fully realized (e.g., by Frege) only by the end of the nineteenth century.
The inability to see this distinction was, as Quine put it, a “confusion between the sign and the object.” No wonder that Wittgenstein mockingly, in our opinion, stated:

“[
\dots
] to say of
two
things that they are identical is nonsense, and to say of
one
thing that it is identical with itself is to say nothing at all.”
[
Wittgenstein, 2001
]
, 5.5303

For Aristotle, the distinction indicated in the last paragraph has not yet been recognized. He determines rather than defines things that should be considered one and the same, if they are indistinguishable by all the attributes that can be applied to them:

“Only to things that are indistinguishable and one in being it generally agreed that all the same attributes belong.”
Topics
, vii.I (
152^{\text{a}}30
); quoted from
[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, section II.3

As regards logic, in full accordance with Aristotle’s view, signs, and not things, are a working tool of argumentation. Then, applying Aristotle’s observation, not to things, but to their names and replacing identity with equality, we could express Aristotle’s observation in symbolic form as follows:

\forall F(F(\alpha)\Leftrightarrow F(\beta))\Rightarrow\alpha\approx\beta).

(7.19)

In philosophical literature (
7.19
) is known as
Leibniz’s principle of identity of indiscernibles
; cf., e.g.,
[
Ishiguru, 1990
]
, section II.1. And again in accordance with Aristotle’s view, the quantification in (
7.19
) is supposed to run over all applicable attributes, or, better to say (in accordance with a new linguistic tune), over all applicable contexts.

However, there is a problem with attributing (
7.19
) to Leibniz, since, as H. Ishiguro points out, “many discussions on extensionality and intensionality or referential transparency and referential opacity are made by using the
salva veritate
principle in the sense of ‘indiscernibility of identicals’, and calling it Leibniz’s law. But this indiscernibility principle is not one that Leibniz himself expressed;” cf.
[
Ishiguru, 1990
]
, section II.1. Leibniz formulated his “salva veritate” principle as follows:

“Those terms of which one can be substituted for the other without affecting truth are identical. (
Eadem sunt, quorum unum alteri substitui potest salva veritate
)” Quoted from
[
Ishiguru, 1990
]
, section II.1; see also
[
Kneale and Kneale, 1962
]
, section V.3

Quoting Leibniz’s
salva veritate
principle, Ishiguro adds the comment:

“The
salva veritate
principle is …an attempt to determine the identity of concepts expressed by words, not by reference to mental images invoked, or any consideration that we can apply the words taken in isolation, but by the role they play in determining the truth of the proposition in which they occur. Intersubstitutivity discovers the identity of the role.”
[
Ishiguru, 1990
]
, section II.1

And a bit further, she continues:

“The Leibnizian
salva veritate
principle which occurs mainly in his logical works and in his treatises on conceptual analysis, is not, I suggest, a general definition of identity. It is simply a principle to determine the identity of concepts.”
[
Ishiguru, 1990
]
, section II.1

Note that, although Aristotle and Leibniz do not make a clear distinction between the
equal
and the
identical
, they employ the
substitution
operation, that is our
replacement
in the sense of Section
3.1
, which we use in the formulations of the deductive systems E1–E3.

We believe that G. Frege was the first who not only made it clear that there is a distinction between the equal and the identical, but also pointed at two features of the former: 1) equality is a relation between terms of a formal language, and 2) the determination of equal terms is of semantic character.

He starts his famous paper
Über Sinn und Bedeutung
with the following discussion.

“Equality
∗
[Frege’s footnote:
∗
I use this word in the sense of identity and understand ‘
a=b
’ to have the sense of ‘
a
is the same as
b
’ or ‘
a
and
b
coincide.’] gives rise to challenging questions which are not altogether easy to answer. Is it a relation? A relation between objects, or between names or signs of objects? In my
Begriffsschrift
I assume the latter. The reasons which seem to favour this are the following:
a=a
and
a=b
are obviously statements of differing cognitive value;
a=a
holds
a priori
and, according to Kant, is to be labeled analytic, while statements of the form
a=b
often contain very valuable extensions of our knowledge and cannot always be established
a priori
.” Quoted from
[
van Heijenoort, 2002
]
, p. 56

Frege further adds:

“Now if we were to regard equality as a relation between that which the names ‘
a
’ and ‘
b
’ designate, it would deem that
a=b
could not differ from
a=a
(i.e. provided
a=b
is true). A relation would thereby be expressed of a thing itself, and indeed one in which each thing stands to itself but to no other thing. What is intended to be said by
a=b
seems to be that signs or names ‘
a
’ and ‘
b
’ designate the same thing, so that those signs themselves would be under discussion; a relation between them would be asserted. But this relation would hold between the names or signs only in so far as they named or designated something.” Quoted from
ibid.
, p. 56

Following Frege’s approach, we use the sign ‘
\approx
’ to denote a relation between equal terms and the sign ‘
=
’ for denoting the fact of identity. The necessity for the employment of the two different signs comes from a “delay” when, in order to assert an equality
\alpha\approx\beta
, we have to leave the linguistic framework of a formal language and enter the semantic framework of an algebra with a valuation
v
in it and, then, check whether
v(\alpha)
and
v(\beta)
are identical.
The delay occurs when the two evaluations,
\alpha\mapsto v[\alpha]
and

\beta\mapsto v[\beta]
, (“different processes of thought,” as Whitehead calls them) are running to arrive at one element of algebra, which fact is stated by writing ‘
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
’.

On the other hand, if we stay in the framework of a formal language and define equality axiomatically, we have to use inference rules that define
\approx
as an equivalence relation (as was noted by Whitehead; see above),
which are exemplified by the axiom and rules E1–
a
and E1–
b
, while the rules E1–
c
, E2–
b
and E3–
b
, which together represent the converse of (
7.19
) in a weakened form, exemplify the principle of
substitutivity
, or that of
indiscernibility of identicals
. This is how W. V. Quine explains this principle.

“It provides that,
given a true statement of identity, one of its two terms may be substituted for the other in any true statement and the result will be true
.”
[
Quine, 1994
]
, VIII Reference and Modality
2
2

2

Although Quine points out that this principle may fail if “the occurrence to be supplanted is not
purely referential
,” (see
ibid.
) this remark is not related to our usage of the aforementioned rules.

The definition of the relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}
is a natural implementation of Ferege’s view on equality in the framework of matrix consequence. We also encounter in
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, section 11.2, a similar definition of what it means that a first-order formula is a “consequence in the class
\mathfrak{R}
of the collection of formulas
\mathfrak{S}
.”
This similarity prompted us to adapt Mal’cev’s approach, having implemented it within the framework of equational consequence. The equational consequence in a language without logical connectives and quantifiers has been studied in
[
Blok and Pigozzi, 1989
]
, chapter 2, however, for a different purpose.

The conception a defining set of equalities and generating symbols was introduced by W. von Dyck in
[
Dyck, 1882
]
, introduction and part 1, § 4 and § 5, where
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
was the class of all groups. This idea was picked up by other authors, including H. Tietze
[
Tietze, 1908
]
, part 4, § 11. A. I. Mal’cev extended this approach to abstract algebras in
[
Mal’cev, 1956
]
and to models of first-order language in
[
Mal’cev, 1958
]
and especially in
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
. (The latter book is a posthumous publication of Mal’cev’s unfinished manuscript.)

The conception of a defining set (of equalities) for an E-matrix (Definition
7.5.2
) is our adaptation of an analogous definition for first-order logic in
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, section 11.2; see also
[
Henkin et al., 1985
]
, remark 0.4.65. Furthermore,
Proposition
7.5.4
and Proposition
7.5.9
correspond to theorem 1 and theorem 4 of
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, section 11.2, respectively; and Proposition
7.5.6
and Proposition
7.5.7
correspond, respectively, to theorem 5 and theorem 6 there. The two last theorems originally appeared in
[
Dyck, 1882
]
and
[
Tietze, 1908
]
, respectively, where they were proved for groups.

Taken by themselves, Proposition
7.6.3
and Proposition
7.6.4
are part of folklore.

Chapter 8
Equational L-Consequence

Substitution does not play a significant role in equational consequence, at least not more than it plays in ordinary matrix consequence. But we get to a different world when we let substitution play a key role. Because of this, the type of consequence which we will discuss in this chapter, as it can be seen after examination, falls into the category of Carnap’s L-concepts; see
[
Carnap, 1942
]
, § 14. Roughly speaking, while in equational consequence the equality
\alpha\approx\beta
is interpreted as an equation, in equational L-consequence it is interpreted as an identity.

8.1
Equational L-consequence

Definition 8.1.1
.

Given a non-empty class
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices, we define: For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\text{$\textsf{{M}}\models X$ {implies} $\textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon$, \emph{for any} $\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}$}.

It is clear that

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon,

(8.1)

but not vice versa. The first part we leave to the reader. (See Exercise
8.1
.
1
.)

To show that the converse of (
8.1
) in general does not hold, we take the class
\mathcal{E}
consisting only of the E-matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{B}}_{2},\varDelta\rangle
. Let us consider the two
\mathcal{L}_{B}
-equalities —
p\approx\top
and
p\approx\neg\top
. It is obvious that

\textsf{{M}}\nvDash p\approx\top
and
\textsf{{M}}\nvDash p\approx\neg\top
. Hence
p\approx\top\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}p\approx\neg\top
. On the other hand, for any valuation
v
in
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
with
v[p]=\mathbf{1}
, we obtain that
v
satisfies
p\approx\top
and refutes
p\approx\neg\top
; that is
p\approx\top\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}p\approx\neg\top
.

The equivalence in (
8.1
) however occurs when
X=\varnothing
, namely

\varnothing\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\Longleftrightarrow\varnothing\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon.

(8.2)

Proposition 8.1.1
.

For any nonempty class
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices, the relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}
is a consequence relation.

Proof.

It suffices to apply Proposition
4.3.8
for
\mathcal{L}
-equalities and the concept of model of type (B); see Section
4.3
.
∎

Given a nonempty class
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices, we denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
the abstract logic corresponding to the consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}
and call it the
equational L-consequence relative to

\mathcal{E}
, or L-
equational logic
(
relative to

\mathcal{E}
) for short. In particular, we employ the class
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
of all E-matrices of type
\mathcal{L}
, we have the abstract logic
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}
.

We note that structurality is a very rare property of equational L-consequence.

Proposition 8.1.2
.

Suppose the set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
of term variables contains at least three variables.
Then, given a class
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices, the consequence relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}
is structural if, and only if,
\mathcal{E}
contains only a trivial E-matrix.

Proof.

Let an E-matrix
M
be trivial. Then
\textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon
, for any equality
\epsilon
.

Conversely, assume that
M
is an arbitrary nontrivial E-matrix from
\mathcal{E}
and
p,q
and
r
are three distinct term variables. It is obvious
that
\textsf{{M}}\not\models p\approx q
and
\textsf{{M}}\not\models p\approx r
. Hence

p\approx q\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}p\approx r
. Now let
\sigma
be the substitution that maps
q
to
p
and is identical on the other variables. Thus, we have:
\textsf{{M}}\models\sigma(p)\approx\sigma(q)

and
\textsf{{M}}\not\models\sigma(p)\approx\sigma(r)
. That is,
\sigma(p)\approx\sigma(q)\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\sigma(p)\approx\sigma(r)
.
∎

Even if only the trivial equational L-consequence is structural, every abstract logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
always has the following property:

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\sigma(\epsilon),

(8.3)

for any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
.

Indeed, suppose
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon
and
\sigma
is an arbitrary
\mathcal{L}
-substitution. Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\varDelta\rangle
be any matrix of
\mathcal{E}
. Assume that
\textsf{{M}}\vDash X
and consider any valuation
v
in
A
. In virtue of Proposition
3.2.3
, there is a valuation
v_{\sigma}
in
A
such that for any equality
\eta
,

v[\sigma(\eta)]\in\varDelta\Longleftrightarrow v_{\sigma}[\eta]\in\varDelta.

Since, by premise,
\textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon
, then, in particular,
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v_{\sigma}}\epsilon
, that is
v_{\sigma}[\epsilon]\in\varDelta
. The latter, through the equivalence above, implies that
v[\sigma(\epsilon)]\in\varDelta
.

Thus we have proved the following proposition.

Proposition 8.1.3
.

Every
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
-theory is closed under substitution.

As it is the case with equational consequence, there is a correlation between equational L-consequence and validity in model theory.

We relate an instantiation of term language
\mathcal{L}
expanded with a binary predicate symbol
\approx
to form a first-order language
\textbf{FO}_{\mathcal{L}}
, where the term variables play a role of individual variables and elementary formulas like
\alpha\approx\beta
are interpreted by the equality of term functions
\beta^{\textsf{A}}
and
\alpha^{\textsf{A}}
in the corresponding first-order model
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{A},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{L}},\varDelta\rangle
. For any elementary formula
\epsilon
, we denote by
\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon
the universal closure of this formula; accordingly for a set
X
of equalities, we denote:

\boldsymbol{\forall}X:=\{\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon\penalty\ |\penalty\ \epsilon\in X\}.

The following equivalence must be obvious:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\textsf{{M}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon&\Longleftrightarrow(\text{$\textsf{{M}}\models_{v}\epsilon$, for any valuation $v$ in $\textsf{{A}}$})\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon,\end{array}

(8.4)

where the first occurrence of ‘
\models
’ denotes the validity in the first-order model
M
(Section
2.4
), and the last one the validity in the sense of Definition
7.2.1
.

Naturally, (
8.4
) implies that

\textsf{{M}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}X\Longleftrightarrow\textsf{{M}}\models X,

with the same distinction of the usage of
\models
in the two last occurrences.

Using (
8.4
), we observe: For any nonempty class
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon\Longleftrightarrow(\textsf{{M}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}X\Rightarrow\textsf{{M}}\models\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon,\penalty\ \text{for any $\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}$}).

(8.5)

Now we obtain the following.

Proposition 8.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a nonempty class of E-matrices such that the class
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
is closed under ultraproducts. Then the abstract logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
is finitary.

Proof.

Indeed, we begin, using (
8.5
):

\begin{array}[]{rcl}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon&\Longleftrightarrow&\text{the set $\boldsymbol{\forall}X\cup\{\boldsymbol{\neg}\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon\}$ is not satisfiable in $\mathcal{E}$};\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\text{for some $X_{0}\Subset X$, the set
$\boldsymbol{\forall}X_{0}\cup\{\boldsymbol{\neg}\boldsymbol{\forall}\epsilon\}$ is not satisfiable}\\
&&\text{in $\mathcal{E}$ [by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:compactness-thm-refined}]};\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\text{for some $X_{0}\Subset X$, $X_{0}\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon$}\penalty\ \text{[again in virtue of\penalty\ \eqref{E:L-con-equivalence-2}]}.\end{array}

∎

The next corollary directly follows from the last proposition.

Corollary 8.1.4.1
.

The abstract logic
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}
is finitary.

Exercises
8.1

1.

Prove the implication (
8.1
).

2.

Complete the proof of Proposition
8.1.1
.

8.2
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}
as a deductive system

In this section we describe
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}
deductively, that is, by means of axioms and inference rules. Our treatment of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}
will similar to that of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
in Section
7.3
.

Indeed, first, we define systems E1s–E3s which obtained, respectively, from the systems E1–E3 the rule of (uniform) substitution as an additional inference rule. We use notation E
∗
​s to refer to any of the systems E1s–E3s.

\begin{array}[]{rc}\text{Substitution:}&\dfrac{\bm{\alpha}\approx\bm{\beta}}{\sigma(\bm{\alpha})\approx\sigma(\bm{\beta})},\end{array}

where
\sigma
is an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution, and
\alpha
and
\beta
are
\mathcal{L}
-terms.

Each relation
\vdash_{\text{E${}^{\ast}$\!s}}
is defined through the notion of formal derivation similar to the one defined in Section
7.3
with the only difference — the substitution rule is allowed in E
∗
​s-derivations.

Next we prove a proposition analogous to Proposition
7.3.2
.

Proposition 8.2.1
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent.

\begin{array}[]{rl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\text{E1s}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X\vdash_{\text{E2s}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{c})&X\vdash_{\text{E3s}}\epsilon.\end{array}

Proof.

Argumentation is literally the same as in the proof of Proposition
7.3.2
, with one additional step. We demonstrate it, proving the case (a)
\Rightarrow
(b). Suppose we have that

\vdash_{\text{E1s}}\epsilon
and want to show that
X\vdash_{\text{E2s}}\epsilon
. Considering any E1s-derivation, we show, by induction on the length of this E1s-derivation, all moves along this derivation can be carried out in E2s. Sine the substitution rule is common in both systems, we have to be concerned about other rules of inference. But this was done in the case ‘(a)
\Rightarrow
(b)’ of the proof of Proposition
7.3.2
.

The other cases are considered analogously.
∎

Proposition 8.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be any nonempty class of E-matrices.
Suppose
\dfrac{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}}{\epsilon}
, where
n\geq 1
, is one of the inference rules of any of E1s–E3s. Then for any matrix
\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}
, if all
\textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon_{n}
hold, then
\textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon
also holds. Consequently, if all
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon_{1},\ldots,X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon_{n}

hold, then
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon
also holds. Hence,

X\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}\!\text{s}}\epsilon\Longrightarrow X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon.

Proof.

We prove the first part only for the substitution rule and leave the rest of the proof to the reader. (Exercise
8.2
.
1
)

Assume that the term variables of an equality
\alpha\approx\beta
constitute a set
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
. Let
\sigma
be an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution according to which
\sigma[p_{i}]=\gamma_{i}
. Let
\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}
, for which we assume that
\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha\approx\beta
. Now let
v
be any valuation in
M
. Next, we define a valuation
v^{\prime}
in
M
such that

v^{\prime}[p_{i}]=v[\gamma_{i}]
. It must be cleat that
v^{\prime}[\alpha]=v[\sigma(\alpha)]
and
v^{\prime}[\beta]=v[\sigma(\beta)]
. By premise, we obtain that

v[\sigma(\beta)]=v[\sigma(\beta)]
.
∎

Given a set
X\cup\{\alpha\approx\beta\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we define:

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X)\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ X\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}\!\text{s}}\alpha\approx\beta.

(8.6)

Proposition 8.2.3
.

Given a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the relation
\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X)
is a congruence on
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

To prove this proposition we apply Proposition
8.2.1
and the definition of E1s.
∎

Our next aim is to prove soundness and completeness. Our proof will almost literally repeat the proof of Proposition
7.3.5
, though with
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}
instead of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
and with
\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}\!\text{s}}
instead of
\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}}
.

Proposition 8.2.4

(soundness and completeness)
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}\!\text{s}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon.

Proof.

The
\Rightarrow
-implication (soundness) is the third part of Proposition
8.2.2
.

Now we prove the
\Leftarrow
-implication (completeness). Let

\epsilon:=\alpha\approx\beta

and
X\not\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}\!\text{s}}\alpha\approx\beta
. Then
(\alpha,\beta)\notin\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X)
and, in virtue of Proposition
8.2.3
,
\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X)\neq\beta/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X)
in the algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X)
.

For convenience, we denote

\textsf{{A}}_{1}:=\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}}(X).

Now, let
\gamma\approx\delta\in X
and
\sigma
be any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution. By definition,
X\vdash_{\text{E}^{\ast}\!\text{s}}\sigma(\gamma)\approx\sigma(\delta)
;
therefore, in virtue of (
8.6
),
(\sigma(\gamma),\sigma(\delta))\in\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X)
,
that is,
\sigma(\gamma)/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}=\sigma(\delta)/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}
.

Since any valuation in
\textsf{{A}}_{1}
is a composition an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution and the canonical map
\eta\mapsto\eta/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}
, this explains that

\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1},\Delta\rangle\models X
.

Let us consider a valuation

v:\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X):\penalty\ p\mapsto p/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X),

for any
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

It must be clear that that
v[(\alpha\approx\beta)]=(\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}},\beta/\penalty\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}})
. Therefore,
\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1},\Delta\rangle\not\models\alpha\approx\beta
.
∎

Remark 8.2.1
.

A congruence
\theta
on an algebra
A
is called
fully invariant
(or
completely invariant
) if for any substitution
\sigma
,

(a,b)\in\theta\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\sigma(a),\sigma(b)\in\theta.

Thus, we see that the fact that
\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}
is fully invariant was essential in the proof of the
\Leftarrow
-implication (completeness) of Proposition
8.2.4
.

The following two corollaries follow immediately from Proposition
8.2.4
.

Corollary 8.2.4.1
.

Given a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, for any equality
\alpha\approx\beta
,

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X)\Longleftrightarrow\alpha\approx\beta\in\textbf{Cn}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}(X).

We have earlier proved that the logic
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}
is finitary; see Corollary
8.1.4.1
. Another way to obtain this result is by
Proposition
8.2.4
.

Exercises
8.2

1.

Complete the proof of Proposition
8.2.2
.

2.

Prove Corollary
8.2.4.1
.

8.3
Lindenbaum-Tarski matrices for logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}

Given a nonempty class
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices, we define:

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\alpha\approx\beta;

(8.7)

and we denote

\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}:=\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(\varnothing).

It is clear that

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \text{for every $\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}$, }\textsf{{M}}\models\alpha\approx\beta.

In virtue of Proposition
8.2.2
, each
\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
is a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

We note that

X\subseteqq Y\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)\subseteq\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(Y);

(8.8)

in particular,

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X).

(8.9)

Definition 8.3.1

(Lindenbaum-Tarski matrices for
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
be an L-equational logic. For an arbitrary set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the E-matrix
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X),\varDelta\rangle
is called a
Lindenbaum-Tarski matrix for
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
relative to

X
. We denote
