\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}:=\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[\varnothing]
and call the latter simply a
Lindenbaum-Tarski matrix for

\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
.

The following lemma is similar to Lemma
7.4.1
and also is an adaptation of Proposition
3.2.5
.

Lemma 8.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
be an
L
-equational logic and
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. For any valuation
v
in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]
, there is an
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
such that for any term
\alpha
,

v[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
. Conversely, for any
\mathcal{L}
-substitution
\sigma
, there is a unique valuation
v_{\sigma}
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
such that for any term
\alpha
,
v_{\sigma}[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
8.3
.
1
)

Analogous to what we did in Section
7.4
, we define a special valuation in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
:

\overline{v}_{X}[p]:=p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X),\penalty\ \text{for any $p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$}.

Extending
\overline{v}_{X}
on all
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we get:

\overline{v}_{X}[\alpha]=\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X),\penalty\ \text{for any $\alpha\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}$}.

Proposition 8.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
be an L-equational logic. For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent.

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\models\epsilon;\\
(\text{c})&\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\models_{\overline{v}_{X}}\epsilon.\end{array}

Hence,
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\models X
and, certainly,
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\models_{\overline{v}_{X}}X
.

Proof.

Let us denote:
\epsilon:=\alpha\approx\beta
. We will consecutively prove
(a)
\Rightarrow
(b)
\Rightarrow
(c)
\Rightarrow
(a).

Case: (a)
\Rightarrow
(b). Let
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\alpha\approx\beta
and
v
be any valuation in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]
.
In virtue of Lemma
8.3.1
, there is a substitution
\sigma
such that
v[\alpha]=\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
and
v[\beta]=\sigma(\beta)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
. On the other hand, according to (
8.3
) and (
8.7
),
\sigma(\alpha)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)=\sigma(\beta)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
, that is
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
.

Case: (b)
\Rightarrow
(c). It is obvious.

Case: (c)
\Rightarrow
(a). If
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\models_{\overline{v}_{X}}\alpha\approx\beta
, then, by definition,

\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
, that is, according to (
8.7
),

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\alpha\approx\beta
.
∎

Remark 8.3.1
.

Comparing Proposition
8.3.1
with Proposition
7.4.2
, we notice the difference in the conditions (b) in both propositions. This difference becomes clear when we recall that
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\models X
and thus
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\models\epsilon
is equivalent to the implication ‘
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\models X\Rightarrow\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\models\epsilon
’.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a nonempty class of E-matrices and
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. We denote:

\mathcal{E}[X]:=\{\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\models X\}.

It is obvious that
\mathcal{E}[X]\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, and, in virtue of Proposition
8.3.1
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \varnothing\vdash_{\mathcal{E}[X]_{\text{L}}}\epsilon.

Now we denote:

\mathcal{E}^{\ast}[X]:=\text{Mod}_{\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{E}[X]).

According to Proposition
2.4.1
, the classes
\mathcal{E}[X]
and
\mathcal{E}^{\ast}[X]
have the same equational theory; in our case, it implies that for any
\epsilon\in\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \varnothing\vdash_{\mathcal{E}^{\ast}[X]_{\text{L}}}\epsilon.

(8.10)

(Exercise
8.3
.
2
)

The importance of the last equivalence is that
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}[X]
, though it is not necessary that
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\in\mathcal{E}
. Thus, to prove that
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon
, one can use
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]
as a separating matrix
(in the sense of Section
5.3
) to show that
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\not\models\epsilon
or even
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]\not\models_{\overline{v}_{X}}\epsilon
. Since the class
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}^{\ast}[X]]
is a variety (Section
2.4
), one of the last two rejections (and hence both) can be achieved if we notice the following.

Proposition 8.3.2
.

Let, given a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
\mathcal{E}^{\ast}[X]
be a class of E-matrices as defined above. Then the algebra of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]
, that if
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
, is a free algebra with generators
\{p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\}
in the variety
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}^{\ast}[X]]
.

Proof.

First, it is obvious that
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
is generated by the set
\{p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\}
.

Now let
A
be an arbitrary algebra from
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}^{\ast}[X]]
, that is the matrix
\textsf{{M}}:=\langle\textsf{{A}},\Delta\rangle
belongs to
\mathcal{E}^{\ast}[X]
. Let us consider a map

f:\{p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\}\longrightarrow|\textsf{{A}}|.

We define a valuation
v
in
A
as follows:

v:p\mapsto f(p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)),

for each
p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

According to Proposition
3.2.1
,
v
can be extended to a homomorphism (denoted also by
v
) of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
into
A
. It must be obvious that

v[\alpha]=f(\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)),

for any
\alpha\in|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}|
.

We notice that

\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)\subseteq\text{ker}(v).

Indeed, suppose
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
. Then

\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)=\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
and, hence,
v[\alpha]=v[\beta]
.

Adapting the definition (
2.5
) to our situation, we obtain:

\text{ker}(v)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)=\{(\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X),\beta/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X))\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha,\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ v[\alpha]=v[\beta]\}.

From Section
2.3
, we know that
\text{ker}(v)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
is a congruence on

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
.

Next, we define the following maps:

\begin{array}[]{l}g:\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)\mapsto(\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X))/\penalty(\text{ker}(v)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X));\\
\\
h:(\alpha/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X))/\penalty(\text{ker}(v)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X))\mapsto\alpha/\penalty\text{ker}(v);\\
\\
j:\alpha/\penalty\text{ker}(v)\mapsto v[\alpha].\end{array}

The map
g
is obviously a homomorphism; in virtue of Proposition
2.3.6
,
h
is an isomorphism; according to Proposition
2.3.4
,
j
is a homomorphism. Then, we define:

\overline{f}:=j\circ h\circ g.

It is obvious that
\overline{f}
is a homomorphism of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)
into
A
.

Finally, we observe:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\overline{f}(p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X))&=j(h(g(p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X))))\\
&=j(h((p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X))/\penalty(\text{ker}(v)/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X))))\\
&=j(p/\penalty\text{ker}(v))\\
&=v[p]=f(p/\penalty\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(X)).\end{array}

Thus
\overline{f}
is an extension of
f
.
∎

Exercises
8.3

1.

Prove Lemma
8.3.1
.

2.

Prove the equivalence (
8.10
).

8.4
Interconnection between
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}

In this section we show that there is an interconnection between abstract logics
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
, where substitution play a key role.

We begin with expanding the operator
Sb
(Section
3.1
) from the set
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
to
\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Namely, for any
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we define:

\textbf{Sb}X:=\{\sigma(\alpha)\approx\sigma(\beta)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sigma\in\mathbf{Sbs}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \alpha\approx\beta\in X\}.

Proposition 8.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a nonempty class of E-matrices with
\mathbf{S}(\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}])\subseteq\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
. Then for any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon.

Proof.

Suppose that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}\epsilon
. That is, for any
\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}
,

\textsf{{M}}\models X\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon.

Now let us take an arbitrary
\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}
and valuation
v_{0}
in
A
, the algebra of
M
. Assume that
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v_{0}}\textbf{Sb}X
. Next we denote:

\textsf{{A}}_{0}:=[\{v_{0}[p]\penalty\ |\penalty\ p\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\}]_{\textsf{{A}}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}_{0}:=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{0},\Delta\rangle.

Since
\textsf{{A}}_{0}
is a subalgebra of
A
, by premise,
\textsf{{A}}_{0}\in\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
and, hence,
\textsf{{M}}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}
. Also, it is clear that
\textsf{{M}}_{0}\models_{v_{0}}\textbf{Sb}X
.

Now, we aim to show that
\textsf{{M}}_{0}\models X
. Let
v
be any valuation in
\textsf{{A}}_{0}
. According to Proposition
3.2.4
, there is a substitution
\sigma
such that
v=v_{0}\circ\sigma
. This implies that for any
\eta\in X
,
v[\eta]=v_{0}[\sigma(\eta)]
. Since
\sigma(\eta)\in\textbf{Sb}X
, then, by premise,
v[\eta]\in\Delta
.

Using (
8.4.1
–
\ast
), we derive that
\textsf{{M}}_{0}\models\epsilon
; in particular,
\textsf{{M}}_{0}\models_{v_{0}}\epsilon
. The latter implies that
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v_{0}}\epsilon
.

Conversely, assume that
\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon
. Let us take an arbitrary
\textsf{{M}}\in\mathcal{E}
and suppose (as a premise) that
\textsf{{M}}\models X
. Then, let us take any valuation
v_{1}
in
M
and any equality
\eta\in\textbf{Sb}X
. By definition, there is a substitution
\sigma
such that
\eta=\sigma(\zeta)
, for some
\zeta\in X
. Now we define:
v_{2}:=v_{1}\circ\sigma
. Since
v_{2}
is a valuation in
M
and, by premise,
v_{2}[\zeta]\in\Delta
,
v_{1}[\eta]\in\Delta
. The latter means that
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v_{1}}\textbf{Sb}X
. By premise, we obtain that
\textsf{{M}}\models_{v_{1}}\epsilon
.
∎

8.5
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
-models

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a class of E-matrices of type
\mathcal{L}
and let
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
be an abstract logic of equational L-consequence which is determined by
\mathcal{E}
. An E-matrix
M
of type
\mathcal{L}
is an
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
-
model
if for any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\epsilon
implies

\textsf{{M}}\models X\Longrightarrow\textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon.

It is important to note that the class of all
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
-models can be larger than the class
\mathcal{E}
. And it is the class of all
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
-models should be taken into account if, in the light of the conception of separating tools (Section
5.3
), we aim to refute
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\epsilon
. For if
M
is an E-matrix such that
\textsf{{M}}\models X
and
\textsf{{M}}\not\models\epsilon
, then
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\epsilon
.

With this in mind, we define:

\textbf{Mod}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X,\epsilon):=\{\textsf{{M}}\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\models X\Rightarrow\textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon\},

where, we remind,
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
is the class of all E-matrices of type
\mathcal{L}
;

\textbf{Mod}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X):=\{\textsf{{M}}\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\models X\};

and as a particular case of the last definition

\textbf{Mod}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\epsilon):=\{\textsf{{M}}\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\models\epsilon\}.

(These definitions imitate definitions in Section
4.3
.)

Applying Proposition
4.3.10
to the new definitions above, we obtain the equality:

\textbf{Mod}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X,\epsilon)=\textbf{Mod}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\epsilon)\cup(\hat{\mathcal{E}}\setminus\textbf{Mod}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X)).

(8.11)

The equality (
8.11
) is especially useful if we intend to separated
X
from
\epsilon
by an E-matrix. With this in mind, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\textsf{{M}}\notin\textbf{Mod}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X,\epsilon)&\Longleftrightarrow\textsf{{M}}\in\textbf{Mod}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(X)\setminus\textbf{Mod}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}(\epsilon)\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\textsf{{M}}\models X\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \textsf{{M}}\not\models\epsilon;\\
&\text{applying Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:EQ[M]=EQ[{M}_si]}, we obtain:}\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\{\textsf{{M}}\}\models X\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\{\textsf{{M}}\}\not\models\epsilon.\\
\end{array}

Thus an E-matrix
M
separates
X
from
\epsilon
if, and only if, every equality of
X
is valid in each matrix of
\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\{\textsf{{M}}\}
and there is a matrix in
\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\{\textsf{{M}}\}
which rejects
\epsilon
. We remind the the class
\mathbf{H}_{\textbf{si}}\{\textsf{{M}}\}
consists of those E-matrices whose carriers are subdirectly irreducible homomorphic images of the carrier of
M
. (Proposition
2.3.12
)

8.6
Examples of logics
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}

We consider two examples — the L-equational consequence determined by the class
\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}
and by the class
\mathcal{E}_{\text{H}}
of Sections
7.6.1
and
7.6.2
, respectively. We denote the corresponding L-equational logics by
\mathcal{E}_{\text{BL}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}
, respectively.

Let
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. It is clear that
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}_{\text{BL}}[X]]
is a variety of Boolean algebras. According to Proposition
2.4.3
, either all algebras of
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}_{\text{BL}}[X]]
are degenerate or
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}_{\text{BL}}[X]]=\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}]
. Thus, in the second case,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{BL}}}\epsilon
if, and only if,
\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}\models\epsilon
. And the latter is equivalent to that a 2-element Boolean E-matrix validates
\epsilon
.
The problem is that if
X
is infinite, we may not know what our case is. Of course, if
X
is finite, then it is easy to check whether
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]
is degenerate or not, since
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}[X]
is not degenerate if, and only, if it contains a 2-element Boolean algebra as its subalgebra, and the last condition is equivalent to whether all equalities of
X
are validated by the 2-element Boolean algebra.

Our next example is the abstract logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}
. Given a set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we obtain a variety
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}[X]]
of Heyting algebras. First of all, we note that there is a continuum of such varieties.
1
1

1

Cf.
[
Blok, 1977
]
and references therein.
Even if we restrict to the finite sets
X
, the problem whether
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}}\epsilon
is algorithmically undecidable; cf.
[
Shehtman, 1978
]
,
[
Chagrov and Zakharyaschev, 1997
]
, section 16.5, with reference to the previous footnote. And here it is appropriate to recall Proposition
8.3.2
and Proposition
2.4.6
. For in the hope to prove that

X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}}\epsilon
, in the light of Proposition
8.3.2
, we can try to find such an algebra
\textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}[X]]
that the matrix

\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\Delta\rangle
refutes
\epsilon
. Such an algebra
A
must be a homomorphic image of
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}
(Proposition
8.3.2
), so all equalities of
X
are validated by
M
(Proposition
2.4.6
).

8.7
L-Equational consequence based on

implicational logic

In this section we use some notions and their notation of Section
7.6
. As in that section, let
\mathcal{S}
be an implicational logic with respect to
\bm{\iota}(p,q)
in a language
\mathcal{L}
.

Let
\sigma
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{L}
-substitution. Then the following equalities are obvious:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\sigma(\bm{\iota}(\alpha,\beta))&=\bm{\iota}(\sigma(\alpha),\sigma(\beta)),\\
\textbf{i}(\sigma(\alpha\approx\beta))=\textbf{i}(\sigma(\alpha)\approx\sigma(\beta))&=\{\bm{\iota}(\sigma(\alpha),\sigma(\beta)),\bm{\iota}(\sigma(\beta),\sigma(\alpha))\},\\
&=\sigma(\textbf{i}(\alpha\approx\beta)).\end{array}

This implies that for any set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

(\sigma(X))^{\textbf{i}}=(\sigma(X^{\textbf{i}}))

and hence

(\textbf{Sb}X)^{\textbf{i}}=\textbf{Sb}X^{\textbf{i}}.

(8.12)

Similarly, for the function
s
, we have:

\textbf{s}(\sigma(\alpha\approx\beta))=\textbf{s}(\sigma(\alpha)\approx\sigma(\beta))=\sigma(\alpha)\leftrightarrow\sigma(\beta).

This in turn implies that for any set
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

(\sigma(X))^{\textbf{s}}=(\sigma(X^{\textbf{s}}))

and hence

(\textbf{Sb}X)^{\textbf{s}}=\textbf{Sb}X^{\textbf{s}}.

(8.13)

Proposition 8.7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an implicational logic with respect to
\bm{\iota}(p,q)
in
\mathcal{L}
. Also, let
\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}
be an L-equational logic with
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
such that
\{\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathbf{1}\rangle\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textsf{{A}}\in\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]\}=\mathbb{Q}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Then for any
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \textbf{i}(\epsilon)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}(X^{\textbf{i}}).

Further, if
\mathcal{S}
is implicative and implicational with respect to
p\rightarrow q
, and, in addition, if
\mathcal{S}
is sound with respect to the inferences rules (
a
–
i
) and (
b
–
i
)
(
Section
4.3.5
)
, then for
any
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\epsilon\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon).

(8.14)

Proof.

The first part is proved as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rl}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\epsilon&\Longleftrightarrow\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\quad[\text{by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:interconnection-E-and-EL}}]\\
\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\textbf{i}(\epsilon)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}((\textbf{Sb}X)^{\textbf{i}})\quad[\text{by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:equational-on-implicational-1}}]\\
\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\textbf{i}(\epsilon)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}}(\textbf{Sb}X^{\textbf{i}})\quad[\text{by\penalty\ \eqref{E:EL-based-on-implicational}}]\\
\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\textbf{i}(\epsilon)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}(X^{\textbf{i}})\quad[\text{by definition\penalty\ \eqref{E:def-of-aLogSub}}].\end{array}

Next, we turn to the second part. Indeed, we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rl}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{\L}}}\epsilon&\Longleftrightarrow\textbf{Sb}X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}}\epsilon\quad[\text{by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:interconnection-E-and-EL}}]\\
\\
&\Longleftrightarrow(\textbf{Sb}X)^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon)\quad[\text{by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{P:equational-on-implicational-1}}]\\
\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\textbf{Sb}X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon)\quad[\text{by\penalty\ \eqref{E:EL-based-on-implicational-2}}]\\
\\
&\Longleftrightarrow X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\text{sub}}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon)\quad[\text{by definition\penalty\ \eqref{E:def-of-aLogSub}}].\end{array}

∎

Below we consider two examples.

First, let us consider the two abstract logics —
Cl
and
\textsf{Cl}^{\star}
(Section
4.3.5
). The former logic, being formulated in the language
\mathcal{L}_{A}
, is implicative and implicational with respect to
p\rightarrow q
. (See Exercise
6.2
.
2
and Exercise
6.3
.
1
.)
On the other hand, the abstract logic
\textsf{Cl}^{\star}
is not even structural (Exercise
4.4
.
3
) However, in view of the second part of Proposition
8.7.1
and Proposition
4.4.2
, we obtain the following.

Proposition 8.7.2
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{BL}}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}_{\text{sub}}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon);\\
(\text{c})&X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}^{\star}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon).\end{array}

In view if Proposition
4.4.3
and the equivalence of (a) and (c) of Proposition
8.7.2
, we conclude that there are only two
\mathcal{E}_{\text{BL}}
-theories — the set of equalities
\epsilon
such that
\textbf{s}(\epsilon)
is a classical tautology and the set
\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. Therefore, for finite sets
X
, the problem whether
X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{BL}}}\epsilon
can be easily decided; this problem even is algorithmically decidable. (See Chapter
10
for other applications of the last concept.)

Next, we define an abstract logic
\textsf{Int}^{\star}
that is based on the abstract logic
Int
(Section
6.2.2
). The
\textsf{Int}^{\star}
-theories are known as
superintuitionistic logics
. Using the same argumentation as in the proof of Proposition
8.7.2
, we get the corresponding proposition in this case.

Proposition 8.7.3
.

For any set
X\cup\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}}\epsilon;\\
(\text{b})&X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}_{\text{sub}}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon);\\
(\text{c})&X^{\textbf{s}}\vdash_{\textsf{Int}^{\star}}\textbf{s}(\epsilon).\end{array}

The last proposition shows that the abstract logic
\mathcal{E}_{\text{BL}}
appears to be complicated. One of the reasons of its analysis is that, similarly to the logic
\textsf{Int}^{\star}
, in view of the equivalence of (a) and (c) of Proposition
8.7.3
, the cardinality of
\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}
-theories equals
2^{\textit{card}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}})}
, providing that
\textit{card}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}})\geq\aleph_{0}
.
2
2

2

See the footnote on p.
1
.
On the other hand, the problem of characterizing some of the

\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}
-theories seem to be quite difficult. One of the examples of such a theory is the
\mathcal{E}_{\text{HL}}
-theory associated with
Medvedev’s logic
which is a finitely approximable but not finitely axiomatizable
\textsf{Int}^{\star}
-theory.
3
3

3

About Medvedev’s logic, see
[
Chagrov and Zakharyaschev, 1997
]
, section 2.9.

8.8
Historical notes

An L-equational consequence first appeared in
[
Birkhoff, 1935
]
.
However, Birkhoff’s approach is not as direct as ours, as well as the ones shown in
[
Pigozzi, 1975
]
, section 1.3, in
[
burris-sankappan81
]
, chapter II, §14, and in
[
Burris, 1998
]
, chapter 3. Therefore, it would be interesting to look at the Birkhoff approach from our point of view on the equational L-consequence.

First of all, Birkhoff does not distinguish E-matrices and their algebras. To facilitate our analysis, in this section, we do not do it too. This allows as to use the phrases such as ‘a homomorphic image of an E-matrix’, ‘a subalgebra of an E-matrix’, and ‘the direct product of a family of E-matrices’. Also, the classes
\mathcal{A}[\mathcal{E}]
and
\mathcal{E}
will not be distinguished.

One of the difficulties of reading
[
Birkhoff, 1935
]
is that he does not make a clear distinction between substitution as an operation on terms and a valuation in algebra. In this regard, we must remember that any valuation in the formula algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
is a substitution.

The third remark, before we begin our analysis, is that Birkhoff deals mainly with
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}
, that is, when the class of algebras (of E-matrices) contains all algebras of type
\mathcal{L}
; and he uses
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\hat{\mathcal{E}}
only to form the variety
\textbf{EQ}_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathcal{E}]
or even the lattice of all such varieties. It was this trend that influenced the further development of Birkhoff’s “equational logic”; cf.
[
Tarski, 1968
,
Pigozzi, 1975
,
Taylor, 1979
]
.

The equivalence between formally inferred equalities from a given set
X
and those which follow from
X
in the logic
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}
is established by Birkhoff in the first statement of Theorem 9. As a formal system, Birkhoff uses E1s. For convenience, we denote:

\textbf{Cn}_{\text{E1s}}(X):=\{\epsilon\in\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ X\vdash_{\text{E1s}}\epsilon\},

where
X\subseteq\textbf{Eq}_{\mathcal{L}}
. We note that we do not claim at this moment that
\textbf{Cn}_{\text{E1s}}
is a consequence operator, although afterwards it is; see Remark
7.3.1
. Thus, the first statement of Theorem 9 reads:

\epsilon\in\textbf{Cn}_{\text{E1s}}(X)\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \hat{\mathcal{E}}[X]\models\epsilon.

(8.15)

The
\Rightarrow
-implication of (
8.15
) is essentially the
\Rightarrow
-implication of Proposition
8.2.4
, that is the soundness of
\vdash_{\text{E1s}}
.

Turning to the
\Leftarrow
-implication of (
8.15
), we first recall that for any substitution
\sigma
,

\epsilon\in\textbf{Cn}_{\text{E1s}}(X)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \sigma(\epsilon)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\text{E1s}}(X).

(8.16)

Further, since
\hat{\mathcal{E}}[X]
is an equational class (or a variety), it contains the algebra
\textbf{LT}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}[X]
(see (
8.6
) and Definition
8.3.1
) which is a free algebra in this variety (Proposition
8.3.2
).
4
4

4

Namely (
8.16
) is used to show that
\textbf{LT}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}[X]
validates all equalities of
X
.
This implies that if

\epsilon\notin\textbf{Cn}_{\text{E1s}}(X)
, then, by definition,
\textbf{LT}_{\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{L}}}[X]
refutes
\epsilon
.

The proof of completeness theorem in the aforementioned works
[
Pigozzi, 1975
]
,
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, and
[
Burris, 1998
]
is similar to ours, where, E2s is used as a formal system. In all these cases, it is demonstrated that the rule E1–
c
is derivable in E2.

Perhaps, under the influence of Tarski for many years, studies of equational L-consequence were limited to considering equational classes, or varieties, of algebras of the same similarity type. Tarski wrote in
[
Tarski, 1968
]
, section 1:

“Two system of equational logic differ only in operational symbols.”

The focus, then, was on equational theories, where

“[by] the
equational theory
of an algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, or a class
K
of algebras, we understand the set of all equations, in the system of logic determined by [a similarity type]
\sigma
, which are identically satisfied in the algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, or in all algebras of
K
.”
Ibid

Thus, in our notation, given a nonempty class
\mathcal{E}
of E-matrices, the algebras of which form a variety, all questions are about the set
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{E}_{\text{L}}}(\varnothing)
; for instance: whether it is decidable?
whether it consists of the equalities derivable from its finite subset (
finitely based theories
)? and some others. The reader can find a detailed discussion of these issues in
[
Pigozzi, 1975
]
and
[
Taylor, 1979
]
.

A. Selman in
[
Selman, 1972
]
goes even further from the L-equational consequence.
He uses the format of Definition
8.3.1
to prove a Birkhoff-style completeness result for any set
X
of quasi-equalities as premises and a quasi-equality
\eta_{1}\land\ldots\land\eta_{n}\rightarrow\epsilon
as a conclusion.

Chapter 9
\mathcal{Q}
-Consequence

9.1
\mathcal{Q}
-Languages

\mathcal{Q}
-languages, as the reader will see below, admit logical constants of a new type. This, however, requires a restructuring of the whole grammar of the formation of judgment. Therefore, we will start with a new formal language and its grammar. As in Section
3.1
, we deal most of the time with a schematic

\mathcal{Q}
-language which we denote by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. This language includes the (
schematic
)
language of terms
,
\mathcal{L}_{T}
, which is extended to the
language of formulas
, which is denoted by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. In this language, we designate a sublanguage,
\mathcal{L}_{F}
, which includes only logical connectives and no quantifiers.

All grammatical categories are listed below as follows.

•

a denumerable (or infinitely countable) set
\mathcal{V}_{T}
of
bound
(individual)
variables
that are ordered by type of
\omega
:

x_{1},\penalty\ x_{2},\penalty\ \ldots;

(9.1)

unspecified bound variables are denoted by the letters
x,y
with or without subscripts; sometimes, we will need to use finite lists of bound variables, they are denoted by over-lined letters like
\overline{x},\ldots
;

•

a denumerable (or infinitely countable) set
\mathcal{U}_{T}
of
free
(individual)
variables
(called also
parameters
) that are ordered by type of
\omega
:

u_{1},\penalty\ u_{2},\penalty\ \ldots;

(9.2)

unspecified free variables are denoted by
u
with or without superscript or with an apostrophe or asterisk; finite (maybe void) lists of bound variables are denoted by over-lined letters like
\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}},\ldots
;

•

a set (maybe empty) of
function symbols
is denoted by
\mathcal{F}_{T}
and the elements of
\mathcal{F}_{T}
by letters
f,g
(with or without subscripts); a nonnegative integer number is assigned to each element
f\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
; this number is called the
arity
of
f
; we denote the arity of
f
by
\#(f)
; the function symbols of arity
0
, if any, are called (individual)
constants
; the set of all constants is denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{T}
(maybe empty); thus
\mathcal{C}_{T}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{T}
;

•

a nonempty set
\mathcal{P}_{F}
of
predicate symbols
which will be denoted by letters
p,q,\ldots
(with or without subscripts); each predicate symbol
p
also has an arity which is greater than or equal to
1
and is denoted by
\#(p)
; the equality symbol ‘
\approx
’ may or may not be in
\mathcal{L}_{F}
; but if it is present, its arity equals
2
;

•

a set (maybe empty)
\mathcal{F}_{F}
of
logical connectives
; unspecified logical connectives will be denoted by letters
F,G,H
; each logical connective has an arity whose value is a nonnegative integer; a logical connective of arity
0
is called a
logical constant
; the set of logical constants (maybe empty) is denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{F}
; thus
\mathcal{C}_{F}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{F}
;

•

a nonempty set
\mathcal{Q}
of (
generalized
)
quantifiers
; each quantifier
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
has an arity greater than
0
.

•

as usual, the parentheses, ‘(’ and ‘)’, are used as punctuation marks.

All these categories, except quantifiers, are the categories of language
\mathcal{L}_{F}
.

The next definition addresses three concepts; the first one, the concept of a
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-
term expression
, is an auxiliary one; it will, however, help us define two other concepts.

Definition 9.1.1

(
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-prototerm and
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-term)
.

\mathcal{L}_{T}
-prototerms are defined inductively by the following clauses:

(a)

each element of the set
\mathcal{V}_{T}\cup\mathcal{U}_{T}\cup\mathcal{C}_{T}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-prototerm;

(b)

if
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
are
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-prototerms and
f\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
has arity
n\geq 1
, then
ft_{1}\ldots t_{n}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-prototerm;

(c)

the
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-prototerms are only those expressions that can be constructed by (a) and (b).

An
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-
term
is an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-prototerm which does not contain bound variables.

The set of all terms is denoted by
\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
.

Similar to the definition of the formula algebra of type
\mathcal{L}
of Section
3.2
,
we define a
term algebra
of type
\mathcal{L}_{T}
, whose carrier is
\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
and the fundamental operations are the function symbols of
\mathcal{F}_{T}
(including the constants of
\mathcal{C}_{T}
). We denote this algebra by
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
; that is,

\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}:=\langle\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}};\mathcal{F}_{F}\rangle.

It is allowed to substitute (in the sense of Section
3.1
) only terms in those prototerms that contain free variables and only for free variables.
Thus, although an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-
substitution
can be regarded as a map
\sigma:\mathcal{U}_{T}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
which can, in the usual way, be extended to a homomorphism (denoted by the same symbol)
\sigma:\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
, we also allow the situations when substitutions apply to prototerms, setting by definition for any
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution
\sigma
,

\sigma(t(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{m}})):=t(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\sigma(u_{i_{1}}),\ldots,\sigma(u_{i_{m}})),

where
x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}
are all bound variables and
u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{m}}
are all parameters that occur in a prototerm
t
.

The
identity substitution
is denoted by
\iota
; that is,

\iota(t)=t,

for any prototerm
t
.

Given an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution
\sigma
, we will often use another substitution which differs from
\sigma
at finitely many elements of its domain.

Let
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
be a nonvoid list of pairwise distinct free variables,
\overline{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
be a list of terms, not necessarily pairwise distinct, and
\sigma
be an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution. Then we define:

\delta_{(\sigma,\overline{u}\backslash\overline{t})}(u^{\prime}):=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}t_{k}&\text{if $u^{\prime}=u_{i_{k}}$}\\
\sigma(u^{\prime})&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\end{cases}

Also, just for given
\overline{u}
and
\overline{t}
as above, we define an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution:

\sigma_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{t}}(u^{\prime}):=\begin{cases}t_{k}&\text{if $u^{\prime}=u_{i_{k}}$}\\
u^{\prime}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If
\overline{u}=(u)
and
\overline{t}=(t)
, we will simply write
\sigma_{u\backslash t}
.

In the sequel, we will often use the notation:

\sigma(\overline{t}):=(\sigma(t_{1}),\ldots,\sigma(t_{n}));

in particular,

\sigma(\overline{u})=(\sigma(u_{i_{1}}),\ldots,\sigma(u_{i_{n}})).

Our next concepts are the (auxiliary) concept of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
formula expression
,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-protoformula and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula. These concepts are based on the signature that includes logical connectives and quantifiers of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. However, it will be convenient to refer to the logical connectives of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
as the
signature of type

\mathcal{L}_{F}
.

Definition 9.1.2

(
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-protoformula, free occurrences of bound variables, and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula)
.

The
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula expressions can be built up according to the following clauses:

(a)

p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
is an
(
atomic
)

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
protoformula
if
p
is a predicate symbol of arity
n
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
are
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-prototerms; all occurrences of bound variables, if any, that occur in
p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})

occur freely
in it, or are
free occurrences
;

(b)

if
F
is a logical connective of arity
n
and
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
are
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-protoformulas, then
FA_{1}\ldots A_{n}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
protoformula
; all bound variables that occur freely in at least one of the protoformulas
A_{i}
,
occur freely
also in
FA_{1}\ldots A_{n}
;

(c)

if
Q
is a quantifier of arity
n
and

A
is a protoformula that contains free occurrences of bound variables
x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}
, then
Qx_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}A
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
protoformula
, in which case
A
is called the
scope

of
Qx_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}
; the free occurrences of bound variables that occur freely in
A
, but are not listed among
x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}
, are
free occurrences
of the protoformula
Qx_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}A
;

(d)

the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
protoformulas
are only those expressions which can be built up by clauses (a)–(c);
free occurrences
of bound variables in a protoformula are only those that can be determined by clauses (a)–(c).

An
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula expression
A
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
formula
if each bound variable occurring in
A
is in the scope of some quantifier; in other words, a protoformula
A
if a formula if it has no free occurrences of bound variables or, possibly, no bound variables at all.

The set of all
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas is denoted by
\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Let
A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
be a protoformula whose free occurrences of bound variables are
x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}
and whose free variables are
u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}}
. Then, denoting

\overline{x}:=(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}})
and
\overline{u}:=(u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
, we occasionally write
A(\overline{x},\overline{u})
or
A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u})
instead of
A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
.

Let
A(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
be a formula containing free variables
u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}
(pairwise distinct), written also as
A(\overline{u})
. Also, let
x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}
be
n
bound variables which do not occur in

A(\overline{u})
. The expression
A(u_{i_{1}}\backslash x_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\backslash x_{i_{n}})
, written also as
A(\overline{u}\backslash\overline{x})
or as

A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}})
or as
A(\overline{x})
, is a protoformula which is obtained by the simultaneous replacement of all occurrences of each
u_{i_{k}}

with
x_{i_{k}}
, respectively. On the other hand, if
\overline{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
are
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-terms (not necessarily pairwise distinct), we denote by
A(x_{i_{1}}\backslash t_{1},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}\backslash t_{n})
, or by
A(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
or by
A(\overline{t})
, the formula obtained by the simultaneous replacement of each occurrence of
x_{i_{k}}
in
A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}})
with
t_{k}
. The same resulting formula,

A(u_{i_{1}}\backslash t_{1},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\backslash t_{n})
, written also as
A(\overline{u}\backslash\overline{t})
, is obtained by substitution of each term
t_{k}
for all occurrences of the free variable
u_{i_{k}}
in the formula
A(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
.

Thus, if
\sigma
is an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution such that
\sigma(u_{i_{k}})=t_{k}
, then we have:

A(\sigma(u_{i_{1}}),\ldots,\sigma(u_{i_{n}}))=A(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})).

This induces the following definition:

\sigma(A(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})):=A(\sigma(u_{i_{1}}),\ldots,\sigma(u_{i_{n}})).

(9.3)

It is obvious that for the identity substitution
\iota
, we have:

\iota(A)=A.

If
X
is a set of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas and
\sigma
is an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution,
we denote:

\sigma(X):=\{\sigma(A)\penalty\ |\penalty\ A\in X\}.

Similar to the definition of the term algebra
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
(or the formula algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{L}}
from Section
3.2
), we define the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
formula algebra

\mathfrak{F}_{Q}
as follows:

\mathfrak{F}_{Q}:=\langle\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}};\mathcal{F}_{F}\rangle.

In the sequel, we will expand
\mathfrak{F}_{Q}
by adding infinite operations corresponding to quantifiers. Also, in the sequel, along with
\mathfrak{F}_{Q}
, we will use its unital expansion.

9.2
Semantics of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}

An algebra
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
is called a
term structure
, or
t
-
structure
for short. In particular,
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}=\langle\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}},\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
is a
t
-structure.

Given a
t
-structure
\mathfrak{A}=\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
, a map

v:\mathcal{U}_{T}\longrightarrow\textsf{A}
which we extend to a homomorphism (denoted by the same letter)
v:\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\longrightarrow\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
is called a
term valuation

(or
t
-
valuation
for short) in the algebra
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
. For each term
t\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
,
we denote by
v[t]
the value of
t
with respect to
v
. The set of the values of all
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-terms is denoted by
v[\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}]
.

We note that any
t
-valuation in
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution, and vice versa. Also,
given an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution
\sigma
and a
t
-valuation in a term structure
\mathfrak{A}
,
v\circ\sigma
is also a
t
-valuation in
\mathfrak{A}
.

In the sequel, we will occasionally use the following notation:

v[\overline{t}]:=(v[t_{1}],\ldots,v[t_{n}]),

where
\overline{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
is a list of
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-terms.

Let
v
be a
t
-valuation in a
t
-structure
\mathfrak{A}=\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
and let
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
, where all parameters of
\overline{u}
are pairwise distinct. Also, let
\overline{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\textsf{A}^{n}
. We will often use the following
t
-valuation related to
v
,
\overline{u}
and
\overline{a}
:

w_{(v,\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a})}[u^{\prime}]:=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}a_{k}&\text{if $u^{\prime}=u_{i_{k}}$}\\
v[u^{\prime}]&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\end{cases}

In addition, given
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
, where the parameters
u_{i_{k}}
are pairwise distinct, and
\overline{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
is a list of
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-terms, we will sometimes use a
t
-valuation:

w_{(v,\overline{u}\backslash\overline{t})}[u^{\prime}]:=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}v[t_{k}]&\text{if $u^{\prime}=u_{i_{k}}$}\\
v[u^{\prime}]&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\end{cases}

If
\overline{u}=(u)
and
\overline{t}=(t)
, we simply write
w_{(v,u\backslash t)}
.

Also, we define a
restricted
\bm{t}
-valuation
as follows.

Assume that all parameters of a term
t
are among
u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{m}}
and
\overline{a}=(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{m}})
where each
a_{i_{k}}\in\textsf{A}
. Then we define
w_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a}}[t]
by induction on the complexity of
t
:

•

w_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a}}[t]=a_{i_{k}}
if
t=u_{i_{k}}
;

•

w_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a}}[t]=f(w_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a}}[t_{1}],\ldots,w_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a}}[t_{n}])

if
t=ft_{1}\ldots t_{n}
.

Definition 9.2.1

(
\mathcal{Q}
-structure)
.

A
\mathcal{Q}
-
structure
of type
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is a system

\mathfrak{A}=\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{F}\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}\rangle
, where
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
is a
t
-structure,
\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{F}\rangle
is an algebra of type
\mathcal{L}_{F}
; further,
each
p\in\mathcal{P}_{F}
with
\#(p)=n
(
n\geq\!1
) is associated with a same-name, but bold, map

\bm{p}:\textsf{A}^{n}\longrightarrow\textsf{B}
, and each quantifier
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
with
\#(Q)=n
(
n\geq\!1
) is associated with a map
\varphi_{Q}\in\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}
that maps each set

\{\bm{q}_{(A(\overline{x}),v)}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\penalty\ |\penalty\ (a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\textsf{A}^{n}\}

to an element of
B
, where
\bm{q}_{(A(\overline{x}),v)}
is a map from
\textsf{A}^{n}
into
B
, for each protoformula
A(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{n}})
with free occurrences of exactly
n
bound variables
\overline{x}=(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{n}})
. (See definition below.)

Remark 9.2.1
.

Definition
9.2.1
does not specify what
\varphi_{Q}
is, but it indicates that every
\varphi_{Q}
is an infinite operation which is defined on a subset of
B
which depends on the values of
\bm{q}_{(A(\overline{x}),v)}
; the latter in turn depends on a protoformula
A(\overline{x})
and a
t
-valuation
v
. Also, as we show below, the mappings
\bm{q}_{(A(\overline{x}),v)}
depend not only on protoformulas
A(\overline{x})
, but also on the mapping
\bm{p}
.

Given a
\mathcal{Q}
-structure
\mathfrak{A}=\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{F}\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}\rangle
and a (restricted or full) term valuation
v
in
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
, we define a
formula valuation
(or
f
-
valuation
for short) of all
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas in the
\mathcal{Q}
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
as a
map
\hat{v}:\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}\longrightarrow\textsf{B}
. The definition of a formula valuation
\hat{v}
is grounded on the complexity of each
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula.

The following notation will be used below. Let
A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}})
be a protoformula whose free occurrences of bound variables are
\overline{x}=(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}})
and whose free variables, if any, are
\overline{u^{\ast}}=(u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
. We assume that the protoformula
A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}})
is obtained from a formula
A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})
, where
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
, by replacement of each
u_{i_{k}}
with
x_{i_{k}}
. Thus all parameters of the last formula partitioned in two lists,
\overline{u}
and
\overline{u^{\ast}}
, where the last list can be empty.

Now we turn to the definition of a formula valuation. In the course of this definition, we introduce two more functions,

\bm{p}_{A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})}:\textsf{A}^{n+m}\longrightarrow\textsf{B}
, where
\overline{u^{\ast}}
can be void and thus
m=0
,
and
\bm{q}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}:\textsf{A}^{n}\longrightarrow\textsf{B}
.

If
A(\overline{u})=p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
, where
p\in\mathcal{P}_{F}
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
are terms, whose parameters are among
u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}
; then

•

\hat{v}[A(\overline{u})]:=\bm{p}(v[t_{1}],\ldots,v[t_{k}])
;

•

\bm{p}_{A(\overline{u})}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}):=\bm{p}(w_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a}}[t_{1}],\ldots,w_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a}}[t_{k}])
,
where
w
is a restricted valuation whose domain is
\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}
,
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
and
\overline{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
.

Next, let
A(\overline{u})=FA_{1}\ldots A_{k}
, where
F\in\mathcal{F}_{F}
and
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
are
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas. Then we define:

•

\hat{v}[FA_{1}\ldots A_{k}]:=F(\hat{v}[A_{1}],\ldots,\hat{v}[A_{k}])
;

•

\bm{p}_{A(\overline{u})}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}):=F(\bm{p}_{A_{1}(\overline{u})}(\overline{a}),\ldots,\bm{p}_{A_{k}(\overline{u})}(\overline{a}))
,
where
\overline{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
.

Further, let
A(\overline{u^{\ast}})=Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})
, where
Q
is a quantifier of arity
n
which precedes a protoformula
A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})
. We first obtain a formula
A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})
, where
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
and
\overline{u^{\ast}}=(u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
, by a simultaneous replacement of each occurrence of
x_{i_{k}}
with
u_{i_{k}}
, providing that the parameters of
\overline{u}
do not occur in
A(\overline{u^{\ast}})
. Then, we successively define:

•

\bm{q}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}):=\bm{p}_{A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},v[\overline{u^{\ast}}])
;
in particular,

\bm{q}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(v[t_{1}],\ldots,v[t_{n}])=\bm{p}_{A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(v[t_{1}],\ldots,v[t_{n}],v[\overline{u^{\ast}}]),

(9.4)

where
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
is a list of
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-terms which are not necessarily pairwise distinct;

•

\hat{v}[Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})]:=\varphi_{Q}(\{\bm{q}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(\overline{a})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{a}\in\textsf{A}^{n}\})
, where each
\varphi_{Q}
is an infinite (perhaps partial) operation which is defined on subsets of
B
and depends on the quantifier
Q
;

•

\bm{p}_{Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(b_{1},\ldots,b_{m}):=\widehat{w_{\overline{u^{\ast}}\backslash\overline{b}}}[Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})]
, where
\overline{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})\in\textsf{A}^{m}
.

Perhaps the last definition, consisting of several concepts, needs clarification.

Given a
\mathcal{Q}
structure
\mathfrak{A}
, it should be clear that an
f
-valuation
\hat{v}
in
\mathfrak{A}
depends on a
t
-valuation
v
in
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
, which is converted to
\hat{v}
through the maps
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
. The former, indexed with an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula
A(\overline{u})
which contains exactly free variables
u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}
arranged as
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
, is a map
\bm{p}_{A(\overline{u})}:\textsf{A}^{n}\longrightarrow\textsf{B}
, which makes
A(\overline{u})
a multiple-valued predicate of arity
n
defined on
A
with its values in
B
.
1
1

1

See Lemma
9.2.2
below.
If a formula
A
has no free variables,
\bm{p}_{A}
is an element of
B
. We have to note that
\bm{p}
and
\bm{p}_{A}
, where a formula
A
is with or without free variables, are not necessarily the same thing, since
\bm{p}
being associated with a predicate symbol
p
has arity
n\geq 1
, while
\bm{p}_{A}
may have the arity equal to
0
.

A map
\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}
, where
A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})
is a protoformula with free occurrences of exactly one bound variable
x
and
v
is a
t
-valuation, is needed to generate the set
\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(a)\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\}\subseteq\textsf{B}
, to which each of infinite unary operations,
\varphi_{\forall}
and
\varphi_{\exists}
, applies to obtain in
B
the values
\hat{v}[\forall xA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})]
or
\hat{v}[\exists xA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})]
, respectively.

Remark 9.2.2
.

Although
\bm{p}
in general depends on the carriers
A
and
B
, we use the same notation,
\bm{p}
, for the except of the cases when
\textsf{A}=\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
and
B
is
\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
or a quotient of the latter; in the first of the last cases, we use the notation
\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}
(defined in Example
9.2.1
below), and in the second
\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}
(defined in Section
9.5
). Accordingly,
we employ the notations:
\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}_{A(\overline{u})}
and
\bm{q}^{\mathbf{L}}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}
in the first case and
\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A(\overline{u})}
, and
\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}
in the second case.

Now we consider some properties pertaining to formula valuations.
The next lemma is a refinement of a definition above.

Lemma 9.2.1
.

Let
(A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})
be a formula whose parameters are partitioned in a list
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
and a list
(
maybe empty
)

\overline{u^{\ast}}=(u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
. Let
\overline{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
be an element of
\textsf{A}^{n}
. Then

\bm{p}_{A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},v[\overline{u^{\ast}}])=\bm{p}_{(A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(w_{(v,\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a})}[\overline{u}],w_{(v,\overline{u}\backslash\overline{a})}[\overline{u^{\ast}}]).

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
9.2
.
2
)

Lemma 9.2.2
.

Let
A(\overline{u})
be a formula, whose free variables are
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
. Then for any
t
-valuation
v
in an arbitrary
\mathcal{Q}
-structure,

\hat{v}[A(\overline{u})]=\bm{p}_{A(\overline{u})}(v[\overline{u}]).

Proof.

We use induction on the complexity of
A(\overline{u})
. We will consider the basis case, leaving the rest of the proof to the reader. (Exercise
9.2
.
3
)

Assume that
A(\overline{u})=p(t_{1}(\overline{u}),\ldots,t_{k}(\overline{u}))
, where
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
are
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-terms whose (free) variables are included in
\overline{u}
. By definition,

\hat{v}[A(\overline{u})]=\bm{p}(v[t_{1}],\ldots,v[t_{k}])=\bm{p}_{(A(\overline{u}),v)}(v[t_{1}],\ldots,v[t_{k}]).

∎

Lemma 9.2.3
.

Let
A(\overline{u})
be a formula, whose free variables are
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
, and let
\sigma
be an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution. Then for any
t
-valuation
v
in any
\mathcal{Q}
-structure,

\displaystyle\widehat{v\circ\sigma}[A(\overline{u})]=\hat{v}[\sigma(A(\overline{u}))]=\hat{v}[A(\sigma(\overline{u})].

Proof.

The proof of the statement can be carried out by induction on the complexity of
A(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
. We show the basis and leave the rest of the proof to the reader. (Exercise
9.2
.
4
)

Indeed, we successively obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\widehat{v\circ\sigma}[p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})]&=\penalty\ \bm{p}(v\circ\sigma[t_{1}],\ldots,v\circ\sigma[t_{n}])\\
&=\penalty\ \bm{p}(v[\sigma(t_{1})],\ldots,v[\sigma(t_{n})])\\
&=\penalty\ \hat{v}[p(\sigma(t_{1}),\ldots,\sigma(t_{n}))]\\
&=\penalty\ \hat{v}[\sigma(p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}))].\end{array}

∎

Definition 9.2.2

(logical
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix)
.

A
logical

\mathcal{Q}
-
matrix

(
or simply
\mathcal{Q}
-
matrix
)
of type
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is a system
\langle\mathfrak{A},D\rangle
, where
\mathfrak{A}=\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{F}\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}},\rangle
is a
\mathcal{Q}
-structure of type
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
D
, called a
logical filter
, is a nonempty subset of
B
. If
D
consists of a single element, we call such a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix
unital
. Denoting the element of
D
by
\mathbf{1}

(
perhaps with a subscript
)
in a unital
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices
\langle\mathfrak{A},D\rangle
, we then replace
\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{F}\rangle
in
\mathfrak{A}
with its unital expansion
\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{F},\mathbf{1}\rangle
and treat the
\mathcal{Q}
-structure

\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{F},\mathbf{1}\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}},\rangle
as a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix.

Now we will consider three examples. The first example is the most important because it will later be developed into the concept of the Lindenbaum
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix (Definition
9.4.1
) and then into the concept of the Lindenbaum-Tarski
\mathcal{Q}
-structure (Definition
9.5.1
). The importance of this example is also highlighted in Proposition
9.2.1
.

Example 9.2.1
.

As our first example, we consider a
\mathcal{Q}
-structure
\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}},\mathfrak{F}_{Q},\mathcal{P}_{F},\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}\rangle
, where by definition, for any
p\in\mathcal{P}
with
\#(p)=n
,
\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}:(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\mapsto p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
. Since any
t
-valuation in this structure is an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution, we fix an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution
\sigma
. Now we want to illustrate what
\hat{\sigma}[A(\overline{u})]
is in this
\mathcal{Q}
-structure under the valuation
\sigma
.

Assume that
A(\overline{u})=p(\overline{t})
with
\#(p)=k
, where
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
and
\overline{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
. Then we have:

•

\hat{\sigma}[p(\overline{t})]=p(\sigma(\overline{t}))
;

•

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}_{A(\overline{u})}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=p(\delta_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{s}}(t_{1}),\ldots,\delta_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{s}}(t_{k}))=\delta_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{s}}(p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}))
,

where

\overline{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}^{n}
.

Next, let
A(\overline{u})=FA_{1}\ldots A_{k}
. Then we have:

•

\hat{\sigma}[A(\overline{u})]=F(\hat{\sigma}[A_{1}],\ldots,\hat{\sigma}[A_{k}])
;

•

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}_{A(\overline{u})}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=F(\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}_{A_{1}(\overline{u})}(\overline{s}),\ldots,\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}_{A_{k}(\overline{u})}(\overline{s}))
;

Now, let
A(\overline{u^{\ast}})=Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})
and
A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})
be a formula, where
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
and
\overline{u^{\ast}}=(u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
, such that neither of
u_{i_{k}}
occurs in the protoformula
A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})
and all
u_{i_{k}}
are pairwise distinct. Then, defining

\bm{q}^{\mathbf{L}}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}):=\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}_{A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n},\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))

and

\displaystyle\varphi_{Q}(\{\bm{q}^{\mathbf{L}}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(\overline{s})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{s}\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}^{n}\}):=\sigma(Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})

\displaystyle=Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}})),

where
\varphi_{Q}\in\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}
, we successively obtain:

•

\hat{\sigma}[Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})]=\sigma(Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}});

•

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}_{Q\overline{x}A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}):=\widehat{\delta_{\overline{u^{\ast}}\backslash\overline{t}}}[Q\overline{x}A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}})]=\delta_{\overline{u^{\ast}}\backslash\overline{t}}(Q\overline{x}A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}))
.

Summarizing the definitions and their simplifications of the last example, we obtain the following.

Proposition 9.2.1
.

Let
\sigma
be an
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution,
A(\overline{u})
and
B(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})
be formulas such that
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
is the list of all parameters of the former and the parameters of the latter are partitioned in two lists —

\overline{u}
and
\overline{u^{\ast}}=(u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
Then the following hold in
\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}},\mathfrak{F}_{Q},\mathcal{P}_{F},\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}\rangle
:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\hat{\sigma}[A(\overline{u})]=\sigma(A(\overline{u}));\\
(\text{b})&\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}_{B(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n},\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))=\delta_{(\sigma,\overline{u}\backslash\overline{s})}(B(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}}));\\
(\text{c})&\bm{q}^{\mathbf{L}}_{(B(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=\delta_{(\sigma,\overline{u}\backslash\overline{s})}(B(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})).\end{array}

Proof.

We leave for the reader to check the statement. (Exercise
9.2
.
6
)
∎

Example 9.2.2
.

Suppose an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-language contains a nonempty set
\mathcal{F}_{T}
of function symbols, a nonempty set
\mathcal{P}_{F}
of predicate symbols, the binary logical connectives:
\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg
, and two unary quantifiers,
\forall
and
\exists
.
Consider a
\mathcal{Q}
-structure
\mathfrak{A}=\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle
, where
\langle\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\};\land,\lor,\rightarrow\rangle
is a 2-element Boolean algebra. Further, given a
t
-valuation
v
and a protoformula
A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})
containing free occurrences of only
x
, we define

\varphi_{\forall}(\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\}):=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{1}&\text{if $\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\}=\textsf{A}$}\\
\mathbf{0}&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\end{cases}

and

\varphi_{\exists}(\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(a)\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\}):=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{1}&\text{if $\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(a)\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\}\neq\varnothing$}\\
\mathbf{0}&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\end{cases}

As a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix, we use the unital expansion

\langle\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
.

It should be clear that the above maps
\varphi_{\forall}
and
\varphi_{\exists}
define the ordinary interpretation of the universal and existential quantifier, respectively, in an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-language exemplifying that of firs-order logic.

The next example may seem artificial.
2
2

2

See a remark in
[
Rosser and Turquette, 1951
]
, p. 51: “To the best of our knowledge, no one has so far found need for quantifiers with either
\beta_{i}>1
or
\gamma_{i}>1
. However, such quantifiers are distinctly possible
\ldots
”

Example 9.2.3
.

Suppose an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-language, besides the mandatory categories, contains only one predicate symbol
p
of arity 3 and one binary quantifier
Q
.
Let
\mathfrak{A}=\langle\langle\mathbb{Z}^{+};\varnothing\rangle,\langle\mathbb{Z}^{+};\varnothing\rangle,\{p\},\{\varphi_{Q}\}\rangle
, where
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is the set of positive integers, is a
\mathcal{Q}
-structure for this language, where the predicate symbol
p
is interpreted as the map
\bm{p}:(a,b,c)\mapsto\penalty\ \gcd(a,b,c)
, where ‘
\gcd(a,b,c)
’ stands for the greatest common divisor of
a,b,c
, and
\varphi_{Q}(X)
, if it is defined, is the maximal element of
X\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. It should be clear that the operation
\varphi_{Q}
is partial. However, since the set

\{\bm{q}_{(p(x,y,u),v)}(a,b)\penalty\ |\penalty\ (a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\times\mathbb{Z}^{+}\}=\{\bm{p}(a,b,v[u])\penalty\ |\penalty\ (a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\times\mathbb{Z}^{+}\}

is always finite, for any
t
-valuation
v
,

\hat{v}[Qxy\,p(x,y,u)]=\varphi_{Q}(\{\bm{q}_{(p(x,y,u),v)}(a,b)\penalty\ |\penalty\ (a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\times\mathbb{Z}^{+}\})=v[u].

More examples will be given in Section
9.6
.

The vocabulary and contents of the next definition are similar to those of Definition
3.2.5
.

Definition 9.2.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathfrak{A},D\rangle
be a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix of type
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. An
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula
A
is
satisfied
by a term valuation
v
if
\hat{v}[A]\in D
, in which case we say that
v

satisfies

A
in
\mathfrak{M}
, symbolically
\mathfrak{M}\models_{v}A
. Further, a formula
A
is
semantically valid

(
or simply
valid
)
in
\mathfrak{M}
, symbolically
\mathfrak{M}\models A
or
\models_{\mathfrak{M}}A
, if
\hat{v}[A]\in D
, for any term valuation
v
in
\mathfrak{M}
. If a formula
A
is not valid in a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix, it is called
invalid
in this matrix or is
rejected
by the matrix or one can say that this matrix
rejects
the formula. If
\hat{v}[A]\notin D
,
v
is a
valuation rejecting
or
Q-matrix!refuting

A
.

Referring to a
t
-
valuation
in a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathfrak{A},D\rangle
, we mean a valuation in the term structure of
\mathfrak{A}
.

Given a valuation
v
in a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix
\mathfrak{M}
and a set
X\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, we denote

\mathfrak{M}\models_{v}X

if
\mathfrak{M}\models_{v}A
for each
A\in X
.

Also, we denote:

X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}A\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ (\mathfrak{M}\models_{v}X\Longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}\models_{v}A\penalty\ \text{for every $t$-valuation $v$ in $\mathfrak{M}$}).

Exercises
9.2

1.

Prove that if a formula
A
contains no free variables, then for any
t
-valuations
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
,
\widehat{v_{1}}[A]=\widehat{v_{2}}[A]
.

2.

Prove Lemma
9.2.1
.

3.

Complete the proof of Lemma
9.2.2
.

4.

Complete the proof of Lemma
9.2.3
.

5.

Let
A(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
be any formula whose all free variables are in the list
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
. And let
\overline{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
and
\overline{t^{\prime}}=(t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n})
be lists of terms not all necessarily distinct. Prove that for any
t
-valuation
v
, if
v[t_{k}]=v[t^{\prime}_{k}]
,
1\leq k\leq n
, then
\widehat{v\circ\sigma_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{t}}}[A]=\widehat{v\circ\sigma_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{t^{\prime}}}}[A]
.

6.

Check the statement of Proposition
9.2.1
.

9.3
\mathcal{Q}
-Consequence

Definition 9.3.1

(
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence)
.

A relation
\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}}
on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}})\times\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfying the conditions (a)–(c) of Definition
4.1.1
is called a
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence.
The consequence operator
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
corresponding to
\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}}
is defined as follows:

A\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{Q}}(X)\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ X\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}}A,

for any set
X\cup\{A\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. A
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence is called
structural
if for any term substitution
\sigma
and any set
X\cup\{A\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \sigma(X)\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}}\sigma(A),

or in terms of operator
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{Q}}
,

A\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{Q}}(X)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \sigma(A)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\sigma(X)).

To refer to
\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}}
or to
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{Q}}
we use the term
abstract logic

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. Abstract logics
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
will also be called
\mathcal{Q}
-
logics
.

Given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, a set
X
of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas satisfying the property
X=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
is called an
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
theory
.
The set of all
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-theories is denoted by
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}
.

The formulas of the theory

\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}:=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(\varnothing)

are called
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
theses
or
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
theorems
.

The concept of implicative logics (Section
6.2.2
) can be extended to abstract logics
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Definition 9.3.2

(implicative
\mathcal{Q}
-logic)
.

A structural logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is called an
implicative
\mathcal{Q}
-logic
if
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
contains a binary connective
\rightarrow
such that for any
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-theory
D
, the following conditions are satisfied:

(a)

A\rightarrow A\in D
;

(b)

A\rightarrow B\in D
and
B\rightarrow C\in D
imply
A\rightarrow C\in D
;

(c)

A\in D
and
A\rightarrow B\in D
imply
B\in D
;

(d)

B\in D
implies
A\rightarrow B\in D
;

(e)

if
A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}\in D
,

B_{i}\rightarrow A_{i}\in D
, where
1\leq i\leq n
, and
F
is any
n
-ary connective from
\mathcal{L}_{F}
, then
FA_{1}\ldots A_{n}\rightarrow FB_{1}\ldots B_{n}\in D
.

Given an abstract
\mathcal{Q}
-logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, for any
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-theory
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
, we define:

(A,B)\in\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ A\rightarrow B\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ B\rightarrow A\in D.

We denote:

\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}:=\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(\varnothing).

Employing the argument used in the proof of Proposition
6.2.5
, we obtain:

Proposition 9.3.1
.

Let
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
be a theory of an implicative
\mathcal{Q}
-logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. Then
\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
is a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{Q}

and
D
is a congruence class with respect to
\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
.

Remark 9.3.1
.

From the point of view of Definition
6.2.1
, the implicative

\mathcal{Q}
-logics are
unital
. We will not develop this concept for all structural
\mathcal{Q}
-logics, but we will use the property indicated in Proposition
9.3.1
for particular ones; see Section
9.6
.

9.3.1
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence defined by
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}
be a (nonempty) set of
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices of type
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. For any set
X\cup\{A\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, we define:

\begin{array}[]{r}X\models_{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}}A\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \text{for any $\mathfrak{M}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}$ and any valuation $v$ in $\mathfrak{M}$},\\
\text{$\mathfrak{M}\models_{v}X$ implies $\mathfrak{M}\models_{v}A$}\end{array}

(9.5)

Proposition 9.3.2
.

The relation
\models_{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}}
defined in (
9.5
) is a structural
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence.

Proof.

That the relation is a consequence relation can be obtained by applying Proposition
4.3.9
to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas
and
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices. Or this can be done directly; this last task is left to the reader. (Exercise
9.3
.
2
) We prove below that
\models_{\mathcal{M}^{Q}}
is structural.

Let
\mathfrak{A}=\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{F}\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\mathcal{Q},\rangle
be a
\mathcal{Q}
-structure and
\langle\mathfrak{A},D\rangle
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}
. Also, assume that
v
is an arbitrary
t
-valuation and
\sigma
is an arbitrary
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution. Since the former is a homomorphism
v:\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\longrightarrow\langle\textsf{A},\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
and the latter a homomorphism
\sigma:\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
, the composition
v\circ\sigma:\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\longrightarrow\langle\textsf{A},\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
is also a homomorphism; that is, the map
v\circ\sigma
is a term valuation in
\langle\textsf{A},\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
.

Let us denote

v_{1}:=v\circ\sigma.

We recall that for any
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-term
t
,

v_{1}[t]=v[\sigma(t)].

(This can be proved by induction on the construction of term
t
; see Exercise
9.3
.
1
.)

In virtue of Lemma
9.2.3
, the property (
\ast
) further implies that for any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula
A
,

\widehat{v_{1}}[A]=\hat{v}[\sigma(A)]

Then the equality (
\ast\ast
) obviously implies that for any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula
A
,

\widehat{v_{1}}[A]\in D\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \hat{v}[\sigma(A)]\in D.

The last equivalence in turn implies that the relation
\models_{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}}
is structural.
∎

We call the consequence relation defined in (
9.5
)
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}
-
consequence
, for a given class
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}
of
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices.

Given a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix
\mathfrak{M}
and an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
,
\mathfrak{M}
is an
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-
model
if for any set
X\cup\{A\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}A\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\models_{\mathfrak{M}}A.

9.3.2
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence defined via inference rules

In this subsection we limit ourselves with a formal language
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
and its fragment
\mathcal{L}_{(\forall\exists)^{+}}
; the former has already been employed in Example
9.2.2
.
We recall that the set of the logical connectives of
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
consists of
binary connectives
\land,\lor,\rightarrow
, and a unary connective
\neg
; the language
\mathcal{L}_{(\forall\exists)^{+}}
contains only the first three binary connectives. Also, both languages have only two unary quantifiers:

\forall
and
\exists
. Thus,
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
is an extension of
\mathcal{L}_{(\forall\exists)^{+}}
. Since we will consider the formulas in these languages together, it will be convenient to call the formulas of both languages
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
-
formulas
. With the last remark in mind, we denote by
\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
the set of all
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
-formulas.
Unspecified
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
-formulas will be denoted by letters
A,B,C
(with indices or without).

We will consider three
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence relations given by three different sets of axiom schemata. To do this, we reproduce the axiom schemata that first appeared in Section
4.3.5
and Section
4.4.4
, provided that the metavariables are replaced with arbitrary
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
-formulas
A,B
and
C
. To emphasize this change, we add the letter ‘Q’ to the name of each schema below.

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{ax1Q}&A\rightarrow(B\rightarrow A),\\
\text{ax2Q}&(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow((A\rightarrow(B\rightarrow C))\rightarrow(A\rightarrow C)),\\
\text{ax3Q}&A\rightarrow(B\rightarrow(A\wedge B)),\\
\text{ax4Q}&(A\wedge B)\rightarrow A,\\
\text{ax5Q}&(A\wedge B)\rightarrow B,\\
\text{ax6Q}&A\rightarrow(A\vee B),\\
\text{ax7Q}&B\rightarrow(A\vee B),\\
\text{ax8Q}&(A\rightarrow C)\rightarrow((B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow((A\vee B)\rightarrow C)),\\
\text{ax9Q}&(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow((A\rightarrow\neg B)\rightarrow\neg A),\\
\text{ax10Q}&\neg\neg A\rightarrow A,\\
\text{ax11Q}&B\rightarrow(\neg B\rightarrow A).\end{array}

To the above list, we add four new axiom schemata:

ax12Q

\forall xA(x)\rightarrow A(x\backslash t)
,

where, given an arbitrary term
t
, the formula
A(x\backslash t)
is obtained from the protoformula
A(x)
by replacing of all free occurrences
of
x
with
t
;

ax13Q

A(t)\rightarrow\exists xA(t\backslash x)
,

where a protoformula
A(t\backslash x)
is obtained from the formula
A(t)
by replacing all occurrences of
t
with a bound variable
x
such that all new occurrences of
x
, if any, are free; this happens in particular if
x
does not occur in
A(t)
;

ax14Q

\forall x(A\rightarrow B(x))\rightarrow(A\rightarrow\forall xB(x))
,

where
A
is a formula that does not contain free occurrences of
x

and
B
is a protoformula that contains free occurrences of
x
;

ax15Q

\forall x(A(x)\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(\exists xA(x)\rightarrow B)
,

where
A
is a protoformula containing free occurrences of
x

and
B
is any formula.

Each axiom schema becomes a corresponding axiom when concrete formulas are employed for
A,B
and
C
.

In addition to the premiseless rules ax1Q–ax15Q, we formulate two other modus rules. First of all, we have

\dfrac{A,A\rightarrow B}{B},

as well as

\dfrac{A(u)}{\forall xA(u\backslash x)},

(9.6)

where a protoformula
A(u\backslash x)
is obtained from a formula
A(u)
by replacing all occurrences of a free variable
u
with a bound variable
x
which does not occur freely in
A(u)
and all new occurrences of
x
in
A(u\backslash x)
are free.

The concept of
derivation
in calculi based on the axiom schemata ax1Q–ax15Q and rules modus ponens and (
9.6
) is slightly different from what was discussed in Section
4.3.5
. We will consider three types of derivation, all of which are based on one and the same scheme. They differ only in what axiom schemata from the above list can be employed. We use a notation ‘
C
Q
’ to denote one of the three calculi which we are going to deal with in this chapter:

•

a calculus
P
Q
is formulated in
\mathcal{L}_{(\forall\exists)^{+}}
and is based on the use of the schemata ax1Q–ax8Q and ax12Q–ax15Q;

•

a calculus
Cl
Q
is in the language
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
and is based on the use of the schemata ax1Q–ax10Q and ax12Q–ax15Q;

•

a calculus
Int
Q
is also in
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
and uses the schemata ax1–ax9Q, ax11Q and ax12Q–ax15Q.

The fact of derivability, that is, the existence of a derivation of the corresponding type will be denoted by a binary relation
\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
between a finite set (possibly empty) of formulas and a formula. Thus, for any set
X\cup\{A\}\Subset\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
, we define
X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
to mean that there is a (nonempty) list of
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
-formulas

A_{1},\ldots,A_{n},

where the last formula,
A_{n}
, is
A
and each formula in the list is either from
X
or one of the axQ-axioms of
C
Q
or can be obtained from two preceding formulas in the list by modus ponens or, if
A(u)
is among preceding formulas and a free variable
u
does not occur in the formulas of
X
, by the rule (
9.6
). If
X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
, we say that
A
is
derivable
from
X
in
C
Q
. We call any such derivation
a
C
Q
-
derivation
.

Let
X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
. Assume that there is a
C
Q
-derivation whose free variables related to every application of (
9.6
) in this derivation constitute a finite list
u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}
of pairwise distinct symbols of (
9.2
). We denote an arbitrary such derivation by

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\upharpoonright\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}.

Lemma 9.3.1
.

Let

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\upharpoonright\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}.

Assume that
u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{n}}
are pairwise distinct parameters that do not occur in
X
and
\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}\cap\{u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{n}}\}=\varnothing
. Then there is a derivation

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\upharpoonright\{u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{n}}\}.

Remark 9.3.2
.

We note that parameters
u_{i_{k}}
may occur in
(\ast\ast)
, but in this case, they are not eliminated by any application of the rule (
9.6
) in
(\ast\ast)
.

Proof.

We prove by induction on the number of applications of the rule (
9.6
) in
(\ast)
related to parameters in the list
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
.

The basis case, that is, when
n=0
, is obvious.

Now we assume that there is at least one application of the rule (
9.6
) in the derivation
(\ast)
. Suppose the transition from
B(u_{i_{k}})
to
\forall xB(x)
is the first such an application of (
9.6
). Then, right after each formula
C(u_{i_{k}})
that contains
u_{i_{k}}
and precedes
B(u_{i_{k}})
, including
B(u_{i_{k}})
itself, we place a formula
C(u_{j_{k}})
obtained from
C(u_{i_{k}})
by replacement of all occurrences of
u_{i_{k}}
with
u_{j_{k}}
. The list of formulas from the very beginning up to
B(u_{j_{k}})
thus obtained is a
C
Q
-derivation where only modus ponens is used. Next, we deduce
\forall xB(x)
from
B(u_{j_{k}})
.

Since all formulas of the original derivation
(\ast)
have been preserved, we have a new formal derivation of
A
from
X
, in which all parameters of the list
\overline{u}
can still be used in applications of (
9.6
), but less number of times, because one application of this rule, related to
u_{i_{k}}
, has been replaced with an application of it related to
u_{j_{k}}
.
∎

Corollary 9.3.2.1
.

Let
X\subseteq Y\Subset\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
. Then

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ Y\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A.

Proof.

Assume that

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\upharpoonright\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}.

Let us take parameters
u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{n}}
that do not occur in
Y
. By Lemma
9.3.1
,

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\upharpoonright\{u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{n}}\}.

Since neither of
u_{j_{k}}
occurs in
Y
, we have that
Y\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
.
∎

As before, we write sometimes
X,A
instead of
X\cup\{A\}
and

\bigwedge X
instead of
A_{1}\land\ldots\land A_{n}
,
where
X=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\}
