, that is a nonempty set of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas.

Lemma 9.3.2
.

For any finite set
X\cup\{A,B\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
,

(X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B)\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\land B.

Proof.

Suppose we have:

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\upharpoonright\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\upharpoonright\{u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}}\}.

Applying Lemma
9.3.1
to each of the two last
C
Q
-derivations, we obtain:

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\upharpoonright\{u^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}^{\prime}\}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\upharpoonright\{u_{j_{1}}^{\prime},\ldots,u_{j_{m}}^{\prime}\}.

Without loss of generality, we can count that
\{[u_{i_{1}^{\prime}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}^{\prime}\}\cap\{u_{j_{1}}^{\prime},\ldots,u_{j_{m}}^{\prime}\}=\varnothing
. Then, if we take the concatenation of the last formal proofs and add to the resulting list the axiom ax3Q,
B\rightarrow(A\land B)
and
A\land B
, we obtain a
C
Q
-derivation of
A\land B
from
X
in
C
Q
.
∎

Lemma 9.3.3
.

For any nonempty finite set
X\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
,
X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge X
.

Proof.

We prove by induction on the number of formulas in
X
. If
X
consists of one formula, then the conclusion is obvious.

Suppose
X=Y\cup\{A\}
, where
Y\neq\varnothing
. By induction hypothesis,

Y\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge Y
and hence
Y,A\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge Y
. On the other hand, it is obvious that
Y,A\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
. Applying Lemma
9.3.2
, we receive that
X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge Y\land A
.
∎

The next lemma is similar to Lemma
6.3.4
.

Lemma 9.3.4

(deduction theorem)
.

For any set
X\cup\{A,B\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
,

X,A\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B.

Proof.

In the proof of this lemma, we use the argument given in the proof of Lemma
6.3.4
. Indeed, the first steps of the both proofs are the same, until, in this proof, we arrive at the consideration when in a given derivation a formula
\forall xA_{k}(u\backslash x)
is obtained from a preceding formula
A_{k}(u)
, where a parameter
u
does not occur in
X,A
. Then, by induction hypothesis,
X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow A_{k}(u)
. Applying (
9.6
), we get that
X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\forall x(A\rightarrow A_{k}(u\backslash x))
. Applying modus ponens to the last formula and ax14Q, we derive that
X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow\forall xA_{k}(u\backslash x))
.
∎

Lemma 9.3.5
.

For any finite nonempty set
X\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
and a formula
A
,

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge X\rightarrow A.

Proof
can be obtained by using Lemma
9.3.4
, Lemma
9.3.3
, and the property:

\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(B\land C)\rightarrow A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow(C\rightarrow A),

for any
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
-formulas
A,B
and
C
. We leave for the reader to complete the proof of this lemma. (Exercise
9.3
.
4
)

Using the concept of a
C
Q
-derivation, we define three relations
\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
on

\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists})\times\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
, one for each of the realizations of
C
Q
, as follows:

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \text{there is $X_{0}\Subset X$ such that $X_{0}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A$}.

(9.7)

Lemmas
9.3.1
–
9.3.4
suffice to show that each
\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
is a
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence relation. However, we postpone this conclusion and first prove one more lemma.

Lemma 9.3.6
.

For any finite set
X
of
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
-formulas and any
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution
\sigma
,

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \sigma(X)\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\sigma(A).

Proof.

Suppose that

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\upharpoonright\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}

and denote by
\mathcal{U}
the set of parameters that occur in
\sigma(X)\cup\{\sigma(A)\}
. First, we assume that
\mathcal{U}\neq\varnothing
, that is

\mathcal{U}=\{u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{k}}\}.

Taking into account Lemma
9.3.1
, without loss of generality, we can assume that

\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}\cap\{u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{k}}\}=\varnothing.

Then, we define a
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution:

\sigma^{\prime}(u):=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}u&\text{if $u\in\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}$}\\
\sigma(u)&\text{if $u\notin\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}$}.\end{array}\end{cases}

We note that for every formula
B\in X\cup\{A\}
,

\sigma^{\prime}(B)=\sigma(B).

Further, suppose that
(\ast)
is the following finite sequence:

A_{1},\ldots,A_{i},\ldots,A.

Let us consider the sequence:

\sigma^{\prime}(A_{1}),\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}(A_{i}),\ldots,\sigma^{\prime}(A).

We aim to show that
(\ast\ast\ast\ast)
is a
C
Q
-derivation of
\sigma(A)
from
\sigma(X)
. In view of the remark
(\ast\ast)
above, it suffices to show that
(\ast\ast\ast\ast)
is a
C
Q
-derivation.

Indeed, let
\sigma^{\prime}(A_{i})
of
(\ast\ast\ast\ast)
is obtained from

A_{i}
of
(\ast\ast\ast)
. We consider here the two main cases: when
A_{i}
is obtained from
A_{k}
and
A_{k}\rightarrow A_{i}
by modus ponens and when
A_{i}=\forall xB(u\backslash x)
and
B(u)
precedes
A_{i}
in
(\ast\ast\ast)
(in which case
u\in\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}
).

The first case is obvious and we turn to the second. We notice that the parameter
u
occurs in
\sigma^{\prime}(B(u))
and does not occur in
\sigma^{\prime}(X)
. Therefore,

\forall x\sigma^{\prime}(B(u))(u\backslash x)=\sigma^{\prime}(\forall xB(u\backslash x)).

∎

Finally, we arrive at the main conclusion of this section.

Proposition 9.3.3
.

Each
\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
is a finitary structural
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence relation. In addition, each
C
Q
is an implicative
\mathcal{Q}
-logic.

Proof.

The main task is obviously to prove that the property (c) of Definition
4.1.1
holds for
\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
.

To do this, assume that for some sets
X,Y
and
Z
of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas,

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B
, for all formulas
B\in Y
, where
Y\neq\varnothing
, and
Y,Z\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
. By definition, we first have that

Y_{0},Z_{0}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\upharpoonright\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}

for some
Y_{0}\cup Z_{0}\Subset\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
, where
Y_{0}
is assumed to be unequal to
\varnothing
.

We denote

Y_{0}:=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}\}.

By premise, we have that

X_{1}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B_{k},

where
X_{1}\cup\ldots\cup X_{k}\Subset X
. Apply Lemma
9.3.1
, we first obtain that

X_{1}\cup\ldots\cup X_{n}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B_{1},\ldots,X_{1}\cup\ldots\cup X_{k}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B_{k}.

In virtue of Lemma
9.3.1
, without loss of generality, we can assume that

X_{1}\cup\ldots\cup X_{n}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B_{1}\upharpoonright\mathcal{U},\ldots,X_{1}\cup\ldots\cup X_{n}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B_{n}\upharpoonright\mathcal{U},

where
\mathcal{U}
is a finite set of parameters with
\mathcal{U}\cap\{u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}}\}=\varnothing
. This allows us to form a
C
Q
-derivation of
A
from
X_{0}\cup Z_{0}
. The latter in turn implies that
X,Z\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
.

The finitariness and structurality of the consequence relation
\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
follows from definition (
9.7
) and Lemma
9.3.6
, respectively.

We leave for the reader to prove that each abstract logic
C
Q
is implicative. (Exercise
9.3
.
6
)
∎

In the sequel we will need the following property.

Proposition 9.3.4
.

For any set
X\Subset\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
,

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A.

Proof.

Using definition (
9.7
), we observe that

\varnothing\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \varnothing\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A.

Therefore we assume that
X\neq\varnothing
.

The
\Leftarrow
-implication is obvious.

To prove the
\Rightarrow
-implication, we assume that

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
. This implies that there is a set
Y\subseteq X
such that

Y\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
. If
Y=\varnothing
, then we also have
Y\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
and hence
X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
.

Now, assume that
Y\neq\varnothing
. In virtue of Lemma
9.3.5
,
\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge Y\rightarrow A
, and, by Lemma
9.3.3
,

X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge X
.

Further, we claim that
\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge X\rightarrow\bigwedge Y
. (We leave for the reader to prove the last property; see Exercise
9.3
.
5
.) Using the last derivability, we conclude that
X\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
.
∎

Exercises
9.3

1.

Prove that for any
t
-valuation
v
and any
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution
\sigma
, for any term
t
,
v\circ\sigma[t]=v[\sigma(t)]
.

2.

Prove that
\models_{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}}
is a consequence relations, by using definition (
9.5
) and Definition
9.2.3
.

3.

Complete the proof of Proposition
9.3.2
, by proving that for any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula
A
, any
t
-valuation
v
and any
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution
\sigma
,
\widehat{v\circ\sigma}[A]=\hat{v}[\sigma(A)]
.

4.

Complete the proof of Lemma
9.3.5

5.

Suppose
Y\subseteq X\Subset\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
and
Y\neq\varnothing
. Then
\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge X\rightarrow\bigwedge Y
; moreover, there is a derivation which does not use the axioms ax12Q–ax15Q and the rule (
9.6
).

6.

Prove that each logic
C
Q
is implicative.

9.4
Lindenbaum
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices

The
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices we discuss in this section are based on the
\mathcal{Q}
-structure of Example
9.2.1
.

Definition 9.4.1

(Lindenbaum
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix)
.

Given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}_{Q}
, for any set
X\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, we define a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix

\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}[X]:=\langle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}},\mathfrak{F}_{Q},\mathcal{P}_{F},\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}\rangle,\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)\rangle
, where for every
p\in\mathcal{P}_{F}
with
\#(p)=n
and any

t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
,
\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
; and the mappings

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}_{A(\overline{u})}
,
\bm{q}^{\mathbf{L}}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}))}
and
\varphi_{Q}
are defined as in Example
9.2.1
.
Each
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}[X]
is called a
Lindenbaum

\mathcal{Q}
-
matrix
for
\mathcal{S}_{Q}

(
relative to
X)
. We denote the Lindenbaum
Q
-matrix
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}[\varnothing]
by
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}
.

Reviewing Example
9.2.1
, we conclude the following.

Proposition 9.4.1
.

Every
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}[X]
is a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix.

Proof
is obvious and is left to the reader. (Exercise
9.4
.
1
)

The next proposition is similar to Proposition
4.3.12
.

Proposition 9.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{Q}
be a structural abstract
\mathcal{Q}
-logic. Then
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}
is an
\mathcal{S}_{Q}
-model. In addition, if
X
is closed under arbitrary
term substitution, then for any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula
A
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}[X]\models A;

in particular,

A\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}\models A.

Proof.

Suppose
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}A
and let
\sigma
be any
t
-substitution.
Since the logic
\mathcal{S}_{Q}
is structural,
\sigma(X)\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}\sigma(A)
, that is,
\sigma(A)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(\sigma(X))
. Thus, if
\sigma(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(\varnothing)
, then, obviously,
\sigma(A)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(\varnothing)
. This shows that
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}
is an
\mathcal{S}_{Q}
-model.

Next, if
X
is closed under
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitutions, then, in particular,
\sigma(X)\subseteq X
. This implies that
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(\sigma(X))\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
and hence
\sigma(A)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
.

Conversely, assume that
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}[X]\models A
. Taking the identity
\mathcal{L}_{T}
-substitution
\iota
, we have:
A=\iota(A)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
, that is,
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}A
.
∎

The next definition is similar to Definition
4.3.2
.

Definition 9.4.2
.

A
(
nonempty
)
family
\mathcal{B}=\{\langle\mathfrak{A},D_{i}\rangle\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices, which may differ by logical filters
D_{i}
, is called a
\mathcal{Q}
-
bundle
. A pair
\langle\mathfrak{A},\{D_{i}\}_{i\in I}\rangle
, where each
D_{i}
is a logical filter in
\mathfrak{A}
, is called an
\mathcal{Q}
-
atlas
. By definition, the
\mathcal{Q}
-
consequence relative to an atlas

\langle\mathfrak{A},\{D_{i}\}_{i\in I}\rangle
is the same as the
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence related the bundle
\{\langle\mathfrak{A},D_{i}\rangle\}_{i\in I}
, which in turn is defined according to (
9.5
).
Given an abstract logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, the bundle
\{\langle\mathfrak{F}_{Q},D\rangle\}_{D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}}
, where
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}
is the set of all
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-theories, is called a
Lindenbaum
\mathcal{Q}
-bundle
relative to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
and the atlas
\textsf{{Lin}}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}]:=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{Q},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}\rangle
, is called a
Lindenbaum
\mathcal{Q}
-atlas
relative to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

According to the last definition and the main definition (
9.5
), for any set
X\cup\{A\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
,

\begin{array}[]{r}X\models_{\textsf{{Lin}}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}]}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \text{for any $D\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}$ and any $\mathcal{L}_{T}$-substitution $\sigma$},\\
\text{if $\sigma(X)\subseteq D$, then $\sigma(A)\in D$}.\end{array}

(9.8)

Proposition 9.4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
be a structural
\mathcal{Q}
-consequence. Then for any set
X\cup\{A\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
,

X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\models_{\textsf{{Lin}}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}]}A.

Proof
is similar to the proof of Proposition
4.3.13
and is left to the reader. (Exercise
9.4
.
2
)

Exercises
9.4

1.

Prove Proposition
9.4.1
.

2.

Prove Proposition
9.4.3
.

9.5
Lindenbaum-Tarski
\mathcal{Q}
-structures

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{Q}
-logic. For any set
X\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, we denote:

(A,B)\in\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ (A,B)\in\theta(\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)).

(9.9)

Thus
\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
is a congruence on
\mathfrak{F}_{Q}
. We employ the above definition when
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is a unital
\mathcal{Q}
-logic.

Definition 9.5.1

(Lindenbaum-Tarski
\mathcal{Q}
-structure)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
be a unital
\mathcal{Q}
-logic and
X\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{Q}}
.
A structure

\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[X]:=\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}},\langle\mathfrak{F}_{Q}/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X),\mathbf{1}_{D}\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\mathcal{Q}\rangle,

where
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
and
\mathbf{1}_{D}
is
D
understood as a congruence class with respect to the congruence
\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(D)
on
\mathfrak{F}_{Q}
, is called

Lindenbaum-Tarski
\mathcal{Q}
-structure

(
of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
)

relative to

D

(
or
relative to

X
)

if each
p\in\mathcal{P}_{F}
with
\#(p)=n
is associate with a map

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}:(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\mapsto p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
. Accordingly, for any
t
-substitution
\sigma
, the value of a formula
A(\overline{u})
in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[X]
under
\sigma
is denoted by
\sigma_{X}[A(\overline{u})]
and is defined inductively on the complexity of this formula in the following way.
If
A(\overline{u})=p(\overline{t})
with
\#(p)=k
, where
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
and
\overline{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
, then we have:

•

\sigma_{X}[p(\overline{t})]:=\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}(\sigma(\overline{t}))/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)=\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}(\sigma(\overline{t}))
;

•

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A(\overline{u})}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}):=\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}(\delta_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{s}}(\overline{t}))/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)=\delta_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{s}}(p(\overline{t}))/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
,

where

\overline{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}^{n}
.

If
A(\overline{u})=FA_{1}\ldots A_{k}
, then

•

\sigma_{X}[A(\overline{u})]:=F(\hat{\sigma}[A_{1}],\ldots,\hat{\sigma}[A_{k}])=\sigma(A(\overline{u}))/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
;

•

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A(\overline{u})}(\overline{s})=F(\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A_{1}(\overline{u})}(\overline{s}),\ldots,\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A_{k}(\overline{u})}(\overline{s}))
;

and for any protoformula
A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}})
, where
\overline{x}=(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}})
,

•

\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(\overline{s}):=\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A(\overline{u},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(\overline{s},\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))
;

and for any
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
,

•

\varphi_{Q}(\{\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(\overline{s})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{s}\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}^{n}\}):=\sigma(Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)

=Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
.

•

\sigma_{X}[Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}},\overline{u^{\ast}})]:=\hat{\sigma}[Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}})]/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)=\sigma(Qx_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{n}}A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X);

•

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{Q\overline{x}A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}})}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}):=\widehat{\delta_{\overline{u^{\ast}}\backslash\overline{t}}}[Q\overline{x}A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}})]/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)=\delta_{\overline{u^{\ast}}\backslash\overline{t}}(Q\overline{x}A(\overline{x},\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
.

We will also use the notations
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[D]
for
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[X]
and
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[X]
for
\mathbf{1}_{D}
, where
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
, and
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}:=\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[\varnothing]
.

In the above definition,
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{Q}/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X),\mathbf{1}_{D}\rangle
is a unital expansion of
\mathfrak{F}_{Q}/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
. In the sequel, we treat
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[X]
as a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix

\langle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}},\mathfrak{F}_{Q}/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(D),\mathcal{P}_{F},\mathcal{Q}\rangle,\{\mathbf{1}_{D}\}\rangle.

Below, instead of
\sigma_{X}
, we occasionally write
\sigma_{D}
, where
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
. It is not difficult to see that
\sigma_{X}=\sigma_{D}
.
(Exercise
9.5
.
1
)

Lemma 9.5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
be a unital
\mathcal{Q}
-logic and
D
be an
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-theory. Then
\iota_{D}
satisfies a formula
A
in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[D]
if, and only if,
A\in D
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
9.5
.
2
)

Similarly to Proposition
6.3.4
, we prove the following.

Proposition 9.5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
be a unital logic and
D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
.
Then for any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formula
A
, the following conditions are equivalent
\,:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}A;\\
(\text{b})&X\models_{\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[D]}A;\\
(\text{c})&\iota_{X}[A]=\mathbf{1}_{D}.\end{array}

In particular, the following conditions are equivalent
\,:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a}^{\ast})&A\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}};\\
(\text{b}^{\ast})&\sigma_{X}[A]=\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}},\penalty\ \textit{for any $t$-substitution $\sigma$};\\
(\text{c}^{\ast})&\iota_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}}[A]=\mathbf{1}_{\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}}.\end{array}

Proof.

Suppose
X\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}A
. Let
\sigma
be an
t
-substitution.
Assume that
\sigma_{X}
satisfies in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[D]
every
B\in X
. This means that for every
B\in X
,
\sigma_{X}[B]=\mathbf{1}_{D}
, that is,
\sigma_{X}[B]\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
. In virtue of Proposition
9.2.1
-a, for every
B\in X
,
\sigma(B)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
, that is,
\sigma(X)\subseteq\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
. Since
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is structural (being unital),
\sigma(A)\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
. Hence
\sigma_{X}
satisfies
A
in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[D]
.

To prove the implication (b)
\Rightarrow
(c), we use Lemma
9.5.1
to observe that
\iota_{D}
satisfies every
B\in X
.

Now, assume that
\iota_{D}[A]=\mathbf{1}_{D}
, that is,

\iota_{D}[A]/\theta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(D)=\mathbf{1}_{D}
. In virtue of Lemma
9.5.1
, we conclude that
A\in\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)
.
∎

D=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{Q}}(X)

.

2.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
be a unital
\mathcal{Q}
-logic and
D
be an
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-theory. Prove that
\iota_{D}
valuates a formula
A
in
\mathbf{LT}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Q}}}[D]
if, and only if,
A\in D
. (
Hint
: Use Proposition
9.2.1
-a.)

9.6
Three
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix consequences

In this section, we return to the languages
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
and
\mathcal{L}_{(\forall\exists)^{+}}
of Section
9.3.2
.
We use these languages to further analyze the consequence relations
\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
, where
C
stands for
P
Q
,
Cl
Q
or
Int
Q
. Therefore, it is convenient to denote the set of formulas, corresponding to a particular relation
\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
, by
\textbf{Fm}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
and call the element of this set
C
Q
-
formulas
.

Accordingly, we consider
\mathcal{Q}
-structures of the following two forms:

\begin{array}[]{rl}i)&\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle\\
ii)&\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle.\end{array}

(9.10)

We call a structure of the first form a
\mathcal{Q}_{p}
-
structure
(or
positive
\mathcal{Q}
-structure
), and that of the second form a
\mathcal{Q}_{a}
-
structure
(or
assertoric
\mathcal{Q}
-structure
). Accordingly, we define the notions of
\mathcal{Q}_{p}
-
matrix
and

\mathcal{Q}_{a}
-
matrix
and, respectively, the notions of
unital
\mathcal{Q}_{p}
-matrix
and

unital
\mathcal{Q}_{a}
-matrix
.

We denote by
\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{P}\text{Q}}[X]
,
\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}}[X]
and
\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{Int}\text{Q}}[X]
the Lindenbaum
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices for
P
Q
,
Cl
Q
, and
Int
Q
, respectively; also, we employ the notation
\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]
to refer to one of these three types of Lindenbaum matrices.
Accordingly, we denote the formula algebra associated with
C
Q
by
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
, that is,

\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}:=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}};\mathcal{F}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\rangle,

where
\mathcal{F}_{\textsf{P}\text{Q}}:=\{\land,\lor,\rightarrow\}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}}=\mathcal{F}_{\textsf{Int}\text{Q}}:=\{\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg\}
.
The set of terms is denoted by
\textbf{Tm}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
.

Proposition 9.6.1
.

Each abstract logic
C
Q
is implicative and, therefore, is unital.

Proof.

When the first statement is proved, we apply Proposition
6.2.5
.

To establish the first statement, it suffices to prove that: For any finite set
Z
,

•

Z\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B
and
Z\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}C\rightarrow D
imply

Z\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(A\land C)\rightarrow(B\land D)

and
Z\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(A\lor C)\rightarrow(B\lor D)
;

•

Z\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B
and
Z\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow C
imply

Z\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow C
; and

•

if
\textsf{C}\text{Q}=\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}
or
\textsf{C}\text{Q}=\textsf{Int}\text{Q}
, then
Z\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B
implies
Z\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\neg B\rightarrow\neg A
.

We leave the task of proving these implications to the reader. (Exercise
9.6
.
2
)

Using Proposition
9.3.3
and Corollary
6.2.5.1
, we conclude that each
C
Q
is unital.
∎

Applying Proposition
9.6.1
, Proposition
6.2.5

and Proposition
9.4.2
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 9.6.1.1
.

For the congruence
\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
defined in (
9.9
), the following equivalences hold:

\begin{array}[]{rl}(A,B)\in\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)&\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow A,\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]\models A\rightarrow B\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ \,\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]\models B\rightarrow A.\\
\end{array}

We note that

X\subseteq Y\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\subseteq\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(Y).

(9.11)

We denote:

\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{C}{\text{Q}}}[X]:=\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}},\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X),\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle.

According to Definition
9.5.1
, we have:

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}:(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\mapsto p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X),

where
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}^{n}
and
p\in\mathcal{P}_{F}
with
\#(p)=n
. Also, specifically for
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
, according to this definition, for any
t
-substitution
\sigma
, we have:

First, if
A(\overline{u})=p(\overline{t})
, where
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
and all
u_{i_{k}}
are pairwise distinct, then

•

\sigma_{X}[p(\overline{t})]:=\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}(\sigma(\overline{t}))=\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}(\sigma(\overline{t}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=p(\sigma(\overline{t}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
;

•

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A(\overline{u})}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}):=\bm{p}^{\mathbf{L}}(\delta_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{s}}(\overline{t}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=\delta_{\overline{u}\backslash\overline{s}}(p(\overline{t}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
,

where
\overline{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}^{n}
.

If
A(\overline{u})=FA_{1}\ldots A_{k}
, then

•

\sigma_{X}[A(\overline{u})]:=F(\sigma_{X}[A_{1}],\ldots,\sigma_{X}[A_{k}])=\hat{\sigma}[A(\overline{u})]/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
;

•

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A(\overline{u})}(\overline{s})=F(\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A_{1}(\overline{u})}(\overline{s}),\ldots,\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A_{k}(\overline{u})}(\overline{s}))
;

and for any protoformula
A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})
and
s\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
,

•

\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(s):=\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{A(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})}(s,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))
;

and for any
Q\in\{\forall,\exists\}
,

•

\varphi_{Q}(\{\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(s)\penalty\ |\penalty\ s\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\}):=\varphi_{Q}(\{\bm{q}^{\mathbf{L}}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(s)\penalty\ |\penalty\ s\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\})/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=\sigma(QxA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=QxA(x,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
;

•

\sigma_{X}[QxA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})]:=\hat{\sigma}[QxA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})]/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=\sigma(QxA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X);

•

\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{QxA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}):=\widehat{\delta_{\overline{u^{\ast}}\backslash\overline{t}}}[QxA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})]/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=\delta_{\overline{u^{\ast}}\backslash\overline{t}}(QxA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
.

Similarly to Proposition
9.2.1
, we summarize the multiple definition above in the following proposition

Proposition 9.6.2
.

Let
\sigma
be an
\mathcal{F}_{T}
- substitution,
A(\overline{u})
and
B(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})
be formulas such that
\overline{u}=(u_{i_{1}},\ldots,u_{i_{n}})
is the list of all parameters of the former and the parameters of the latter are partitioned in two lists —

(u)
and
\overline{u^{\ast}}=(u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
with
u\notin\overline{u^{\ast}}
. Then the following hold in
\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}},\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X),\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle
:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\sigma_{X}[A(\overline{u})]=\sigma(A(\overline{u}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X);\\
(\text{b})&\bm{p}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{B(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})}(s,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))=\delta_{(\sigma,u\backslash s)}(B(u,\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X);\\
(\text{c})&\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(B(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(s)=\delta_{(\sigma,u\backslash s)}(B(u,\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X).\end{array}

For the next proposition, we also need the following relation on
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
.

A/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B.

The soundness of
\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
can be easily shown. For this, one needs to prove that if
A/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}(X)}=C/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
and
B/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=D/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
, then

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}C\rightarrow D.

(Exercise
9.6
.
1
)

Lemma 9.6.1
.

The relation
\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
is a partial order on
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X),\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]\rangle
so that

\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]
is a greatest element.

Proof.

We prove that the relation
\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
is transitive and that
\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]
is a greatest element.

Indeed, suppose
A/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
and
B/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}C/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
. By definition, it means that

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B
and
X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow C
, that is,
for some
X_{1},X_{2}\Subset X
,
X_{1}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B
and
X_{2}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow C
. In virtue of Corollary
9.3.2.1
,
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B
and
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow C
. This implies that
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow C
. Hence
X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow C
.

Now, if
A
is an
C
Q
-thesis, then for any formula
B
,
\varnothing\Vdash B\rightarrow A
. Hence
B/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
. It remains to note that
A/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]
.
∎

Proposition 9.6.3
.

Let
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
. Then the following are true:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\mathfrak{A}_{1}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{P}\text{Q}}/\theta_{\textsf{P}\text{Q}}(X),\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{P}\text{Q}}[X]\rangle\penalty\ \textit{is a relatively pseudo-complemented lattice};\\
(\text{b})&\mathfrak{A}_{2}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}}/\theta_{\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}}(X),\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}}[X]\rangle\penalty\ \textit{is a Boolean algebra, where $x\rightarrow y:=\neg x\lor y$};\\
(\text{c})&\mathfrak{A}_{3}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{Int}\text{Q}}/\theta_{\textsf{Int}\text{Q}}(X),\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{Int}\text{Q}}[X]\rangle\penalty\ \textit{is a Heyting algebra}.\end{array}

In addition, the relation
\leq_{i}
defined in each
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
according to (
2.7
), or equivalently according to (
2.8
), coincides with the corresponding relation
\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
.
Moreover, if the set of parameters not occurring in the formulas of
X
is infinite, then for any
t
-substitution
\sigma
and any protoformula
A(x)
with free occurrences of only
x\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
, both

\bigwedge\{\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(x),\sigma)}(t)\penalty\ |\penalty\ t\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\}\penalty\ \textit{and}\penalty\ \bigvee\{\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(x),\sigma)}(t)\penalty\ |\penalty\ t\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\}

exist, where
\bigwedge
and
\bigvee
are the operations of a greatest lower bound and a least upper bound, respectively, with respect to
\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
in

\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)
. Consequently, defining

\varphi_{\forall}
as
\bigwedge
and
\varphi_{\exists}
as
\bigvee
in a

\mathcal{Q}
-matrix
\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}},\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X),\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle
, we have:

\sigma_{X}[\forall xA(x)]=\bigwedge\{\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(x),\sigma)}(t)\penalty\ |\penalty\ t\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\}

(9.12)

and

\sigma_{X}[\exists xA(x)]=\bigvee\{\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(x),\sigma)}(t)\penalty\ |\penalty\ t\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\}.

(9.13)

Proof.

Since the restriction of any Heyting algebra to the signature
\langle\land,\lor,\rightarrow\rangle
is a relatively pseudo-complemented lattice and in view of that the sentential fragments of
\textsf{P}\text{Q},\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}
and
Int
Q
are coincident, respectively, with
\textsf{P},\textsf{Cl}
and
Int
, in virtue of Proposition
9.5.1
, we observe that (a)–(c) above hold.

To show that the relation
\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}
is coincident with the corresponding
\leq_{i}
, we consider the following equivalences:

\begin{array}[]{rl}A/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)&\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\rightarrow B\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ A\rightarrow B\in\textbf{Cn}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ A\rightarrow B/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X]\\
&\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ A/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\rightarrow B/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=\mathbf{1}_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}[X].\end{array}

To prove (
9.12
) and (
9.13
), we fix a protoformula
A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})
with free occurrences of a single bound variable
x
and a finite list

\overline{u^{\ast}}=(u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
of all parameters that occur in this protoformula. Further, we consider a formula
A(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})
obtained from
A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})
by replacing each free occurrence of
x
with a parameter
u
, providing that
u
does not occur in this protoformula.

In view of Proposition
9.6.2
, we have:

\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(t)=\widehat{\delta_{(\sigma,u\backslash t)}}[A(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})]/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)=A(t,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X).

That is,

\{\bm{q}^{\mathbf{LT}}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),\sigma)}(t)\penalty\ |\penalty\ t\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\}=\{A(t,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\penalty\ |\penalty\ t\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}\}.

We recall that, in virtue of ax12Q,

\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\forall xA(x,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))\rightarrow A(t,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}})),

for an arbitrary
t\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
. Hence,

\forall xA(x,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A(t,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X).

On the other hand, assume that for some formula
B
,

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow A(t,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}})),

for any
t\in\mathbf{Tm}_{\mathcal{L}_{T}}
. In particular,

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow A(u^{\prime},\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}})),

where
u
is a parameter which occurs neither in
X
nor in
B
. This means that for some set
Y\Subset X
,

Y\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow A(u,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}})).

We observe that
u
does not occur in the formulas of
Y
. Then, by the rule (
9.6
),

Y\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\forall x(B\rightarrow A(x,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}})).

Applying modus ponens to the last formula and ax14Q, we derive that

Y\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow\forall xA(x,\sigma(u^{\ast})),

that is,

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}B\rightarrow\forall xA(x,\sigma(u^{\ast})).

Hence,

B/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X)\leq_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\forall xA(x,\sigma(\overline{u^{\ast}}))/\theta_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}(X).

This completes the proof of (
9.12
). We leave for the reader to prove (
9.13
). (Exercise
9.6
,
3
)
∎

The last proposition induces the following definition.

Definition 9.6.1

(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{P}}
-matrix,
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{B}}
-matrix, and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{H}}
-matrix)
.

A unital
\mathcal{Q}_{p}
-matrix
\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle
is called a
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{P}}
-
matrix
if
\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a relatively pseudo-complemented lattice, and for any
t
-valuation
v
in
\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle
and any formulas
\forall xA(x,\overline{u})
and
\exists xA(x,\overline{u})
, both

\bigwedge\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u}),v)}(a,v[\overline{u}])\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\}
and
\bigvee\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u}),v)}(a,v[\overline{u}])\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\}
, where
\bigwedge
is a greatest lower bound and
\bigvee
is a least upper bound, exist so that
\varphi_{\forall}
and
\varphi_{\exists}
are defined as
\bigwedge
and
\bigvee
, respectively.

A unital
\mathcal{Q}_{a}
-matrix
\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle
is called a
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{B}}
-
matrix
if
\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
, where
x\rightarrow y:=\neg x\lor y
, is a Boolean algebra;
it is called a
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{H}}
-
matrix
if
\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a Heyting algebra, providing that the other conditions and definitions in the two last cases are the same as for
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{P}}
-matrices.

Referring collectively to each of these
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices, we use the term

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-
matrix
.

Thus, according to the definition of an
f
-valuation (Section
9.2
), in all
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrices, for any
t
-valuation
v
and protoformula
A(x,\overline{u})
,

\hat{v}[\forall xA(x,\overline{u})]:=\bigwedge\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u}),v)}(a,v[\overline{u}])\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\}

(9.14)

and

\hat{v}[\exists xA(x,\overline{u})]:=\bigvee\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u}),v)}(a,v[\overline{u}])\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\}.

(9.15)

Corollary 9.6.3.1
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
such that the set of parameters not occurring in the formulas of
X
is infinite. Then each
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{C}{\text{Q}}}[X]
is a
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix.

Definition 9.6.2

(logics
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{P}}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{B}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{H}}
)
.

Abstract logics
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{P}}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{B}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{H}}
are defined as the consequence relations that are determined, respectively, by the classes of
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{P}}
-matrices,
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{B}}
-matrices, and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{H}}
-matrices. The corresponding consequence relations are denoted by
\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{P}}}
,
\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{B}}}
, and
\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{H}}}
. Referring to each of these consequence relations, we employ the notation
\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}}
.

We aim to show that every
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix is a
C
Q
-model. We will do it by means of the following lemma which exemplifies the simplest case of this property.

Lemma 9.6.2
.

Every
C
Q
-thesis is valid in any
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix; in other words,

\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}}A.

Proof.

In view of Proposition
9.3.4
, it suffices to prove the implication:

\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}}A;

in other words, every formula
A
derivable in
C
Q
is valid in any
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix.

We prove the last implication by induction on the length of a
C
Q
-derivation of a formula
A
. The “sentential axiom schemata” ax1Q–ax11Q are easy and we leave the base case, concerning these schemata, to the reader (Exercise
9.6
.
4
) and move to the schemata ax12Q–ax15Q.

Let us fix a
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix:

\mathfrak{M}=\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg,\mathbf{1}\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle

and a
t
-valuation
v
in
\mathfrak{M}
.

We aim to show that
\hat{v}[\forall xA(x)\rightarrow A(u\backslash t)]=\mathbf{1}
, that is,
\hat{v}[\forall xA(x)]\leq\hat{v}[A(u\backslash t)]
, where
\leq
is the partial order in
\langle\textsf{B};\mathcal{F}_{F},\mathbf{1}\rangle
which is determined by
\land
(or by
\lor
) from
\mathcal{F}_{F}
. The formula
A(u)
may have other free variables, different from
u
; we denote them by
\overline{u^{\ast}}=(u_{j_{1}},\ldots,u_{j_{m}})
.

According to (
9.14
),

\hat{v}[\forall xA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})]=\bigwedge\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(a)\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\},

and, in virtue of Lemma
9.2.3
and Lemma
9.2.2
,

\displaystyle\hat{v}[A(u\backslash t)]=\bm{p}_{A(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})}(v\circ\sigma_{u\backslash t}[u],v\circ\sigma_{u\backslash t}[\overline{u^{\ast}}])

\displaystyle=\bm{p}_{A(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})}(v[t],v[\overline{u^{\ast}}]),

In virtue of (
9.4
), the desired conclusion is obvious.

Since

\hat{v}[\exists xA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})]=\bigvee\{\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(a)\penalty\ |\penalty\ a\in\textsf{A}\},

in virtue of Lemma
9.2.2
,
\hat{v}[A(u\backslash t)]\leq\hat{v}[\exists xA(x)]
.

We leave for the reader to show that the axioms ax14Q and ax15Q are also valid in any
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix. (Exercise
9.6
.
5
)

Now suppose a formula
B
is obtained by modus ponens from
A
and
A\rightarrow B
. By induction hypothesis, for any
t
-valuation
v
is any
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-structure

\hat{v}[A]=\hat{v}[A]\rightarrow\hat{v}[B]=\mathbf{1}
. This implies that
\hat{v}[B]=\mathbf{1}
.

Finally, suppose, by induction hypothesis,
w[A(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})]=\mathbf{1}
, where
u\notin\overline{u^{\ast}}
, for any valuation
w
in
\mathfrak{M}
.

Assume, for contradiction,
\hat{v}[\forall xA(x,\overline{u^{\ast}})]\neq\mathbf{1}
.
Then, because of
(\ast)
, there is an element
a\in\textsf{A}
such that
\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(a)\neq\mathbf{1}
. With the purpose of deriving a contradiction, we define:

w_{(v,u\backslash a)}[u^{\prime}]=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cl}a&\text{if $u^{\prime}=u$}\\
v[u^{\prime}]&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\end{cases}

By definition,

\displaystyle\widehat{w_{(v,u\backslash a)}}[A(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})]=\bm{p}_{A(u,\overline{u^{\ast}})}(a,v[\overline{u^{\ast}}])

\displaystyle=\bm{q}_{(A(x,\overline{u^{\ast}}),v)}(a)\neq\mathbf{1}.

A contradiction.
∎

Proposition 9.6.4

(soundness)
.

Every
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix is a
C
Q
-model; in other words,

X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}}A.

Proof.

Suppose
X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
, that is, for some set
Y\Subset X
,
Y\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
. In virtue of Lemma
9.3.5
,
\Vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}\bigwedge Y\rightarrow A
. By Lemma
9.6.2
,
\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}}\bigwedge Y\rightarrow A
, which in particular implies that
Y\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}}A
. And by monotonicity, we conclude that
X\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}}A
.
∎

Proposition 9.6.5

(limited completeness)
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}_{\forall\exists}
such that the set of parameters not occurring in the formulas of
X
is infinite. Then

X\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}}A\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ X\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A.

Proof.

Assume that
X\not\vdash_{\textsf{C}\text{Q}}A
. According to Proposition
9.5.1
, the algebra
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{C}{\text{Q}}}[X]
validates all formulas of
X
and refutes
A
. On the other hand, in virtue of Corollary
9.6.3.1
,
\mathbf{LT}_{\textsf{C}{\text{Q}}}[X]
is a
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix. Thus
X\not\vdash_{\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}}A
.
∎

Remark 9.6.1
.

The reader should remember that each abstract logic
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
is a
\mathcal{Q}
-matrix consequence. Let
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}
be the abstract class of unital
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices of the type
\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\textsf{{B}}_{2},\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\}\rangle
. Suppose that the semantics of language
\mathcal{L}_{\forall\exists}
is defined as in Example
9.2.2
. Then the well-known completeness of first-order logic reads:

X\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ X\models_{\mathcal{M}^{Q}}A.

Comparing the last equivalence with Proposition
9.6.5
when
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Cl}
, we note that the completeness expressed by the last equivalence is reached at the cost of narrowing the class of determining
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices. However, due to the fact that the relation
\Vdash_{\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}}
is known to be undecidable, to show that
X\not\vdash_{\textsf{Cl}\text{Q}}A
, it could be easier if we have a class of
Cl
Q
-models as large as possible.

.)

9.6.1
(
Hint

9.3.2.1
and Lemma
9.3.4
).

3.

Complete the proof of Proposition
9.6.3
by proving (
9.13
).

4.

Prove that every
C
Q
-axiom in the list ax1Q–ax11Q is valid in any
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix.

5.

Show that the axioms ax14Q and ax15Q are valid in any
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{C}}
-matrix.

9.7
Historical notes

Algebraic treatment of predicate language systems, which was discussed in this chapter,
4
4

4

The reader can find other algebraic approaches to first-order languages ​​in the theory of polyadic algebras and of cylindrical algebras; see
[
Halmos, 1962
]
and
[
Henkin et al., 1985
]
, respectively.
began with A. Mostowski, who demonstrated in
[
Mostowski, 1948
]
that if ordinary quantifiers,
\forall
and
\exists
, are interpreted as a greatest lower bound and a least upper bound, respectively, in a complete lattice, then

\Vdash_{\textsf{Int}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \models_{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}}A

(9.16)

holds, where
A
is a sentence and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
is the class of
\mathcal{Q}
-structures

\langle\langle\textsf{A};\mathcal{F}_{T}\rangle,\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{F},\{\varphi_{\forall},\varphi_{\exists}\},\rangle
, where
\langle\textsf{B};\land,\lor,\rightarrow,\neg\rangle
is a complete Heyting algebra.

Mostowski states clearly the aim of his approach:

“
\dots
to outline a general method which permits us to establish the intuitionistic non-deducibility of many formulas
\dots
”
[
Mostowski, 1948
]
, p. 204

As the reader can see, Mostowski’s approach is consistent with the conception of separating tools (Section
5.3
) and, therefore, the spirit of this book.

Using his method, Mostowski shows that the formula
\neg\neg(\forall x\,\neg\neg p(x)\rightarrow\neg\neg\forall x\,p(x))
is not derivable in
Int
Q
, the result which had earlier been established by S. C. Kleene and D. Nelson in a different way.

Regarding (
9.16
), we note that the difference between it and the
\Rightarrow
-implication of Lemma
9.6.2
is that, firstly, the formula
A
in Lemma
9.6.2
is not necessarily a sentence, and secondly, a
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{H}}
-matrix is not necessarily a complete Heyting algebra, for greatest lower bounds and least upper bounds must exist but for some subsets of
B
.

A decisive step towards strengthening Mostowski’s approach was made in
[
Henkin, 1950
]
. At first glance, this does not look like a strengthening, because Henkin used the calculus
Im
Q
, which is based on the schemata ax1Q–ax2Q and ax12Q–ax15Q and, therefore, is weaker than
Int
Q
. Henkin showed however that the implication of type (
9.16
) can be enhanced by equivalence:

\Vdash_{\textsf{Im}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \models_{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}}A,

(9.17)

for any sentence
A
, where
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
is the class of
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices, where
\langle\textsf{B};\rightarrow,\mathbf{1}\rangle
is a complete positive implication algebra; see
[
Rasiowa, 1974
]
, chapter II, section 2.

Lemma
9.6.2
does not discuss the relationship between
\Vdash_{\textsf{Im}\text{Q}}
and a consequence relation based on corresponding
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices. However, such an equivalence could be easily proved.
In this event, the difference between (
9.17
) and such an enhancement of the equivalence in Lemma
9.6.2
would be that, firstly, in the enhanced lemma,
A
could be any formula, and secondly, the
\mathcal{Q}
-matrices involved would be not necessarily complete implication algebras, but implication algebras where greatest lower bounds and least upper bounds but partially exist.

In the final section of
[
Henkin, 1950
]
, it is shown how the main results can be extended to
Int
Q
,
Cl
Q
and a modal predicate calculus
S4
Q
(based on sentential modal system
S4
). However, the remark concerning the comparison of the equivalence of Lemma
9.6.2
and the equivalences regarding these systems remains in force.

Independently of Henkin, H. Rasiowa established in
[
Rasiowa, 1951
]
the following equivalences:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Vdash_{\textsf{Int}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \models_{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}}A,\penalty\ \text{for any sentence $A$};\\
\Vdash_{\textsf{S4}\text{Q}}A\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \models_{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}_{3}}A,\penalty\ \text{for any sentence $A$}.\end{array}

Here
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}_{3}
is the class of complete topological Boolean algebras; see
[
Rasiowa, 1974
]
, supplement. Rasiowa makes an important remark:

“The assumption that the lattices under consideration are complete was made only in order to ensure that all the least upper bounds and greatest lower bounds appearing in an interpretation of formulas exist. However, it may happen that, by a certain interpretation of
m
-argument predicates as mappings from
J^{m}
into a suitable lattice
\mathfrak{A}
, all the least upper bounds and all the greatest lower bounds appearing in the interpretation of formulas exist in spite of the fact that
\mathfrak{A}
is incomplete.”
[
Rasiowa, 1974
]
, supplement, introduction

There have been obtained numerous results, mostly by H. Rasiowa and R. Sikorski, according to which completeness theorems for the theses of the above-mentioned abstract logics, as well as for the predicate calculus of minimal logic and of constructive logic with strong negation, are proved for specific classes of
\mathcal{Q}
-structures. (See references in
[
Rasiowa, 1974
]
, supplement.) In this regard, we would like, however, to quote D. Scott, who wrote:

“The ‘first-order disease’ is most plainly seen in the book by Rasiowa and Sikorski (1963). They had for a number of years considered Boolean-valued models of first-order sentences (as had Tarski, Mostowski, Halmos, and many others working on algebraic logic). Unfortunately they spent most of the time considering
logical validity
(truth in all models) rather than the construction of possibly interesting
particular
models.” (D. Scott, Foreword to
[
Bell, 2011
]
)

The models mentioned by Scott are discussed, for example, in
[
Bell, 2011
]
; see also references there.

The concept of unary generalized quantifiers is rooted in the numerical quantifiers of A. Tarski; cf.
[
Tarski, 1994
]
, section 20. A further explication of unary generalized quantifiers was given in
[
Mostowski, 1957
]
. While the quantifiers of both Tarski and Mostowski operate with a set of two truth values, N. Rescher proposed the use of multivalued logic for a set of truth values; see
[
Rescher, 1969
]
, chapter 2, section 29, and references therein.

Our definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}
-consequence (definition (
9.5
)) is similar to a more restricted definition of consequence that was introduced in
[
Mal’cev, 1973
]
, section 11.2. Namely, in his definition of defining collection of first-order atomic formulas, Mal’cev employed the notion of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}
-consequence,
X\models_{\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{Q}}}A
, in the framework of Example
9.2.2
and for the case when X is a set of atomic formulas.

Chapter 10
Decidability

From a very general point of view, one can see the following two ways of defining abstract logic: (a) using a set of axioms or axiom schemata and a set of inference rules, that is, by defining a deductive system, and (b) using a class of logical matrices. In each case, the following questions naturally arise.

(d.1)

Is there an effective procedure (in the sense of Section
2.5
) that, for a given finite deductive system, decides whether the abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
defined by this system is consistent, that is, whether
\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
?

(d.2)

Is there an effective procedure that for two given finite deductive systems decides whether these deductive systems are weakly equivalent, that is, do the abstract logics defined by these systems have the same set of theorems?

(d.3)

Is there an effective procedure that, for two given finite deductive systems, decides whether these deductive systems are equivalent, that is, do they define the same abstract logic?

Similarly, if abstract logics are defined by finite matrices, the following questions arise.

(m.1)

Is there an effective procedure, which, for a given finite matrix
M
, decides whether the abstract logic
\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}
defined by this matrix is nontrivial, that is, whether
L\textsf{{M}}\neq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
?

(m.2)

Is there an effective procedure that for two given finite matrices decides whether these matrices are weakly equivalent, that is, whether the abstract logics defined by these matrices have the same set of theorems?

(m.3)

Is there an effective procedure that given two finite sets of finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrices decides whether these sets define the same abstract logic? In view of Proposition
4.3.14
, this problem is equivalent to the following problem: Is there an effective procedure that, given two finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlases, decides whether these atlases are equivalent, that is whether they define the same abstract logic?

We do not ask whether a finite deductive system has theorems because to have theorems, it has to contain axioms or rules without premises. On the other hand, we do not ask whether an abstract logic defined by a finite matrix is nontrivial, for if the matrix contains at least one non-designated element, formula
p
, where
p
is a variable, is not a theorem. In addition, in questions m.1 and m.2 we take a finite matrix and not a finite set of finite matrices because any finite set of finite matrices has the same set of theorems as their direct product.

Problems (m.1) and (m.2) can be formulated for finite atlases. Let us observe that if
\mathcal{M}=\langle{\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}}\rangle
is an atlas, then

\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}\!=\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}}=L\textsf{{M}},

where
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\bigcap\mathcal{D}\rangle
; that is,
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}(\varnothing)=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}}(\varnothing)
, although the identity
\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}(X)\!=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}}(X)
, for all
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, is not necessarily true.
Thus, the problems (m.1) and (m.2) for finite atlases can be reduced to finite matrices.

It was observed in
[
Kuznecov, 1963
]
that the problems (d.1)–(d.3) are undecidable for a broad class of naturally defined deductive systems. On the other hand, the problems (m.1)–(m.3) have positive solutions for finite matrices: to the questions (m.1) and (m.2) are due to J. Łoś (cf.
[
Łoś, 1949
, Propositions 25 and 27*]
) and J. Kalicki (cf.
[
Kalicki, 1948
,
Kalicki, 1952
]
); the positive solutions to (m.3) were found in
[
Citkin, 1975
]
,
[
Zygmunt, 1983
]
and
[
Devyatkin, 2013
]
.

In this chapter we will look at solutions to the problems (m.1)–(m.3).

10.1
Abstract logics defined by finite atlases

In this chapter, we suppose that a language
\mathcal{L}
contains only a finite number of connectives and countably infinite set of variables. For each
n>0
, we denote by
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
the set of all
\mathcal{L}
-formulas containing variables only from the set
\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
. If
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we denote:

{X}^{(n)}:=X\cap\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}.

In what follows, for abstract logics defined by atlases and the notions of indistinguishably and restricted Lindenbaum algebra will play a key role.

10.1.1
Indistinguishable formulas

Definition 10.1.1
.

Given an algebra
A
of type
\mathcal{L}
, formulas
\alpha,\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
are
A
-
indistinguishable
, in symbols
\alpha\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta
, if

v[\alpha]=v[\beta],\textit{ for every valuation }v\text{ in }\textsf{{A}}.

(10.1)

It is not hard to see that
A
-indistinguishability relation (
10.1
) is an equivalence relation on
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
. (Exercise
10.1
.
1
)

Proposition 10.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
be an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas. Then, for any
\{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime}\}\cup X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, whenever
\alpha\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta^{\prime}
,

X,\alpha\models_{\mathcal{M}}\beta\iff X,\alpha^{\prime}\models_{\mathcal{M}}\beta^{\prime}.

Proof
immediately follows from definition of
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
and definition of the consequence relation defined by an atlas. (Exercise
10.1
.
2
)

Thus, if
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
is an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas, then, for each
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
-theory
D
and any formulas
\alpha,\beta
such that
\alpha\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta
,

\alpha\in D\iff\beta\in D.

(10.2)

That is, for each
\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

\alpha/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\ \subseteq D\ \text{ or }\alpha/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\cap\ D=\varnothing,

i.e. the
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-equivalence classes (aka
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-
cosets
) do not cross the boundaries of
D
(cf. Fig.
10.4
).

\begin{array}[]{lcr}{\kern 14.27847pt\hbox{${}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{}{}{5898240.0}}\end{picture}\vrule 17.94028pt\raise-15.0pt\hbox{$\hbox{${{\displaystyle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}}}$}\kern-40.11945pt\kern 40.11945pt$}\kern-58.05972pt\kern 90.0pt$}}\end{array}

Figure 10.1:
\sim_{{}_{\textsf{{A}}}}
-Equivalence partitioning

The notion of a depth of formula is instrumental in describing the complete sets of representatives of
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
.

Definition 10.1.2
.

Let
\alpha
be an
\mathcal{L}
-formula. The
depth
of
\alpha

(
denoted by
d(\alpha)
)
is a number defined inductively:

d(p)=0
for each variable
p
of
\mathcal{L}
,
d(c)=0
for each constant
c
of
\mathcal{L}
; also, if
F
is an
n
-ary connective of
\mathcal{L}
and
\alpha=F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
, then

d(\alpha)=1+\max(d(\alpha_{1}),\dots,d(\alpha_{n})).

It is not hard to see that
d(\alpha)
is a maximal nestedness of connectives in
\alpha
, or that
d(\alpha)
is a length of the longest branch in the formula-tree of
\alpha
. (See Section
3.1
.)

Example 10.1.1
.

Let
A
be an arbitrary nontrivial Boolean algebra. Then, every formula
\alpha
is
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-equivalent to its disjunctive normal form
\beta
. It is not hard to see that
d(\beta)\leq 3
: variables have depth equal to 0, the negations of variables have depth equal to 1, the conjunctions of variables and negations of variables have depth equal to at most 2, and the disjunctions of the conjunctions of the variables and their negations have depth equal to at most 3. We note that, even though the depth of disjunctive normal form does not exceed 3, the formula may be very long.

Lemma 10.1.1
.

Suppose that
\alpha,\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that
\alpha\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta
. Also, let
\gamma[\alpha]
be a formula having an occurrence of
\alpha
, while
\gamma[\beta]
be a formula obtained from
\gamma[\alpha]
by replacing this occurrence of
\alpha
with
\beta
. Then
\gamma[\alpha]\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\gamma[\beta]
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
10.1
.
3
)

Proposition 10.1.2
.

Let
A
be an
\mathcal{L}
-algebra and
X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
be a set of formulas of depth
<n
. If for each formula
\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
of depth
\leq n
, there is formula
\alpha^{\prime}\in X
such that
\alpha\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\alpha^{\prime}
, then for any formula
\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
there is a formula
\beta^{\prime}\in X
such that
\beta\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be a formula of depth
m
. If
m\leq n
the statement is trivial. Let
m=n+k
and we prove the statement by induction on
k
.

Basis
. The case
k=0
is trivial.

Assumption
. Assume that for each formula
\alpha
of depth
\leq n+s
, there is a formula
\alpha^{\prime}\in X
such that
\alpha\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\alpha^{\prime}
.

Step
. Let
\alpha
be a formula of depth
n+s+1
. Then for some
m
-ary connective
F\in\mathcal{L}
,
\alpha=F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}
. By the induction assumption, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, because
d(\alpha_{i})\leq n+s
, there is a formula
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\in X
such that
\alpha_{i}\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}
, that is for every valuation
v
in
A
,
v[\alpha_{i}]=v[\alpha_{i}^{\prime}]
. Then, in virtue of Lemma
10.1.1
, for each valuation
v
in
A
,

v[F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}]=F(v[\alpha_{1}],\dots,v[\alpha_{m}])=F(v[\alpha_{1}^{\prime}],\dots,v[\alpha^{\prime}_{m}])=v[F\alpha^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\alpha^{\prime}_{n}].

and hence,
F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}F\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\alpha_{m}^{\prime}
. Let us observe that because all
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}
are in
X
, we have
d(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})<n
and hence,
d(F\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\alpha_{m}^{\prime})\leq n
. By the assumption of the theorem, there is a formula
\beta\in X
such that
F\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\alpha_{m}^{\prime}\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta
and, hence,
F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta
.
∎

Let
A
be an algebra. Any set
\textbf{Fm}_{\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
satisfying the properties

•

\bigcup\{\alpha/\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}}\}=\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, and

•

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}}
,
\alpha/\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}=\beta/\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
if, and only if,
\alpha=\beta
,

is called a
complete set of representatives
of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}/\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
.

We observe the following.

Proposition 10.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}_{1}\rangle
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle
be two
\mathcal{L}
-atlases. And let
\textbf{Fm}_{\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}}
be a complete set of representatives of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}/\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
. Then the following properties hold:

\begin{array}[]{cl}(\text{a})&\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}}=\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}},\textit{ if, and only if, }\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}}\cap\textbf{Fm}_{\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}}=\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}}\cap\textbf{Fm}_{\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}};\\
(\text{b})&\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}}\subseteq\,\vdash_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}}\penalty\ \textit{if, and only if, for each}\penalty\ \alpha\cup X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}},\penalty\ X\models_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}\alpha\penalty\ \textit{entails}\penalty\ X\models_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}\alpha.\end{array}

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
10.1
.
4
)

10.1.2
Matrix equivalences.

We recall the definition of matrix equivalence introduced in
[
Zygmunt, 1983
]
.

Definition 10.1.3
.

Suppose that
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
is an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix. An equivalence relation
\sim
on
A
is a
matrix equivalence

(
on
M
)
if for every
a,b\in|\textsf{{A}}|
,
a\in D
and
a\sim b
entails
b\in D
. Respectively, a congruence
\theta
on
A
is a
matrix congruence
if it is a matrix equivalence.
Accordingly, given an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
, an equivalence relation
\sim
on
A
is an
atlas equivalence
if for each
D\in\mathcal{D}
and every
a,b\in|\textsf{{A}}|
, if
a\in D
and
a\sim b
, then
b\in D
, and a congruence
\theta
on
A
is an
atlas congruence
if
\theta
is an atlas equivalence.

It should be clear that if
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
is an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas, a relation
\sim
on
A
is an atlas equivalence (or matrix congruence) if, and only if,
\sim
is a matrix equivalence (or matrix congruence) for each matrix
\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
, where
D\in\mathcal{D}
.

The identity relation is an example of matrix congruence.

If
\mathcal{S}
is an abstract logic, then an equivalence
\sim
on
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
is a matrix equivalence if the following holds: For any
\alpha,\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

\alpha\sim\beta\implies\alpha\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\beta\text{ and }\beta\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}\alpha.

It is not hard to see that relation
\sim
defined as

\alpha\sim\beta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\alpha\vdash_{S}\beta\text{ and }\beta\vdash_{S}\alpha

is a matrix equivalence on
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}
, that is, on the Lindenbaum matrix of
\mathcal{S}
.

Suppose that
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
is an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix. Consider the partition
\{D,|A|\setminus D\}
. This partition defines a matrix equivalence which obviously is the greatest matrix equivalence on
M
. If
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
is an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas and
\sim_{D}
is a greatest matrix equivalence on
\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
, where
D\in\mathcal{D}
, then
\bigcap\{\sim_{D}\penalty\ |\penalty\ D\in\mathcal{D}\}
is the greatest atlas equivalence on
\mathcal{M}
. (See Exercise
10.1
.
5
.)

Thus, each
\mathcal{L}
-atlas (
\mathcal{L}
-matrix) has the greatest atlas equivalence (matrix equivalence). Let us note that this equivalence needs not to be a congruence. Nevertheless, the following holds.

Proposition 10.1.4

(
[
Brown and Suszko, 1973
]
, theorem 8)
.

Each
\mathcal{L}
-atlas has the greatest atlas congruence
(
matrix congruence
)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
. Recall that the class of all congruences on
A
is a complete lattice in which the join
\bigvee_{i\in I}\theta_{i}
of congruences is defined as follows (cf.
[
Burris and Sankappanavar, 1981
]
, theorem 4.7):

\displaystyle(a,b)\in\bigvee_{i\in I}\theta_{i}\iff\text{ there are }a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in\textsf{{A}}\text{ such that }

\displaystyle a=a_{0},a_{n}=b\text{ and }(a_{0},a_{1})\in\theta_{i_{1}},\dots,(a_{n-1},a_{n})\in\theta_{n}.

It is not hard to see that if
\theta_{i}
are atlas (or matrix) congruences, then their join is an atlas (or matrix) congruence. Thus, the join of all atlas (or matrix) congruences is the greatest atlas (or matrix) equivalence.
∎

Proposition 10.1.5
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
be a logical matrix. Then relation
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is a matrix congruence on the Lindenbaum matrix
\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{{M}}}=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}},\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}}(\varnothing)\rangle
.

Proof.

It should be clear that
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is an equivalence relation. Moreover, if
\alpha\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}}=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}}(\varnothing)
, then
v[\alpha]\in D
, for all valuations
v
. If
\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\beta\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\alpha
, then, by definition of
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
,
v[\beta]=v[\alpha]
and, hence,
v[\beta]\in D
, for all valuations
v
. Thus,
\beta\in\bm{T}_{\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}}
and
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is a matrix congruence.

Suppose that
F
is an
n
-ary connective,
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\dots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{n},\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\alpha_{i}\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Then, by definition of
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
, for each valuation
v
,
v[\alpha_{i}]=v[\beta_{i}]
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and, hence,

v[F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}]=F(v[\alpha_{1}],\dots,v[\alpha_{n}])=F(v[\beta_{1}],\dots,v[\beta_{n}])=v[F\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}].

and hence,
F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}F\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}
.
∎

Corollary 10.1.5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
be an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas. Then
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is an atlas congruence on the Lindenbaum atlas of the abstract logic defined by
\mathcal{M}
.

10.1.3
Restricted Lindenbaum matrices and atlases

Let
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
denote the subset of all formulas from
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
containing only the variables
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{S}
is an abstract logic and
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
is its Lindenbaum atlas. Then, for any
n>0
, one can define a
restricted Lindenbaum atlas
:

\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{S}}^{(n)}:=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{(n)}\rangle,\text{ where }\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{(n)}=\{D\cap\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ T\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\}.

Let us observe that if
T
is an
\mathcal{S}
-theory, that is
T\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}
, then the set
{T}^{(n)}
is closed in
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
relative to
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
in the following sense:

\text{for any }\alpha\cup X\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)},\,X\vdash_{S}\alpha\text{ and }X\subseteq{T}^{(n)}\implies\alpha\in{T}^{(n)}.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}
is structural and
\sigma
is a substitution mapping all variables into
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
, then for any
\mathcal{S}
-theory
T
,
\sigma(T)\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
and is closed in
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
relative to
\vdash_{\mathcal{S}}
.

In this chapter, dealing chiefly with abstract logics determined by
\mathcal{L}
-atlases and matrices, we use the following notation:

•

\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}:=\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}
and
\Sigma_{\textsf{{M}}}:=\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}}
, where
\mathcal{M}
is an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas and, respectively,
M
is an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix;

•

\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{M}}:=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}\rangle
and
\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{{M}}}:=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}},\Sigma_{\textsf{{M}}}\rangle
;

•

\Sigma^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}:=\{T\cap\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ T\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}\}
and
\Sigma^{(n)}_{\textsf{{M}}}:=\{T\cap\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ T\in\Sigma_{\textsf{{M}}}\}
, where
\mathcal{M}
is an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas and, respectively,
M
is an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix;

•

\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}:=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)},\Sigma^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}\rangle
and
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\textsf{{M}}}:=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)},\Sigma^{(n)}_{\textsf{{M}}}\rangle
;

•

\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]:=\textbf{Cn}_{\mathcal{S}_{\textsf{{M}}}}(\varnothing)
and

\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}]:=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]\cap\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
, where
M
is an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix;
1
1

1

Although
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]=L\textsf{{M}}
(the logic of
M
), however, it will be convenient to use the notations
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]
and
\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}]
in the same context.

•

given two
\mathcal{L}
-atlases
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
,

\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \models_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}\subseteq\,\models_{\mathcal{M}_{2}};

accordingly,

\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}];

•

given two
\mathcal{L}
-matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
,

\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \models_{\textsf{{M}}_{1}}\subseteq\,\models_{\textsf{{M}}_{2}};

accordingly,

\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{df}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\penalty\ \mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}].

The notion of restricted Lindenbaum atlas is just an example of a more general concept.

Definition 10.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{1}\rangle
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle
be
\mathcal{L}
-atlases.
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is called a
subatlas
of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
if
\textsf{{A}}_{1}
is a subalgebra of
\textsf{{A}}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\{D\cap|\textsf{{A}}_{1}|\penalty\ |\penalty\ D\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\}
. In particular, given
\mathcal{L}
-matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1},D_{1}\rangle
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{2},D_{2}\rangle
,
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
is a
submatrix
of
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
if
\textsf{{A}}_{1}
is a subalgebra of
\textsf{{A}}_{2}
and
D_{1}=D_{2}\cap|\textsf{{A}}_{1}|
.

Thus, in the terminology of Definition
10.1.4
,
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}
is a subatlas of
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\textsf{{M}}}
is a submatrix of
\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{{M}}}
.

Proposition 10.1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{1}\rangle
be a subatlas of
\mathcal{M}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle
. Then
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]\subseteq\,\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
. In particular, if an
\mathcal{L}
-matrix
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
is a submatrix of a matrix
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
, then
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\subseteq\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]
.

Proof.

Suppose
X\models_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}\alpha
. Let
v
be a valuation in
\textsf{{A}}_{1}
such that
v[X]\subseteq D
, where
D\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
. By definition, there is a logical filter
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
such that
D=D^{\prime}\cap\textsf{{A}}_{1}
. Thus,
v[X]\subseteq D^{\prime}
. Hence, by premise,
v[\alpha]\in D^{\prime}
, which implies that
v[\alpha]\in D
.

The second statement is obvious.
∎

An obvious consequence of Proposition
10.1.6
is the following.

Corollary 10.1.6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas. Then
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}]\,=\,\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{M}}]\preccurlyeq\,\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}]
. In particular,
\bm{T}[\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{{M}}}]\subseteq\bm{T}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\textsf{{M}}}]
.

Proof.

The first equality follows from Corollary
4.3.12.1
, the first inclusion from Proposition
10.1.6
.
∎

We note that every atlas congruence on
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{M}}
(matrix congruence on
\mathbf{Lin}_{\textsf{{M}}}
) can be restricted to
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}
(respectively, to
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\textsf{{M}}}
).

Let us observe that for any
\mathcal{L}
-matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
, relation
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
depends only on
A
and does not depend on
D
and hence, Proposition
10.1.5
yields that for any
D\subseteq|\textsf{{A}}|
,
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is a matrix congruence on Lindenbaum matrix
\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}},\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]\rangle
. Thus, the following holds.

Corollary 10.1.6.2
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D_{1}\rangle
and
\textsf{{M}}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D_{2}\rangle
be
\mathcal{L}
-matrices. Then

D_{1}\subseteq D_{2}\implies\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\ \subseteq\ \bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}.

Proof.

Indeed, if
D_{1}\subseteq D_{2}
, then
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\subseteq\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
and because
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is a matrix equivalence and the
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-cosets do not cross boundaries of
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]
and
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
(cf. Fig.
10.2
),

\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\ \subseteq\ \bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
.

\begin{array}[]{lcr}{\kern 49.73819pt\hbox{${}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{}{}{5898240.0}}\end{picture}\vrule
