Figure 10.2:
\sim_{{}_{\textsf{{A}}}}
-Equivalence partitioning

∎

In some cases, we the equality
\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{M}}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}]
may hold.

Proposition 10.1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
be an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas and
A
be generated by elements
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
. Then
\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{M}}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}]
.

Proof.

In view of Corollary
10.1.6.1
, it remains to prove that
\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{M}}]
, that is, for any set
X\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
,

X\models_{\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}}\alpha\implies X\models_{\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{M}}}\alpha;

and, in virtue of of the first equality of Corollary
10.1.6.1
, it suffices to show that

X\models_{\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}}\alpha\implies X\models_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha.

For contradiction, assume that there is a valuation
v
in
A
such that

v[X]\subseteq D\text{ and }v[\alpha]\notin D,\text{ for some }D\in\mathcal{D}.

We aim to prove that there is a substitution
\sigma
in
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}
such that

\sigma(X)\subseteq T^{(n)}\text{ while }\sigma(\alpha)\notin T^{(n)},\text{ for some theory }T^{(n)}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{(n)}.

Indeed, valuations in
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}
are substitutions mapping the variables of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}
into
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
. Assume that
\alpha
contains variables only from a set
\{q_{1},\dots,q_{m}\}
. Then, because
A
is generated by elements
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
, there are formulas
\alpha_{j}
in variables
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
and such that for every
j=1,\dots,m
,

v[q_{i}]=\alpha_{j}[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}].

Let us consider a substitution
\sigma
:

\sigma(q)=\begin{cases}\alpha_{j}\text{ if }q=q_{j},\\
\alpha_{1}\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

It should be clear that
\sigma
is a valuation in
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}=\langle\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)},\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{(n)}\rangle
.

Let

T:=\{\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sigma[X]\vdash_{S[\mathcal{M}]}\beta\}.

Then,
{T}^{(n)}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}^{(n)}
and
\sigma(X)\subseteq{T}^{(n)}
. We aim to show that
\sigma(\alpha)\notin{T}^{(n)}
.

Let
v^{\prime}
be a valuation in
A
such that
v^{\prime}:p_{k}\mapsto a_{k}
, where
k=1,\dots,n
. We observe that
v=v^{\prime}\circ\sigma
(cf. Fig.
10.3
). Therefore,

v^{\prime}[\sigma(\alpha)]=v[\alpha]\notin D,

while for each
\beta\in X
,

v^{\prime}[\sigma(\beta)]=v[\beta]\in D.

Hence,
\sigma(X)\models_{\mathcal{M}}\sigma(\alpha)
and consequently,
\sigma(\alpha)\notin{T}^{(n)}
.
∎

{{\textstyle v}}

{{\textstyle\sigma}}

{{\textstyle v^{\prime}}}

{{\displaystyle q_{j}}}

{{\displaystyle a_{j}}}

{{\displaystyle\alpha_{j}}}

Figure 10.3:
Refuting valuation

We observe that it follows from Proposition
10.1.7
that if a algebraic carrier of an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
is generated by
n
elements, then the logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
coincides with the abstract logic determined by the Lindenbaum atlas
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}
. Thus, if an algebra
A
is finite, say of
\textit{card}(\textsf{{A}})=n
, the logic
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
coincides with
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}}
. This conclusion is important because, as we will see in Proposition
10.1.8
, in contrast to the cardinality of
\mathbf{Lin}_{\mathcal{M}}
, the cardinality of
\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\mathcal{M}}
is finite.

Corollary 10.1.7.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
is a finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlas of cardinality
n
. Then for every
m\geq n
,
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}_{\mathcal{M}}]
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
10.1
.
6
)

Corollary 10.1.7.2
.

Suppose that
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
is a finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrix of cardinality
n
. Then

\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]=\bm{T}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(n)}_{\textsf{{M}}}]
.

We also note the following corollaries.

Corollary 10.1.7.3
.

Suppose that
\textsf{{M}}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D_{1}\rangle
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D_{2}\rangle
are
\mathcal{L}
-matrices and algebra
A
is generated by
n
elements
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
. Then

\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\iff\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}].

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
10.1
.
7
)

Corollary 10.1.7.4
.

Suppose that
\textsf{{M}}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D_{1}\rangle
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D_{2}\rangle
are finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrices of cardinality
n
. Then

\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\iff\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}].

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
10.1
.
8
)

A very important property of equivalence
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
, which will be used for constructing an effective procedure, is formulated in the following proposition.

Proposition 10.1.8
.

For any finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlas
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
and any natural number
n>0
, the set
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is finite.

Proof.

We need to prove that
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
partitions set
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
in a finite number of equivalence classes. The proof follows from the observation that each formula
\alpha\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
can be regarded as a function on
A
in
n
variables:

f_{\alpha}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=v[\alpha],

where
v
is a valuation with
v:p_{i}\mapsto a_{i}
. From the definition of
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
, it follows that for any
\alpha,\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
,

\alpha\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta\iff f_{\alpha}=f_{\beta}.

Hence, the cardinality of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
does not exceed the cardinality of the set of all
n
-ary functions on
A
, that is
\textit{card}(\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}})\leq k^{k^{n}}
, where
k=\textit{card}(\textsf{{A}})
. ∎

Suppose that
\sim
is an equivalence relation on a set
A
. A subset of
A
containing exactly one element from each
\sim
-equivalence class is called a
complete set of representatives
of
A/\!\!\sim
. Proposition
10.1.8
entails that if
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
is a finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrix, then a complete set of representatives of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is finite. Moreover, there is an effective procedure that, given a finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrix or
\mathcal{L}
-atlas, delivers a complete set of representatives of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
.

Indeed, let us observe that we can enumerate
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
; see Section
2.5.2
and Section
2.5.3
. To that end, we enumerate the formulas in order of growing depth: we take all formulas of depth 0, and then we construct all formulas of depth
n
based on formulas of lesser depth.

Because algebra
A
is finite, for any two formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
, we can easily determine whether
\alpha\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta
or not. Thus, after obtaining a new formula during enumeration of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
, we decide whether this formula is
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-equivalent to a preceding formula, and if it is, we omit this formula, otherwise we retain this formula as a representative of the respective
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-coset. And we stop when we determine that such a process does not produce any new representatives. Proposition
10.1.2
gives us a way to determine when to stop: if we did not retain any of formulas of depth
n+1
, we have obtain representatives of all
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-cosets.

Exercises
10.1

1.

Prove that that the relation
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
of
A
-indistinguishability is an equivalence on
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

2.

Prove Proposition
10.1.1
.

3.

Suppose that
\alpha,\beta\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that
\alpha\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\beta
. Also, let
\gamma[\alpha]
be a formula having an occurrence of
\alpha
, while
\gamma[\beta]
be a formula obtained from
\gamma[\alpha]
by replacing this occurrence of
\alpha
with
\beta
. Then
\gamma[\alpha]\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\gamma[\beta]
(Lemma
10.1.1
).

4.

Prove Proposition
10.1.3
.

5.

Prove that if
\mathcal{M}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\mathcal{D}\rangle
is an
\mathcal{L}
-atlas and
\sim_{D}
is a greatest matrix equivalence on
\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
, where
D\in\mathcal{D}
, then
\bigcap\{\sim_{D}\penalty\ |\penalty\ D\in\mathcal{D}\}
is the greatest atlas equivalence on
\mathcal{M}
.

6.

Prove Corollary
10.1.7.1
.

7.

Prove Corollary
10.1.7.3
.

8.

Prove Corollary
10.1.7.4
.

10.2
Enumerating procedure

We consider the formulas of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
as words of a finite alphabet (Section
2.5.2
). Actually, the set
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
is an effectively decidable word set. (Exercises
10.2
.
1
)

Now we aim to show that, given a finite algebra
A
, a complete set
of representatives of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
can be effectively generated.

If
A
has one element, then all formulas of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
are equivalent to each other with respect to
\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
. Thus a set consisting of any formula of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
is a complete set of representatives.

Now we assume that
A
has more than one element. We describe a generating procedure inductively.

Step

1
. We include all variables of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
as representatives of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
. Since
\textit{card}(\textsf{{A}})>1
, for any two distinct variables
p_{i},p_{j}\in\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
, there is a valuation that maps
p_{i}
in one element of
A
and
p_{j}
in a distinct element of
A
, that is,
p_{i}\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}p_{j}
does not hold.

If two
\mathcal{L}
-constants coincide in
A
, we include only one of them as a representative of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
.

Step

n
. For each
k
-ary connective
F\in\mathcal{L}
, we construct all formulas
F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
, where each
\alpha_{i}
has previously been selected as a representative and at least one of
\alpha_{i}
has depth
n-1
, i.e.
\alpha_{i}
has been selected on the Step
n
. Then we compare the formula
F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
with all previously selected representatives. If
F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
is
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-equivalent to one of them, we skip it; otherwise, we add
F\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
as a representative and thus extending the list.

If we have not selected a new representative on the Step
n
, then, by Proposition
10.1.2
, we have already selected representatives of all
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-cosets and thus obtained a complete set of representatives.

Example 10.2.1
.

Let
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\{\mathbf{0},\bm{\tau},\mathbf{1}\};\lor,\mathbf{0}\rangle
, where
\lor
is defined as in
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
(Section
3.2.4
), and let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\{\mathbf{1}\}\rangle
.

To obtain a complete set of representatives of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
, we do the following:

\begin{array}[]{l|l|l}\text{depth}&\text{formulas}&\text{representatives}\\
0&p,\mathbf{0}&p,\mathbf{0}\\
1&p\lor p,p\lor\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}\lor p,\mathbf{0}\lor\mathbf{0}&\\
\end{array}

It is not hard to see that neither formula
p
or
\mathbf{0}
is valid in
M
and thus,the logic of
M
is trivial.

Exercises
10.2

1.

Show that the set
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
is effectively decidable.

2.

Using Proposition
6.3.10
, prove that the formulas defined in (
6.12
) form a complete set of representatives of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
, where
A
is a Rieger-Nishimura algebra (Section
6.3.4
).

10.3
Solution to Problem (m.1)

We need to demonstrate that there is an effective procedure that, given a finite matrix
M
, determines whether
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]\neq\varnothing
.

The idea behind such a procedure is this: given a finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
, we enumerate the formulas of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}
, and for each formula, we test whether it belongs to
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]
, that is, we check whether the formula is valid in
M
. If we have found a valid formula,
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]\neq\varnothing
and we stop. The problem arises if
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]=\varnothing
, because in this case, the procedure does not stop. To alleviate this problem, we do the following.

(a)

Firstly, we reduce the problem to formulas in one variable: because set
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]
is closed relative to substitutions, if
\alpha\in\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]
, then
\alpha^{(p)}\in\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]
, where
\alpha^{(p)}
is a formula obtained from
\alpha
by substituting
p
for each variable. That is,
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]=\varnothing
if and only if
\bm{T}^{(1)}[\textsf{{M}}]=\varnothing
, and we need to list only formulas from
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}
, although, if
\mathcal{L}
contains connectives,
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}
is still an infinite set and we need to make further reductions.

(b)

Since the algebra
A
is finite, by Proposition
10.1.8
, the set
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}/\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is finite. In addition, if one of the representatives of
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-cosets is in
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]
, then all of them are in
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]
. Hence, it suffices to test just one of the representatives from each
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-coset.

(c)

All we need is to be able to enumerate the representatives of
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-cosets: one for each class, and we will construct a procedure that does precisely this. Then, for each representative
\alpha
we can check whether
\alpha
is valid in
M
; and if so, clearly
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]\neq\varnothing
, or we will exhaust the list of representatives and conclude that
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]=\varnothing
.

90.0pt$}}\end{array}

Figure 10.4:
\sim_{{}_{\textsf{{A}}}}
-Equivalence partitioning

Example 10.3.1
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},\{\mathbf{1}\}\rangle
, where
\textsf{{A}}=\langle\{\mathbf{0},\bm{\tau},\mathbf{1}\};\to,\mathbf{0}\rangle
and
\to
is defined as in
\textsf{{G}}_{3}
(Section
3.2.4
). We construct a complete set of representatives of
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}/\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
as follows.

\begin{array}[]{l|l|l}\text{depth}&\text{formulas}&\text{representatives}\\
0&p,\mathbf{0}&p,\mathbf{0}\\
1&p\to p,p\to\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}\to p,\mathbf{0}\to\mathbf{0}&p\to p,p\to\mathbf{0}\\
2&(p\to p)\to p,p\to(p\to p),(p\to p)\to\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}\to(p\to p),&\\
2&(p\to p)\to(p\to p),&\\
2&(p\to p)\to(p\to\mathbf{0}),(p\to\mathbf{0})\to(p\to p),&\\
2&(p\to\mathbf{0})\to p,p\to(p\to\mathbf{0}),(p\to\mathbf{0})\to\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}\to(p\to\mathbf{0}),&(p\to\mathbf{0})\to p\\
2&(p\to\mathbf{0})\to(p\to p),(p\to\mathbf{0})\to(p\to\mathbf{0}),&\\
3&((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p)\to p,p\to((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p),&((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p)\to p\\
3&((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p)\to\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}\to((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p)&\\
3&((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p)\to(p\to p),(p\to p)\to((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p),&\\
3&((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p)\to(p\to\mathbf{0}),(p\to\mathbf{0})\to((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p),&\\
3&((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p)\to((p\to\mathbf{0})\to p)&\end{array}

We leave to the reader to verify that every formula of depth
4
obtained from the selected representatives is
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-equal to one of the selected representatives. (Exercise
10.3
.
1
)

It is clear that abstract logic defined by
M
is not trivial: formula
p\to p
is a theorem.

Remark 10.3.1
.

In Example
10.3.1
, we could have stopped after selecting
p\to p
as a representative. We did not do this to illustrate how the enumeration procedure works.

Exercises
10.3

1.

Complete Example
10.3.1
by proving that every formula of depth
4
obtained from the selected representatives is
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-equal to one of the selected representatives.

2.

Let
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\};\land,\lor,\{\mathbf{1}\}\rangle
, where
\land
and
\lor
are defined as in the matrix
\textsf{{B}}_{2}
(Section
3.2.1
). Prove that
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]
is empty.

10.4
Solution to Problem (m.2)

Definition 10.4.1
.

Two
\mathcal{L}
-matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
are said to be
weakly equivalent
if they are weakly adequate for the same abstract logic.

One can rephrase the above definition:
\mathcal{L}
-matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
are weakly equivalent if
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
. In terms of Lindenbaum matrices, two matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
are weakly equivalent if and only if the Lindenbaum matrices of their respective abstract logic coincide.

We consider a bit more general problem: Is there an effective procedure that, given two finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
, determines whether
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\subseteq\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
? Clearly, if there is such a procedure, we also have a procedure that determines whether
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
.

To solve this problem we will do the following.

(a)

We will reduce the problem to a case when the matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
have the same carrier, more precisely, when
\textsf{{M}}_{i}=\langle\textsf{{A}};D_{i}\rangle
, where
i\in\{1,2\}
, and
D_{2}\subseteq D_{1}
and we need to determine whether
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
; cf. Section
10.4
.

(b)

We can reduce the problem even further to formulas on
n
variables: by Corollary
10.1.7.4
,
if
n
is cardinality of
A
, then
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
if, and only if,
\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
.

(c)

Furthermore, by Corollary
10.1.6.2
,
D_{2}\subseteq D_{1}
entails
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\subseteq\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
and hence,
\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\ \subseteq\ \bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
.
Thus, we need only to determine whether
\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}\ \subseteq\ \bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
.

By Proposition
10.1.8
,
\textbf{Fm}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
is a finite set. Thus, using the enumerating procedure from Section
10.3
, for each selected representative
\alpha
we can check whether
\alpha\in\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
and if it is, we can check whether
\alpha\in\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]
. It is clear that
\alpha\in\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{i}]
if and only if
\alpha
is valid in
\textsf{{M}}_{i}
and because matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{i}
are finite, we can effectively test whether
\alpha\in\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{i}]
or not. During such a procedure, either we found a formula
\alpha
such that
\alpha\in\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
while
\alpha
such that
\alpha\notin\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]
, that is,
\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\neq\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
, or after we have obtain a complete set of representatives, we can conclude that
\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\neq\bm{T}^{(n)}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
.

Reduction to the matrices with the same algebraic carrier

Let us consider the following operations on matrices.

Definition 10.4.2
.

Suppose that
\textsf{{M}}_{i}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{i},D_{i}\rangle
, where
i=1,2
, are
\mathcal{L}
-matrices. Then, let

\displaystyle(\text{a})\quad\textsf{{M}}_{1}\leftthreetimes\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1}\times\textsf{{A}}_{2},D_{1}\times|\textsf{{A}}_{2}|\rangle;

\displaystyle(\text{b})\quad\textsf{{M}}_{1}\rightthreetimes\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1}\times\textsf{{A}}_{2},|\textsf{{A}}_{1}|\times D_{2}\rangle;

\displaystyle(\text{c})\quad\textsf{{M}}_{1}\times\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1}\times\textsf{{A}}_{2},D_{1}\times D_{2}\rangle;

\displaystyle(\text{d})\quad\textsf{{M}}_{1}+\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1}\times\textsf{{A}}_{2},|D_{1}|\times|\textsf{{A}}|\cup|\textsf{{A}}_{1}|\times D_{2}\rangle.

As we can see, all matrices on the right hand side have the same carrier.

Proposition 10.4.1
.

Let
\textsf{{M}}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1},D_{1}\rangle
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{2},D_{2}\rangle
be
\mathcal{L}
-matrices. Then

\displaystyle(\text{a})\quad\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}\leftthreetimes\textsf{{M}}_{2}]=\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}];

\displaystyle(\text{b})\quad\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}\rightthreetimes\textsf{{M}}_{2}]=\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}];

\displaystyle(\text{c})\quad\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}\times\textsf{{M}}_{2}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\cap\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}];

\displaystyle(\text{d})\quad\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}+\textsf{{M}}_{2}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\cup\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}].

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
10.4
.
1
)

Corollary 10.4.1.1
.

Suppose that
\textsf{{M}}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1},D_{1}\rangle
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{2},D_{2}\rangle
. Then

(a)

\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\iff\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}\leftthreetimes\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}\rightthreetimes\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
;

(b)

\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\subseteq\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\iff\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}\times\textsf{{M}}_{2}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}\leftthreetimes\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
;

(c)

\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]\iff\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}\times\textsf{{M}}_{2}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}+\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
.

Proof.

We note that if matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
are finite, there is an effective procedure for constructing matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}\leftthreetimes\textsf{{M}}_{2}
,
\textsf{{M}}_{1}\rightthreetimes\textsf{{M}}_{2}
,
\textsf{{M}}_{1}\times\textsf{{M}}_{2}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{1}+\textsf{{M}}_{2}
. Moreover, Corollary
10.4.1.1
(b) entails that the problems to determine by given finite matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{1}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{2}
whether (i)
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]\subseteq\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
and whether (ii)
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{1}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{2}]
are equivalent.
∎

Prove Proposition
10.4.1
.

2.

Let
\mathbf{M}_{1}=\langle(\{\mathbf{0},a,\mathbf{1}\},\land,\lor,\to,\neg),\{\mathbf{1}\}\rangle
and
\mathbf{M}_{2}=\langle(\{\mathbf{0},a,\mathbf{1}\},\land,\lor,\to,-),\{\mathbf{1}\}\rangle
, where
\land,\lor,\to
and
\neg
are defined as in Heyting algebra, and
-

is Łukasiewicz’s involutive negation:
-\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{1},-a=a,-\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{0}
. For
\to
is a Heyting implication, the sets
\bm{T}[\mathbf{M}_{1}]
and
\bm{T}[\mathbf{M}_{2}]
are closed under modus ponens. Prove that
\bm{T}[\mathbf{M}_{1}+\mathbf{M}_{2}]
is not closed under modus ponens.
(Hint: Observe that
\neg\neg(p\lor\neg p)\in\bm{T}[\mathbf{M}_{1}]
and
\neg\neg(p\lor\neg p)\to(p\lor\neg p)\in\bm{T}[\mathbf{M}_{2}]
, while
(p\lor\neg p)\notin\bm{T}[\mathbf{M}_{1}]\cup\bm{T}[\mathbf{M}_{2}]=\bm{T}[\mathbf{M}_{1}+\mathbf{M}_{2}]
.)

10.5
Solution to Problem (m.3)

Given two finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlases
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, we need to determine whether
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
and
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]
, where
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
and
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]
are abstract logics defined by these atlases.

To solve this problem we take a path similar to the path we took to solve the problem of weak equivalence of matrices, and we will do the following.

(a)

We can reduce the problem to a case when the atlases
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
have the same carrier, let say
A
(cf. Proposition
10.5.1
).

(b)

We can reduce the problem further: if
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
are
\mathcal{L}
-atlases of cardinalities
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
,
by Corollary
10.1.7.1
, for
m=\max(n_{1},n_{2})
,

\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]]\text{ and }\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]]

and thus,

\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]\iff\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]].

(c)

Similarly to Step (c) from Section
10.4
, we can obtain a complete representatives of
\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}/\!\!\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
, let say
X
. Because by Proposition
10.1.8
the set
X
is finite, we can check whether for every
\alpha\cup Y\subseteq X
,

Y\models_{\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]}\alpha\quad\text{ yields }\quad Y\models_{\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]}\alpha

and in such a way we can to determine whether
S[\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]]\preccurlyeq S[\mathbf{Lin}^{(m)}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]]
, or not.

Reduction to the atlases with the same algebraic carrier

Suppose that
\mathcal{M}_{i}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{i},\mathcal{D}_{i}\rangle,i=1,2
are
\mathcal{L}
-atlases. Then, let

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{M}_{1}\leftthreetimes\mathcal{M}_{2}:=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1}\times\textsf{{A}}_{2},\{D\times|\textsf{{A}}_{2}|\penalty\ |\penalty\ D\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\}\rangle,\\
\mathcal{M}_{1}\rightthreetimes\mathcal{M}_{2}:=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1}\times\textsf{{A}}_{2},\{|\textsf{{A}}_{1}|\times D\penalty\ |\penalty\ D\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\}\rangle.\\
\end{array}

(10.3)

As we can see, atlases
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
have the same carrier.

Proposition 10.5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{1}\rangle
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}\rangle
be
\mathcal{L}
-atlases. Then

\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}\leftthreetimes\mathcal{M}_{2}]
and
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}\rightthreetimes\mathcal{M}_{2}]
.

Proof
is left to the reader. (Exercise
10.5
.
1
)

Thus, there is an effective procedure to determine whether two finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlases are equivalent. Because there is a procedure that

Remark 10.5.1
.

Let us observe that for any
\mathcal{L}
-atlases
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, by Proposition
10.5.1
,
S[\mathcal{M}_{1}\leftthreetimes\mathcal{M}_{2}]=S[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
and because
S[\mathcal{M}_{1}\times\mathcal{M}_{2}]=S[\mathcal{M}_{1}]\cap S[\mathcal{M}_{2}]
,

S[\mathcal{M}_{1}]\preccurlyeq S[\mathcal{M}_{2}]\iff S[\mathcal{M}_{1}\leftthreetimes\mathcal{M}_{2}]=S[\mathcal{M}_{1}\times\mathcal{M}_{2}].

Thus, the problems to determine by two finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlases
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
whether
\mathcal{S}{\mathcal{M}_{1}}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]
and whether
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]
are equivalent.

By Proposition 4.4.10, for any finite set
\textsf{{M}}_{i}
, where
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, of finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrices, there is a finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlas
\mathcal{M}
defining the same abstract logic. The proof of Proposition 4.4.10 gives an effective procedure how to construct
\mathcal{M}
. Therefore, there is an effective procedure which, given two finite sets of finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrices, determines whether these sets define the same abstract logic.

We note that Corollary
10.1.7.1
holds for the abstract logics understood as multiple conclusion consequence relations. Hence, the procedure that solves the problem of equivalence of two finite sets of finite matrices can be easily modified fo the multiple conclusion consequence relations.

.

\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}\leftthreetimes\mathcal{M}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}\rightthreetimes\mathcal{M}]=\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}]
from the definition (

), without using Proposition
10.5.1
.

10.6
Historical notes

In
[
Łoś, 1949
]
Łoś observed that if
S
is an abstract logic defined by a logical matrix
\textsf{{M}}=\langle\textsf{{A}},D\rangle
, for any
n>0
, one can consider a functional matrix:
\textsf{{M}}^{(n)}:=\langle\textsf{{A}}^{(n)},D^{(n)}\rangle
, where
\textsf{{A}}^{(n)}
is a set of all functions
f:|\textsf{{A}}|^{n}\longrightarrow|\textsf{{A}}|
,
D^{\prime}\subseteq\textsf{{A}}^{(n)}
consisting of all functions from
\textsf{{A}}^{(n)}
taking values only from
D
, and connectives are defined pointwise. Moreover, one can consider a submatrix
\textsf{{M}}_{e}^{(n)}
of
\textsf{{M}}^{(n)}
consisting of all functions expressible via superposition of fundamental operations of
A
. Such submatrices are closely related to restricted Lindenbaum matrices, and their interrelations were studied in
[
Łoś, 1949
, §15]
. In particular, if
M
is a finite matrix of cardinality
m
, then
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}_{e}^{(n)}]
for any
n\geq m
.

An effective procedure for enumerating logical functions on
n
variables defined on a finite matrix was suggested in
[
Wernick, 1939
]
. Let us observe that given a finite matrix
M
, one can effectively construct matrix
\textsf{{M}}_{e}^{(n)}
using approach similar to enumerating procedure described in Section
10.1.3
: recall that two formulas
\alpha
and
\beta
are
\sim_{\textsf{{A}}}
-equivalent if and only if
\alpha
and
\beta
define the same function.

Proposition 25 from
[
Łoś, 1949
]
states that abstract logic
\mathcal{S}
of a finite matrix
M
has no theorem if and only if the set of designated elements of
\textsf{{M}}_{e}^{(1)}
is empty and hence, there is an effective procedure to determine whether abstract logic defined by a finite matrix has theorems or not.

Two matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{i}=\langle\textsf{{A}}_{i},D_{i}\rangle,i=1,2
are
matrix isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism
\varphi
between algebras
\textsf{{A}}_{i}
such that
\varphi(D_{1})=D_{2}
. Proposition 27* states that for any two finite matrices
\textsf{{M}}^{\prime}
and
\textsf{{M}}^{\prime\prime}
of cardinalities
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
,
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}^{\prime}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}^{\prime\prime}]
if and only if matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{e}^{\prime(n)}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{e}^{\prime\prime(n)}
, where
n=\max(n_{1},n_{2})
, are matrix isomorphic. Thus, given two finite matrices
\textsf{{M}}^{\prime}
and
\textsf{{M}}^{\prime\prime}
, one can effectively construct matrices
\textsf{{M}}_{e}^{\prime(n)}
and
\textsf{{M}}_{e}^{\prime\prime(n)}
and check whether these matrices are matrix isomorphic and in such a way, to determine whether
\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}^{\prime}]=\bm{T}[\textsf{{M}}^{\prime\prime}]
.

An effective procedure to determine whether an abstract logic defined by a given finite matrix has theorems was suggested in
[
Kalicki, 1950b
]
. In
[
Kalicki, 1952
]
, Kalicki developed further his approach from his 1950 paper and constructed an effective procedure to determine whether two finite logical matrices define the abstract logics with the same sets of theorems, and he applied the same ideas to finite algebras: to determine whether two finite algebras having the same finite similarity type, have the same sets of valid equalities (in other words, whether two given finite algebras generate the same variety).

In
[
Kalicki, 1950a
]
Kalicki introduced and studied the operations on matrices: the direct product and sum of matrices (Definition
10.4.2
(c) and (d)).

Existence of the greatest matrix congruence was observed in
[
Porte, 1965
]
. We used a proof suggested in
[
Brown and Suszko, 1973
]
.

In
[
Citkin, 1975
]
, Citkin, using an approach similar to
[
Łoś, 1949
]
, described an effective procedure which given two finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlases determined whether thee atlases define the same abstract logic.
2
2

2

More precisely, the procedure in
[
Citkin, 1975
]
is for Smiley matrices, which can be viewed as atlases.

Later, in
[
Zygmunt, 1983
]
suggested an effective procedure that given two finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlases
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
(ramified matrices in Zygmunt’s terms), determines whether
\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]\preccurlyeq\mathcal{S}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]
.

In
[
Devyatkin, 2013
]
, the Kalicki’s approach from
[
Kalicki, 1950b
]
was used to construct an effective procedure which by two finite
\mathcal{L}
-matrices determines whether these matrices define the same abstract logic.

Even though there are effective procedures to determine equivalence of two finite
\mathcal{L}
-atlases, these procedures have a high complexity: it follows from
[
Bergman and Slutzki, 2000
]
that even problem to determine by two finite algebras whether they generate the same quasivariety, it is NP-complete. And for algebraziable logics, the latter problem is equivalent to the problem of determining equivalence of two finite matrices.
